## XX The Reverend Thomas Broad’s Exposition of Romans 8:7 / 二十、托马斯·布罗德牧师讲解《罗马书》八章七节

===Original===
Such was the Coleman household when Mr. Thomas Broad called one fine Monday afternoon about three months after he had been at college. He had preached his first sermon on the Sunday before, in a village about twelve miles from London in a northeasterly direction, somewhere in the flat regions of Essex. Mr. Thomas was in unusually good humour, for he had not broken down, and thought he had crowned himself with glory. The trial, to be sure, was not very severe. The so-called chapel was the downstairs living-room of a cottage holding at a squeeze about five-and-twenty people. Nevertheless, there was a desk at one corner, with two candles on either side, and Mr. Thomas was actually, for the first time, elevated above an audience. It consisted of the wheelwright and his wife, both very old, half a dozen labourers, with their wives, and two or three children. The old wheelwright, as he was in business, was called the “principal support of the cause.” The “cause,” however, was not particularly prosperous, nor its supporters enthusiastic. It was “supplied” always by a succession of first-year’s students, who made their experiments on the *corpus vile* here. Spiritual teaching, spiritual guidance, these poor peasants had none, and when the Monday came they went to their work in the marshes and elsewhere, and lived their blind lives under grey skies, with nothing left in them of the Sunday, save the recollection of a certain routine performed which might one day save them from some disaster with which flames and brimstone had something to do. It was not, however, a reality to them. Neither the future nor the past was real to them; no spiritual existence was real; nothing, in fact, save the most stimulant sensation. Once upon a time, a man, looking towards the celestial city, saw “The reflection of the sun upon the city (for the city was of pure gold), so exceeding glorious that he could not as yet with open face behold it, save through an instrument made for that purpose;” but Mr. Thomas Broad and his hearers needed no smoked glass now to prevent injury to their eyes. Mr. Thomas had put on a white neckerchief, had mounted the desk, and had spoken for three-quarters of an hour from the text, “The carnal mind is at enmity with God.” He had received during the last three weeks his first lectures on the “Scheme of Salvation,” and his discourse was a reproduction of his notes thereon. The wheelwright and his wife, and the six labourers with their wives, listened as oxen might listen, wandered home along the lanes heavy-footed like oxen, with heads towards the ground, and went heavily to bed. The elder student who had accompanied Mr. Thomas informed him that, on the whole, he had acquitted himself very well, but that it would be better, perhaps, in future to be a little simpler, and avoid what “may be called the metaphysics of Redemption.”

“No doubt,” said he, “they are very attractive, and of enormous importance. There is no objection to expound them before a cultivated congregation in London; but in the villages we cannot be too plain—that, at least, is my experience. Simply tell them we are all sinners, and deserve damnation. God sent His Son into the world. If we believe in Him we shall be saved; if not, we shall be lost. There is no mystery in that; everybody can understand it; and people are never weary of hearing the old old gospel.”

Mr. Thomas was well contented with himself, as we have said, when he knocked at Zachariah’s door. It was opened by Pauline. He took off his hat and smiled.

===Chinese===
托马斯·布罗德先生登门造访时，科尔曼家的光景便是如此——那是一个晴朗的礼拜一下午，他进学院约有三个月了。前一个礼拜日，他在一座村子里讲了生平头一篇道；那村子在伦敦东北方向约十二英里开外，坐落在埃塞克斯（Essex）平坦乡野的什么地方。托马斯先生心情好得出奇，因为他没有出岔子，自觉已经给自己戴上了荣耀的冠冕。诚然，这场考验并不十分严峻。那座所谓的礼拜堂，是一所村舍楼下的起居室，挤一挤约莫容得下二十五个人。尽管如此，一角上摆着一张讲桌，两支蜡烛分立两侧，而托马斯先生竟生平头一遭，被抬到了听众之上。听众是老车匠和他的老伴——两位都上了年纪——半打雇工各携妻子，还有两三个孩子。老车匠因为自己开着买卖，被称作「圣工的主要支柱」。然而那项「圣工」并不怎样兴旺，支持它的人也不怎样热心。讲道之职向来由一届接一届的一年级学生轮班「供应」，他们就在这里的 *corpus vile*（供试验的贱物）身上做他们的试验。属灵的教导，属灵的引领，这些可怜的农人是一概没有的；礼拜一到来，他们便到沼泽地和别处做工去了，在灰色天空底下过着他们昏盲的日子，礼拜日在他们身上不曾留下什么，只剩对一套行过场面的规程的记忆——那规程说不定有一天能救他们脱离某种与硫磺烈火有点瓜葛的灾殃。然而这于他们并不是一件实在的东西。未来于他们不实在，过去也不实在；没有什么属灵的存在是实在的；说实在的，除了最刺激官觉的感觉之外，什么都谈不上实在。从前有一个人，朝望天城，看见「日光照在这城上所起的反辉（因为这城是纯金的），极其荣耀，他还不能敞着脸观看，只能借着一具为此目的而造的器物来看」；而如今托马斯·布罗德先生和他的听众，却全然用不着烟熏的玻璃片来护眼了。托马斯先生系上一条白领巾，登上讲桌，就着经文「原来体贴肉体的，就是与神为仇」讲了三刻钟。前三个星期里，他刚听了「救恩体系」的头几堂课，他的讲道便是那些笔记的翻版。车匠夫妇、六个雇工和他们的妻子，像牛一样听着，又像牛一样，脚步沉滞、头颅低垂，沿着小巷一路走回家去，沉沉地上床睡了。陪同前来的那位高年级学生告诉他：总的说来他表现得很不错，只是往后或许还是简单一点为好，避免那些「或可称为救赎之形而上学」的东西。

「毫无疑问，」他说，「那些东西非常引人入胜，也极其紧要。在伦敦有教养的会众面前阐释它们，无可非议；可在乡下，我们越浅白越好——至少我的经验如此。只管告诉他们：我们都是罪人，理当沉沦。上帝差祂的儿子到世上来。我们若信祂，就必得救；若不信，就必灭亡。这里面没有一点奥秘；人人都能明白；人对这古而又古的福音，是百听不厌的。」

如前所说，托马斯先生叩响撒迦利亚家门的时候，正对自己十二分地满意。开门的是波琳。他脱下帽子，面带微笑。

===Original===
“My name is Broad. I come from Cowfold, and know the Allens very well. I am now living in London, and having heard of you so often, I thought I should like to call.”

“Pray come in,” she said; “I am very glad to see you. I wish my father were here.”

He was shown into the little front room, and after some inquiries about his relations Pauline asked him where was his abode in London.

“At the Independent College. I am studying for the ministry.”

===Chinese===
「我姓布罗德。我从考福尔德来，同艾伦一家很熟。我如今住在伦敦，时常听人说起您，心想应该来拜访拜访。」

「请进，」她说，「见到您我很高兴。只愿我父亲在家才好。」

他被让进那间小小的前厅，波琳略略问了几句他家里人的情形，随后问起他在伦敦的住处。

「在不信国教学院。我在预备领受牧职。」

===Original===
Pauline was not quite sure what “the ministry” meant; but as Mr. Thomas had yesterday’s white tie round his neck—he always “dirtied out” the Sunday’s neckerchief on Monday, and wore a black one on the other weekdays—she guessed his occupation.

“Dear me! you must be tired with walking so far.”

“Oh no, not tired with walking; but the fact is I am a little Mondayish.”

“A little what?”

Mr. Thomas giggled a little. “Ah, you young ladies, of course, don’t know what that means. I had to conduct a service in the country yesterday, and am rather fatigued. I am generally so on Mondays, and I always relax on that day.” This, it is to be remembered, was his first Monday.

===Chinese===
「牧职」究竟是什么，波琳不十分了然；但见托马斯先生颈间还系着昨日的那条白领巾——他照例在礼拜一还「接着用脏」礼拜日的那条领巾，其余平日才换黑的——也就猜出了他的职业。

「哎呀！走了这么远的路，您一定累了。」

「哦不，走路倒不累；只是实话对您说，我有点儿礼拜一倦怠。」

「有点儿什么？」

托马斯先生咯咯笑了两声。「啊，你们年轻小姐当然不懂这是什么意思。我昨天下乡主领了一场礼拜，这会儿颇有些疲乏。我礼拜一向来如此，这一天我总要松快松快。」——不过要知道，这还是他生平头一个礼拜一。

===Original===
Pauline regretted very much that she had no wine in the house; neither had they any beer. They were not total abstainers, but nothing of the kind was kept in their small store-closet.

“Oh, thank you; never mind.” He took a bottle of smelling-salts from the mantelpiece and smelt it. The conversation flagged a little. Pauline sat at the window, and Mr. Thomas at the table. At last he observed.

“Are you alone all day?”

“Generally, except on Sunday. Father does not get home till late.”

===Chinese===
波琳深为懊恼，家里没有酒，啤酒也没有。他们父女并非滴酒不沾的人，只是那只小小的储藏柜里从不备这一类东西。

「哦，多谢；不必费心。」他从壁炉台上拿起一瓶嗅盐来嗅了嗅。谈话有些冷场。波琳坐在窗边，托马斯先生坐在桌旁。末了他开言道。

「您整天都一个人在家吗？」

「通常是，除了礼拜日。父亲要到很晚才到家。」

===Original===
“Dear me! And you are not dull nor afraid?”

“Dull or afraid! Why?”

“Oh, well,” he sniggered, “dull—why, young ladies, you know, usually like society. At least,” and he laughed a little greasy laugh at his wit, “we like theirs. And then—afraid—well, if my sister were so attractive”—he looked to see if this pretty compliment was effective—“I should not like her to be without anybody in the house.”

Pauline became impatient. She rose. “When you come again,” she said, “I hope my father will be here.”

===Chinese===
「哎呀！那您不觉得闷，也不害怕吗？」

「闷！害怕！为什么？」

「哦，这个嘛，」他嗤嗤地笑道，「闷——呃，年轻小姐们，您知道，通常是爱交际的。至少，」他为自己的俏皮话发出一声油腻腻的笑，「我们爱慕她们的交际。再说——害怕——唔，倘若我妹妹生得这样动人」——他瞟了一眼，看这句漂亮的恭维是否奏效——「我就不愿意让她独自一人守着屋子。」

波琳不耐烦起来。她站起身。「您下回来的时候，」她说，「望我父亲能在家里。」

===Original===
Mr. Thomas rose too. He had begun to feel awkward. For want of something better to say, he asked whose was the portrait over the mantelpiece.

“Major Cartwright.”

“Major Cartwright! Dear me, is that Major Cartwright?” He had never heard of him before, but he did not like to profess ignorance of a Major.

“And this likeness of this young gentleman?” he inquired, looking at Pauline sideways, with an odious simper on his lips. “Nobody I know, I suppose?”

“My father when he was one-and-twenty.” She moved towards the door. Mr. Thomas closed his fat eyes till they became almost slits, simpered still more effectively, as he thought, trusted he might have the pleasure of calling again, and departed.

Pauline returned, opened the window and door for ten minutes, and went upstairs. When she saw her father she told him briefly that she had entertained a visitor, and expressed her utter loathing of him in terms so strong that he was obliged to check her. He did not want a quarrel with any of Isaac’s friends.

===Chinese===
托马斯先生也站了起来。他已开始觉得不自在了。一时想不出更好的话说，便问壁炉台上那幅画像画的是谁。

「卡特赖特少校。」

「卡特赖特少校！哎呀，这就是卡特赖特少校么？」他从未听说过这个人，却又不肯在一位于少校面前自认无知。

「那么这位年轻绅士的肖像呢？」他侧眼觑着波琳，唇边挂着一抹可憎的傻笑，问道，「想来总不是我认得的人吧？」

「是我父亲二十一岁时的像。」她朝门口走去。托马斯先生眯起那双肉滚滚的眼睛，直到几乎只剩两条缝，自以为愈发得体地傻笑着，表示盼望日后还有登门拜访的荣幸，然后告辞而去。

波琳回到屋里，把窗和门敞开十分钟透了通风，随后上楼去了。见到父亲时，她三言两语说了家里来过客，接着便用极重的言辞诉说对那人的深恶痛绝，重得父亲不得不出言止住她。他可不想同艾萨克的任何一个朋友闹翻。

===Original===
Mr. Thomas, having returned to the college, did not delay to communicate by mysterious hints to his colleagues that he was on visiting terms with a most delightfully charming person, and sunned himself deliciously in their bantering congratulations. About three weeks afterwards he thought he might safely repeat his visit; but he was in a difficulty. He was not quite so stupid as not to see that, the next time he went, it ought to be when her father was present, and yet he preferred his absence. At last he determined he would go about teatime. He was quite sure that Mr. Coleman would not have returned then; but he could assume that he had, and would propose to wait for him. He therefore duly presented himself at half-past five.

“Good evening, Miss Coleman. Is your father at home?”

“No, not yet,” replied Pauline, holding the door doubtingly.

“Oh, I am so sorry;” and, to Pauline’s surprise, he entered without any further ceremony. She hardly knew what to do; but she followed him as he walked into the room, where she had just laid the tea-things and put the bread and butter on the table.

===Chinese===
托马斯先生回到学院，不多耽搁，便用种种神秘的暗示让同窗们知道，他正与一位娇媚动人的小姐有着登门之谊，随后在他们打趣的道贺声里美滋滋地晒着太阳。约莫三个星期之后，他觉得再去一趟是稳妥的了；只是他有一桩为难处。他还不算蠢到看不出：下一回去，理当趁着姑娘的父亲在家；可他偏盼着他不在。末了他打定主意，趁吃茶的时辰去。他确信那个钟点科尔曼先生断乎还没回家；却可以装作他已回来，提出要等他。于是五点半，他如期出现在门前。

「晚上好，科尔曼小姐。令尊在家吗？」

「还没有，」波琳答道，迟疑地扶着门。

「哦，真遗憾。」说罢竟不再等让，径自走了进来，教波琳好生诧异。她一时不知如何是好，只得跟着他走进屋里——她刚在那里摆好茶具，把面包黄油放上了桌。

===Original===
“Oh, tea!” he cried. “Dear me, it would be very rude of me to ask myself to tea, and yet, do you know, Miss Coleman, I can hardly help it.”

“I am afraid my father will not be here till eight.” He sat down.

“That is very unfortunate. You will tell him I came on purpose to see him.”

Pauline hesitated whether she should or should not inform Mr. Thomas that his presence was disagreeable, but her father’s caution recurred to her, and she poured out a cup for her visitor.

It was one of his peculiarities that tea, of which he took enormous quantities, made him garrulous, and he expatiated much upon his college. By degrees, however, he became silent, and as he was sitting with his face to the window, he shifted his chair to the opposite side, under the pretence that the light dazzled his eyes. Pauline shifted too, apparently to make room for him, but really to get farther from him.

===Chinese===
「哦，用茶呢！」他叫道。「哎呀，我自己开口讨茶喝，未免太失礼了；可是您知道吗，科尔曼小姐，我实在按捺不住。」

「恐怕我父亲八点以前回不来。」他坐下了。

「那太不巧了。请您告诉他，我是特意来见他的。」

要不要让托马斯先生明白，他的在场讨人嫌，波琳犹豫了一下；但父亲谨慎的叮嘱回到心头，她便给客人斟了一杯茶。

他的癖性之一，是茶——他茶量大得惊人——喝多了便要饶舌，于是大谈特谈起他的学院来。然而渐渐地他不作声了；他本坐在面朝窗子的地方，便把椅子挪到对面去，借口光线晃得他眼睛发花。波琳也挪了挪，看样子是给他腾地方，其实是想离他更远些。

===Original===
“Do people generally say that you take after your mother?” he said.

“I believe I am like my mother in many things.”

Another pause. He became fidgety; the half smile, half grin which he almost perpetually wore passed altogether from his face, and he looked uncomfortable and dangerous. Pauline felt him to be so, and resolved that, come what might, he should never set foot in the house again.

“You have such black hair,” he observed.

===Chinese===
「人家是不是常说，您生得随母亲？」他说。

「我想我在许多地方像我的母亲。」

又一阵沉默。他坐立不安起来；那副几乎永远挂在脸上的半笑半龇的神气，这会儿整个从脸上褪了下去，他显得又局促，又危险。波琳也觉出了这份危险，拿定主意：无论出什么事，再不能让他踏进这个家门一步。

「您的头发这样黑。」他搭讪道。

===Original===
She rose to take away the tea-things.

“I am afraid,” said she, “that I must go out; I have one or two commissions to execute.”

He remained seated, and observed that surely she would not go alone.

“Why not?” and having collected the tea-things, she was on the point of leaving. He then rose, and she bade him goodbye. He held out his hand, and she took it in hers, but he did not let it go, and having pulled it upwards with much force, kissed it. He still held it, and before the astonished Pauline knew what he was doing his arm was round her waist. At that moment the little front gate swung back. Nobody was there; but the Reverend Thomas was alarmed, and in an instant she had freed herself, and had placed the table between them.

“What do you mean, you Gadarene pig, you scoundrel, by insulting a stranger in this way?” she cried. “Away! My father will know what to do with you.”

===Chinese===
她起身去收茶具。

「恐怕，」她说，「我得出门一趟；还有一两件差事要办。」

他仍坐着不动，说敢情她总不至于一个人出门吧。

「为什么不能？」茶具已经收拢，她正要离去。他这才起身，她向他道了别。他伸出一只手来，她伸手相握，他却攥住不放，猛地往上一提，吻了那只手。他仍旧攥着；惊呆了的波琳还没弄清他在干什么，他的一条胳膊已经搂上了她的腰。恰在此时，临街的小栅门哐当一声弹了回去。门外空无一人；可这位托马斯牧师着了慌，转瞬之间，她已挣脱出身，把一张桌子横亘在两人之间。

「你是什么意思，你这加大拉的猪，你这恶棍，竟这样侮辱一个外乡人！」她厉声喊道。「滚！我父亲自会知道该怎么处置你。」

===Original===
“Oh, if you please, Miss Coleman, pray say nothing about it, pray do not mention it to your father; I do not know what the consequences will be; I really meant nothing; I really did not”—which was entirely true.

“You who propose to teach religion to people! I ought to stop you; but no, I will not be dragged into the mud.”

A sudden thought struck her. He was shaky, and was holding on by the table. “I will be silent,” she cried—what a relief it was to him to hear her say that!—“but I will mark you,” and before he could comprehend what she was doing she had seized a little pair of scissors which lay near her, had caught his wrist, and had scored a deep cross on the back of the hand. The blood burst out and she threw him a handkerchief.

“Take that and be gone!”

He was so amazed and terrified, not only at the sight of the blood, but at her extraordinary behaviour, that he turned ghastly white. The pain, however, recalled him to his senses; he rolled the handkerchief over the wound, twisted his own round it too, for the red stain came through Pauline’s cambric, and departed. The account current in the college was, that he had torn himself against a nail in a fence. The accident was a little inconvenient on the following Sunday, when he had to preach at Hogsbridge Corner; but as he reproduced the sermon on the carnal mind, which he knew pretty well by heart, he was not nervous. He had made it much simpler, in accordance with the advice given on a former occasion. He had struck out the metaphysics and had put in a new head—“Neither indeed *can* be.” “The apostle did not merely state a fact that the carnal mind was not subject to the law of God; he said, ‘Neither indeed *can* be.’ Mark, my brethren, the force of the neither can.”

===Chinese===
「哦，求求您，科尔曼小姐，千万别声张，千万别向我父亲提起；我不知道会有什么后果；我真的没有别的意思；真的没有」——这话倒完完全全是真的。

「你这样的人，竟还打算教导世人宗教！我本该拦住你；可是不，我不愿被拖进泥潭里去。」

她忽然心生一念。他正抖着，扶着桌子才站得稳。「我可以不说，」她喊道——听到这句应许，他心里是何等的宽慰啊！——「但我要给你做个记号。」不等他明白过来她在做什么，她已抓起手边的一把小剪刀，攥住他的手腕，在他手背上深深划了一个十字。血登时涌了出来，她丢给他一方手帕。

「拿去，滚吧！」

他又是惊骇又是惶恐——不仅因为看见了血，更因为她这异乎寻常的举动——脸色白得死人一般。然而疼痛唤回了他的神智；他把手帕裹在伤口上，又把自己那条缠在外面，因为红斑已经从波琳的麻纱帕上渗了出来——随后走了。学院里通行的说法是：他叫栅栏上的一枚钉子给划破了。这点意外到下一个礼拜日稍感不便，因为那天他要在霍格斯布里奇角（Hogsbridge Corner）讲道；不过他把那篇论体贴肉体之心思的讲道原样重讲了一遍——内容他早已烂熟于胸——所以并不紧张。依照前次得到的劝告，他已把这篇道改得简单多了。形而上学的部分删去了，添上了一个新的分点——「也是不能服」。「使徒并不只是陈述一个事实，说体贴肉体的心思不服神的律法；他说：『也是不能服。』弟兄们哪，请留意『也是不能』这几个字的分量。」

## IV A Friend of the People / 四、人民之友

===Original===
The Friends of the People continued their meetings, and Zachariah attended regularly, although, after about three months’ experience, he began to doubt whether any advance was being made. The immediate subject of discussion now was a projected meeting in Spitalfields, and each branch of the Society was to organise its own contingent. All this was perfectly harmless. There was a good deal of wild talk occasionally; but it mostly came from Mr. Secretary, especially when he had had his beer. One evening he had taken more than enough, and was decidedly staggering as he walked down Lamb’s Conduit Street homewards. Zachariah was at some distance, and in front of him, in close converse, were his shoemaking friend, the Major, and a third man whom he could not recognise. The Secretary swayed himself across Holborn and into Chancery Lane, the others following. Presently they came up to him, passed him, and turned off to the left, leaving him to continue his troubled voyage southwards. The night air, however, was a little too much for him, and when he got to Fleet Street he was under the necessity of supporting himself against a wall. He became more and more seditious as he became more and more muddled, so that at last he attracted the attention of a constable who laid hold of him and locked him up for the night. In the morning he was very much surprised to find himself in a cell, feeling very miserable, charged with being drunk and disorderly, and, what was ten times worse, with uttering blasphemy against the Prince Regent. It may as well be mentioned here that the greatest precautions had been taken to prevent any knowledge by the authorities of the proceedings of the Friends of the People. The Habeas Corpus Act was not yet suspended, but the times were exceedingly dangerous. The Friends, therefore, never left in a body nor by the same door. Watch was always kept with the utmost strictness, not only on the stairs, but from a window which commanded the street. No written summons was ever sent to attend any meeting, ordinary or extraordinary. Mr. Secretary, therefore, was much disconcerted when he found that his pockets were emptied of all his official documents. He languished in his cell till about twelve o’clock, very sick and very anxious, when he was put into a cab, and, to his great surprise, instead of being taken to a police court, was carried to Whitehall. There he was introduced to an elderly gentleman, who sat at the head of a long table covered with green cloth. A younger man, apparently a clerk, sat at a smaller table by the fire and wrote, seeming to take no notice whatever of what was going on. Mr. Secretary expected to hear something about transportation, and to be denounced as an enemy of the human race; but he was pleasantly disappointed.

“Sorry to see a respectable person like you in such a position.”

Mr. Secretary wondered how the gentleman knew he was respectable; but was silent. He was not now in an eloquent or seditious humour.

“You may imagine that we know you, or we should not have taken the trouble to bring you here. We should merely have had you committed for trial.”

===Chinese===
人民之友社的会议照常继续，撒迦利亚每会必到，只是约莫三个月的经历下来，他开始怀疑究竟有没有什么进展。眼下议到的题目，是在斯皮塔菲尔兹（Spitalfields）筹划的一场集会，社里的各个分部分头组织各自的人马。这一切全都无害之至。偶尔也颇有几分狂放的高论，但多半出自秘书先生之口，尤其是在他灌过啤酒之后。一天晚上，他喝得过了量，沿兰姆康迪特街（Lamb's Conduit Street）走回家去，步子分明已经摇摇晃晃。撒迦利亚落在后面一段距离，他前头有三个人正凑在一处密谈——他的鞋匠朋友、少校，还有一个他认不出来的人。秘书摇摇摆摆穿过霍尔本（Holborn），进了大法官巷（Chancery Lane），那三个人跟在后面。不多时，他们赶上他，又越过他，向左一拐走掉了，撇下他自个儿继续那趟坎坷的南下航程。然而夜里的空气他也有些受不住，走到舰队街（Fleet Street），便不得不靠墙撑住身子。他越醉越糊涂，话也越发大逆不道，末了终于惹起一名巡警的注意，把他一把揪住，关押了一夜。第二天早晨，他发觉自己身处牢房，大为错愕，浑身难受——罪名是酗酒滋事，更糟十倍的，是对摄政王口出亵渎之言。在此不妨顺带一提：为了不让当局探知人民之友的种种活动，最周密的防范早就一一做到了。人身保护法（Habeas Corpus Act）尚未停效，可时局凶险已极。因此之故，人民之友散会从不结伴而行，也不走同一扇门。放哨向来极其严紧，不但在楼梯口，还从一扇俯临街道的窗子里守望。无论寻常会议还是非常会议，从不出具书面传唤。因此，秘书发觉口袋里的公文函件被掏了个精光时，心里大为着慌。他在牢房里恹恹地苦熬到十二点来钟，又难受又焦灼，随后被扶进一辆马车；叫他大吃一惊的是，车并没有驶往警察法庭，却把他载到了白厅（Whitehall）。在那里，他被引见给一位老绅士；那人坐在一张铺着绿呢的长桌上首。火炉边一张小桌前坐着个年轻些的人，看模样是个文书，只管低头书写，对眼前的事仿佛全不理会。秘书满心以为要听到些流放之类的宣判，还要被痛斥为人类公敌；结果却落了个愉快的失望。

「眼看你这样一位体面人落到这般田地，实在令人惋惜。」

秘书暗自纳罕，这位绅士凭什么知道他体面；但他没有作声。他此刻既没有雄辩的兴致，也没有犯上的心气。

「你可以想见，我们是掌握你底细的，否则也不必费这个事把你请到这儿来，径直把你交付审判也就是了。」

===Original===
The Secretary thought of his empty pockets. In truth it was the Major who had emptied them before he crossed Holborn; but of course he suspected the constable.

“You must be aware that you have exposed yourself to heavy penalties. I prefer, however, to think of you as a well-meaning but misguided person. What good do you think you can do? I can assure you that the Government are fully aware of the distress which prevails, and will do all they can to alleviate it. If you have any grievances, why not seek their redress by legitimate and constitutional means?”

The Secretary was flattered. He had never been brought face to face with one of the governing classes before. He looked round; everything was so quiet, so pacific; there were no fetters nor thumbscrews; the sun was lighting up the park; children were playing in it, and the necessity for a revolution was not on that particular spot quite apparent.

A messenger now entered carrying some sandwiches and a little decanter of wine on a tray, covered with the whitest of cloths.

===Chinese===
秘书想起自己空空如也的口袋。其实，趁他还没穿过霍尔本就把口袋掏空的，正是少校；不过他自然疑心到巡警头上。

「你必定明白，你已使自己面临重罚。不过，我宁愿把你看作用意虽好、却误入歧途的人。你想想，你又能成得了什么事？我可以向你担保，政府对目下普遍的困苦知之甚详，也必将竭尽所能加以纾解。你若有何冤屈，为何不循合法的、合乎宪政的途径，求一个申雪呢？」

秘书觉得受宠若惊。他还从不曾同统治阶级的一员面对面打过交道。他环顾四周；一切都是那么安静，那么祥和；没有镣铐，也没有夹指的刑具；太阳正照亮着园林；孩子们在园中嬉戏，而革命的必要，在这么一个所在，实在看不出个分明。

这时进来一名仆役，端着一只托盘，上盖一方雪白无比的桌布，摆着几块三明治和一小瓶葡萄酒。

===Original===
“It struck me,” continued the official, taking a sandwich and pouring out a glass of wine, “when I heard of your arrest, that I should like myself to have a talk with you. We really are most loth to proceed to extremities, and you have, I understand, a wife and children. I need not tell you what we could do with you if we liked. Now, just consider, my friend. I don’t want you to give up one single principle; but is it worth your while to be sent to jail and to have your home broken up merely because you want to achieve your object in the wrong way, and in a foolish way? Keep your principles; we do not object; but don’t go out into the road with them. And you, as an intelligent man, must see that you will not get what you desire by violence as soon as you will by lawful methods. Is the difference between us worth such a price as you will have to pay?”

The Secretary hesitated; he could not speak; he was very faint and nervous.

“Ah, you’ve had nothing to eat, I dare say.”

The bell was rung, and was answered immediately.

“Bring some bread and cheese and beer.”

===Chinese===
「我一听说你被捕，」那官员接着说，一面拿起一块三明治，斟出一杯酒，「就想亲自同你谈一谈。我们实在极不愿把事情做绝；而且据我了解，你有妻室儿女。倘使我们愿意，能把你办成什么样，这无须我多说。来，好好想想，我的朋友。我并不要你放弃一条原则；可是，单只因为你想用错误的、愚蠢的方式去达到目的，就值得你去坐牢、去闹得家破人散么？原则你只管留着，我们并无异议；只是别带着它们上街去。而你，作为一个明白人，总该看得出：靠暴力得到你想要的东西，断不会比走合法路子来得快。你我之间的那点分歧，值得你付出这样的代价么？」

秘书迟疑着；他说不出话来；他浑身发虚，心里发慌。

「啊，想来你还什么都没有吃吧。」

铃一摇，立刻有人应声而来。

「拿些面包、干酪和啤酒来。」

===Original===
The bread and cheese and beer were brought.

“Sit down there and have something; I will go on with my work, and we will finish our talk afterwards.”

The Secretary could not eat much bread and cheese, but he drank the beer greedily.

When he had finished the clerk left the room. The Commissioner﻿—for he was one of the Commissioners of His Majesty’s Treasury﻿—followed him to the door, closed it, not without satisfying himself that the constable was at his post outside, returned to his seat, opened his drawer, saw that a pistol and five guineas were there, and then began:

===Chinese===
面包、干酪和啤酒端了上来。

「坐到那边吃点东西；我接着办我的事，回头咱们再把话说完。」

秘书吃不下多少面包干酪，啤酒却贪婪地大口喝了下去。

他吃完之后，那文书便退了出去。专员——原来他是国王陛下财政部（His Majesty's Treasury）的专员之一——跟到门口，把门关上，关之前还特意确认那巡警仍在门外守着岗位，随后回到座位，拉开抽屉，看清一支手枪和五几尼（guinea）金币都在里头，这才开腔：

===Original===
“Now, look here, my dear sir, let me speak plainly with you and come to an understanding. We have made inquiries about you; we believe you to be a good sort of fellow, and we are not going to prosecute you. We do hope, however, that, should you hear anything which is—well—really treasonable, you will let us know. Treason, I am sure, is as dreadful to you as it is to me. The Government, as I said before, are most desirous of helping those who really deserve it; and to prove this, as I understand you are out of work, just accept that little trifle.”

The guineas were handed to Mr. Secretary, who looked at them doubtfully. With the beer his conscience had returned, and he broke out:

“If you want me to be a d﻿—﻿—﻿d spy, d﻿—﻿—﻿d if I do!” The Commissioner was not in the least disconcerted. “Spy, my man!﻿—who mentioned the word? The money was offered because you haven’t got a sixpence. Haven’t I told you you are not required to give up a single principle? Have I asked you to denounce a single companion? All I have requested you to do, as an honest citizen, is to give me a hint if you hear of anything which would be as perilous to you as to me.”

The Secretary after his brief explosion felt flaccid. He was subject to violent oscillations, and he looked at the five guineas again. He was very weak﻿—weak naturally, and weaker through a long course of alcohol. He was, therefore, prone to obscure, crooked, silly devices, at any rate when he was sober. Half drunk he was very bold; but when he had no liquor inside him he could *not* do what was straight. He had not strength sufficient, if two courses were open, to cast aside the one for which there were the fewer and less conclusive reasons, and to take the proper path, as if no other were before him. A sane, strong person is not the prey of reasons: a person like Mr. Secretary can never free himself from them, and after he has arrived at some kind of determination is still uncertain and harks back. With the roar of the flames of the Cities of the Plain in his ears, he stops, and is half afraid that it was his duty after all to stay and try and put them out. The Secretary, therefore, pondered again. The money was given on no condition that was worth anything. For aught he knew, the Commissioner had his books and papers already. He could take the guineas and be just as free as he was before. He could even give a part of it to the funds of the Friends. There obtruded, moreover, visions of Newgate, and his hands slowly crept to the coins.

===Chinese===
「喏，你听我说，亲爱的先生，容我把话跟你说个透亮，咱们达成个谅解。我们查过你的底细，相信你这人不坏，并不打算起诉你。不过我们诚心希望，倘若你听到什么——呃——真正够上叛国的动静，你能让我们知道。叛国这桩事，我敢保，在你听来同在我听来一样可怕。政府，正如我方才所言，极愿帮助那些真正值得帮助的人；为证此言不虚——听说你眼下没有活计——这点小意思，你就收下吧。」

那五几尼递到了秘书先生手里，他迟疑地望着。随着啤酒下肚，他的良心也回来了，于是脱口发作：「你要是想叫我当那号该死的奸细，见鬼去，我才不干！」专员丝毫不曾慌乱。「奸细，我的朋友！——这话是谁提起的？给你这笔钱，不过因为你连六便士也拿不出。我不是告诉过你，一条原则也不必你放弃么？我可曾要你告发一个同伴？我请你去做的，无非是尽一个诚实公民的本分：倘若听到什么对你我同样凶险的事，给我递个风声罢了。」

这通短暂的发作过后，秘书只觉得浑身发软。他的心绪素来剧烈摇摆，于是又朝那五几尼望了一眼。他非常软弱——天生软弱，长年的酒又给他更添一层软弱。因此他惯会弄些暧昧、歪斜、愚蠢的伎俩，至少在清醒时如此。半醉时他胆大包天；可肚里没有酒的时候，他却*干*不出堂堂正正的事来。每当面前摆着两条路，他总没有足够的气力，把那条理由又少又站不住的路抛开，径自踏上正路，仿佛眼前再无旁路一般。神志健全而坚强的人，不会沦为种种理由的猎物；像秘书先生这样的人，却永远挣脱不出它们的掌心，纵然打定了某种主意，仍旧拿捏不定，走两步又折回头。耳中轰响着平原诸城（Cities of the Plain）的烈焰，他停下脚来，心里还半存着畏惧：说不定留下来试着救那场火，到底才是他的本分。于是秘书又思量起来。这笔钱给得并不附带任何像样的条件。据他所知，说不定专员早把他的簿册文书搜到了手。这几尼他尽管收下，仍旧同先前一样自由。他甚至可以拿出一部分，捐给人民之友的基金。何况纽盖特（Newgate）的种种景象又一再闯到眼前，于是他的双手慢慢地挪向了那几枚金币。

===Original===
“I am a Radical, sir, and I don’t mind who knows it.”

“Nothing penal in that. Every man has a right to his own political creed.”

The fingers crept closer and touched the gold.

“If I thought you wanted to bribe me, I’d rot before I had anything to do with you.”

The Commissioner smiled. There was no necessity to say anything more, for the guineas were disappearing and finally, though slowly, chinked down into Mr. Secretary’s pocket.

===Chinese===
「我是个激进派（Radical），先生，谁知道了我也满不在乎。」

「这没什么可罚的。人人都有权抱持自己的政治信条。」

那几根手指又挪近了些，碰到了金币。

「我要是认为你想贿赂我，我宁可烂掉，也绝不跟你再有任何瓜葛。」

专员微微一笑。再不必多说什么了，因为那几尼正渐渐没了踪影，终于——尽管是慢慢地——叮当一声落进了秘书先生的口袋。

===Original===
The Commissioner held out his hand.

The Secretary before he took it looked loftier than ever.

“I hope you understand me, sir, clearly.”

“I *do* understand you clearly.”

===Chinese===
专员伸出手来。

秘书还没去握，神气却比先前愈发倨傲了。

「我希望你把我的话听得清清楚楚，先生。」

「我*是*听得清清楚楚。」

===Original===
The Secretary shook the hand; the Commissioner went with him to the door.

“Show this gentleman downstairs.”

The constable, without a look of surprise, went downstairs, and Mr. Secretary found himself in the street.

Mr. Commissioner drank another glass of wine, and then pencilled something in a little memorandum book, which he put under the pistol. The drawer had two locks, and he carefully locked both with two little keys attached to a ribbon which he wore round his neck.

===Chinese===
秘书握了那只手；专员陪他走到门口。

「送这位先生下楼去。」

那巡警毫无诧异之色，领他下了楼，秘书先生便发觉自己已经站在街上。

专员先生又饮了一杯酒，随后用铅笔在一本小记事簿上记下点什么，把它放到手枪底下。抽屉上装着两把锁，他用挂在颈间一条缎带上的两把小钥匙，仔仔细细把两把锁都锁上了。

## XXVI A Professional Consultation / 二十六、专业咨询

===Original===
Three months passed, during which the Allens’ pew was vacant at Tanner’s Lane. George remained at home with his only child, or was at his mother’s, or, shocking to relate, was in the fields, but not at chapel; nor were any of his family there. During the whole of these three months one image was forever before his eyes. What self-accusations! Of what injustice had he not been guilty? Little things, at the time unnoticed, turns of her head, smiles, the fall of her hair﻿—oh, that sweet sweet brown hair!﻿—all came back to him, and were as real before him as the garden wall. He thought of her lying in her grave﻿—she whom he had caressed﻿—of what was going on down there, under the turf, and he feared he should go mad. Where was she? Gone, forever gone﻿—gone before he had been able to make her understand how much he really loved her, and so send her to sleep in peace. But was she not in heaven? Would he not see her again? He did not know. Strange to say, but true, he, a member of Tanner’s Lane Church, who had never read a sceptical book in his life, was obliged to confess, perhaps not consciously, but none the less actually, he did not know.

In those dark three months the gospel according to Tanner’s Lane did nothing for him, and he was cast forth to wrestle with his sufferings alone. It is surely a terrible charge to bring against a religious system, that in the conflict which has to be waged by every son of Adam with disease, misfortune, death, the believers in it are provided with neither armour nor weapons. Surely a real religion, handed down from century to century, ought to have accumulated a store of consolatory truths which will be of some help to us in time of need. If it can tell us nothing, if we cannot face a single disaster any the better for it, and if we never dream of turning to it when we are in distress, of what value is it? There is one religious teacher, however, which seldom fails those who are in health, and, at last, did not fail him. He was helped by no priest and by no philosophy; but Nature helped him, the beneficent Power which heals the burn or scar and covers it with new skin.

At the end of the three months the Reverend John Broad received a brief note from Mr. Allen announcing that their pew at the chapel could be considered vacant, and that the subscription would be discontinued. Within a week Mr. Broad invited Brother Bushel, Brother Wainwright the cart-builder and blacksmith, and Brother Scotton the auctioneer, to a private meeting at his own house. In a short speech Mr. Broad said that he had sought a preliminary conference with them to lay before them the relationship in which the Allens stood to the church in Tanner’s Lane. They had formally ceased to attend his ministrations, but of course, as yet, they remained on the church books. It was a matter which he, as the minister of the flock, felt could not any longer be overlooked. He would say nothing of the part which the Allens had taken in the late unhappy controversies which had distracted the town, excepting that he considered they had displayed a heat and animosity inconsistent with their professions and detrimental to the best interests of the cause.

===Chinese===
三个月过去了，这三个月里，艾伦家在坦纳斯巷的包座一直空着。乔治或守着独子待在家里，或是在他母亲那边，或者——说来骇人——干脆待在田野里，只是不上礼拜堂去；他一家人也一个都没去。这整整三个月里，有一个身影始终浮在他眼前。多少痛切的自责！他有什么亏待她的事没做过？当时不曾留意的种种小事——她侧头的模样、她的微笑、她披垂的头发——啊，那甜蜜蜜、甜蜜蜜的棕色秀发！——如今全都回到他眼前，真切得就像花园的围墙。他想到她躺在坟墓里——他曾经抚爱过的她——想到草皮底下正在进行着的一切，他怕自己要发疯。她在哪里？去了，永远地去了——他还来不及让她明白他实在爱她有多深、好让她安然长眠，她就去了。可是她不是在天上么？他不是还能再见她么？他不知道。说来奇怪，却又是实情：他，坦纳斯巷教会的一名会友，平生从未读过一本怀疑派的书，却不得不承认——也许并非有意识地承认，却千真万确地承认——他不知道。

在那黑暗的三个月里，坦纳斯巷所传的那套福音帮不了他分毫，他被撇在一边，独自去同自己的苦难角力。每逢亚当的每个子孙不得不与疾病、厄运、死亡交战，而一种宗教体系竟既不给它的信众甲胄、也不给他们兵器——这罪名加在这样的体系头上，委实是可怕的。一种真正的宗教，一代又一代传下来，总该积攒下一批安慰人的真理，在我们急难之时能帮上一把。倘若它无一言可告，倘若有了它，我们面对任何一场灾祸也不见得从容一分，倘若我们困厄之时做梦也想不到向它求告，那它还有什么价值？不过，有一位宗教教师，凡是身体康健的人它很少辜负，到头来也果真没有辜负他。没有牧师帮得了他，也没有哲学帮得了他；帮了他的是大自然，是那治愈灼伤的瘢痕、又给它覆上新皮的仁慈力量。

三个月期满，约翰·布罗德牧师收到艾伦先生一封简短的便函，告知他们家在礼拜堂的包座可以视作空出，认捐也将一并停缴。不出一个星期，布罗德先生便邀了布舍尔弟兄、造大车兼打铁的韦恩赖特弟兄（Wainwright）和拍卖商斯科顿弟兄（Scotton），到自己家里来开一次私下会议。布罗德先生作了一篇短短的致辞，说他请诸位来，是要先开一场预备会议，把艾伦家眼下同坦纳斯巷教会所处的关系摆到大家面前。他们已正式不再来听他讲道，不过当然，此刻他们的名字还留在教会的名册上。这件事，他作为羊群的牧者，觉得再不能置之不问。至于艾伦家在近来搅得全城不宁的那场不幸争端里所扮演的角色，他不欲置评，只说他认为，他们所表露的火气与敌意，既不合乎他们的信仰告白，也有损于圣工的最大利益。

===Original===
“I agree with that, Mr. Broad,” interrupted Mr. Bushel; “and I may say that, as you know, if you had done nothing, *I should*; for how any member of the﻿—gospel﻿—could live in﻿—and go on﻿—peace harmony with all men in the Church of Christ, I, at, least﻿—that’s my opinion.” Mr. Bushel was shortnecked, and shook his head always while he was talking, apparently in order to disengage his meaning, which consequently issued in broken fragments.

Mr. Broad resumed﻿—“I may, however, observe that George Allen was in company with the intoxicated mob which devastated Cowfold; and although he has asserted that he merely endeavoured to control its excesses﻿—and such appears to be the view taken by the civil authorities who have prosecuted the perpetrators of the outrages﻿—we, as members, my dear brethren, of Christ’s Body, have to be guided by other considerations. While upon this subject of George Allen, I may say, with as much delicacy as is permissible to a faithful minister of God’s holy Word, that I fear George has been﻿—a﻿—h’m﻿—what shall I say?﻿—at least led astray by an unhappy intimacy with a female residing in the metropolis who is an infidel. I have no doubt in my own mind that the knowledge of this fact accelerated the departure of my dear daughter, whose sorrow was of a twofold character﻿—sorrow, in the first place, with regard to her husband’s unfaithfulness, causing her thereby much personal affliction, which, however, endureth but for a moment, for she now inherits a far more exceeding weight of glory”﻿—Mr. Broad’s weekday and extempore quotations from the Bible were always rather muddled﻿—“and, in the second place, sorrow for her husband’s soul. I think we have distinct evidence of this intimacy, which I shall be able to produce at the proper moment. We have all observed, too, that whilst the Allens have not latterly attended Divine Service at Tanner’s Lane, they have not seceded to another place of worship. Finally, and by way of conclusion, let me remark that I have wrestled long with the Lord to know what was my duty towards these apostates and towards the Church of Christ. I considered at first I ought to remonstrate privately with Mr. Allen; but, alas! he has shown a recalcitrant disposition whenever I have attempted to approach him. I have consulted Brother Bushel on the subject; indeed, I may say that Brother Bushel had previously intimated to me the necessity of taking some steps in the matter, and had assured me that he could not any longer occupy the prominent position which he now occupies in the church﻿—so much, I may say, to our own edification and advantage﻿—if something were not done. We think, therefore, that the church should be privately convoked for deliberation. Brother Wainwright, what counsel have you to give?”

Brother Wainwright always had a heavy account with Brother Bushel. He was a little man, with a little round head covered with straggling hair, which came over his forehead. He sat with his hat between his knees, looked into it, scratched his head, and said with a jerk, “Oi agree with Brother Bushel.”

===Chinese===
「这话我赞成，布罗德先生，」布舍尔先生插嘴道；「而且我可以说，您是知道的，您要是什么都没做，*我*也要做的；因为教会——福音——里的人，怎么能住——而且还在——基督的教会里同众人和睦，我，反，正——就是这么个意见。」布舍尔先生脖子短，说话时头总是摇个不停，像是要把他的意思摇松抖落出来，于是那意思便总是一截一截、碎着出来。

布罗德先生接着说——「不过我可以指出，乔治·艾伦当时同那伙蹂躏了考福尔德的酗酒暴民是在一起的；尽管他声言自己只是想约束暴徒的过火之处——对那些暴行的凶犯提起公诉的民政当局，看法似乎也是如此——但我们，作为基督身体——我亲爱的弟兄们——的肢体，却须循别的考量行事。既然说到乔治·艾伦这个人，我不妨以一位上帝圣言的忠仆所容许的极限分寸，说一句：我怕乔治已经——呃——嗯——怎么说好呢？——至少是被一段不幸的私交引上了歧路，那对方，是住在伦敦城里的一个不信教女子。我心里毫不怀疑，我亲爱的女儿的离世所以提前，正因她知道了这桩事；她的忧伤本是双重的——第一重，是为着丈夫的不忠，这使她本人深受苦痛，不过这苦楚不过是至暂的，因为她如今承受的是极重无比的荣耀」——布罗德先生平日即席引用圣经，向来引得相当夹缠——「第二重，是为她丈夫的灵魂忧伤。我想，我们有确凿的凭据证明这层私交，适当的时候我自能拿出来。我们也全都看在眼里：艾伦家近来虽不来坦纳斯巷守礼拜，却也并未转投别的敬拜之所。末了，作为结论，请容我说：我曾长久与主摔跤，要明白我对这些叛道者、对基督的教会当尽的本分。我起初以为应当私下规劝艾伦先生；可是，唉！每逢我设法接近他，他都显出一副顽梗不驯的架势。这件事我已同布舍尔弟兄商议过；的确，我可以说，布舍尔弟兄先前就已向我示意，此事有必要采取若干步骤，并向我保证，倘若再无所举措，他便无法再居他如今在教会中所居的显要位置——那位置，我可以说，于我们自身的造就与益处良多。因此我们以为，应当悄悄召集教会共议。韦恩赖特弟兄，您有何指教？」

韦恩赖特弟兄在布舍尔弟兄名下总记着一笔重账。他是个小个子，顶着一颗小小的圆脑袋，头发稀稀拉拉，披垂到额前。他坐着，帽子夹在两膝当中，两眼望着帽子里面，搔了搔脑袋，猛地一迸说：「俺赞成布舍尔弟兄。」

===Original===
“Brother Scotton, what do you say?”

Brother Scotton was a Cowfold man, tall and thin, superintendent of the Sunday-school, and to a considerable extent independent of village custom. He was not only an auctioneer, but a land surveyor; he also valued furniture, and when there were any houses to be let, drew up agreements, made inventories, and had even been known to prepare leases. There was always, therefore, a legal flavour about him, and he prided himself on his distant professional relationship to full-blown attorneyhood. It was tacitly understood in Cowfold that his opinion in certain cases was at least equal to that of Mortimer, Wake, Collins & Mortimer who acted as solicitors for half the county. Mr. Scotton, too, represented Cowfold urban intelligence as against agricultural rusticity; and another point in his favour was, that he had an office﻿—no shop﻿—with a wire blind in the window with the words, “Scotton, Land Agent, Auctioneer, and Appraiser,” painted on it. On Mr. Broad’s present appeal for his verdict, he put himself in a meditative attitude, stretched out his legs to their full length, threw his head back, took his lower lip in his left hand, pulled up his legs again, bent forward, put his hands on his knees, and looked sideways at Mr. Broad.

“I suppose that Mr. Allen and his son will have the charges communicated to them, Mr. Broad, and be summoned to attend the meeting?”

“What do you say, Brother Bushel?”

===Chinese===
「斯科顿弟兄，您怎么说？」

斯科顿弟兄是考福尔德本地人，又高又瘦，主日学的监督，在相当程度上不受村俗的拘束。他不但是拍卖商，还是土地测量员；他也会给家具估价，每逢有房屋招租，他便起草协议、开列清单，甚至有人知道他还拟过租契。因此他身上总带着一股法律的气味，并颇以此自得，自命同羽毛丰满的律师行当沾着一层远房的专业亲缘。考福尔德人心照不宣地认定：在某些案子上，他的见解至少抵得上莫蒂默、韦克、柯林斯与莫蒂默（Mortimer, Wake, Collins & Mortimer）事务所——替半个郡当律师的就是那一家。斯科顿先生还代表着考福尔德的城里智识，足以同种地的土气分庭抗礼；他的又一样长处，是他有一间事务所——不是铺面——窗上挂着金属网帘，上头写着「斯科顿，地产代理、拍卖商兼估价师」几个字。这回布罗德先生出言请他裁断，他先摆出一副冥想的姿势：两腿伸到最直，头向后一仰，左手捏住下唇，随即又把腿收回来，身子前俯，两手按在膝上，斜眼瞧着布罗德先生。

「我想，布罗德先生，那些指控总归要通知艾伦先生父子，并且传他们到会吧？」

「您怎么说，布舍尔弟兄？」

===Original===
“Don’t see no use in it. All very well them lawyers”﻿—a snap at Scotton﻿—“come and argyfy﻿—I hate argyfying, I do myself﻿—never seed no good on it. Get rid of a man﻿—I do. ‘Sickly sheep infects the flock and pisons all the rest.’ ” These last words formed part of a hymn of which Brother Bushel was fond.

“What do you say, Brother Wainwright?”

Brother Wainwright, although he could do nothing but agree with Brother Bushel, and never did anything but agree with him, preferred to make a show of reflection. He again looked in his hat, shut his mouth fast; again scratched his head; again shook it a little, and with another jerk, as if announcing a conclusion at which he had arrived with great certainty, but after a severe mental effort, he said:

“Oi go with Brother Bushel, *Oi* do.”

===Chinese===
「看不出有啥用处。当律师的倒是会说——」这话是冲着斯科顿咬的——「来了就抬杠——俺恨抬杠，俺自个儿就恨——从来没见抬出啥好来。把人撵走——就得这么办。『病羊祸害羊群，把其余的也全毒坏。』」末了这两句是一首圣诗里的句子，布舍尔弟兄素来最喜爱那首诗。

「您怎么说，韦恩赖特弟兄？」

韦恩赖特弟兄虽然除了附和布舍尔弟兄以外一无所能，也除了附和布舍尔弟兄以外一无所为，却偏爱摆出一番深思的样子。他又朝帽子里望了望，嘴巴闭得紧紧的；又搔了搔头；又把头微微摇了摇，然后猛地再一迸，仿佛宣布一个他极有把握、却是在一番剧烈的思想劳作之后才得出的结论似的，他说：

「俺跟着布舍尔弟兄，*俺*是跟着的。」

===Original===
“Well,” said Scotton, extending his legs again and gazing at the ceiling, “I must nevertheless be permitted to adhere﻿—”

“Adhere,” interrupted Bushel. “What’s the use of talking like that? You always adhere﻿—what for, I should like to know?”

Scotton went on with dignity, not noticing the attack.

“Adhere, I was about to say, Mr. Broad, to my previously expressed opinion. I am not at all sure that the Allens have not a legal status, and that an action would not lie if we proceeded without due formalities. Tanner’s Lane, you must recollect, is in a peculiar position, and there is an endowment.”

===Chinese===
「那好，」斯科顿说着，又把腿伸直，眼望天花板，「我仍须获准坚持——」

「坚持，」布舍尔打断他。「这么说话有啥用？你老是坚持呀坚持的——图个啥，俺倒想知道？」

斯科顿不去理会这一击，自顾端庄地往下说。

「坚持，我方才正要说，布罗德先生——坚持我先前表示过的意见。艾伦家未必没有一种法律上的地位，我们若不循正当形式径自行事，人家未必不能对我们提起诉讼，这一点我全然不敢肯定。坦纳斯巷，您必须记得，处境特殊，而且有一份教俸基金。」

===Original===
Mr. Scotton had this advantage over Cowfold generally, that if he knew nothing about the law himself, excepting so far as bids at a sale were concerned, Cowfold knew less, and the mention of the endowment somewhat disturbed Mr. Broad’s mind.

“Brother Bushel is no doubt quite justified in his anxiety to avoid discussion, which will in all probability lead to no useful result; but, on the other hand, it will be as well, perhaps, to proceed with caution.”

“Well,” ejaculated Bushel, “do as yer like; you’ll see you’ll get in an argyfication and a mess, you take my word on it.”

“Suppose,” said Mr. Broad, his face shining as he spoke, “we hit upon a third course, the via media, you know, Brother Scotton”﻿—Brother Scotton nodded approvingly, as much as to say, “*I* know; but how about Bushel?”﻿—“the via media, and have a friendly meeting of the most influential members of the church﻿—a majority﻿—and determine upon a course of action, which we can afterwards ratify at the formal meeting, at which the Allens will be present. We shall in this way, it seems to me, prevent much debate, and practically arrive at a conclusion beforehand.”

===Chinese===
斯科顿先生比考福尔德一般人占便宜的地方在于：说到法律，他自己固然一无所知——拍卖场上的竞价不算——考福尔德懂得的却更少；而「教俸基金」几个字一出口，布罗德先生的心绪便有些纷乱了。

「布舍尔弟兄亟欲避免争论，那争论十有八九不会有什么有益的结果，他自然全然在理；不过从另一方面说，行事宜加谨慎，或许也是好的。」

「唉，」布舍尔脱口道，「随你们的便；你们等着瞧吧，准得陷进一场抬杠、一摊烂泥，俺这话就搁在这儿。」

「假定，」布罗德先生说，说话间满面生光，「我们选定第三条路，那条中间路线（via media），您是知道的，斯科顿弟兄」——斯科顿弟兄赞许地点了点头，那神情无异于说：「*我*自然知道；可布舍尔呢？」——「走中间路线，先由教会里最有势力的会友——一个多数——开一次友好的会议，议定行动方针，随后再在正式会议上追认，届时艾伦家也将到场。依我看，这样我们便能省去许多争辩，实际上是预先就把结论拿到了手。」

===Original===
“Yes,” said Scotton﻿—very slowly. “I don’t see, at the present moment, any particular objection; but I should not like to commit myself.”

“How does it strike you, Brother Bushel?”

“Arter that, I suppose Scotton ull want some sort of a dockyment sent. I’m agin all dockyments. Why, what’ll Allen do? Take it over to Collins﻿—Mortimer﻿—stamp it, ten-and-sixpenny stamp. What will yer do then?”

“No, Brother Bushel; I apprehend that it will be my duty as pastor to write to the Allens a simple letter﻿—a simple pastoral letter﻿—announcing that a church meeting will be convened at a certain hour in the vestry, to consider some statements﻿—charges﻿—naming them﻿—not going into unnecessary detail, and requesting their attendance.”

===Chinese===
「是的，」斯科顿说——说得极慢。「此刻我看不出有什么特别可反对的；只是我不愿把自己套进去。」

「您觉得怎么样，布舍尔弟兄？」

「这之后，俺估摸着斯科顿还要打发个啥文书送过去。俺反对一切文书。可不是么，艾伦会咋办？拿去交给柯林斯——莫蒂默——盖上印花，十先令六便士的印花。到时候你们咋办？」

「不是的，布舍尔弟兄；我想，作为牧者，我的本分是给艾伦家写一封平常的信——一封平常的牧函——通知他们，教会将于某时在副堂召开会议，审议若干陈述——指控——列出名目——不涉不必要的细节，并请他们出席。」

===Original===
“That’s better; that wouldn’t be a dockyment, I s’pose; and yet praps he might stamp that. Resolution arterwards. Time they were out of it. Come on, Wainwright, gettin’ dark.”

“Well then, we agree,” said Mr. Broad﻿—“happily agree; and I trust that the Lord will yet prosper His Zion, and heal the breaches thereof. Will any of you take any refreshments before you go? Will you, Brother Bushel?”

Brother Bushel did not believe in Mr. Broad’s refreshments, save those which were spiritual, and declined them with some abruptness, preferring much a glass of hot brown brandy and water at the inn where his horse was. Brother Wainwright would have taken anything, but was bound to follow Brother Bushel, who was about to give him a lift homewards; and Brother Scotton was a teetotaller, one of the first who was converted to total abstinence in Cowfold, and just a trifle suspected at Tanner’s Lane, and by Bushel in particular, on that account. Water-drinking was not a heresy to which any definite objection could be raised; but Tanner’s Lane always felt that if once a man differed so far from his fellows as not to drink beer and spirits, there was no knowing where the division might end. “It was the thin end of the wedge,” Mr. Broad observed confidentially to Bushel once when the subject was mentioned.

===Chinese===
「这就好了；那就不算文书了，俺琢磨着；可没准儿他连那个也能盖上印花呢。决议往后放。早该把他们撵出去了。走吧，韦恩赖特，天快黑了。」

「那么，我们意见一致了，」布罗德先生说——「圆满地一致；我信托主必还要兴旺祂的锡安，医治它的破口。临走之前，哪位用些点心么？您呢，布舍尔弟兄？」

布舍尔弟兄不信布罗德先生的点心那一套——只除了属灵的那一种——便有些生硬地谢绝了，宁可去他拴马的那家客栈，喝上一杯滚热的褐色掺水白兰地。韦恩赖特弟兄原是什么都肯吃的，却不得不跟着布舍尔弟兄走——人家要顺路捎他回家；斯科顿弟兄则是个戒酒主义者，考福尔德头一批皈依滴酒不沾的人之一，单为这一点，在坦纳斯巷就有点儿受疑，布舍尔疑得尤其厉害。喝水这件事，本算不得一种可以明言反对的异端；可坦纳斯巷总觉得，一个人一旦同旁人离得这么远，连啤酒和烧酒都不沾唇，这分道扬镳会闹到哪一步，可就没人说得准了。「那是楔子最薄的那一头，」有一回提到这个话头，布罗德先生曾向布舍尔私下里说过。

===Original===
The preliminary meeting, therefore, was held, and Mr. Broad having communicated the charges against the Allens﻿—absenting themselves from public worship, disturbance of the peace of the church, intercourse with infidel associates, and finally so far as George was concerned, “questionable behaviour,” as Mr. Broad delicately put it, “with an infidel female”﻿—it was determined to call them to account. There was some difference of opinion, however. It was thought by some that all reference to the election, direct or indirect, should be avoided, for the majority in Tanner’s Lane was certainly not Tory. But Brother Bushel seemed to consider this the head and front of the offence, and declared that if this were not part of the indictment he would resign. He also was opposed to giving the Allens any information beforehand, and, if he had been allowed to have his own way, would not have permitted them to attend. He would have them “cut off,” he said, “there and then, summararlilly.” He got into great difficulties with this last word, and before he could get rid of it had to shake his head several times. Others thought it would be dangerous to act in this style; and there seemed no chance of any agreement, until Mr. Broad once more “healed the incipient division” by proposing another via media, which was carried. It was determined that there should be only an allusion to the political charge. It was to be subsidiary. In fact, it was not to be a political charge at all, but a moral charge, although, as Mr. Broad privately explained to Brother Bushel, it would come to the same thing in the end. Then Mr. Broad, as he had suggested at an earlier stage, was himself to write a letter to the Allens, stating in “general terms” the dissatisfaction felt by the church and its minister with them, and requesting their appearance in the vestry on the day named. Brother Scotton was still malcontent, but as he was in a minority he held his peace. He resolved, however, on his own account, to acquaint the Allens with what had happened, and prepare them. They were no particular friends of his, but Bushel also was no particular friend, and his auctioneering trade had at least educated him, in the disputes amongst buyers, to hold the scales of justice a little more evenly than they were held by Bushel’s hands.

Neither George nor his father were much disturbed by any of the items in Scotton’s information nor by Mr. Broad’s letter, save the reference to Pauline. It is true it was very remote, but the meaning, especially after Scotton’s explanation, was obvious, and George was in a fury which his father found it very difficult to repress. For himself George did not care, but he did care that Pauline’s name should not be dragged into the wretched squabble. Father and son both agreed that the case should be laid before Zachariah; but when Mr. Allen came back from London he merely said, in answer to George’s inquiries, that Zachariah and himself were in perfect accord, and that at the meeting George was not to interfere.

===Chinese===
于是预备会议开过了，布罗德先生把对艾伦家的各项指控一一摆明——不来公众礼拜、扰乱教会的平安、与不信教之徒往来，末了，单就乔治而论，还有布罗德先生委婉措辞的、与「一名不信教女子」的「可疑行止」——大家决定把他们传来问责。不过也有几分意见相左。有人认为，凡涉及选举的话，无论直接间接，一概应当避开，因为坦纳斯巷的多数会友肯定不是托利党。可布舍尔弟兄似乎认定，罪状的要害全在这一条，声言这一条若不列入诉状，他就要辞掉不干。他也反对事先向艾伦家透露任何消息，倘若由着他做主，连准他们到场都不会。要把他们「革除」，他说，「当场，简——简而了之地」。他为最后这个词吃足了苦头，连摇了好几次头，才算把它甩脱。旁人却觉得这样办事未免冒险；眼见得毫无一致的指望，幸而布罗德先生又提出一条中间路线，「医好了初起的分裂」，遂获通过。议定：政治那一条只点到为止，居于从属地位；实际上，它压根算不得一条政治罪状，而算一条道德罪状——尽管布罗德先生私下向布舍尔弟兄解释过，归根到底原是一回事。随后，照布罗德先生先前提议的，仍由他本人致函艾伦家，用「笼统的措辞」说明教会及其牧师对他们的不满，请他们于指定的日子到副堂出席。斯科顿弟兄仍旧怏怏不满，但既居少数，便缄口不言。然而他私心拿定主意，要把经过情形告知艾伦家，让他们有个准备。他们算不得他什么特别的朋友，可布舍尔也算不得；何况他那门拍卖的营生，至少在买家们的争执里把他调教过一番，叫他端起正义的天平来，比布舍尔那双手端得稍稍平正些。

斯科顿透露的种种条款，还有布罗德先生的信，都不曾怎样惊动乔治和他父亲，唯独提到波琳的那一节是个例外。诚然，那话说得极隐约曲折，可其中的意思，尤其经斯科顿一解说，便昭然若揭，乔治登时怒不可遏，他父亲费了好大的劲才把他按捺住。为他自己，乔治毫不在意；可波琳的名字竟要被拖进这场乌七八糟的口角里去，这他在意。父子俩一致认为，这案子应当摆到撒迦利亚面前去；可是艾伦先生从伦敦回来，答复乔治的探问，只说撒迦利亚同他完全一条心，还说开会那天，乔治不得插手。

## XVIII A Religious Picnic / 十八、宗教野餐

===Original===

Occasionally, in the summer months, Tanner’s Lane indulged in a picnic; that is to say, the principal members of the congregation, with their wives and children, had an early dinner, and went in gigs and four-wheel chaises to Shott Woods, taking hampers of bread, cake, jam, butter, ham, and other eatables with them. At Shott Woods, in a small green space under an immense oak, a fire was lighted and tea was prepared. Mr. Broad and his family always joined the party. These were the days when Dissenters had no set amusements, and the entertainment at Shott mainly consisted in getting the sticks for the fire, fetching the water, and waiting on one another; the waiting being particularly pleasant to the younger people. Dancing, of course, was not thought of. In 1840 it may safely be said that there were not twenty Independent families in Great Britain in which it would have been tolerated, and, moreover, none but the rich learned to dance.

No dancing-master ever came into Cowfold; there was no music-master there; no concert was ever given; and Cowfold, in fact, never “saw nor heard anything;” to use a modern phrase, save a travelling menagerie with a brass band. What an existence! How *did* they live? It’s certain, however, that they did live, and, on the whole, enjoyed their life.

The picnics were generally on a Monday, as a kind of compliment to Mr. Broad, who was supposed to need rest and change on Monday, and who was also supposed not to be able to spare the time on any other day. About a month after the conversation recorded in the previous chapter Tanner’s Lane was jogging along to Shott on one of its excursions. It was a brilliant, blazing afternoon towards the end of August. The corn stood in shocks, and a week with that sun would see it all stacked. There was no dreary suburb round Cowfold, neither town nor country, to shut out country influences. The fields came up to the gardens and orchards at the back of half the houses, and flowed irregularly, like an inundation, into the angles of the streets. As you walked past the great gate of the Angel yard you could see the meadow at the bottom belonging to Hundred Acres. Consequently all Cowfold took an interest in agriculture, and knew a good deal about it. Every shopkeeper was half a farmer, and understood the points of a pig or a horse. Cowfold was not a town properly speaking, but the country a little thickened and congested. The conversation turned upon the crops, and more particularly upon turnips and drainage, both of them a new importation. Hitherto all the parishes round had no drainage whatever, excepting along the bottoms of the ridges, and the now familiar red pipes had just made their appearance on a farm belonging to a stranger to those parts﻿—a young fellow from Norfolk. Everybody was sceptical, and called him a fool. Everybody wanted to know how water was going to get through fifteen inches of heavy land when it would lie for two days where a horse trod. However, the pipes went in, and it so happened that the first wet day after they were laid was a Sunday. The congregation in Shott Church was very restless, although the sermon was unusually short. One by one they crept out, and presently they were followed by the parson. All of them had collected in the pouring rain and were watching the outfall in the ditches. To their unspeakable amazement the pipes were all running! Shott scratched its head and was utterly bewildered. A new idea in a brain not accustomed to the invasion of ideas produces a disturbance like a revolution. It causes giddiness almost as bad as that of a fit, and an extremely unpleasant sensation of having been whirled round and turned head over heels. It was the beginning of new things in Shott, the beginning of a breakdown in its traditions; a belief in something outside the ordinary parochial uniformities was forced into the skull of every man, woman, and child by the evidence of the senses; and when other beliefs asked, in the course of time, for admittance they found the entrance easier than it would have been otherwise.

===Chinese===

每逢夏季，坦纳斯巷偶尔也要纵情一回，办一次野餐；这就是说，会众里的头面人物携妻带子，早早用过正餐，乘着双轮轻马车和四轮轻便马车往肖特树林（Shott Woods）去，随身带去一篮一篮的面包、糕饼、果酱、黄油、火腿和别的吃食。到了肖特树林，在一株参天橡树下的一小片草地上，火生起来，茶也备下了。布罗德先生（Mr. Broad）一家回回随行。那是不信国教者还没有什么固定消遣的年代，肖特的乐子主要就在替火堆拾柴、打水，还有彼此侍候；这侍候，在年轻人尤其觉得受用。跳舞么，当然想也不去想。可以说直到 1840 年，全英国容得下跳舞的独立派家庭凑不满二十户；再说，除了有钱人家，也没人学过跳舞。

从没有跳舞教师进过考福尔德（Cowfold）；那里没有音乐教师；一场音乐会也没开过；用句时新话来说，考福尔德除了一个带铜管乐队的巡回动物园，简直「什么也没见过，什么也没听过」。这算过的什么日子！他们到底是*怎么*活下来的？不过可以肯定，他们确确实实活了下来，而且总的说来，日子过得还有滋有味。

野餐多半定在星期一，算是给布罗德先生的一点敬意：大家认定他星期一需要歇息、需要换换环境，又认定别的日子他抽不出这个工夫。上一章记下那番谈话之后约莫一个月，坦纳斯巷又一次坐着马车，颠颠晃晃地往肖特出游。那是八月末一个晴朗炽烈的下午。庄稼已束成禾堆，有这样的日头，再晒一个星期便可尽数上垛。考福尔德四周没有沉闷的城郊——那种不城不乡、把田园气息遮得严严实实的地方。田地一直漫到半数人家屋后的园圃和果园，又像漫涨的洪水，参差不齐地灌进街道的角落。你从天使旅馆院子的大门前经过，便能望见尽头那片属于「百亩田」（Hundred Acres）农场的草场。因此全考福尔德都关心农事，对农事也颇为在行。每个店主都是半个庄稼汉，相猪相马都识得门道。严格说来，考福尔德算不得一座城镇，只是稠密了些、拥挤了些的乡野。车上的话题于是转到收成上，尤其是芜菁和排水，这两样都是新近传进来的物事。在那以前，周围各教区根本谈不上排水，至多顺着垄沟底儿淌一淌；如今人人看惯的红瓦管，才刚在一家外地人的农场上露面——那是个从诺福克（Norfolk）来的年轻人。人人将信将疑，都叫他傻子。人人都纳闷：水在马蹄印里尚且能存上两天，又怎么钻得透十五英寸黏重的土地？可是瓦管终究埋下去了，说来也巧，铺好后的头一个雨天恰逢星期日。肖特教堂里的会众坐立不安，尽管那天的布道比往常短得出奇。他们一个个溜了出去，不多时，教区牧师本人也跟着出来了。众人都聚在倾盆大雨里，盯着水沟的出水口看。瓦管竟一根根全在淌水，真叫他们惊得说不出话来！肖特挠着头，全然摸不着头脑。一个不惯于观念入侵的头脑里闯进一个新观念，激起的震荡不啻一场革命。它叫人晕眩得几乎像发了急病，叫人极不痛快地觉得自己被抡起来打了个转，栽了个倒栽葱。这是肖特新事物的开端，是它旧传统崩塌的开端；感官拿出了证据，把一种「教区一成不变的常规之外别有天地」的信念，硬生生塞进了每一个男人、女人和孩子的脑壳；待到日后别的信念前来叩门求纳，便发现进门比原本会有的情形容易多了。

===Original===

The elderly occupants of the Tanner’s Lane gigs and chaises talked exclusively upon these and other cognate topics. The sons and daughters talked about other things utterly unworthy of any record in a serious history. Delightful their chatter was to them. What does it signify to eighteen years what is said on such an afternoon by seventeen years, when seventeen years is in a charming white muslin dress, with the prettiest hat? Words are of importance between me and you, who care little or nothing for one another. But there is a thrice blessed time when words are nothing. The real word is that which is not uttered. We may be silent, or we may be eloquent with nonsense or sense﻿—it is all one. So it was between George Allen and Miss Priscilla Broad, who at the present moment were sitting next to one another. George was a broad, hearty, sandy-haired, sanguine-faced young fellow of one and twenty, eldest son of the ironmonger. His education had been that of the middle classes of those days. Leaving school at fourteen, he had been apprenticed to his father for seven years, and had worked at the forge down the backyard before coming into the front shop. On weekdays he generally wore a waistcoat with sleeves and a black apron. He was never dirty; in fact, he was rather particular as to neatness and cleanliness; but he was always a little dingy and iron-coloured, as retail ironmongers are apt to be. He was now in charge of the business under his father; stood behind the counter; weighed nails; examined locks brought for repair; went to the different houses in Cowfold with a man under him to look at boiler-pipes, the man wearing a cap and George a tall hat. He had a hard, healthy, honest life, was up at six o’clock in the morning, ate well, and slept well. He was always permitted by his father to go on these excursions, and, in fact, they could not have been a success without him. If anything went wrong he was always the man to set it right. If a horse became restive, George was invariably the one to jump out, and nobody else thought of stirring. He had good expectations. The house in which the Allens lived was their own. Mr. Allen did a thriving trade, not only in Cowfold, but in all the country round, and particularly among the village blacksmiths, to whom he sold iron. He had steadily saved money, and had enlarged the original little back parlour into a room which would hold comfortably a tea-party of ten or a dozen.

Miss Priscilla Broad was framed after a different model. Her face was not much unlike that of one of those women of the Restoration so familiar to us in half a hundred pictures. Not that Restoration levity and Restoration manners were chargeable to Miss Priscilla. She never forgot her parentage; but there were the same kind of prettiness, the same sideways look, the same simper about the lips, and there were the same flat unilluminated eyes. She had darkish brown hair, which fell in rather formal curls on her shoulder, and she was commonly thought to be “delicate.” Like her sister and brother, she had never been to school, on account of the “mixture,” but had been taught by her mother. Her accomplishments included Scripture and English history, arithmetic, geography, the use of the globes, and dates. She had a very difficult part to play in Cowfold, for she was obliged to visit freely all Tanner’s Lane, but at the same time to hold herself above it and not to form any exclusive friendships. These would have been most injudicious, because, in the first place, they would have excited jealousy, and, in the next place, the minister’s daughter could not be expected to be very intimate with anybody belonging to the congregation. She was not particularly popular with the majority, and was even thought to be just a bit of a fool. But what could she have been with such surroundings? The time had passed when religion could be talked on weekdays, and the present time, when ministers’ children learn French, German, and Latin, and read selected plays of Shakespeare, had not come. Miss Priscilla Broad found it very difficult, also, to steer her course properly amongst the young men in Cowfold. Mrs. Broad would not have permitted any one of them for a moment to dream of an alliance with her family. As soon might a Princess of the Blood Royal unite herself with an ordinary knight. Miss Broad, however, as her resources within herself were not particularly strong, thought about little or nothing else than ensnaring the hearts of the younger Cowfold males﻿—that is to say, the hearts which were converted, and yet she encouraged none of them, save by a general acceptance of little attentions, by little mincing smiles, and little mincing speeches.

===Chinese===

坦纳斯巷马车上年长的一辈，谈来谈去不出这些以及相邻的话题。儿女们谈的却是另外一些东西，在一部严肃的历史里根本不值一记。可这些闲话在他们自己听来何等甘美。人家十七岁，正穿一身迷人的白色细纱裙，戴一顶顶好看的帽子，在这样的下午说了些什么，对十八岁的人又有什么打紧？言语之所以要紧，是在你我之间——你我彼此本来就没多少情分，甚至全无情分。然而世上有一段三重有福的时光，言语什么也不是。真正的话，是那句没有说出口的。我们可以沉默，也可以口若悬河，说的无论是傻话还是正经话——全都一样。乔治·艾伦（George Allen）和普丽西拉·布罗德小姐（Miss Priscilla Broad）之间便是如此，此刻两人恰好并肩而坐。乔治二十一岁，是个肩宽背厚、性情爽朗、黄沙色头发、红脸膛的年轻人，五金铺老板的长子。他受的是那个年代中产阶级的教育。十四岁离校，跟父亲当了七年学徒，先在后院的铁匠炉边做工，然后才进到前头的铺面。平日里他惯常穿一件带袖子的背心，系一条黑围裙。他从不邋遢；说实在的，他还相当讲究干净利落；只是身上总带着一点洗不脱的烟灰色和铁灰色，零售五金商多半如此。如今他在父亲手下掌管铺子；站柜台；称钉子；验看送来修理的锁；带着手下一个伙计到考福尔德各处人家去查看锅炉管道，伙计戴便帽，乔治戴高礼帽。他过着劳苦、健康、本分的日子，清晨六点起身，吃得好，睡得香。这等出游，父亲一向准他去；说实在的，没有他，出游就办不成个样子。哪儿出了岔子，把它收拾妥帖的总是他。马要是发起犟性，跳下车去的必定是乔治，旁人连挪一挪的念头都没有。他的前程着实可观。艾伦家住的房子是自家的产业。艾伦先生买卖兴隆，不仅在考福尔德，四乡八里都有主顾，尤其做的是各村铁匠的生意，卖铁给他们。他一文一文稳稳地攒下钱来，把原先那间小小的后客厅扩建出去，成了一间屋子，开一场十来位客人的茶会也坐得宽宽绰绰。

普丽西拉·布罗德小姐却是照着另一个模子造出来的。她的脸，同王政复辟时期（the Restoration）的那些妇人——我们在五十来幅画像里看熟了的——颇有几分相像。倒不是说复辟时代的轻浮和复辟时代的做派能记到普丽西拉小姐的账上。她从不忘记自己的出身门第；只是那份俏丽是同一类的，那斜斜的一瞥是同一类的，唇边那种似笑非笑是同一类的，还有那同样一双平板板、不见光彩的眼睛。她一头深棕色的头发，卷成端端正正的鬈发垂在肩上，人们一向说她「身子娇弱」。她和她的兄妹一样，从没进过学堂——只因顾忌学校里「品流混杂」——功课都是母亲教的。她学得的才艺包括圣经、英国史、算术、地理、地球仪和天球仪的用法，还有历史年代。她在考福尔德要扮演的角色极难：她不得不随意走访坦纳斯巷的每一户，同时又须端然自居于其上，不得同任何人结成独一份的友谊。独一份的友谊最不聪明：一则要惹人嫉恨，二则牧师的女儿原就不该同会众里的什么人过分亲近。她在多数人那里并不怎么得人缘，甚至被认为有几分傻。可是处在那样的境地里，她还能成个什么样子呢？平日里也能谈宗教的时代已经过去，牧师家的儿女学法文、德文、拉丁文、读莎士比亚剧本选段的时代还没有到来。普丽西拉·布罗德小姐还发觉，要在考福尔德的年轻男子中间把稳航向，同样是桩难事。他们当中随便哪一个，想入非非要同她家联姻，布罗德太太一息也容不得他存这个念头。正如王室的公主也断然嫁不得一个寻常的骑士。然而布罗德小姐自身内在的凭借本不算丰厚，她心里盘算的，除了俘获考福尔德年轻男子的心，几乎没有别的——就是说，俘获那些归信了主的心——可她又一个也不当真鼓励，只是泛泛地领受些小小的殷勤，报以小小的、矫怯的微笑，和小小的、矫怯的言语。

===Original===

“Such a beautiful day,” said George, “and such pleasant company!”

“Really, Mr. Allen, don’t you think it would have been pleasanter for you in front?”

“What did you say, my dear?” came immediately from her mother, the ever-watchful dragon just before them. She forthwith turned a little round, for the sun was on her left hand, and with her right eye kept Priscilla well in view for the rest of the journey.

===Chinese===

「多好的天气，」乔治说，「同伴又多可心！」

「说真的，艾伦先生，您不觉得坐到前头去，您会更痛快些么？」

「你说什么，亲爱的？」她母亲——坐在他们前头、时刻警戒着的那条母龙——立刻接了腔。她随即略略转过一点身子，好在太阳正在她左手边，她便用右眼把普丽西拉一路盯得牢牢的，直到抵达。

===Original===

In the chaise behind pretty much the same story was told, but with a difference. In the back part were Mr. Thomas Broad and Miss Fanny Allen. The arrangement which brought these two together was most objectionable to Mrs. Broad; but unfortunately she was a little late in starting, and it was made before she arrived. She could not, without insulting the Allens, have it altered; but she consoled herself by vowing that it should not stand on the return journey in the dusk. Miss Fanny was flattered that the minister’s son should be by her side, and the minister’s son was not in the least deterred from playing with Miss Fanny by the weight of responsibility which oppressed and checked his sister. He did not laugh much; he had not a nature for wholesome laughter, but he chuckled, lengthened his lips, half shut his eyes; asked his companion whether the rail did not hurt her, put his arm on the top, so that she might lean against it, and talked in a manner which even she would have considered a little silly and a little odd, if his position, that of a student for the ministry, had not surrounded him with such a halo of glory.

===Chinese===

后面那辆马车里演的几乎是同一出戏，只是略有不同。坐在后座的是托马斯·布罗德先生（Mr. Thomas Broad）和范妮·艾伦小姐（Miss Fanny Allen）。这个把两人凑作一处的座位安排，最惹布罗德太太的嫌；无奈她动身晚了一步，安排在她赶到之前已经定下了。要改动，就免不了开罪艾伦家；她只得自宽自解，暗暗起誓：回程趁着暮色赶路，断不容这安排再成立。牧师的儿子竟坐在自己身旁，范妮小姐受宠若惊；而那份压着他姐姐、拘束着他姐姐的责任重负，却丝毫吓不住这位牧师之子，他照样同范妮小姐厮闹。他不常出声大笑；他不是生来会爽朗发笑的人，只是嗤嗤地乐，抻长嘴唇，把眼睛眯起一半；问同座的姑娘车栏硌不硌着她；把胳膊搭在栏顶上，好让她倚着；又絮絮地说话，那副腔调，若不是他那攻读神学、预备当牧师的身分给他罩上一圈那样的荣光，连她自己也要觉得有几分傻气、几分古怪。

===Original===

Presently Shott Woods were reached; the parcels and hampers were unpacked, the fire was lit, the tea prepared, and the pastor asked a blessing. Everybody sat on the grass, save the reverend gentleman and his wife, who had chairs which had been brought on purpose. It would not have been considered proper that Mr. or Mrs. Broad should sit upon the grass, and indeed physically it would have been inconvenient to Mr. Broad to do so. He ate his ham in considerable quantities, adding thereto much plumcake, and excusing himself on the ground that the ride had given him an appetite. The meal being over, grace was said, and the victuals that were left were repacked. About an hour remained before the return journey began. This was usually passed in sauntering about or in walking to the springs, a mile away, down one of the grass drives. Mrs. Broad never for a moment lost sight of Thomas, and pressed him as much as possible into her service; but when Mrs. Allen announced that the young people had all determined to go to the springs, Mrs. Broad could not hold out. Accordingly off they started, under strict orders to be back by eight. They mixed themselves up pretty indiscriminately as they left their seniors; but after a while certain affinities displayed themselves, George being found with Priscilla, for example, and Thomas with Fanny. The party kept together; but Thomas and Fanny lagged somewhat till they came to a little opening in the underwood, which Thomas said was a shortcut, and he pressed her to try it with him. She agreed, and they slipped out of sight nearly, but not, quite, unobserved. Thomas professed himself afraid Fanny might be tired, and offered his arm. She again consented, not without a flutter, and so they reached a clearing with three or four paths branching from it. Thomas was puzzled, and as for Fanny, she knew nothing. To add to their perplexity some drops of rain were felt. She was a little frightened, and was anxious to try one of the most likely tracks which looked, she thought, as if it went to the springs, where they could take shelter in the cottage with the others. Thomas, however, was doubtful, and proposed that they should stand up in a shed which had been used for faggot-making. The rain, which now came down heavily, enforced his arguments, and she felt obliged to stay till the shower had ceased.

===Chinese===

不多时，肖特树林到了；包裹和食篮一一打开，火生起来，茶备好，牧师作了谢饭祷告。人人都坐在草地上，只有那位可敬的先生和他夫人坐着特意带来的椅子。布罗德先生或布罗德太太坐到草地上，人家会觉得不成体统；何况就布罗德先生的身量而言，当真那样坐也实在不便。他火腿吃了不少，又添上许多葡萄干蛋糕，还替自己开脱，说是坐了一路车，走出了胃口。吃罢，作了餐后感恩，剩下的吃食重新装好。离开车回程，还有约莫一个钟头。这点时间通常用来四处闲逛，或者沿着一条草径走到一英里外的泉水边。布罗德太太一刻也不让托马斯离开视线，还尽量支使他当差跑腿；可艾伦太太一宣布年轻人已经商量好同去泉水边，布罗德太太就再也拗不住了。于是他们出发，领了严令：八点前务必回来。离开长辈时，他们混杂得不分彼此；可过了一会儿，几股同气相求的引力便各自显了形，譬如乔治挨到了普丽西拉身边，托马斯挨到了范妮身边。一行人还聚在一处走；只是托马斯和范妮渐渐落了后，来到矮树丛里一个小小的豁口跟前，托马斯说这是一条近道，撺掇她同他一道试试。她应允了，两人便从众人眼里溜了出去——几乎无人瞧见，却不是全然无人瞧见。托马斯表白说，只怕范妮走乏了，把胳膊递了过去。她又应允了，心里不免扑腾了一下，于是走到一片小空地上，从那里岔出三四条小径。托马斯犯了难；至于范妮，更是全然不辨东西。偏在这时，天上又落下雨点来，越发叫两人没了主意。她有点害怕，急着想挑一条看着最像样的道儿——她想那仿佛是通向泉水那边的——到了那边，就能同大伙儿一道在农舍里躲避。托马斯却拿不准，提议两人到一间从前用来捆柴的棚屋里去站一站。这时雨下大了，倒帮着把他的话坐实了，她只得留下，等阵雨过去。

===Original===

“Only think, Fanny,” he said, “to be here alone with you!”

He called her Fanny now; he had always called her Miss Allen before.

“Yes,” said she, not knowing what answer to make.

“You are cold,” he added, with a little trembling in his voice and a little more light than usual in his eyes.

“Oh no, I am not cold.”

===Chinese===

「你想想看，范妮，」他说，「竟能同你两个人单独待在这儿！」

他此刻叫她范妮了；从前他一向称她艾伦小姐。

「是啊，」她应道，不知道该怎样回答才好。

「你冷了，」他又说，声音里带一丝微颤，眼里比平日多出一点光。

「噢，不，我不冷。」

===Original===

“I know you are,” and he took her hand; “why, it is quite cold.”

“Oh dear no, Mr. Thomas, it is really not cold,” and she made a movement to withdraw it, but it remained.

The touch of the hand caused his voice to shake a little more than before.

“I say you are cold; come a little closer to me. What will your mamma say if you catch a chill?” and he drew Fanny a little nearer to him. The thick blood now drove through him with increasing speed: everything seemed in a mist, and a little perspiration was on his forehead. His arm found its way round Fanny’s waist, and he pressed her closer and closer to him till his hot lips were upon her cheek. She made two or three futile attempts to release herself; but she might as well have striven with that brazen, red-hot idol who was made to clasp his victims to death. She was frightened and screamed, when suddenly a strong man’s voice was heard calling “Fanny, Fanny.” It was her brother. Knowing that she and Thomas had no umbrellas, he had brought them a couple.

===Chinese===

「我知道你冷，」他说着握住了她的手；「你瞧，这手多凉。」

「哎呀，不，托马斯先生，真的不凉，」她动了动，想把手抽回来，手却没有抽回来。

这一触，他的声音便抖得比方才更厉害了些。

「我说你冷，你就是冷；再靠近我一点。你要是着了凉，你妈妈会怎么说？」他说着把范妮又往身边拉近了些。那浓稠的血此刻在他周身越涌越急：一切都仿佛罩在雾里，他额上沁出细细的汗珠。他的胳膊绕上了范妮的腰，把她越搂越紧，直到他滚烫的嘴唇贴上她的脸颊。她挣扎了两三下，全然无用；她还不如去同那尊铜铸的、烧得通红、专为把祭物搂抱至死的神像角力。她吓坏了，尖叫起来——就在这时，忽听得一个壮实男子的声音喊道：「范妮！范妮！」是她的哥哥。他知道她和托马斯没带伞，特意送了两把来。

===Original===

“But, Fanny,” he cried, “did I not hear you scream? What was the matter?”

“Nothing,” hastily interposed Thomas; “she thought she saw it lighten.” Fanny looked at Thomas for a moment; but she was scared and bewildered, and held her peace.

The three went down to the rendezvous together, where the rest of the party had already assembled. Mrs. Broad had been very uneasy when she found that Thomas and Fanny were the only absentees, and she had urged George the moment she saw him to look for his sister without a moment’s delay. The excuse of the rain was given and accepted; but Mrs. Broad felt convinced from Fanny’s forward look that she had once more thrown herself in the way of her beloved child, her delicate Samuel. She was increasingly anxious that he should go to college, and his papa promised at once to transmit the application. Meanwhile, in the few days left before the examination, he undertook to improve Thomas where he was weakest, that is to say, in Systematic Theology, and more particularly in the doctrine of the Comforter.

===Chinese===

「可是，范妮，」他喊道，「我刚才不是听见你尖叫了么？出了什么事？」

「没什么，」托马斯急忙插嘴道；「她以为看见闪电了。」范妮朝托马斯望了片刻；可她又惊又乱，终于没有作声。

三人于是一同回到会合的地点，其余的人早已聚齐在那里。发现缺席的只有托马斯和范妮两个，布罗德太太心下好生不安，一见乔治便催他即刻去找妹妹，一刻也不许耽搁。下雨的说法摆出来，也被认可了；可布罗德太太凭范妮那副上赶着的神情断定，这丫头又一头凑到她的心肝、她那娇贵的撒母耳跟前去了。她愈发急着要送他上大学，他爸爸一口应承，即刻把申请递送上去。于是，趁考试前剩下的那几天，他担当起给托马斯补足弱项的功课——也就是系统神学（Systematic Theology），尤其是保惠师（the Comforter）的教义。

## IX A Strain on the Cable / 九、缆绳吃紧

===Original===
Bow Street was completely at fault, and never discovered the secret of that assassination. It was clear that neither the Major nor Coleman were the murderers, as they had been noticed at some distance from the spot where the Secretary fell by several persons who described them accurately. Nor was Caillaud suspected, as the constable testified that he passed him on the opposite side of the street, as he followed the Secretary. The only conclusion, according to Bow Street, which was free from all doubt was, that whoever did the deed was a committee consisting of a single member. A reward of £500 did not bring forward anybody who knew anything about the business. As for Caillaud, his daughter and the Major, the next morning saw them far on the way to Dover, and eventually they arrived at Paris in safety. Zachariah, when he reached home, found his wife gone. A note lay for him there, probably from the same hand which warned the Major, telling him not to lose an instant, but to join in Islington one of the mails to Manchester. His wife would start that night from St. Martin’s le Grand by a coach which went by another road. He was always prompt, and in five minutes he was out of the house. The fare was carefully folded by his unknown friend in the letter. He just managed as directed, to secure a place, not by the regular Manchester mail, but by one which went through Barnet and stopped to take up passengers at the Angel. He climbed upon the roof, and presently was travelling rapidly through Holloway and Highgate. He found, to his relief, that nobody had heard of the murder, and he was left pretty much to his own reflections. His first thoughts were an attempt to unravel the mystery. Why was it so sudden? Why had no word nor hint of what was intended reached him? He could not guess. In those days the clubs were so beset with spies that frequently the most important resolutions were taken by one man, who confided in nobody. It was winter, but fortunately Zachariah was well wrapped up. He journeyed on, hour after hour, in a state of mazed bewilderment, one thought tumbling over another, and when morning broke over the flats he had not advanced a single step in the determination of his future path. Nothing is more painful to a man of any energy than the inability to put things in order in himself—to place before himself what he has to do, and arrange the means for doing it. To be the passive victim of a rushing stream of disconnected impressions is torture, especially if the emergency be urgent. So when the sun came up Zachariah began to be ashamed of himself that the night had passed in these idiotic moonings, which had left him just where he was, and he tried to settle what he was to do when he reached Manchester. He did not know a soul; but he could conjecture why he was advised to go thither. It was a disaffected town, and Friends of the People were very strong there. His first duty was to get a lodging, his second to get work, and his third to find out a minister of God under whom he could worship. He put this last, not because it was the least important, but because he had the most time to decide upon it. At about ten o’clock at night he came to his journey’s end, and to his joy saw his wife waiting for him. They went at once to a small inn hard by, and Mrs. Coleman began to overwhelm him with interrogation; but he quietly suggested that not a syllable should be spoken till they had had some rest, and that they should swallow their supper and go to bed. In the morning Zachariah rose and looked out of the window. He saw nothing but a small backyard in which some miserable, scraggy fowls were crouching under a cart to protect themselves from the rain, which was falling heavily through the dim, smoky air. His spirits sank. He had no fear of apprehension or prosecution, but the prospect before him was depressing. Although he was a poor man, he had not been accustomed to oscillations of fortune, and he was in an utterly strange place, with five pounds in his pocket, and nothing to do.

===Chinese===
弓街（Bow Street）的官探全然无从措手，始终没有侦破那桩刺杀的内情。梅特兰少校与科尔曼显然都不是凶手：有好几个人远远望见他们，离秘书倒下的地方隔着好一段路，把两人的模样描述得分毫不差。凯约也无可怀疑，因为警吏作证说，他跟踪秘书时，凯约是在街对面与他错身而过的。照弓街看来，唯一无可置疑的结论是：干下这桩事的，无论其人为谁，都是一个只有单一名成员的委员会。悬赏 500 镑，也没有引出任何一个知情的人来。至于凯约、他女儿和少校三人，第二天一早便已远在去多佛尔（Dover）的路上，后来平平安安到了巴黎（Paris）。撒迦利亚回到家里，发觉妻子已经走了。家里给他留着一封短信，大约就出自向少校报警的那同一只手，叮嘱他一刻也不可耽搁，赶到伊斯灵顿（Islington）去搭一班去曼彻斯特的邮车。他妻子当夜将从圣马丁勒格朗（St. Martin’s le Grand）乘一辆走另一条路的马车动身。他素来行事干脆，不出五分钟便出了门。车钱已由那位不知名的朋友细心折好，附在信里。他照着信上的指点，总算抢到了一个座位——不是搭定班的曼彻斯特邮车，而是搭一辆取道巴尼特（Barnet）、在天使旅馆（the Angel）停靠载客的车。他攀上车顶，不多时便疾驰着穿过霍洛韦（Holloway）与海格特。让他宽心的是，车上还没有人听说那桩命案，他大可以独自想自己的心事。他起初一门心思，是想解开这个谜。事情为何这样仓猝？图谋所在，为何连一言半语的暗示都不曾传到他耳中？他猜不透。那些年月，会社里密探环伺，往往最要紧的决议便由一个人独断，对谁也不吐露。时值冬天，所幸撒迦利亚穿裹得严实。他一小时一小时地赶路，神思恍惚，心乱如麻，一个念头压过一个念头；及至晨光破晓照上平野，他对前路的抉择仍未有寸进。对一个有魄力的人来说，最痛苦莫过于无法把心里的事理出个头绪——把该做的事摆到自己面前，把行事的步骤安排妥当。任凭一连串互不连贯的印象如急流般裹挟而去，全然身不由己，这是一种刑罚，事势危急时尤其如此。于是日头升起时，撒迦利亚开始为自己害臊：一整夜竟耗在这些痴痴的怔忡里，人还在原地踏步；他便试着定一定，到了曼彻斯特之后该当如何。那里他一个熟人也没有，但人家为何劝他去，他猜得出。那是一座人心思乱的城市，人民之友（Friends of the People）的势力在那里极盛。他的头一桩事是寻个落脚处，第二桩是寻活计，第三桩是寻一位可以在他座下敬拜神的牧师。他把这一桩搁在末尾，倒不是因为它最不要紧，而是因为斟酌的工夫，数它最宽裕。夜里十点钟光景，他到了行程的尽头，喜出望外地看见妻子正在等他。两人随即走进近旁一家小客栈，科尔曼太太劈头便要细细盘问他；他却不声不响地提议，先歇息歇息，一个字的正事也不忙说，晚饭囫囵吞下，便去睡觉。早晨撒迦利亚起身，望向窗外。目之所及只有一个小后院，几只可怜的瘦骨嶙峋的鸡缩在一辆板车底下躲雨；雨正下得紧，穿过昏暗烟蒙的空气落下来。他的心往下一沉。他不怕被捕，也不怕吃官司，可眼前的光景令人丧气。他虽是个穷人，却不曾惯于这般大起大落的颠簸；如今置身全然生疏的地方，口袋里揣着五镑钱，无事可做。

===Original===
He was, however, resolved not to yield, and thought it best to begin with his wife before she could begin with him.

“Now, my dear, tell me what has happened, who sent you here, and what kind of a journey you have had?”

“Mr. Bradshaw came about seven o’clock, and told me the Government was about to suppress the Friends of the People; that you did not know it; that I must go to Manchester; that you would come after me; and that a message would be left for you. He took me to the coach, and paid for me.”

“Mr. Bradshaw! Did he tell you anything more?”

“No; except that he did not think we should be pursued, and that he would send our things after us when he knew where we were.”

===Chinese===
然而他打定主意不肯认输，心想与其等妻子先发制人，不如自己先开口。

「好了，亲爱的，给我讲讲出了什么事，是谁送你来的，一路上可还顺利？」

「布拉德肖先生七点钟前后来了，告诉我政府就要取缔人民之友；说你还不知情；说我必须去曼彻斯特；说你随后就来；还说会给你留下话。他送我上了车，替我付了车钱。」

「布拉德肖先生！他还同你说了些什么？」

「没有了；只说他想他们不至于追查我们，等知道了我们的下落，就把我们的东西捎来。」

===Original===
“You have not heard anything more, then?”

“No.”

“You haven’t heard that the Secretary was shot?”

“Shot! Oh dear! Zachariah, what will become of us?”

Her husband then told her what he knew, she listening with great eagerness and in silence.

===Chinese===
「这么说，你没再听到别的消息？」

「没有。」

「你没听说秘书中了枪？」

「中枪了！天哪！撒迦利亚，我们可怎么办啊？」

于是丈夫把知道的一五一十讲给她听，她屏息敛声，听得急切。

===Original===
“Oh, Zachariah, what will become of us?” she broke out again.

“There is no reason to worry yourself, Jane; it is perfectly easy for me to prove my innocence. It is better for us, however, to stay here for a time. The Government won’t go any further with us; they will search for the murderer—that’s all.”

“Why, then, are we sent here and the others are let alone? I suppose the Major is not here?”

“I cannot say.”

“To think I should ever come to this! I haven’t got a rag with me beyond what I have on. I haven’t got any clean things; a nice sort of creature I am to go out of doors. And it all had nothing to do with us.”

===Chinese===
「哦，撒迦利亚，我们往后可怎么得了？」她又迸出这么一句。

「简，你没有理由这样担惊受怕；要洗清我的干系，再容易不过。只是我们不妨在此地待上一阵。政府不会再来为难我们；他们要缉查的是凶手——仅此而已。」

「那么，为什么偏把我们打发到这儿来，别人却安然无事？我想梅特兰少校总不在这儿吧？」

「这我说不好。」

「想想我竟落到这步田地！身上穿的之外，我连一块换洗的布片都没带；干净衣裳一件也没有；我这副模样还怎么出门见人。再说这一切原与我们毫不相干。」

===Original===
“Nothing to do with us! My dear Jane, do you mean that we are not to help other people, but sit at home and enjoy ourselves? Besides, if you thought it wrong, why did you not say so before?”

“How was I to know what you were doing? You never told me anything; you never do. One thing I do know is that we shall starve and I suppose I shall have to go about and beg. I haven’t even another pair of shoes or stockings to my feet.”

Zachariah pondered for a moment. His first impulse was something very different; but at last he rose, went up to his wife, kissed her softly on the forehead, and said:

“Never mind, my dear; courage, you will have your clothes next week. Come with me and look out for a lodging.”

Mrs. Zachariah, however, shook herself free—not violently, but still decidedly—from his caresses.

“Most likely seized by the Government. Look for a lodging! That’s just like you! How can I go out in this pouring rain?”

===Chinese===
「与我们毫不相干！我亲爱的简，难道你的意思是，我们只该坐在家里安享清福，不去帮扶别人？况且，你若觉得这事不对，先前为什么不说？」

「你做的事，教我怎么知道？你从不跟我说什么；从来也不。我只知道一件事，我们快要饿死了，只怕我还得挨家挨户去讨饭。我脚上连双替换的鞋袜都没有。」

撒迦利亚沉吟了片刻。他头一个冲动原是全然另一副样子；但末了他站起身，走到妻子跟前，在她额头上轻轻亲了一下，说道：

「别放在心上，亲爱的；打起精神来，下个星期你的衣裳就到了。跟我一道去找住处吧。」

不料撒迦利亚太太挣脱了他的爱抚——不算激烈，却十分决绝。

「多半早叫政府抄没了。找住处！你就是这样的人！这样的大雨，教我怎么出门？」

===Original===
Zachariah lately, at any rate, had ceased to expect much affection in his wife for him; but he thought she was sensible, and equal to any complexity of circumstances, or even to disaster. He thought this, not on any positive evidence; but he concluded, somewhat absurdly, that her coldness meant common sense and capacity for facing trouble courageously and with deliberation. He had now to find out his mistake, and to learn that the absence of emotion neither proves, nor is even a ground for suspecting, any good whatever of a person; that, on the contrary, it is a ground for suspecting weakness, and possibly imbecility.

Mrs. Coleman refused to go out, and after breakfast Zachariah went by himself, having first inquired what was a likely quarter. As he wandered along much that had been before him again and again once more recurred to him. He had been overtaken by calamity, and he had not heard from his wife one single expression of sympathy, nor had he received one single idea which could help him. She had thought of nothing but herself, and even of herself not reasonably. She was not the helpmeet which he felt he had a right to expect. He could have endured any defect, so it seemed, if only he could have had love; he could have endured the want of love if only he could have had a counsellor. But he had neither, and he rebelled, questioning the justice of his lot.
