He had a letter now and then from Mr. Bradshaw and it was a sore trial to him that nobody could be found in Manchester to take the place of that worthy man of God. He could not attach himself definitely to any church in the town, and the habit grew upon him of wandering into this or the other chapel as his fancy led him. His comrades often met on Sunday evenings. At first he would not go; but he was afterwards persuaded to do so. The reasons which induced him to alter his mind were, in the first place, the piety, methodistic most of it, which was then mixed up with politics; and secondly, a growing fierceness of temper, which made the cause of the people a religion. From 1816 downwards it may be questioned whether he would not have felt himself more akin with any of his democratic friends, who were really in earnest over the great struggle, than with a sleek half Tory professor of the gospel, however orthodox he might have been. In 1816 the situation of the working classes had become almost intolerable. Towards the end of the year wheat rose to a quarter, and incendiarism was common all over England. A sense of insecurity and terror took possession of everybody. Secret outrages, especially fires by night, chill the courage of the bravest, as those know well enough who have lived in an agricultural county, when, just before going to bed, great lights are seen on the horizon; when men and women collect on bridges or on hill tops, asking "Where is it?" and when fire-engines tearing through the streets arrive useless at their journey's end because the hose has been cut. One evening in November 1816, Zachariah was walking home to his lodgings. A special meeting of the club had been called for the following Sunday to consider a proposal made for a march of the unemployed upon London. Three persons passed him—two men and a woman—who turned round and looked at him and then went on. He did not recognise them, but he noticed that they stopped opposite a window, and as he came up they looked at him again. He could not be mistaken; they were the Major, Caillaud, and his daughter. The most joyous recognition followed, and Zachariah insisted on their going home with him. It often happens that we become increasingly intimate with one another even when we are shut out from all intercourse. Zachariah had not seen the Major nor Caillaud nor Pauline for two years, and not a single thought had been interchanged. Nevertheless he was much nearer and dearer to them than he was before. He had unconsciously moved on a line rapidly sweeping round into parallelism with theirs. The relationship between himself and his wife during those two years had become, not openly hostile, it is true, but it was neutral. Long ago he had given up the habit of talking to her about politics, the thing which lay nearest to his heart just then. The pumping effort of bringing out a single sentence in her presence on any abstract topic was incredible, and so he learned at last to come home, though his heart and mind were full to bursting, and say nothing more to her than that he had seen her friend Mrs. Sykes, or bought his tea at a different shop. On the other hand, the revolutionary literature of the time, and more particularly Byron, increasingly interested him. The very wildness and remoteness of Byron's romance was just what suited him. It is all very well for the happy and well-to-do to talk scornfully of poetic sentimentality. Those to whom a natural outlet for their affection is denied know better. They instinctively turn to books which are the farthest removed from commonplace and are in a sense unreal. Not to the prosperous man, a dweller in beautiful scenery, well married to an intelligent wife, is Byron precious, but to the poor wretch, say some City clerk, with an aspiration beyond his desk, who has two rooms in Camberwell and who before he knew what he was doing made a marriage—well—which was a mistake, but who is able to turn to that island in the summer sea, where dwells Kaled, his mistress—Kaled, the Dark Page disguised as a man, who watches her beloved dying:—

> "Who nothing fears, nor feels, nor heeds, nor sees,
>
>  Save that damp brow which rests upon his knees;
>
>  Save that pale aspect, where the eye, though dim,
>
>  Held all the light that shone on earth for him."

===Chinese===
我们如今得把脚步放快一些。1815 年年初，撒迦利亚发现自己已在曼彻斯特（Manchester）安顿下来。就他而论，那个多事之年在自身境遇上并未带来什么外在变化。他加入了汉普登俱乐部（Hampden Club）——奥格登（Ogden）和巴姆福德（Bamford）都是那里的会员；然而梅特兰也好，凯约也好，他都再没听到半点音讯。布拉德肖牧师隔三差五来信，而曼彻斯特竟寻不出一个人能顶替那位上帝的良仆，这叫他深以为苦。他无法明确归属城里的任何一家教会，渐渐养成一种习惯，兴之所至，撞进哪家礼拜堂便听哪家。同志们常在礼拜日晚上聚会。起初他不肯去，后来被劝得改了主意。促使他回心转意的缘由，一是那时同政治搅在一处的敬虔之风——大半带着循道宗的气味；二是一股愈烧愈烈的火气，把人民的事业烧成了一种宗教。自 1816 年往后，只怕当真要问一句：随便哪一位真心投身这场大斗争的民主朋友，与他的亲缘，怕不都比一位油光水滑、半托利式的福音信奉者——无论那人体统何等纯正——更深一层？1816 年，劳工阶级的处境已几乎忍无可忍。临近年底，麦价涨至每夸特（quarter）的高点，纵火案在英格兰全境此起彼伏。一种朝不保夕的恐怖攫住了每一个人。暗地里的暴行，尤其是夜半的火警，能叫最勇敢的人也心头发寒——凡在农业郡住过的人都深知此中滋味：就寝时分，地平线上腾起大片火光；男男女女聚在桥头、聚在山顶，问「是哪儿着了？」；救火车嘶吼着穿街过巷，赶到地头却一无用处，因为水管早叫人割断了。1816 年 11 月的一个傍晚，撒迦利亚正走回寓所。俱乐部已定于下个礼拜日召开特别会议，审议一项提议：由失业者向伦敦进军。三个人从他身边走过——两男一女——回过头打量了他一眼，又接着往前去。他没有认出来，却留意到他们在对面一扇窗前停住，待他走近，又望了他一眼。错不了，是少校、凯约和他的女儿。随后的相认欢喜到了极处，撒迦利亚非要他们同他回家不可。人情往往如此：纵然音讯断绝，彼此反倒日渐亲密。撒迦利亚已有两年不曾见到少校、凯约和波琳，彼此连一个念头也不曾交换过。然而比起从前，他离他们更近，也更亲。他不知不觉沿着一条轨迹移动，那轨迹正迅速兜转过来，与他们的趋于平行。那两年间，他与妻子的关系，虽不能说公然反目，却已归于冷淡。他早已改掉了同她谈政治的习惯——而政治正是他那时心尖上的事。当着她的面，就任何一个抽象话题挤出一句整话来，都费劲得叫人难以置信，像是从深井里往上抽水；于是他终于学会了到家之后——尽管满心满怀涨得几乎迸裂——至多说一句他遇见了她的朋友赛克斯太太（Mrs. Sykes），或是换了一家铺子买茶叶。另一方面，这个时代的革命文字，尤其是拜伦，越来越叫他着迷。拜伦传奇故事里那股荒放辽远之气，恰恰合他的脾胃。幸福而衣食无虞的人，尽可以轻蔑地讥诮诗歌的多愁善感。那些被剥夺了感情天然出口的人，心里更有数。他们本能地转向那些离平淡最远、在某种意义上并不真确的书。拜伦之于富足之人——住惯美景、娶了聪慧妻子的人——算不得珍贵；他之于那个可怜虫——比方说伦敦城里的某个小职员，胸怀大志而不甘案牍，在坎伯韦尔（Camberwell）住着两个房间，不知不觉结了一门——唉——结错了的亲——才是无价之宝；因为这个人还能遁向那夏日海洋中的岛屿，卡莱德（Kaled）——他的恋人——就住在那里；卡莱德，那个假扮男装的黑肤侍童，守着她的爱人咽气：

> 「她无所惧，无所感，无所念，无所见，
>
> 　唯余那枕在她膝头的濡湿额头；
>
> 　唯余那苍白的面容——那双眼睛虽已黯淡，
>
> 　却为他收尽人世间一切照临的光明。」

===Original===
When they came indoors, and Mrs. Zachariah heard on the stairs the tramp of other feet besides those of her husband, she prepared herself to be put out of temper. Not that she could ever be really surprised. She was not one of those persons who keep a house orderly for the sake of appearances. She would have been just the same if she had been living alone, shipwrecked on a solitary island in the Pacific. She was the born natural enemy of dirt, dust, untidiness, and of every kind of irregularity, as the cat is the born natural enemy of the mouse. The sight of dirt, in fact, gave her a quiet kind of delight, because she foresaw the pleasure of annihilating it. Irregularity was just as hateful to her. She could not sit still if one ornament on the mantelpiece looked one way and the other another way, and she would have risen from her deathbed, if she could have done so, to put a chair straight. She was not, therefore, aggrieved in expectancy because she was not fit to be seen. It was rather because she resented any interruption of domestic order of which she had not been previously forewarned. As it happened, however the Major came first, and striding into the room, he shook her hand with considerable fervour and kissed it gallantly. Her gathering ill-temper disappeared with the promptitude of a flash. It was a muddy night; the Major had not carefully wiped his boots, and the footmarks were all over the floor. She saw them, but they were nothing.

"My dear Mrs. Coleman, how are you? What a blessing to be here again in your comfortable quarters."

"Really, Major Maitland, it is very good of you to say so. I am very glad to see you again. Where have you been? I thought we had lost you forever."

Caillaud and his daughter had followed. They bowed to her formally, and she begged them to be seated.

"Then, my dear madam," continued the Major, laughing, "you must have thought me dead. You might have known that if I had not been dead I must have come back."

She coloured just a trifle, but made no reply further than to invite all the company to have supper.

===Chinese===
一行人进了门，撒迦利亚太太在楼梯上听见丈夫之外另有旁人的脚步，便先攒好了一股要发作的火气。倒不是说她真会吃惊。她不是那种为了体面才把屋子收拾齐整的人。纵使她孤身一人、流落在太平洋上一座荒岛，她也仍旧是这个样子。她天生就是污垢、灰尘、凌乱和一切失序的死敌，恰如猫天生是鼠的死敌。说实在的，看见污垢，她心里反而有一份安静的欢喜，因为她预见到把污垢荡涤一净时的快意。失序之于她同样可恨。壁炉台上的摆件若是一件朝这边、一件朝那边，她便坐不安席；但凡还有一口气，她连临终的病榻也要爬起来，把一把椅子摆正。所以，她这预支的恼怒，并非因为自己仪容不整、见不得客，而是因为家中的秩序竟遭到一场事先不曾知会的搅扰。恰巧少校头一个进门，他大步跨进屋里，热热络络地握住她的手，还殷勤地吻了一下手背。她那正往上涌的火气，闪电一般霎时消散。那晚道路泥泞，少校没有仔细擦净靴子，地板上满是脚印。她看见了，却全不当一回事。

「亲爱的科尔曼太太，您好吗？又能回到您这舒适的居所，真是一桩福气。」

「哪里的话，梅特兰少校，您这么说太客气了。再见到您我高兴得很。这些日子您上哪儿去了？我还当我们永远把您给丢了哩。」

凯约和女儿跟在后头进来。两人向她规规矩矩行礼，她请他们落座。

「那么，亲爱的夫人，」少校笑着接下去，「您一定当我是死了。您本该想到，我若没有死，就必定会回来。」

她的脸微微红了一下，没有再答话，只请满座客人一同用晚饭。

===Original===
Zachariah was somewhat surprised. He did not know what sort of a supper it could be; but he was silent. She asked Pauline to take off her bonnet, and then proceeded to lay the cloth. For five minutes, or perhaps ten minutes, she disappeared, and then there came, not only bread and cheese, but cold ham, a plentiful supply of beer, and, more wonderful still, a small cold beefsteak pie. Everything was produced as easily as if it had been the ordinary fare, and Zachariah was astonished at his wife's equality to the emergency. Whence she obtained the ham and beefsteak pie he could not conjecture. She apologised for having nothing hot; would have had something better if she had known, etc., etc., and then sat down at the head of the table. The Major sat on her right, Pauline next to him, and opposite to Pauline, Caillaud and Zachariah. Their hostess immediately began to ask questions about the events of that fatal night when they all left London.

The Major, however, interposed, and said that it would perhaps be better if nothing was said upon that subject.

"A dismal topic," he observed; "talking about it can do no good, and I for one don't want to be upset by thinking about it just before I go to bed."

"At least," said Zachariah, "you can tell us why you are in Manchester?"

"Certainly," replied the Major. "In the first place, Paris is not quite so pleasant as it used to be; London, too, is not attractive; and we thought that, on the whole, Manchester was to be preferred. Moreover, a good deal will have to be done during the next twelvemonth, and Manchester will do it. You will hear all about it when your club meets next time."

"You've been in Paris?" said Mrs. Zachariah. "Isn't it very wicked?"

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚倒有些诧异。他说不上这样一顿晚饭能是个什么光景，但他没有作声。她请波琳摘下软帽，随即动手铺桌布。她人消失了五分钟，或者十分钟，随后端出来的，不光是面包和干酪，还有冷火腿、备得足足的啤酒，更叫人称奇的是，还有一小只冷的牛肉馅饼。各样东西上得从容，仿佛家常便饭便是如此，撒迦利亚对妻子这份应变的能耐暗暗吃惊。这火腿和牛肉馅饼她从何处变出来，他无从悬想。她为没有热菜致歉，又说早知有客，本会备下些更好的，如此如此，然后在桌上首坐定。少校坐在她右手，波琳挨着少校，波琳对面是凯约和撒迦利亚。女主人随即开口，问起那一夜的事——他们所有人撤离伦敦的那个要命的夜晚。

少校却拦住了，说这个题目还是不提为好。

「丧气的话题，」他说。「谈它没有半点好处，单说我吧，我可不愿意临睡前想它，想得心绪翻腾。」

「至少，」撒迦利亚说，「你们总可以讲讲，为什么到曼彻斯特来吧？」

「当然，」少校答道。「一来，巴黎不像从前那么惬意了；伦敦也没什么吸引人之处；我们通盘想想，还是曼彻斯特可取。再者，往后这一年里头有许多事要办，而曼彻斯特正是办事的地方。等你们俱乐部下次聚会，自会听到原委。」

「你们去过巴黎？」撒迦利亚太太说。「那地方不是邪恶性得很吗？」

===Original===
"Well, that depends on what you call wicked."

"Surely there cannot be two opinions on that point."

"It does seem so; and yet when you live abroad you find that things which are made a great deal of here are not thought so much of there; and, what is very curious, they think other things very wrong there of which we take no account here."

"Is that because they are not Christians?"

"Oh dear no; I am speaking of good Christian people; at least so I take them to be. And really, when you come to consider it, we all of us make a great fuss about our own little bit of virtue, and undervalue the rest—I cannot tell upon whose authority."

"But are they not, Major, dreadfully immoral in France?"

===Chinese===
「唔，那要看您把什么称作邪恶了。」

「在这一点上，总不至于有两种意见吧。」

「看去确是这样；然而人在外国住下来，就会发现此地大惊小怪的事，彼处并不那么当回事；还有一桩顶奇怪的，他们那边以为大谬不然的事，我们这里反倒毫不在意。」

「这是因为他们不是基督徒么？」

「哎呀不是；我说的是善良的基督徒，至少我把他们看作是。说实在的，细想起来，我们人人都为自己那一小片德行大吹大擂，把其余的一概看轻——奉的是谁的教命，我说不上来。」

「可是少校，那边的人不是伤风败俗得厉害么？」

===Original===
Pauline leaned over her plate and looked Mrs. Coleman straight in the face.

"Mrs. Coleman, you are English; you—"

Her father put up his hand; he foresaw what was coming, and that upon this subject Pauline would have defied all the rules of hospitality. So he replied calmly, but with the calm of suppressed force:

===Chinese===
波琳俯身向着自己的盘子，直直地望着科尔曼太太的脸。

「科尔曼太太，您是英国人；您——」

她父亲抬手拦住；他已料到下文，知道在这个题目上，波琳会把待客的一切规矩都抛到九霄云外。于是他平静地答话，那平静里压着一股蓄势之力：

===Original===
"Mrs. Coleman, as my daughter says, you are English; you are excusable. I will not dispute with you, but I will tell you a little story."

"Will you not take some more beer, Mr. Caillaud, before you begin?"

"No, thank you, madam, I have finished."

Caillaud pushed away his plate, on which three parts of what was given him, including all the ham, remained untouched, and began—his Gallicisms and broken English have been corrected in the version now before the reader:

===Chinese===
「科尔曼太太，正如我女儿所说，您是英国人；您情有可原。我不与您争辩，我只想讲一个小故事。」

「开讲之前，凯约先生，您不再用一点啤酒么？」

「不了，谢谢您，夫人，我用好了。」

凯约推开盘子——盘中的东西原封剩下四分之三，火腿一概未动——便讲了起来。他的法式腔调和磕磕绊绊的英语，在读者眼前这个版本里已作了订正：

===Original===
"In 1790 a young man named Dupin was living in Paris, in the house of his father, who was a banker there. The Dupins were rich, and the son kept a mistress, a girl named Victorine. Dupin the younger had developed into one of the worst of men. He was strictly correct in all his dealings, sober, guilty of none of the riotous excesses which often distinguish youth at that age, and most attentive to business; but he was utterly self-regarding, hard, and emotionless. What could have induced Victorine to love him I do not know; but love him she did, and her love instead of being a folly, was her glory. If love were always to be in proportion to desert, measured out in strictest and justest huckstering conformity therewith, what a poor thing it would be! The love at least of a woman is as the love of the Supreme Himself, and just as magnificent. Victorine was faithful to Dupin; and poor and handsome as she was, never wronged him by a loose look. Well, Dupin's father said his son must marry, and the son saw how reasonable and how necessary the proposal was. He did marry, and he cut himself adrift from Victorine without the least compunction, allowing her a small sum weekly, insufficient to keep her. There was no scene when they parted, for his determination was communicated to her by letter. Three months afterwards she had a child of whom he was the father. Did she quietly take the money and say nothing? Did she tear up the letter in a frenzy and return him the fragments? She did neither. She wrote to him and told him that she would not touch his gold. She would never forget him, but she could not be beholden to him now for a crust of bread. She had done no wrong hitherto—so she said, Mrs. Coleman; I only repeat her words—they are not mine. But to live on him after he had left her would be a mortal crime. So they separated, a victim she—both victims, I may say—to this cursed thing we call Society. One of the conditions on which the money was to have been given was, that she should never again recognise him in any way whatever. This half of the bargain she faithfully observed. For some months she was alone, trying to keep herself and her child, but at last she was taken up by a working stonemason named Legouvé. In 1793 came the Terror, and the Dupins were denounced and thrown into the Luxembourg. Legouvé was one of the Committee of Public Safety. It came to the recollection of the younger Dupin as he lay expecting death that he had heard that the girl Victorine had gone to live with Legouvé, and a ray of light dawned on him in his dungeon. He commissioned his wife to call on Victorine and implore her to help them. She did so. Ah, that was a wonderful sight—so like the Revolution! Madame Dupin, in her silks and satins, had often passed the ragged Victorine in the streets, and, of course, had never taken the slightest notice of her. Now Madame was kneeling to her! Respectability was in the dust before that which was not by any means respectable; the legitimate before the illegitimate! Oh, it was, I say, a wonderful sight in Victorine's wretched garret! She was touched with pity, and, furthermore, the memory of her old days with Dupin and her love for him revived. Legouvé was frightfully jealous, and she knew that if she pleaded Dupin's cause before him she would make matters worse. A sudden thought struck her. She went to Couthon and demanded an audience.

===Chinese===
「1790 年，一个名叫杜潘（Dupin）的年轻人住在巴黎他父亲家中，父亲是当地的银行家。杜潘家道殷实，儿子养着一位情妇，是个名叫维克托琳（Victorine）的姑娘。小杜潘长成了顶坏的那一类人。他往来交易一丝不苟，饮食有节，全然没有那个年纪的青年常犯的狂荡放纵，而且极其勤于业务；然而他极端自私，心硬如铁，不通情感。维克托琳究竟为何会爱上他，我不知道；可她确确实实爱了，而且她这份爱非但不是一桩痴愚，反倒是她的荣光。倘若爱总得同受之与否相称，像市侩称斤论两那样一丝不差、分毫不爽地量出来，爱该是件多么寒碜的东西！至少，一个女人的爱，就如同至高者本身的爱，同样壮丽。维克托琳对杜潘忠贞不贰；她尽管又贫寒又美貌，却从不曾用一个轻浮的眼色亏负他。后来，杜潘的父亲说儿子该娶亲了，儿子也看得出这提议何等在理、何等必要。他果然娶了亲，随即把维克托琳一刀两断，没有半分愧疚，只按周许给她一小笔钱，少得不够糊口。分手时连一场吵闹也没有，因为他的决断是用信通知她的。三个月后她生下一个孩子，孩子的父亲正是他。她可曾悄悄收下钱、一声不吭？可曾在暴怒中撕了信、把碎片寄还给他？她两样都没做。她写信告诉他，她不碰他的金子。她永远不会忘记他，可她如今不能受他一片面包的恩惠。她至今没有做过错事——她是这样说的，科尔曼太太；我不过转述她的话——那不是我的话。可是在他撇下她之后再靠他养活，便是弥天大罪。两人就这样分了手——她是牺牲品，恕我直言，是两个牺牲品——献给了我们唤作『社会』的这个可诅咒的东西。那笔钱原讲定了一个条件：她从此无论如何不得再认他。约定的这一半，她信守不渝。有几个月她独自挣扎，拉扯自己和孩子，末了同一位名叫勒古韦（Legouvé）的石匠工人过活。1793 年恐怖统治（the Terror）来临，杜潘一家遭人告发，投进了卢森堡监狱（the Luxembourg）。勒古韦是救国委员会（Committee of Public Safety）的成员。小杜潘躺在牢里等死，忽然记起曾听说维克托琳那姑娘跟勒古韦过活去了，地牢里顿时透进一线光亮。他托妻子去见维克托琳，恳求她援手。她去了。啊，那是何等奇伟的一幕——多像大革命啊！杜潘太太绫罗绸缎，在街上同衣衫褴褛的维克托琳擦肩而过何止一回，自然从来不曾把她放在眼里。如今太太竟跪倒在她面前！体面在毫不体面者面前匍匐于尘土；名正言顺的向名不正言不顺的下拜！噢，我说呀，在维克托琳那间破顶楼里，这真是奇伟的一幕！她动了怜悯，况且，同杜潘共度的旧日和对他的爱一齐在心头复活。勒古韦妒火极旺，她知道，若当着他的面为杜潘求情，只会把事情弄得更糟。她灵机一动，去见库东（Couthon），要求面谈。

===Original===
" 'Couthon,' she said, 'are the Dupins to die?'

" 'Yes, tomorrow.'

" 'Dupin the younger is the father of my child.'

" 'And he has deserted you, and you hate him. He shall die.'

" 'Pardon me, I do not hate him.'

" 'Ah, you love him still; but that is no reason why he should be spared, my pretty one. We must do our duty. They are plotters against the Republic, and must go.'

===Chinese===
「『库东，』她说，『杜潘一家要死么？』」

「『是的，明天。』」

「『小杜潘是我孩子的父亲。』」

「『他抛弃了你，你恨他。他得死。』」

「『恕我直言，我不恨他。』」

「『啊，你还爱着他；可这算不得留他一命的理由，我的美人。我们得恪尽本分。他们是阴谋反对共和国的人，得打发他们上路。』」

===Original===
" 'Couthon, they must live. Consider; shall that man ascend the scaffold with the thought in his heart that I could have rescued him, and that I did not; that I have had my revenge? Besides, what will be said?—that the Republic uses justice to satisfy private vengeance. All the women in my quarter know who I am.'

" 'That is a fancy.'

" 'Fancy! Is it a fancy to murder Dupin's wife—murder all that is good in her—murder the belief in her forever that there is such a thing as generosity? You do not wish to kill the soul? That is the way with tyrants, but not with the Republic.'

===Chinese===
「『库东，他们必须活。您想想：难道要让那个人踏上断头台的时候，心里还揣着一个念头，说我本可以救他、却没有救，说我报了仇？再说，人家会怎么说？——说共和国拿公义来偿私怨。我住的那一带，女人们谁不知道我的底细。』」

「『那是你的臆想。』」

「『臆想！杀害杜潘的妻子——杀害她身上一切善良的成分——永远扼杀她心中对世间尚存慷慨这回事的信念，这也叫臆想么？您总不愿意连灵魂也杀掉吧？那是暴君的行径，不是共和国的行径。』」

===Original===
"Thus Victorine strove with Couthon, and he at last yielded. Dupin and his father were released that night, and before daybreak they were all out of Paris and safe. In the morning Legouvé found that they were liberated, and on asking Couthon the reason, was answered with a smile that they had an eloquent advocate. Victorine had warned Couthon not to mention her name, and he kept his promise; but Legouvé conjectured but too truly. He went home, and in a furious rage taxed Victorine with infidelity to him, in favour of the man who had abandoned her. He would not listen to her, and thrust her from him with curses. I say nothing more about her history. I will only say this, that Pauline is that child who was born to her after Dupin left her. I say it because I am so proud that Pauline has had such a mother!"

===Chinese===
「维克托琳就这样同库东力争，他终于让了步。当夜杜潘父子便获释了，天亮之前，一家人都已出了巴黎，安然脱身。早晨勒古韦得知他们放了，问库东缘故，得到的回答含着微笑：他们有一位能言善辩的辩护人。维克托琳事先告诫过库东不要提她的名字，他信守了诺言；可是勒古韦猜得再准不过。他回到家，怒不可遏，责问维克托琳对他不贞，偏护的正是那个抛弃她的男人。他不听她分辩，咒骂着把她逐出门去。她后来的遭遇，我不再多讲。我只想说一句：波琳，就是杜潘离开她之后她生下的那个孩子。我这样说，是因为波琳有过这样一位母亲，我与有荣焉！」

===Original===
"Pauline her daughter!" said Zachariah. "I thought she was your daughter."

"She is my daughter: I became her father."

Everybody was silent.

===Chinese===
「波琳是她的女儿！」撒迦利亚说。「我还当她是您的女儿哩。」

「她是我的女儿：是我做成了她的父亲。」

满座默然。

===Original===
"Ah, you say nothing," said Caillaud; "I am not surprised. You are astonished. Well may you be so that such a creature should ever have lived. What would Jesus Christ have said to her?"

The company soon afterwards rose to go.

"Goodbye, Mrs. Coleman," said the Major in his careless way; "I am glad to find Manchester does not disagree with you. At least, I should think it does not."

"Oh no, Major Maitland, I like it quite as well as London. Mind, you promise to come again *soon*—very soon."

===Chinese===
「啊，你们不作声，」凯约说；「我并不奇怪。你们吃惊了。世上竟活过这样一个人，你们尽可以吃惊。基督若见了她，会说什么呢？」

不多时，宾客便起身告辞。

「再会，科尔曼太太，」少校用他那副满不在乎的口气说。「我很高兴，看来曼彻斯特跟您处得不坏。至少，我想是不坏的。」

「哦不坏，梅特兰少校，我喜欢这里，跟喜欢伦敦一个样。记着，您可答应了要*快*些再来——很快很快。」

===Original===
The Major had gone downstairs first. She had followed him to the first landing, and then returned to bid Pauline and Caillaud goodbye. She stood like a statue while Pauline put on her hat.

"Good night, madam," said Caillaud, slightly bowing.

"Good night, madam," said Pauline, not bowing in the least.

"Good night," she replied, without relaxing her rigidity.

As soon as they were in the street Pauline said, "Father, I abhor that woman. If she lives she will kill her husband."

===Chinese===
少校先下楼去了。她送他到第一段楼梯口，又折回来向波琳和凯约道别。波琳戴帽子的时候，她像一尊雕像般伫立着。

「晚安，夫人。」凯约说着，微微一躬。

「晚安，夫人。」波琳说，礼数半点全无。

「晚安。」她答道，僵直丝毫不减。

一到街上波琳就说：「父亲，我憎恶那个女人。她再活下去，是会把她丈夫害死的。」

===Original===
Mrs. Coleman, on the other hand, at the same moment said, "Zachariah, Pauline and Caillaud cannot come to this house again."

"Why not?"

"Why not, Zachariah? I am astonished at you! The child of a woman who lived in open sin!"

He made no reply. Years ago not a doubt would have crossed his mind. That a member of Mr. Bradshaw's church could receive such people as Caillaud and Pauline would have seemed impossible. Nevertheless, neither Caillaud nor Pauline were now repugnant to him; nor did he feel that any soundless gulf separated them from him, although, so far as he knew his opinions had undergone no change.

===Chinese===
科尔曼太太这边，同一刻也在说：「撒迦利亚，波琳和凯约不能再进这个家的门了。」

「为什么？」

「为什么，撒迦利亚？我真没想到你会这样！一个公开活在罪中的女人生下的孩子！」

他没有回答。若在多年以前，他心里本不会有半丝犹疑。布拉德肖牧师教会的一名会友竟能接待凯约和波琳这样的人，那时在他看来断无可能。然而此刻，凯约也好，波琳也好，都不再令他憎厌；他也不觉得有什么测不出底的深渊横在他们与他之间，尽管据他所知，自己的见解并未更改。

===Original===
Mrs. Coleman forbore to pursue the subject, for her thoughts went off upon another theme, and she was inwardly wondering whether the Major would ever invite her to the theatre again. Just as she was going to sleep, the figure of the Major hovering before her eyes, she suddenly bethought herself that Pauline, if not handsome, was attractive. She started, and lay awake for an hour. When she rose in the morning the same thought again presented itself, to dwell with her hence forwards, and to gnaw her continually like vitriol.

===Chinese===
科尔曼太太没有再追问下去，因为她的心思早飘到另一件事上，暗自琢磨少校还会不会再请她去看戏。就在将睡未睡、少校的身影还在眼前浮动的当口，她忽然想到：波琳虽说不上美貌，却自有动人之处。她猛地一惊，睁着眼躺了一个钟头。早晨起身，同一个念头又冒了上来，从此常驻不去，像镪水一样，时时刻刻咬啮着她。

## XV End of the Beginning / 十五、开端之终

===Original===
The trial took place at Lancaster. Zachariah was sorely tempted to go; but, in the first place, he had no money, and, in the second place, he feared arrest. Not that he would have cared two pins if he had been put into jail; but he could not abandon his wife. He was perfectly certain what the result would be, but nevertheless, on the day when the news was due, he could not rest. There was a mail coach which ran from Lancaster to Liverpool, starting from Lancaster in the afternoon and reaching Liverpool between eleven and twelve at night. He went out about that time and loitered about the coach-office as if he were waiting for a friend. Presently he heard the wheels and the rapid trot of the horses. His heart failed him, and he could almost have fainted.

“What’s the news?” said the clerk to the coachman. “All the whole d——d lot convicted, and one of ’em going to be hung.”

“One of them hung! Which one is that?”

“Why, him as killed the soldier, of course—the Frenchman.”

===Chinese===
审判在兰卡斯特（Lancaster）开庭。撒迦利亚心痒难熬，很想去一趟；只是第一他没有钱，第二他怕被捕。倒不是说自己蹲了大狱会有半分在意；而是他抛不下妻子。结果会怎样，他心里一清二楚，然而到了消息该传来的那一天，他还是安不下心来。有一班邮政马车跑兰卡斯特到利物浦（Liverpool）一线，午后从兰卡斯特发车，夜里十一点到十二点之间抵达利物浦。他约莫在那个时辰出了门，在马车行门首踱来踱去，装作等一位朋友的样子。不多时，他听见了车轮声和急促的马蹄声。他一阵心慌，几乎要晕倒。

「有什么新闻？」文员问车夫。「那帮该死的，一股脑儿全定了罪，其中一个还要给绞死。」

「绞死一个！是哪一个？」

「嗐，不就是杀了大兵的那个嘛——那个法国佬。」

===Original===
“A d——d good job too,” replied the clerk. “I should like to serve every ——— Frenchman in the country the same way.”

Zachariah could not listen any longer, but went home, and all night long a continuous series of fearful images passed before his eyes—condemned cells, ropes, gallows and the actual fall of the victim, down to the contortion of his muscles. He made up his mind on the following day that he would see Caillaud before he died, and he told his wife he was going. She was silent for a moment, and then she said:

“You will do as you like, I suppose: but I cannot see what is the use of it. You can do no good; you will lose your place here; it will cost you something; and when you get there you may have to stop there.”

===Chinese===
「那也是件该死的好事，」文员答道，「我巴不得把国内每一个法国佬都照这么收拾一遍。」

撒迦利亚再也听不下去，转身回了家。一整夜，一连串骇人的景象在他眼前接连掠过——死囚牢房，绳索，绞架，还有受刑者坠落的那一瞬，连他肌肉的抽搐都清晰可见。第二天，他打定主意，要在凯约死前见他一面，便对妻子说了自己要去。她沉默了片刻，然后说道：

「我瞧你还是想怎样就怎样；可我看不出这有什么用处。你去也帮不上忙；这边的差事要丢掉；一路还得花费；到了那儿，说不定人还得扣在那儿。」

===Original===
Zachariah could not restrain himself.

“Good God!” he cried, “you hear that one of my best friends is about to be hung, and you sit there like a statue—not a single word of sympathy or horror—you care no more than a stone. *Use* of going! I tell you I will go if I starve, or have to rot in jail all my lifetime. Furthermore, I will go this instant.”

He went out of the room in a rage, rammed a few things into a bag, and was out of the house in ten minutes. He was excusably unjust to his wife—excusably, because he could not help thinking that she was hard, and even cruel. Yet really she was not so, or if she was, she was not necessarily so, for injustice, not only to others, but to ourselves, is always begotten by a false relationship. There were multitudes of men in the world, worse than Zachariah, with whom she would have been, not only happier, but better. He, poor man, with all his virtues, stimulated and developed all that was disagreeable in her.

He was in no mood to rest, and walked on all that night. Amidst all his troubles he could not help being struck with the solemn, silent procession overhead. It was perfectly clear—so clear that the heavens were not a surface, but a depth, and the stars of a lesser magnitude were so numerous and brilliant that they obscured the forms of the greater constellations. Presently the first hint of day appeared in the east. We must remember that this was the year 1817, before, so it is commonly supposed, men knew what it was properly to admire a cloud or a rock. Zachariah was not, therefore, on a level with the most ordinary subscriber to a modern circulating library. Nevertheless he could not help noticing—we will say he did no more—the wonderful, the sacredly beautiful, drama which noiselessly displayed itself before him. Over in the east the intense deep blue of the sky softened a little. Then the trees in that quarter began to contrast themselves against the background and reveal their distinguishing shapes. Swiftly, and yet with such even velocity that in no one minute did there seem to be any progress compared with the minute preceding, the darkness was thinned, and resolved itself overhead into pure sapphire, shaded into yellow below and in front of him, while in the west it was still almost black. The grassy floor of the meadows now showed its colour, grey green, with the dew lying on it, and in the glimmer under the hedge might be discerned a hare or two stirring. Star by star disappeared, until none were left, save Venus, shining like a lamp till the very moment almost when the sun’s disc touched the horizon. Half a dozen larks mounted and poured forth that ecstasy which no bird but the lark can translate. More amazing than the loveliness of scene, sound, and scent around him was the sense of irrestisible movement. He stopped to watch it, for it grew so rapid that he could almost detect definite pulsations. Throb followed throb every second with increasing force, and in a moment more a burning speck of gold was visible, and behold it was day! He slowly turned his eyes away and walked onwards.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚再也按捺不住。

「老天爷！」他嚷道，「你听见我最要好的朋友之一就要被绞死了，却坐在那儿像尊雕像——没有半句同情，没有半句惊骇——你跟一块石头差不多。还问去有什么『用处』！告诉你，哪怕挨饿，哪怕一辈子烂在牢里，我也要去。不但如此，我这就动身。」

他怒气冲冲地走出房间，胡乱往提包里塞了几样东西，不出十分钟便出了家门。他这样冤屈妻子，情有可原——之所以情有可原，是因为他不由得不觉得她心硬，甚至残忍。可她其实并不是那样；或者纵然是那样，也未必非那样不可，因为不义——无论施于他人，还是施于自身——总是由一种错位的关系孕育而生。世上有的是比撒迦利亚更不如的男人，跟了他们，她非但会更幸福，人也会变得更良善。而他呢，可怜的人，纵有一身美德，却偏把她身上一切惹人嫌恶的东西都催发出来了。

他无心歇息，索性走了一整夜。满怀愁苦之中，他仍不由得被头顶那场庄严而无声的行进撼动了。夜空澄澈极了——澄澈得天穹不成其为一层穹面，而是一道深渊，小星辰又多又亮，竟把大星座的形貌都淹没了。不久，东方透出了破晓的第一丝消息。我们得记住，那是 1817 年，照通行的说法，还在人们懂得怎样真正欣赏一朵云、一块石头的年代之前。因此，撒迦利亚还够不上如今流通图书馆最寻常订户的那个水准。然而他仍旧不由自主地注意到——我们不妨说，他仅止于注意到——那出在他面前无声铺展开来的、奇妙而圣洁之美的戏剧。东方深沉的湛蓝先是柔和了一点。接着，那一方的树木开始在背景上衬出轮廓，显出各自独特的形貌。黑暗迅疾地稀薄下去，却又那样匀速，任一分钟与前一分钟相比都显不出进展；头顶上化为一色纯净的蓝宝石，在他前方和脚下晕出鹅黄，西边却还近乎墨黑。草地的绿茵这时显出了颜色，灰蒙蒙的绿，露水铺在上面；树篱脚下微光朦胧，隐约可辨一两只走动的野兔。星星一颗接一颗隐去，直到一颗不剩，只剩金星（Venus）像一盏灯似地亮着，几乎一直亮到日轮触到地平线的那一刻。六只云雀腾上天空，倾泻出那种狂喜——除了云雀，没有一只鸟能把这份狂喜传达出来。比周遭景致、声响与芬芳的可爱更教人惊异的，是那种不可抗拒的运行之感。他停下脚步凝望，因为天色运行得那样迅疾，他几乎能觉出一下下分明的搏动。一次搏动接着一次搏动，每一秒都在加力；再过片刻，一点燃烧的金斑显出来了——看哪，天亮了！他缓缓收回目光，继续赶路。

===Original===
Lancaster was reached on the second evening after he left Liverpool. He could not travel fast nor long together, for he was not yet completely strong. He secured a bed in a low part of the town, at a public-house, and on the morning of the third day presented himself at the prison door. After some formalities he was admitted, and taken by a warder along a corridor with whitewashed walls to the condemned cell where Caillaud lay. The warder looked through a grating, and said to Zachariah that a visitor was already there. Two were not allowed at a time, but he would tell the prisoner that somebody was waiting for him.

“Let’s see, what’s your name?” said the warder. Then it suddenly struck him that he had been fool enough, in the excitement of entering the prison, to sign his real name in the book. There was no help for it now, and he repeated that it was Coleman.

“Ah yes, Coleman,” echoed the man, in a manner which was significant.

“Who is the other visitor?” said Zachariah.

“It is his daughter.”

His first thought was to ask to be let in, but his next was, that it would be profanity to disturb the intercourse of father and child, and he was silent. However, he had been announced, and Caillaud appeared at the grating begging permission for his friend to enter. It was at first refused; but presently something seemed to strike the jailer, for he relented with a smile.

===Chinese===
离开利物浦后的第二天傍晚，他到了兰卡斯特。他走不快，也连走不远，因为身子还没有完全复元。他在城中低洼地段的一家酒馆里弄到一个铺位，第三天一早便到监狱门口报到。经过几道手续，他获准入内，由一名狱卒领着，走过一条四壁刷得雪白的走廊，来到凯约躺着的死囚牢房。狱卒朝铁栅里望了望，对撒迦利亚说，已经有一位探视的人先在了；一次不许进两个人，不过他可以去告知犯人，说有人在等他。

「让我瞧瞧，你叫什么名字？」狱卒说。撒迦利亚这才猛然想起，自己进监狱时一时心慌，竟糊涂到在簿子上签下了真名。如今无可挽回，他只好再说一遍：科尔曼。

「啊，是，科尔曼。」那人应声道，神气里大有文章。

「另一位探视的人是谁？」撒迦利亚问。

「是他的女儿。」

他起先想请求放自己进去，转念又想，搅扰这对父女的相聚未免亵渎，便没有作声。可是他已经通报上去了，凯约出现在铁栅前，恳求准许他的朋友进去。起初遭到了拒绝；但不一会儿，那狱卒像是想起了什么，含笑松了口。

===Original===
“You won’t want to come again?” he observed interrogatively.

“No; that is to say, I think not.”

“No; that is to say, I think not,” he repeated slowly, word for word, adding, “I shall have to stay with you while you are together.”

Zachariah entered, the warder locking the door behind him, and seating himself on the edge of the bedstead, where he remained during the whole of the interview, jingling his keys and perfectly unmoved.

The three friends spoke not a word for nearly five minutes. Zachariah was never suddenly equal to any occasion which made any great demands upon him. It often made him miserable that it was so. Here he was, in the presence of one whom he had so much loved, and who was about to leave him forever, and he had nothing to say. That could have been endured could he but have *felt* and showed his feeling, could he but have cast himself upon his neck and wept over him, but he was numbed and apparently immovable. It was Caillaud who first broke the silence.

===Chinese===
「你不会还想再来吧？」他带着探询的口气说。

「不来了；或者说，我想是不会再来了。」

「不来了；或者说，我想是不会再来了。」他一个字一个字地慢慢重复了一遍，又补上一句，「你们在一处的时候，我得待在旁边。」

撒迦利亚走了进去，狱卒反手锁上门，自己在床架沿上坐下——整个会面期间他就坐在那儿，钥匙拨得叮当乱响，人却纹丝不动。

三个朋友将近五分钟没有说一句话。撒迦利亚这个人，凡是突然遇到要他倾力应办的场面，从来都招架不住。事情每每如此，常叫他痛苦。此刻他就在他如此深爱、而即将永诀的人面前，竟无话可说。倘若他还能有所『感受』、还能把感受流露出来，倘若他还能扑上前搂住他的脖颈、伏着他恸哭，这本都还忍受得了；可他麻木了，看上去无动于衷。先打破沉默的是凯约。

===Original===
“It appears I shall have to console you rather than you me; believe me, I care no more about dying, as mere dying, than I do about walking across this room. There are two things which disturb me—the apprehension of some pain, and bidding goodbye to Pauline and you, and two or three more.”

There was, after all, but just a touch needed to break up Zachariah and melt him.

“You are happier than I,” he cried. “Your work is at an end. No more care for things done or undone; you are discharged, and nobly discharged, with honour. But as for me!”

“With honour!” and Caillaud smiled. “To be hung like a forger of banknotes—not even to be shot—and then to be forgotten. Forgotten utterly! This does not happen to be one of those revolutions which men remember.”

===Chinese===
「看来倒得由我来安慰你，而不是你安慰我了；请相信，死这件事，单就死而言，我不比走过这间屋子更放在心上。搅扰我的只有两件事——一是怕受些疼痛，二是同波琳和你，还有两三位朋友道别。」

说到底，只消轻轻一触，就能击溃撒迦利亚，把他融化。

「你比我幸福，」他喊道。「你的差事到头了。做了的、没做的，一概不必再操心；你卸了役，而且是光荣地卸了役，带着荣誉。可我呢！」

「还荣誉呢！」凯约笑了。「像伪造钞票的骗子一样给绞死——连枪毙都轮不上——然后被人忘掉。忘得一干二净！这场革命，碰巧不属于人们肯记住的那一种。」

===Original===
“No! men will not remember,” said Pauline, with an elevation of voice and manner almost oratorical. “Men will not remember, but there is a memory in the world which forgets nothing.”

“Do you know,” said Caillaud, “I have always loved adventure, and at times I look forward to death with curiosity and interest, just as if I were going to a foreign country.”

“Tell me,” said Zachariah, “if there is anything I can do.”

“Nothing. I would ask you to see that Pauline comes to no harm, but she can take care of herself. I have nothing to give you in parting. They have taken everything from me.”

===Chinese===
「不！人是不会记住的，」波琳说，声调与姿态昂扬，几乎带上了演说家的气概。「人是不会记住的，可世间自有一种记忆，什么都不忘。」

「你知道吗，」凯约说，「我素来热爱冒险，有时我怀着好奇与兴味盼望死亡，就像要去一个异邦似的。」

「告诉我，」撒迦利亚说，「有什么我能效劳的没有。」

「没有。我本想托你照看波琳，别叫她受什么伤害，可她自己照管得了自己。临别我没有什么可以送你。他们把我的一切都夺走了。」

===Original===
“What a brute I am! I shall never see you again, and I cannot speak,” sobbed Zachariah.

“Speak! What need is there of speaking? What is there which can be said at such a time? To tell you the truth, Coleman, I hardly cared about having you here. I did not want to imperil the calm which is now happily upon me; we all of us have something unaccountable and uncontrollable in us, and I do not know how soon it may wake in me. But I did wish to see you, in order that your mind might be at peace about me. Come, goodbye!”

Caillaud put his hand on Zachariah’s shoulder.

“This will not do,” he said. “For my sake forbear. I can face what I have to go through next Monday if am not shaken. Come, Pauline, you too, my child, must leave me for a bit.”

===Chinese===
「我真是头畜生！从此再也见不着你了，我却连话都说不出，」撒迦利亚哽咽道。

「说！哪里用得着说？这样的时刻，又有什么话可说？跟你说句实话，科尔曼，你来，我几乎并不情愿。我不想拿如今幸好笼盖着我的这份安宁去冒险；我们每个人身上都有些说不清、管不住的东西，我不知道它会在我心里多快醒来。可我确实愿意见你一面，好让你为我安心。来吧，别了！」

凯约把手搭在撒迦利亚的肩上。

「这样可不行，」他说。「看在我的份上，忍一忍。只要不受震动，下星期一要过去的那一关，我经受得起。来，波琳，你也是，我的孩子，也得离开我一会儿了。」

===Original===
Zachariah looked at Pauline, who rose and threw her shawl over her shoulders. Her lips were tightly shut, but she was herself. The warder opened the door. Zachariah took his friend’s hand, held it for a moment, and then threw his arms round his neck. There is a pathos in parting which the mere loss through absence does not explain. We all of us feel it, even if there is to be a meeting again in a few months, and we are overcome by incomprehensible emotion when we turn back down the pier, unable any longer to discern the waving of the handkerchief, or when the railway train turns the curve in the cutting and leaves us standing on the platform. Infinitely pathetic, therefore, is the moment when we separate forever.

Caillaud was unsettled for an instant, and then, slowly untwining the embrace, he made a sign to Pauline, who took Zachariah’s hand and led him outside; the heavy well-oiled bolt of the lock shooting back under the key with a smooth strong thud between them. She walked down the corridor alone, not noticing that he had not followed her, and had just passed out of sight when an officer stepped up to him and said:

“Your name is Coleman?”

“Yes.”

“Sorry to hear it. My name is Nadin. You know me, I think. You must consider yourself my prisoner.”

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚望了波琳一眼；她站起身来，把披肩披上肩头。她的双唇抿得紧紧的，但她还是她自己。狱卒开了门。撒迦利亚握住朋友的手，握了片刻，随即张开双臂搂住他的脖颈。离别自有一种悲怆，不是单靠天各一方的失落所能解释的。我们人人都感受得到，哪怕几个月后就要重逢；当我们转身走下码头，再也望不见挥动的手帕，或是当火车转过路堑的弯道、把我们撇在站台上时，我们总会被一种难以索解的情绪压倒。因此，当我们从此永诀，那一刻的悲怆更是无穷无尽。

凯约刹那间乱了方寸，随即缓缓解开这一抱，向波琳递了个眼色；波琳拉起撒迦利亚的手，把他领了出去；门锁那根上足了油的沉重门闩随着钥匙一转顺滑地弹回，在两人之间发出一声沉实有力的钝响。她独自沿走廊走去，没有留意他没有跟上来；她的身影刚刚消失，一名警官便走到他跟前，说道：

「你叫科尔曼？」

「是。」

「听了遗憾。我姓纳丁（Nadin）。我想你认得我。你得把自己看作我的犯人了。」

===Original===
Zachariah was in prison for two years. He had not been there three months when his wife died.

---

Let us now look forward to 1821; let us walk down one of the new streets just beginning to stretch northwards from Pentonville; let us stop opposite a little house, with a little palisade in front, enclosing a little garden five and twenty feet long and fifteen feet broad; let us peep through the chink between the blind and the window. We see Zachariah and Pauline. Another year passes; we peep through the same chink again. A cradle is there, in which lies Marie Pauline Coleman; but where is the mother? She is not there, and the father alone sits watching the child.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚在狱中关了两年。入狱不出三个月，他的妻子便死了。

---

现在让我们把目光投向 1821 年；让我们走进佩恩顿维尔（Pentonville）以北那些正开始向北伸展的新街中的一条；让我们在一座小屋对面站定，屋前立着一道小小的木栅，围出一方长二十五英尺、宽十五英尺的小小的园子；让我们从百叶窗与窗棂之间的缝隙向里窥望。我们看见撒迦利亚和波琳。又一年过去；我们再从同一道缝隙窥望。屋里多了一只摇篮，摇篮里躺着玛丽·波琳·科尔曼（Marie Pauline Coleman）；可母亲在哪儿？她不在，只有父亲独自坐着，守着孩子。

## Endnotes / 尾注

===Original===
1. “O ze (ye) my feris (companions) and deir freyndis, quod he,

Of bywent perillis not ignorant ben we,

Ze have sustenit gretir dangeris unkend,

Like as hereof God sall make sone ane end:

The rage of Silla, that huge sweste (whirlpool) in the se

Ze have eschapit and passit eik (each) have ze:

The euer (pot) routand (roaring) Caribdis rokkis fell

The craggis quhare monstruous Cyclopes dwell:

Ze are expert: pluk up zour harts, I zou pray,

This dolorous drede expell and do away.

Sum tyme thereon to think may help perchance.”

—Gawin Douglas.

===Chinese===
1. 「哦你们（ye），我的同伴们（companions）和亲爱的朋友们，」他说，

往昔经历的危难，我们并非无知，

你们承受过更重、前所未知的凶险，

一如上帝不久也将给这一场一个尽头：

斯库拉的狂暴——那海上巨大的漩涡（whirlpool）——

你们已经逃脱，而且也一一（each）闯了过去：

那大锅（pot）般咆哮（roaring）的卡律布狄斯，那凶险的岩礁，

还有巨怪的库克洛佩斯栖居的巉岩：

你们都是过来人：振作起你们的心来，我求你们，

把这凄惨的恐惧驱散、抛开。

有时回想一番，或许不无助益。

——加文·道格拉斯（Gawin Douglas）。

===Original===
“Endure and conquer! Jove will soon dispose,

To future good, our past and present woes.

With me, the rocks of Scylla you have tried;

Th’ inhuman Cyclops, and his den defied.

What greater ills hereafter can you bear!

Resume your courage, and dismiss your care.

An hour will come, with pleasure to relate

Your sorrows past, as benefits of fate.”

—Dryden.

↩︎

===Chinese===
「忍受并战胜吧！朱庇特不久自会安排，

把我们往昔与眼下的愁苦，化作日后的善果。

你们随我闯试过斯库拉的礁岩，

也藐视过那残忍的库克洛佩斯和他的兽窟。

此后还有什么更大的灾殃，是你们承受不起的！

重振你们的勇气，撇开你们的忧虑。

终有一刻到来，你们将怀着欣悦追述

往日的悲痛，视之为命运的恩惠。」

——德莱顿（Dryden）。

↩︎

===Original===
> “Per various casus, per tot discrimina rerum,
> Tendimus in Latium; sedes ubi fata quietas
> Ostendunt. Illic fas regna resurgere Trojae.
> Durate, et vosmet rebus servate secundis.”
> — Virgil

===Chinese===
> “历尽种种变故，阅遍万般艰危，
> 我们向拉丁姆（Latium）前行；命运在那方
> 为我们昭示安宁的居所；在那里，特洛伊的王国理当重振。
> 坚持下去，且为顺遂的时运保全己身。”
> ——维吉尔（Virgil）

===Original===
> “By diuers casis, sere parrellis and sufferance
> Unto Itaill we ettill (aim) quhare destanye
> Has schap (shaped) for vs ane rest and quiet harbrye
> Predestinatis thare Troye sall ryse agane.
> Be stout on prosper fortoun to remane.”
> — Gwain Douglas’s translation

===Chinese===
> “历诸般际遇，经多番危难与苦忍，
> 我们意在奔赴意大利；在那方，命数
> 已为我等铺就一处安歇的静港；
> 凭着前定，特洛伊必将在彼重兴。
> 且坚毅自持，以待昌隆之运。”

## XXIII Further Development / 二十三、事态的进一步展开

===Original===

Six months afterwards Priscilla was about to give birth to her firstborn. At Mrs. Allen’s earnest request old nurse Barton had been engaged, who nursed Mrs. Allen when George came into the world, and loved him like her own child. As a counterpoise, Mrs. Broad, who had desired a nurse from a distance, whom she knew, installed herself with Priscilla. Nurse Barton had a great dislike to Mrs. Broad, although she attended Mr. Broad’s ministrations at Tanner’s Lane. She was not a member of the church, and never could be got to propose herself for membership. There was, in fact, a slight flavour of Paganism about her. She was considered to belong to the “world,” and it was only her age and undoubted skill which saved her practice amongst the Tanner’s Lane ladies. There was a rival in the town; but she was a younger woman, and never went out to any of the respectable houses, save when Mrs. Barton was not available.

The child was safely born, and as soon as nurse Barton could be spared for an hour or two she went to Mrs. Allen, whom she found alone. The good woman then gave Mrs. Allen her opinions, which, by the way, she always gave with prefect frankness.

“Thank the Lord-i-mercy this ’ere job, Mrs. Allen, is near at an end. If it ’adn’t been my dear boy George’s wife, never would I have set foot in that ’ouse.”

“Why not?”

===Chinese===

六个月以后，普丽西拉就要临盆了。应艾伦太太的恳切请求，家里请来了老保姆巴顿（nurse Barton）——当年乔治出世，就是她看护的艾伦太太，她也把乔治当自家的孩子一般疼爱。作为抗衡，布罗德太太索性亲自搬来同住，照看普丽西拉——她原是打算从远处请一位自己熟识的保姆的。巴顿太太对布罗德太太厌恶得很，虽说她也常去坦纳斯巷听布罗德先生讲道。她不是教会成员，也从来劝不动她自荐入会。她身上，说实话，隐隐带着一股异教气。众人都把她归入「世俗之人」一类，全凭她那把年纪和无可置疑的手段，才在坦纳斯巷的太太们中间保住了自己的营生。镇上倒有一个同行对头；不过那人年轻些，除非巴顿太太腾不开身，体面人家是从来请不到她的。

孩子平安落了地。巴顿保姆一等到能抽开一两个钟头，便去看艾伦太太，见她正独自一人。于是这位好心肠的女人便向艾伦太太发表起自己的意见来——顺便说一句，她发表意见，向来是十二分直率的。

「感谢主啊慈悲，这桩差事，艾伦太太，总算快到头了。要不是为了我那宝贝乔治的媳妇，我断不会踏进那宅子一步。」

「为什么不肯？」

===Original===

“Why not? Now, Mrs. Allen, you know as well as I do. To see that there Mrs. Broad! She might ’ave ordered me about; that wouldn’t a been nothin’; but to see ’er a orderin’ *’im*, and a ridin’ on ’im like a wooden rockin’-’orse, and with no more feelin’! A nasty, prancin’, ’igh-’eaded creatur’. Thinks I to myself, often and often, if things was different I’d let yer know, that I would; but I ’eld my tong. It ’ud a been wuss for us all, p’r’aps, if I ’adn’t.”

“I should think so,” said Mrs. Allen; “remember she is the minister’s wife.”

“Minister’s wife!” repeated Mrs. Barton, and with much scorn. “And then them children of hern. Lord be praised I never brought such things as them into the world. That was her fine nuss as she must get down from London; and pretty creaturs they are!”

“Hush, hush; George has one of them, and she is mine.”

“I can’t ’elp it, ma’am, I must speak out. I say as he ought to ’ave married somebody better nor ’er; though I don’t mind a tellin’ of yer she’s the best of the lot. Why did the Lord in heaven, as sent Jesus Christ to die for our souls, let my George ’ave such a woman as that? What poor silly creaturs we all are!” and the old woman, bending her head down, shook it mournfully and rubbed her knees with her hand. She was thinking of him as he lay in her lap years and years ago, and pondering, in her disconnected, incoherent way, over the mysteries which are mysteries to us as much as to her.

===Chinese===

「为什么不肯？嗳，艾伦太太，这您跟我一样清楚。瞧瞧那位布罗德太太！她要支使支使我，倒也罢了，算不得什么；可瞧她支使起*他*来那副样子，把他当一匹木头摇马骑，还一点儿不带感情！一个张狂作态、眼高于顶的东西。我心里常常想，常常想，若不是有所顾忌，我早叫她认识认识我了，真会的；可我把舌头管住了。兴许，幸亏我管住了，不然咱们大伙儿还得更遭殃。」

「我也这么想，」艾伦太太说，「别忘了，她可是牧师的太太。」

「牧师的太太！」巴顿太太重复了一遍，满腔的不屑。「还有她那几个孩子呢。赞美主，我从没把这样的玩意儿接到世上来过。亏她还要特地从伦敦请下那么一位好保姆；瞧瞧养出来的这些俊俏玩意儿！」

「嘘，嘘；乔治娶的正是其中一个，而她如今是我的人了。」

「忍不住，太太，我非说不可。我总说，他本该娶个比她强的；不过我也不妨告诉您，她已经是那一窝里顶好的一个了。那位差耶稣基督来替我们灵魂受死的天上的主，怎么偏偏叫我那乔治摊上这么一个女人呢？咱们人类真是些可怜的傻东西啊！」老妇人低下头去，凄然摇着，一只手揉着膝盖。她想着多年前躺在她怀里的他，用她那东一句西一句、连不成片的方式，思忖着那些奥秘——那些对我们和对她一样、始终都是奥秘的东西。

===Original===

Mrs. Broad, who was in constant attendance upon Priscilla, at the very earliest moment pronounced the baby a Flavel, and made haste to tell father and mother so. There was no mistaking a refinement, so to say, in the features and an expression in the eye. George, of course, was nearly banished for a time, and was much with his father and mother. At length, however, the hour arrived when the nurse took her departure, and, Mrs. Broad having also somewhat retired, he began to see a little more of his wife; but it was very little. She was altogether shut up in maternal cares﻿—closed round, apparently, from the whole world. He was not altogether displeased, but he did at times think that she might give him a moment now and then, especially as he was greatly interested in the coming county election. It was rather too early in the day for a Free Trader to stand as a candidate, but two Whigs, of whom they had great hopes, had been put up, and both George and his father were most energetic in canvassing and on committees.

Mr. Broad had decided not to vote. He did not deny that his sympathies were not with the Tories, but as a minister of religion it would be better for him to remain neutral. This annoyed the Allens and damaged their cause. At a meeting held by the Tories one of the speakers called upon the audience to observe that all the respectable people, with very few exceptions, were on their side. “Why,” cried he, “I’ll bet you, my friends, all Lombard Street to a china orange that they don’t get even the Dissenting parson to vote for the Radicals. Of course he won’t, and why? Just because he’s a cut above his congregation, and knows a little more than they do, and belongs to the intelligent classes.”

George bethought himself that perhaps he might do something through Priscilla to influence her mother, and he determined to speak to her about it. He came home one evening after attending a committee, and found supper ready. Priscilla was downstairs, sitting with the door open.

===Chinese===

日夜守在普丽西拉身边的布罗德太太，最早一刻便断定这婴孩是个弗拉维尔家的人，还忙不迭把这话告诉了孩子的爹娘。那眉眼间——不妨这样说——的一股清秀之气，还有眼睛里的一副神情，是错认不了的。乔治呢，自然一度几乎被扫地出门，成天待在他爹娘身边。不过保姆终于动身离去，布罗德太太也稍稍退避，他这才开始多见到妻子一些；可也还是少得很。她整个人都闭锁在为娘的操心里——看样子，是把整个世界都关在了外头。他倒也并非全然不悦，只是有时不免想，她偶或也该分他片刻才好，何况他此刻正为即将到来的郡选举热心得很。让一个自由贸易派（Free Trader）出面当候选人，在当时还嫌太早，不过已经推出了两位辉格党人，大家对他们都寄予厚望，乔治和他父亲在拉票和委员会上都劲头十足。

布罗德先生已打定主意不投票。他并不否认自己的同情不在托利党一边，可是作为一名宗教牧师，还是保持中立为妙。这叫艾伦一家气恼，也坏了他们的大事。托利党开的一次集会上，有个演讲的人请听众留意：体面人物除寥寥数人外，全都站在他们那一边。「嗳，」他嚷道，「朋友们，我拿整条朗伯德街（Lombard Street）跟你们赌一只中国橙子，他们也休想弄得连那个不信国教的牧师都投激进派的票。他当然不会投，为什么？就因为他比他的会众高出一头，比他们多知道一点儿，属于有智识的阶级。」

乔治转念想到，或许可以借普丽西拉去打动她母亲，便决意同她说一说。一天晚上，他开完委员会回家，见晚饭已经备好。普丽西拉在楼下坐着，房门敞开着。

===Original===

“Hadn’t we better shut the door?” said George; “it is rather cold.”

“No, no, George; I shouldn’t hear the baby.”

“But Ellen is upstairs.”

“Yes; but then she might go to sleep.”

“My dear,” began George, “I wish your father could be got to vote straight. You see that by not doing so he goes against all the principles of the Independents. Ever since they have been in existence they have always stood up for freedom, and we are having the large yellow flag worked with the words, Civil and Religious Liberty. It will be a bad thing for us if he holds aloof. I cannot understand,” he continued, getting eloquent, “how a Dissenting minister can make up his mind not to vote against a party which has been answerable for all the oppression and all the wrongs in English history, and for all our useless wars, and actually persecuted his predecessors in this very meetinghouse in which he now preaches. Besides, to say nothing about the past, just look at what we have before us now. The Tories are the most bitter opponents of Free Trade. I can’t tell you how I feel about it, and I do think that if you were to speak to your mother she would perhaps induce him to change his mind.”

===Chinese===

「咱们还是把门关上，好不好？」乔治说，「天怪冷的。」

「不，不，乔治；关了门我就听不见宝宝了。」

「可埃伦（Ellen）在楼上呢。」

「是呀；可她兴许会睡着的。」

「亲爱的，」乔治开腔道，「我巴不得能劝得你父亲把票投正。你想，他不投，就违背了独立派的全部原则。独立派自立以来，从来都站在自由这一边，咱们正赶制一面大黄旗，绣上『公民与宗教自由』（Civil and Religious Liberty）几个字。他要是袖手旁观，对咱们可是件坏事。我实在不明白，」他接着说，越说越激昂，「一个不信国教的牧师，怎么下得了决心，不去投票反对那个政党——英格兰历史上一切压迫、一切冤屈，咱们打的那一场场无谓的战争，桩桩件件都要归咎于它，而且就在他如今讲道的这座礼拜堂里，它当真迫害过他的前辈。再说，过去的事且撇开不提，只看看眼前摆着的。托利党是自由贸易最凶恶的死对头。我说不出我心里是什么滋味，我倒真觉得，你若去同你母亲说一说，她说不定能劝得他回心转意。」

===Original===

It was a long time since he had said so much all at once to his wife.

“George, George, I am sure he’s awake!” and she was off out of the room in an instant. Presently she returned.

“Mamma came here this afternoon and brought his hood﻿—a new one﻿—such a lovely hood!﻿—and she says he looks more than ever like a Flavel in it.”

“I don’t believe you listened to a word of what I was saying.”

“Oh yes, I did; you always think I don’t listen; but I can listen to you and watch for him too.”

===Chinese===

他已经好一阵子没对妻子一口气说这么多话了。

「乔治，乔治，我敢保他醒了！」话音未落，她人已经出了屋。不多时又回来了。

「妈妈今天下午来过，给宝宝带来一顶兜帽——顶新的——多可爱的一顶兜帽啊！——她说宝宝戴上它，比以往任何时候都更像弗拉维尔家的人了。」

「我看我方才说的话，你一个字也没听进去。」

「噢，不，我听着呢；你总以为我不听；可我既能听你说话，又能留心着他呀。」

===Original===

“What did I say?”

“Never mind, I know.”

“I cannot understand,” he said sullenly, and diverted for a moment from his subject, “why mamma should be always telling *you* he is a Flavel.”

“Well, really, George, why shouldn’t she? Tryphosa said the other day that if you were to take away grandpapa Flavel’s wig and bands from the picture in the *Evangelical Magazine* he would be just like him.”

“It seems to me,” replied George, “that if there’s any nonsense going about the town, it always comes to you. People don’t talk such rubbish to me.”

===Chinese===

「那我说了些什么？」

「不用管，我知道。」

「我就是不明白，」他闷闷地说，一时岔开了本题，「妈妈干吗总对*你*念叨他是个弗拉维尔。」

「哎呀，说真的，乔治，她为什么不该说呢？前两天特里福莎还说，若把《福音派杂志》（*Evangelical Magazine*）画像里弗拉维尔外祖父的假发和颈带拿掉，宝宝就活脱脱是他了。」

「依我看，」乔治答道，「镇上但凡有什么蠢话在流传，总会传到你耳朵里来。人家可不对我说这种废话。」

===Original===

What the effect of this speech might have been cannot be told, for at this moment the baby did really cry, and Priscilla departed hastily for the night. She never spoke to her mother about the election, for, as George suspected, she had not paid the slightest attention to him; and as to exchanging with her mother a single word upon such a subject as politics, or upon any other subject which was in any way impersonal﻿—she never did such a thing in her life.

It was the uniform practice of the Reverend John Broad to walk down the main Street of Cowfold on Monday morning, and to interchange a few words with any of his congregation whom he might happen to meet. This pastoral perambulation not only added importance to him, and made him a figure in Cowfold, but, coming always on Monday, served to give people some notion of a preoccupation during the other days of the week which was forbidden, for mental reasons, on the day after Sunday. On this particular Monday Mr. Broad was passing Mr. Allen’s shop, and seeing father and son there, went in. Mr. Allen himself was at a desk which stood near the window, and George was at the counter, in a black apron, weighing nails.

After an unimportant remark or two about the weather, Mr. Allen began in a cheery tone, so as to prevent offence:

“Mr. Broad, we are sorry we cannot persuade you to vote for the good cause.”

Mr. Broad’s large mouth lengthened itself, and his little eyes had an unpleasant light in them.

“Brother Allen, I have made this matter the subject of much meditation, and I may even say of prayer, and I have come to the conclusion it will be better for me to occupy a neutral position.”

===Chinese===

这句话会有什么效果，就无从知道了，因为正在此时，宝宝当真哭了起来，普丽西拉便匆匆而去，当夜没有再回来。她始终没有同母亲提起选举的事——正如乔治疑心的那样，她压根儿没留意听他；至于同她母亲就政治这样的题目、或任何多少不涉自身的题目交谈上一句——这样的事，她生平从不曾做过。

约翰·布罗德牧师有个一成不变的惯例：礼拜一上午，沿考福尔德的大街走一遭，同碰巧遇上的会众中人说上几句。这趟教牧巡行不但给他平添了几分分量，使他在考福尔德成了个人物，而且总在礼拜一露面，还让人们隐约觉出，一周其余的日子里他另有心事在身——那种心事，出于保养精神的缘故，礼拜日的次日是禁止操办的。就在这个礼拜一，布罗德先生路过艾伦先生的铺子，望见父子俩都在，便走了进去。艾伦先生本人坐在靠窗的一张书桌前，乔治站在柜台后头，系着条黑围裙，正称钉子。

就天气敷衍了两三句不相干的闲话之后，艾伦先生用爽快的口吻开了腔，为的是免得开罪于人：

「布罗德先生，我们很遗憾，劝不动您为正义的一方投票。」

布罗德先生那张阔嘴往长里拉了拉，一双小眼睛里透出叫人不舒服的光。

「艾伦弟兄，这件事我已经反复默想，甚至可以说反复祷告，如今得出的结论是：我最好置身于一个中立的地位。」

===Original===

“Why, Mr. Broad? You cannot doubt on which side the right lies.”

“No; but then there are so many things to be considered, so many responsibilities, and my first care, you see, must be the ministerial office and the church which Providence has placed in my charge.”

“But, Mr. Broad, there are only two or three of them who are Tory.”

“Only old Bushel and another farmer or two,” interrupted George.

Mr. Broad looked severely at George, but did not condescend to answer him.

===Chinese===

「哎呀，布罗德先生？正义在哪一边，您不会有所怀疑吧。」

「不会；可是要考虑的事情这么多，责任这么重，您瞧，我头一件要挂心的，必须是牧师圣职，和天意交付我照管的那间教会。」

「可是，布罗德先生，他们里头不过两三个托利党人罢了。」

「不过是老布舍尔（Bushel），外加一两个种庄稼的。」乔治插嘴道。

布罗德先生严厉地瞪了乔治一眼，却不屑屈尊答他的话。

===Original===

“Those two or three, Brother Allen, require consideration as much as ourselves. Brother Bushel is, I may say, a pillar of the cause, a most faithful follower of the Lord; and what are political questions compared with that? How could I justify myself if my liberty were to become a stumbling-block to my brother. The house of God without Brother Bushel to give out the hymns on Sunday would, I am sure, not be the same house of God to any of us.”

“But, Mr. Broad, do you think he will be so silly as to be offended because you exercise the same right which he claims for himself?”

“Ah, Brother Allen﻿—offended! You remember, no doubt, the text, ‘Wherefore, if meat make my brother to offend, I will eat no flesh while the world standeth.’ ”

It is a very good thing to have at one’s elbow a Bible of rules for our guidance; but unfortunately we relieve ourselves very often of the most necessary inquiry whether the rule applies to the particular case in hand. Mr. Allen had the greatest possible respect for St. Paul, but he felt sure the apostle was where he had no business to be just at that particular moment. George also saw the irrelevance of the quotation, and discerned exactly where it did not fit.

“Mr. Broad, I am sure I don’t pretend to know what St. Paul thought as well as you do﻿—of course not﻿—but do you think that voting is like eating meat? Is it not a duty to express our convictions on such questions as those now before the country? It didn’t much matter whether a man ate meat which had been offered to an idol or not, but it does matter how we are governed.”

===Chinese===

「那两三个人，艾伦弟兄，同我们自己一样，也须加以体恤。布舍尔弟兄，我可以说，是圣工的一根柱石，是主极忠心的跟随者；同这个一比，政治问题又算得了什么？倘若我的自由竟成了我弟兄的绊脚石，我怎能为自己辩白呢。礼拜日若没有布舍尔弟兄领唱圣诗，神的殿在我们任何人看来，定然就不是原来那座神的殿了。」

「可是，布罗德先生，您行使的不过是他自己也要求为自己保留的同一种权利，您以为他会傻到因此见怪吗？」

「啊，艾伦弟兄——见怪！想必您记得那节经文：『所以，食物若叫我弟兄跌倒，我就永远不吃肉。』」

手边有一部指引我们言行的法则之书，原是极好的事；可惜我们常常借此免掉了那最必要的一问：这条法则是否适用于手头这桩事。艾伦先生对圣保罗敬重到了极点，可他确信，恰恰在这一刻，这位使徒跑到了他不该来的地方。乔治也看出这节引文文不对题，并且分毫不差地看出了它在哪儿合不上榫。

「布罗德先生，我当然不敢冒称能像您那样通晓圣保罗的心思——自然不敢——可是您当真觉得，投票同吃肉是一回事吗？在当今摆到国家面前的这些问题上表明自己的信念，难道不是一桩本分吗？一个人吃不吃祭过偶像的肉，本无关紧要；可是我们受怎样的治理，却大有干系。」

===Original===

Mr. Broad turned round on George, and smiled with a smile which was certainly not a sign of affection, but otherwise did not notice him.

“Well, Mr. Broad,” continued Mr. Allen, “all I can say is, I regret it; and I am sure you will excuse me if I also say that we too deserve some consideration. You forget that your refusal to declare yourself may be stumbling-block to *us*.”

“I hope not, I hope not. George, how is Priscilla, and how is her child? Are they both quite well?” and with a pontifical benediction the minister moved away. When he got home he consulted the oracle; not on his knees, but sitting in his armchair; that is to say, Mrs. Broad at the Monday afternoon tea, and she relieved his anxiety. There was no fear of any secession on the part of the Allens, connected as they were with them through Priscilla. On the other hand, Brother Bushel, although he gave out the hymns, had already had a quarrel with the singing pew because they would not more frequently perform a tune with a solo for the double bass, which he always accompanied with his own bass voice, and Mr. Broad had found it difficult to restore peace; the flute and clarinet justly urging that they never had solos, and why the double bass, who only played from ear, and not half as many notes as they played, should be allowed to show off they didn’t know. Mr. Bushel, too, contributed ten pounds a year to the cause, and Piddingfold Green Chapel was but a mile farther off from him than Cowfold. There were allies of the Allens in Tanner’s Lane, no doubt; but none of them would be likely to desert so long as the Allens themselves remained. Therefore Providence seemed to point out to Mr. and Mrs. Broad that their course was clear.

===Chinese===

布罗德先生朝乔治转过身去，微微一笑，那笑容绝非亲热的表示，除此之外便再不理会他。

「唉，布罗德先生，」艾伦先生接着说，「我只能说，我很遗憾；而且请原谅我还要说一句：我们自己也值得一点儿体恤。您忘了，您不肯表明立场，对*我们*也可能成为绊脚石。」

「但愿不会，但愿不会。乔治，普丽西拉好吗？她的孩子好吗？娘儿俩都还好吧？」牧师便这样带着一派主教式的祝福，拔步走了。到家之后，他求问了神谕——不是跪着问，而是坐在扶手椅里问；也就是说，问的是礼拜一下午茶桌上的布罗德太太——她解除了他的焦虑。艾伦一家是断不至于退会的，两家既有普丽西拉这层牵连。另一方面，布舍尔弟兄呢，圣诗虽由他领唱，却早已同歌唱席拌过一场嘴，怪他们不肯多奏一曲带低音提琴独奏的调子——那曲子他总要用自家的男低音跟着和——布罗德先生费了好大劲才把风波抚平；长笛和单簧管振振有词地分辩：独奏从来轮不到他们，那把低音提琴，不过凭耳朵拉拉，音符还不及他们一半多，凭什么偏许它出风头，他们实在想不通。再说，布舍尔先生每年为圣工捐十镑，而皮丁戈尔德绿地礼拜堂（Piddingfold Green Chapel）离他，又只比考福尔德远上一英里。坦纳斯巷无疑也有艾伦家的同盟；但只要艾伦家自己还在，他们谁也不见得会倒戈。于是，天意仿佛向布罗德先生夫妇指明：他们的路是一清二楚的。

## XXIV “I Came Not to Send Peace, but a Sword” / 二十四、「我来并不是叫地上太平，乃是叫地上动刀兵」

===Original===
Mr. Allen, having business in London, determined to go on Saturday, and spend the next day with Zachariah. Although he always called on his old friend whenever he could do so, he was not often away from home on a Sunday. He also resolved to take George with him. Accordingly on Saturday morning they were up early and caught a coach on the North Road. The coaches by this time had fallen off considerably, for the Birmingham railway was open, and there was even some talk of a branch through Cowfold; but there were still perhaps a dozen which ran to places a good way east of the line. Father and son dismounted at the George and Blue Boar, where they were to sleep. Sunday was to be spent with the Colemans, whom George had seen before but very seldom; never, indeed, since he was a boy.

Zachariah still went to Pike Street Chapel, but only in the morning to hear Mr. Bradshaw, who was now an old man, and could not preach twice. On that particular Sunday on which Zachariah, Pauline, Mr. Allen, and George heard him he took for his text the thirteenth verse of the twelfth chapter of Deuteronomy: “Take heed to thyself that thou offer not thy burnt offerings in every place thou seest.” He put down his spectacles after he had read these words, for he never used a note, and said: “If your religion doesn’t help you, it is no religion for you; you had better be without it. I don’t mean if it doesn’t help you to a knowledge of a future life or of the way to heaven. Everybody will say his religion does that. What I do mean is, that the sign of a true religion﻿—true for *you*, is this﻿—Does it assist you to bear your own private difficulties?﻿—does it really?﻿—not the difficulties of the schools and theology, but those of the parlour and countinghouse; ay, difficulties most difficult, those with persons nearest to you?﻿ ﻿… Everybody ought to have his *own* religion. In one sense we are all disciples of Christ, but nevertheless each man has troubles peculiar to himself, and it is absurd to expect that any book system will be sufficient for each one of us at all points. You must make your own religion, and it is only what you make yourself which will be of any use to you. Don’t be disturbed if you find it is not of much use to other persons. Stick to it yourself if it is really your own, a bit of yourself. There are, however, in the Book of God universal truths, and the wonderful thing about them is, that they are at the same time more particularly adapted to you and me and all our innermost wants than anything we can discover for ourselves. That is the miracle of inspiration. For thousands and thousands of years some of the sayings here have comforted those who have well nigh despaired in the desert of the world. The wisdom of millions of apostles, of heroes, of martyrs, of poor field labourers, of solitary widows, of orphans of the destitute, of men driven to their last extremity has been the wisdom of this volume﻿—not their own, and yet most truly theirs.﻿ ﻿… Here is a word for us this morning: ‘Take heed to thyself that thou offer not thy burnt offerings in every place thou seest.’ Ah, what a word it is! You and I are not idolaters, and there is no danger of our being so. For you and me this is not a warning against idolatry. What is it for us then? Reserve yourself; discriminate in your worship. Reserve yourself, I say; but what is the implication? What says the next verse? ‘In the place which the Lord shall choose;’ that is to say, keep your worship for the Highest. Do not squander yourself, but, on the other hand, before the shrine of the Lord offer all your love and adoration. What a practical application this has!﻿ ﻿… I desire to come a little closer to you. What are the consequences of not obeying this Divine law? You will not be struck dead nor excommunicated, you will be simply *disappointed*. Your burnt offering will receive no answer; you will not be blessed through it; you will come to see that you have been pouring forth your treasure, and something worse, your heart’s blood﻿—not the blood of cattle﻿—before that which is no God﻿—a nothing, in fact. ‘Vanity of vanities,’ you will cry, ‘all is vanity.’ My young friends, young men and young women, you are particularly prone to go wrong in this matter. You not only lay your possessions but yourselves on altars by the roadside.”

It was the first time George had ever heard anything from any public speaker which came home to him, and he wondered if Mr. Bradshaw knew his history. He interpreted the discourse after his own way, and Priscilla was ever before him.
===Chinese===
艾伦先生在伦敦有生意要办，决计礼拜六动身，第二天同撒迦利亚一起过。他每逢有机会总要去看这位老友，但礼拜日离家出门的时候却不多。这回他还打定主意带上乔治。于是礼拜六清早，父子俩早早起身，赶上了大北路上一班驿车。这年月驿车的生意已经大不如前，因为伯明翰铁路通了车，甚至有人议论要修一条经过考福尔德的支线；不过开往铁路线以东远处市镇的班车，大约还有十来班。父子二人在乔治与蓝野猪旅馆（George and Blue Boar）门前下车，当晚便在那里歇宿。礼拜日则到科尔曼家去度。科尔曼家的人乔治从前见过，只是极少；说实在的，打他还是个孩子那阵以后，就再没见过。

撒迦利亚仍旧上派克街礼拜堂去，但只去早堂听布拉德肖先生讲道——他如今已是老人，讲不动一天两场了。撒迦利亚、波琳、艾伦先生和乔治一同听他讲道的那个礼拜日，他选的经文是《申命记》十二章十三节：「你要谨慎，不可在你所看中的各处献燔祭。」读完这节经文，他把眼镜摘下来——他讲道从来不用讲稿——说道：「你的宗教若帮不了你，它便不是给你的宗教；没有它反倒更好。我不是说，它若不能叫你认识来世、认识上天堂的路——这个，人人都会说自己的宗教做得到。我要说的是：一种真宗教——对*你*而言真——的记号在这里：它能帮你扛起你个人的难处吗？——真能吗？——不是学校和神学里的那些难处，是客厅里、账房里的难处；是啊，最难之又难的难处，是同你最亲近的人之间的？……人人都该有自己的宗教。从一层意义上说，我们都是基督的门徒，然而各人有各人独有的苦楚，指望任何一套书本上的体系处处够我们每一个人用，是荒唐的。你必须亲手造出自己的宗教来；唯有你自己造出来的，才当真于你有益。倘若你发觉它对旁人没多大用处，不必为此不安。只要它真是你自己的，是你身上的一块，你就自己抱住它不放。然而神的书中有普世的真理，奇妙的是，这些真理同时比我们自己能寻出来的任何东西，更贴切地合乎你我、合乎我们内心至深处的需要。这便是默示的奇迹。几千年来，这里面有些话，安慰过那些在世界的荒漠里几乎绝望的人。千百万使徒、英雄、殉道者、贫苦田工、孤身寡妇、赤贫的孤儿、被逼到绝境之人的智慧，向来就是这一卷书的智慧——不是他们自己的，却又最真切地是他们的。……今早有一句话给我们众人：『你要谨慎，不可在你所看中的各处献燔祭。』啊，这是何等的一句话！你我并非拜偶像的人，也全无沦为拜偶像者的危险。对你我而言，这不是防备偶像崇拜的警诫。那么它于我们是什么呢？保守你自己；在你的敬拜里分辨取舍。我说，保守你自己；可这话里含着什么？下一节怎么说？『惟独耶和华所选择的地方』；这就是说，把你的敬拜留给至高者。不可糟蹋你自己；但另一面，在主的坛前，要献上你全部的爱慕和尊崇。这话何等切实可用！……我愿再凑近你们一步。不遵行这条神律的后果是什么？你不会被立时击杀，也不会被逐出教门，你只会单单地*失望*。你的燔祭得不到回应；你不能靠它蒙福；你终会看清，你一直在把你的财宝——还有更重的，你的心血——不是牛羊的血——倾倒在那不是神的物事面前——其实是个虚无。你要喊道：『虚空的虚空……凡事都是虚空。』年轻的朋友们，青年男子们、青年女子们，你们在这件事上尤其容易走错。你们不但把财物、更把你们自己，摆上了路旁的祭坛。」

这是乔治有生以来头一回，从当众讲道的人口里听到直捣他心窝的话。他不禁纳闷，布拉德肖先生是不是知道他的来历。他照自己的路数去解读这篇道，普丽西拉的身影始终横在他眼前。

===Original===
They came back to the little house, and sat down to dinner in the little front room. There were portraits on the walls﻿—nothing else but portraits﻿—and the collection at first sight was inconsistent. Major Cartwright was still there; there were also Byron, Bunyan, Scott, Paine, Burns, Mr. Bradshaw, and Rousseau. It was closely expressive of its owners. Zachariah and Pauline were private persons; they were, happily for them, committed to nothing, and were not subsidised by their reputations to defend a system. They were consequently free to think at large, and if they admired both Bunyan and Rousseau, they were at liberty to do so. Zachariah, in a measure, and a very large measure, had remained faithful to his earliest beliefs﻿—who is there that does not?﻿—and although they had been modified, they were still there; and he listened to Mr. Bradshaw with the faith of thirty years ago. He also believed in a good many things he had learned without him, and perhaps the old and the new were not so discordant as at first sight they might have seemed to be. He was not, in fact, despite all his love of logic, the “yes *or* no” from which most people cannot escape, but a “yes *and* no”; not immorally and through lack of resolution, but by reason of an original receptivity and the circumstances of his training. If he had been merely a student the case would have been different but he was not a student. He was a journeyman printer; and hard work has a tendency to demolish the distinctions of dialectics. He had also been to school outside his shop, and had learned many lessons, often confusing and apparently contradictory. Blanketeer marches; his first wife; the workhouse imprisonment; his second wife; the little Pauline had each come to him with its own special message, and the net result was a character, but a character disappointing to persons who prefer men and women of linear magnitude to those of three dimensions.

After dinner the conversation turned upon politics and Mr. Allen described his interview with Mr. Broad, regretting that the movement in the district round Cowfold would receive no countenance from the minister of the very sect which ought to be its chief support.
===Chinese===
他们回到那所小屋，在小前屋里坐下吃饭。墙上挂着画像——除画像外别无一物——这一批收藏乍看之下颇不调和。卡特赖特少校（Major Cartwright）还挂在原处；此外还有拜伦（Byron）、班扬（Bunyan）、司各特（Scott）、潘恩（Paine）、彭斯（Burns）、布拉德肖先生和卢梭（Rousseau）。这满墙的像，恰恰道尽了主人的为人。撒迦利亚和波琳是平民之身；所幸他们不受任何牵累，也不曾靠名声领着津贴去卫护一套体系，因而能放开手脚自由地想；倘若他们既敬班扬、又敬卢梭，尽可以这样敬。撒迦利亚在很大程度上——很大的程度上——仍忠于他最早的信仰——世上谁不如此呢？——那信仰虽经修正，却依然还在；他听布拉德肖先生讲道时，怀的还是三十年前的信心。他也信好些不经这位先生而自己学到的东西；而新与旧或许并不像乍看之下那样互不相容。实际上，尽管他酷爱逻辑，他却不是大多数人挣不脱的那种「非此即彼」，而是一个「亦此亦彼」；并非出于不德、出于优柔寡断，而是出于天生的兼容并蓄，和他所受教养的境遇。倘若他仅仅是个书生，情形又会不同；可他不是书生。他是个印刷工匠，而繁重的劳作自会把辩证法的细界夷平。他还在自己的铺子之外上过学堂，学过许多课业，往往教人糊涂，表面还自相矛盾。背毯队行军；他的第一任妻子；济贫院那段囚禁；他的第二任妻子；小波琳——每一桩都带着它独有的训诲来到他身上，而汇总的结果是一种品格；只是这种品格，在偏爱单线平面的人物、不喜立体人物的人看来，未免叫人失望。

饭后，话头转到政治上。艾伦先生讲了他同布罗德先生会面的情形，深为痛惜：考福尔德周遭一带的这场运动，竟得不到那个本该作它头号支柱的教派的牧师半点青眼。

===Original===
“A sad falling off,” said Zachariah, “from the days, even in my time, when the Dissenters were the insurrectionary class. Mr. Bradshaw, last Sunday, after his sermon, shut his Bible, and told the people that he did not now interfere much in political matters; but he felt he should not be doing his duty if he did not tell those whom he taught which way they ought to vote, and that what he had preached to them for so many years would be poor stuff if it did not compel them into a protest against taxing the poor for the sake of the rich.”

“Yes,” replied Mr. Allen; “but then Broad never has taught what Bradshaw teaches; he never seems to me to see anything clearly; at least he never makes me see anything clearly; the whole world is in a fog to him.”

“From what I have heard of Mr. Broad,” said Pauline, “I should think the explanation of him is very simple; he is a hypocrite﻿—an ordinary hypocrite. What is the use of going out of the way to seek for explanations of such commonplace persons?”

“Pauline, Pauline,” cried Zachariah, “you surely forget, my child, in whose company you are!”

“Oh, as for that,” said George, “Miss Coleman needn’t mind me. I haven’t married Mr. Broad, and my father is quite right. For that matter, I believe Miss Coleman is right too.”
===Chinese===
「可悲的衰落啊，」撒迦利亚说，「想当年——就在我这些年月里——不信国教者还是造反的阶级呢。上个礼拜日，布拉德肖先生讲完道，合上圣经，对众人说，他如今不大过问政事；但他觉得，若不告诉他教导的人该投哪一边的票，便算不得尽了本分；还说，他向他们讲了这么些年的道，倘若不能迫使他们挺身抗议那种为着富人的缘故向穷人征税的勾当，那些道便是些可怜的破烂了。」

「是啊，」艾伦先生答道，「可是布罗德从来没有讲过布拉德肖讲的东西；在我看，他仿佛从来不曾把什么事看清楚过；至少他从来不曾叫我看清楚过什么；整个世界在他眼里是一团雾。」

「照我听到的关于布罗德先生的种种，」波琳说，「我看要解释他再简单不过：他是个伪君子——一个普普通通的伪君子。为这号千篇一律的人物绕道去寻什么解释，有什么用处？」

「波琳，波琳，」撒迦利亚喊道，「孩子，你莫忘了自己是在什么人跟前说话！」

「哦，那个嘛，」乔治说，「科尔曼小姐不必顾忌我。我娶的又不是布罗德先生，我父亲说得全对。说起来，我相信科尔曼小姐也没说错。」

===Original===
“Well,” said Mr. Allen, “it is rather strong to say a man is an ordinary hypocrite, and it is not easy to prove it.”

“Not easy to prove it,” said Pauline, shifting a little her chair and looking straight at Mr. Allen, with great earnestness; “hypocrisy is the one thing easiest to prove. I can tell whether a man is a hypocrite before I know anything else about him. I may not for a long time be able to say what else he may be, but before he speaks, almost, I can detect whether he is sincere.”

“You women,” said Zachariah, with a smile, “or you girls rather, are so positive. Just as though the world were divided like the goats and the sheep in the gospel. That is a passage that I never could quite understand. I never, hardly, see a pure breed either of goat or sheep. I never see anybody who deserves to go straight to heaven or who deserves to go straight to hell. When the judgment day comes it will be a difficult task. Why, Pauline, my dear, I am a humbug myself.”

“Ah well, I have heard all that before; but, nevertheless, what I say is true. Some men, using speech as God meant men to use it, are liars, and some are not. Of course not entirely so, nor at all times. We cannot speak mere truth; we are not made to speak it. For all that you are not a liar.”

“Anyhow, I shall go on,” said Mr. Allen. “We shall have a desperate fight, and shall most likely lose; but no Tory shall sit for our county if I can help it.”
===Chinese===
「不过，」艾伦先生说，「说一个人是普普通通的伪君子，话未免太重，况且也不容易证明。」

「不容易证明，」波琳说，一边把椅子稍稍挪了挪，直望着艾伦先生，神情极为恳切，「伪君子是天下顶容易证明的东西。不等我知道一个男人别的任何事情，我就能断定他是不是伪君子。他还有什么别的名堂，我或许许久都说不上来；可是不等他开口——差不多不等他开口——我就察觉得出他真不真诚。」

「你们这些女人哪，」撒迦利亚含着笑说，「或者说你们这些姑娘家，就是这样说一不二。倒好像世界当真像福音书里的山羊绵羊那样分作两栏。那一段经文我从来没能十分明白。山羊也罢，绵羊也罢，我几乎从没见过纯种。我从没见过谁配直上天堂，也没见过谁配直下地狱。审判的日子来到，那可是一桩难办的差事。嗳，波琳，我亲爱的，我自己就是个招摇撞骗的家伙。」

「嗐，这一套我早听过了；可是我说的依然不错。有些人，把言语用在上帝要人用处用的正道上，仍是撒谎的；有些则不是。当然不是彻头彻尾地撒谎，也不是时时刻刻地撒谎。纯粹的真话我们说不出；我们造出来就不是说纯真话的。话虽如此，你却不是个撒谎的人。」

「不管怎样，我还是要干下去，」艾伦先生说。「有一场恶战要打，多半要输；可是只要我拦得住，托利党人就休想坐上我们郡的议席。」

===Original===
“Of course you will go on,” said Zachariah. “So shall I go on. We are to have a meeting in Clerkenwell tomorrow night, although, to tell you the truth, I don’t feel exactly the interest in the struggle which I did in those of five-and-twenty years ago, when we had to whisper our treasons to one another in locked rooms and put sentries at the doors. You know nothing about those times, George.”

“I wish I had,” said George, with an unusual passion, which surprised his father and caused Pauline to lift her eyes from the table and look at him. “I only wish I had. I can’t speak as father can, and I often say to myself I should like to take myself off to some foreign country where men get shot for what they call conspiracy. If I knew such a country I half believe I would go tomorrow.”

“Which means,” said Pauline, “that there would be an end of you and your services. If you care anything for a cause, you can do something better than get shot for it; and if you want martyrdom, there is a nobler martyrdom than death. The Christians who were trundled in barrels with spikes in them deserve higher honour than those who died in a moment, before they could recant. The highest form of martyrdom, though, is not even living for the sake of a cause, but living without one, merely because it is your duty to live. If you are called upon to testify to a great truth, it is easy to sing in flames. Yes, yes, Mr. George, the saints whom I would canonise are not martyrs for a cause, but those who have none.”

George thought that what Pauline said﻿—just as he had thought of Mr. Bradshaw’s sermon﻿—seemed to be said for him; and yet what did she know about him? Nothing. He was silent. All were silent, for it is difficult to follow anybody who pitches the conversation at so high a level; and Zachariah, who alone could have maintained it, was dreaming over his lost Pauline and gazing on the sacred pictures which were hung in the chamber of his heart. Just at that moment he was looking at the one of his wife as a girl; the room in which he was sitting had gone; he was in the court near Fleet Street; she had cleared the space for the dance; she had begun, and he was watching her with all the passion of his youth. The conversation gradually turned to something more indifferent, and the company broke up.
===Chinese===
「你们当然要干下去，」撒迦利亚说。「我也要干下去。明晚我们在克拉肯韦尔有个集会，虽然实对你们说，我对这场斗争，已经不像二十五年前那些斗争那样提得起劲了——那时候，我们得在锁死的屋子里彼此咬耳朵说谋逆的话，还得在门口设岗放哨。那些年月的事，乔治，你是一概不知。」

「我倒盼着知道，」乔治说，那股异乎寻常的激愤叫他的父亲吃了一惊，也叫波琳把目光从桌面上抬起来望他。「我巴不得知道。我不像父亲那样能讲；我常常对自己说，我真想一走了之，到一个人们会为他们所谓的阴谋吃枪子的外国去。倘若我知道有这么一个国家，我几乎相信我明天就动身。」

「那就是说，」波琳说，「你这个人连同你这点用处就此一笔勾销。倘若你对一项事业真有几分在意，你能做的事，比挨一枪强得多；倘若你要的是殉道，还有比死更高贵的殉道。那些被塞进带铁钉的木桶里推着滚的基督徒，比那些来不及反悔便一瞬间死掉的，更配得尊荣。然而殉道的至高一式，甚至不是为着一项事业而活，而是没有事业也照样活下去，只因活着是你的本分。倘若你蒙召为一桩大真理作证，那么在火焰里唱歌是容易的。是啊，是啊，乔治先生，我要封圣的圣徒，不是那些为事业殉道的人，而是那些根本没有事业的人。」

乔治觉得波琳这话——正如他觉得布拉德肖先生那篇道一样——像是单为他说的；然而她知道他什么呢？一无所知。他沉默了。众人都沉默了，因为谁把话提到这么高的调门上，旁人便难以为继；而唯独接得住的撒迦利亚，正在梦怀他失去了的波琳，凝望着悬在他心房里的那些神圣画像。恰在此刻，他望着的是妻子做姑娘时的那一幅；他坐着的这间屋子已经不见了；他在舰队街近旁的那方庭院里；她清出了跳舞的空场；她已经起舞，他正怀着青春的全部炽热望着她。谈话渐渐转到无关痛痒的事上去，这一伙人便散了。

===Original===
On the Monday George and his father went home. It is very depressing, after being with people who have been at their best, and with whom we have been at our best, to descend upon ordinary existence. George felt it particularly as he stood in the shop on Tuesday morning and reflected that for the whole of that day﻿—for his father was out﻿—he should probably not say nor hear a word for which he cared a single straw. But there was to be an election meeting that evening, and Mr. Allen was to speak, and George, of course, must be there. The evening came, and the room at the Mechanics’ Institute, which had just been established in Cowfold, was crowded. Admission was not by ticket, so that, though the Whigs had convened it, there was a strong muster of the enemy. Mr. Allen moved the first resolution in a stirring speech, which was constitutionally interrupted with appeals to him to go home and questions about a grey mare﻿—“*How about old Pinfold’s grey mare?*”﻿—which seemed conclusive and humorous to the last degree. Old Pinfold was a well-known character in Cowfold, horse-dealer, pig-jobber, attendant at races, with no definite occupation, and the grey mare was an animal which he managed to impose upon Mr. Allen, who sued him and lost. When Mr. Allen’s resolution had been duly seconded, one Rogers, a publican, got up and said he had something to say. There was indescribable confusion, some crying, “Turn him out;” others “Pitch into ’em, Bill.” Bill Rogers was well known as the funny man in Cowfold, a half-drunken buffoon, whose wit, such as it was, was retailed all over the place; a man who was specially pleased if he could be present in any assembly collected for any serious purpose and turn it into ridicule. He got upon a chair, not far from where George sat, but refused to go upon the platform. “No, thank yer my friends, I’m best down here; up there’s the place for the gentlefolk, the clever uns, them as buy grey mares!”﻿—(roars of laughter)﻿—“but, Mr. Chairman, with your permission”﻿—and here Bill put his had upon his chest and made a most profound bow to the chair, which caused more laughter﻿—“there is just one question I should like to ask﻿—not about the grey mare, sir”﻿—(roars of laughter again)﻿—“but I see a young gentleman here beknown to us all”﻿—(points to George)﻿—“and I should just like to ask him, does his mother-in-law﻿—not his mother, you observe, sir﻿—does his mother-in-law know he’s out?” Once more there was an explosion, for Mr. Broad’s refusal to take part in the contest was generally ascribed to Mrs. Broad. George sat still for a moment, hardly realising his position, and then the blood rose to his head; up crashed across the forms, and before the grin had settled into smoothness on Bill’s half-intoxicated features there was a grip like that of a giant on his greasy coat collar; he was dragged amidst shouts and blows to the door, George nothing heeding, and dismissed with such energy that he fell prostrate on the pavement. His friends had in vain attempted to stop George’s wrathful progress; but they were in a minority.
===Chinese===
礼拜一，乔治和父亲回了家。同那些拿出最好一面的人聚处、自己也拿出最好一面之后，再落回寻常的生计，是很败兴的事。礼拜二清早，乔治站在铺子里，想到这一整天——父亲外出了——他多半连一句他稀罕的话也说不出口、听不进耳，这滋味他尝得格外真切。可是当天傍晚有一场选举集会，艾伦先生要登台演说，乔治当然非在场不可。傍晚到了，考福尔德新近开办的技工学会（Mechanics' Institute）里那间屋子挤得水泄不通。入场不凭票，所以虽是辉格党人召集的会，对头那边也结结实实来了一大队人马。艾伦先生用一篇振奋人心的演说提出第一项决议；演说照例不断被打断——有人高声劝他回家睡觉去，有人拿一匹灰母马做起文章——「平福尔德老汉那匹灰母马怎么说？」——这话在他们听来确凿无比、滑稽无比。平福尔德老汉是考福尔德的一位名人，贩马、贩猪、赛马场上的常客，说不上有什么正经营生；那匹灰母马，是他设法硬塞给艾伦先生的一头牲口，艾伦先生告了他一状，结果官司打输了。待艾伦先生的决议照章有人附议之后，一个姓罗杰斯的酒馆老板站起来，说他有话要说。场内顿时乱得难以形容，有人喊「轰他出去」，有人喊「动手揍啊，比尔」。比尔·罗杰斯（Bill Rogers）是考福尔德出了名的活宝，一个半醉的丑角，他那点子俏皮话，不拘成色如何，满城都有人贩售；每逢人家为正经事聚在一处，他能挤进去把一场会搅成笑柄，便是他顶得意的乐事。他爬上一把椅子——离乔治落座的地方不远——却说什么也不肯上讲台。「不啦，多谢您各位，我在这底下最自在；上头那地方，是留给老爷们、能人们、买灰母马的爷们儿的！」——（哄堂大笑）——「不过呢，主席先生，您若恩准」——说到这儿，比尔把一只手按在胸口，朝主席深深一躬，又惹起一阵大笑——「我只想问一个小小的问题——不问灰母马，先生」——（又是哄堂大笑）——「只是我瞧见这儿有位年轻绅士，咱们大伙儿全认得」——（指了指乔治）——「我就想问问他，他丈母娘——您留意，先生，不是他娘——他丈母娘可知道他出来了？」全场又是一阵爆炸，因为布罗德先生不肯插手这场较量，众人都归咎于布罗德太太。乔治呆坐了片刻，几乎不明白自己的处境，随后血直往头上涌；他哗啦啦冲过一条条长凳，不等比尔那副半醉的嘴脸把狞笑熨平，一只巨掌般的铁钳已经扣住他那油亮的衣领；他在吼声与拳脚之间被拖向门口——乔治浑然不理会——又被一膀子猛劲甩出去，直挺挺扑倒在人行道上。他的朋友们想拦住乔治这怒火冲冲的去路，只是白费劲；他们到底是少数。

===Original===
Next Saturday a report of the scene appeared in the county newspaper, giving full particulars, considerably exaggerated; and Mr. Broad read all about it to Mrs. Broad on Saturday afternoon, in the interval between the preparation of his two sermons. He had heard the story on the following day; but here was an authentic account in print. Mrs. Broad was of opinion that it was shocking; so vulgar, so low; her poor dear Priscilla, and so forth. Mr. Broad’s sullen animosity was so much stimulated that it had overcome his customary circumspection, and on the Sunday evening he preached from the text, “Pure religion and undefiled before God and the Father is this, To visit the fatherless and widows in their affliction, and to keep himself unspotted from the world.” Mr. Broad remarked that the Apostle James made no mention here of the scheme of redemption; not because that was not the chief part of religion, but because he was considering religion in the aspect﻿—he was very fond of this word “aspect”﻿—which it presented to those outside the Church. He called upon his hearers to reflect with him for a few moments, in the first place, upon what religion was not; secondly, upon what it was; and thirdly, he would invite their attention to a few practical conclusions. He observed that religion did not consist in vain strife upon earthly matters, which only tended towards divisions in the Body of Christ. “At such a time as this, my brethren, it is important for us to remember that these disputes, especially if they are conducted with unseemly heat, are detrimental to the interests of the soul and give occasion to the enemy to blaspheme.” When Mr. Broad came to the secondly, and to that subdivision of it which dealt with freedom from worldly spots, he repeated the words with some emphasis, “ ‘Unspotted from the world.’ Think, my friends, of what this involves. Spots! The world spots and stains! We are not called upon to withdraw ourselves from the world﻿—the apostle does not say that﻿—but to keep ourselves unspotted, uncontaminated he appears to mean, by worldly influence. The word unspotted in the original bears that interpretation﻿—uncontaminated. Therefore, though we must be in the world, we are not to be *of* the world, but to set an example to it. In the world!
