“A thousand thanks. If faith now can really cure, I shall be well in a week.”

Mrs. Zachariah smiled again.

“Are you Dissenters?” he asked abruptly.

“Yes. Independents.”

===Chinese===
“天哪，那可真受罪！我丈夫有风湿，觉得塔弗搽剂（Tarver’s embrocation）比什么都管用。我给您装一点儿，您试试好吗？”

“那就感激不尽了。”科尔曼太太装满一个空瓶，从壁炉旁的橱柜里取出一方叠好的牛皮纸，解开一卷麻绳，包成一个齐整的小包裹，递给少校。

“多谢多谢。要是信心真能治病，不出一个礼拜我就该痊愈了。”

撒迦利亚太太又笑了。

“你们是不信国教者（Dissenters）么？”他突兀地问。

“是的。我们是独立派（Independents）。”

===Original===
“I am not surprised. Ever since Cromwell’s days you have always been on the side of liberty; but are you strict﻿—I don’t know exactly what to call it﻿—go to the prayer-meetings﻿—and so on?”

“We are both members of the church, and Mr. Coleman is a deacon,” replied Mrs. Zachariah, with a gravity not hitherto observable.

She looked out of the window, and saw him coming down the street. She placed the kettle nearer the fire, put the tea in the teapot, and sat down again. He came upstairs, went straight into his bedroom, cleaned himself as much as possible, changed his coat, and entered. The Major, being pressed, consented to take tea, and Mrs. Zachariah was a cheerful and even talkative hostess, to the surprise of at least one member of the company. She sat next to her husband, and the Major sat opposite. Three silver spoons and silver sugar-tongs had been put on the table. Ordinarily the spoons were pewter. Zachariah, fond of sugar, was in the habit of taking it with his fingers﻿—a practice to which Mrs. Zachariah strongly objected, and with some reason. It was dirty, and as his hands were none of the whitest, the neighbouring lumps became soiled, and acquired a flavour which did not add to their sweetness. She had told him of it a score of times; but he did not amend, and seemed to think her particularity rather a vice than a virtue. So it is that, as love gilds all defects, lack of love sees nothing but defect in what is truly estimable. Notwithstanding the sugar-tongs, Zachariah﻿—excusable, perhaps, this time, considering the warmth of the speech he was making against the late war﻿—pushed them aside, and helped himself after the usual fashion. A cloud came over Mrs. Zachariah’s face; she compressed her lips in downright anger, pushed the tongs towards him with a rattle, and trod on his foot at the same time. His oration came to an end; he looked round, became confused, and was suddenly silent; but the Major gallantly came to the rescue by jumping up to prevent Mrs. Zachariah from moving in order to put more water on the tea.

===Chinese===
“这不奇怪。自克伦威尔的时代起，你们就一贯站在自由一边；不过你们持守得严不严——我不知道该怎么说才好——去不去祷告会——等等？”

“我们夫妇都是教会的会友，科尔曼先生还是执事。”撒迦利亚太太答道，神情里添了一种先前未曾见过的庄重。

她往窗外一望，看见他正沿街走来。她把水壶挪得离火近一些，将茶叶放进茶壶，重新坐下。他上了楼，径直进了卧室，把自己尽量拾掇干净，换了件外衣，这才走进屋来。少校推让了一番，终于肯用茶；撒迦利亚太太做了一回兴致勃勃、甚至称得上健谈的主妇，叫在座至少一人暗暗吃惊。她挨着丈夫坐，少校坐在对面。桌上摆了三把银匙、一把银糖夹；平日用的匙子都是白镴的。撒迦利亚嗜甜，惯于直接伸手指拿糖——这个习惯撒迦利亚太太极其反对，而且不无道理：一来不干净，二来他的手又不算最白净，挨着的糖块便遭了殃，染上一股味道，甜得不再纯粹。这事她说过二十来次，他却不改，反倒觉得她这份讲究与其说是美德，不如说是毛病。世情正是如此：爱情给一切缺陷镀金；没有爱情，则连真正可敬的东西也只看得出缺陷来。这一回，尽管糖夹就在手边，撒迦利亚——或许情有可原，他正讲得慷慨激昂，抨击着刚打完的那场战争——却把它推到一旁，照老习惯伸指头拿糖。撒迦利亚太太脸上罩了一层阴云；她抿紧嘴唇，当真动了气，哗啦一声把糖夹朝他一推，同时在桌子底下踩了他一脚。他的宏论就此收场；他环顾左右，一时慌乱，猛地噤了声；亏得少校殷勤救场，一跃而起，抢在撒迦利亚太太前头去添开水，免得她起身。

===Original===
“Excuse me, pray;” but as he had risen somewhat suddenly to reach the kettle, he caught the tablecloth on his knee, and in a moment his cup and saucer and the plate were on the floor in twenty pieces, and the tea running all over the carpet. Zachariah looked at his wife, and expected to see her half frantic. But no; though it was her best china, she stopped the Major’s apologies, and assured him, with something almost like laughter, that it was not of the slightest consequence. “Tea doesn’t stain; I hope it has not gone on your coat;” and producing a duster from the cupboard, the evil, save the loss of the crockery, was remedied in a couple of minutes.

At half-past seven o’clock the Major and Zachariah departed. They walked across the top of Hatton Garden, and so onwards till they came to Red Lion Street. Entering a low passage at the side of a small public-house, they went up some stairs, and found themselves opposite a door which was locked. The Major gave three taps and then paused. A moment afterwards he tapped again twice; the lock was turned, and he was admitted. Zachariah found himself in a spacious kind of loft. There was a table running down the middle, and round it were seated about a dozen men, most of whom were smoking and drinking beer. They welcomed the Major with rappings, and he moved towards the empty chair at the head of the board.

“You’re late, chairman,” said one.

“Been to fetch a new comrade.”

“Is that the cove? He looks all right. Here’s your health, guv’nor, and d﻿—﻿—﻿n all tyrants.” With that he took a pull at the beer.

===Chinese===
“恕我失礼；”可他起身去够水壶时猛了些，膝盖带住了桌布，转眼间他的茶杯、茶碟连同盘子便摔在地板上，碎成二十来片，茶水泼得满地毯都是。撒迦利亚看看妻子，只当她要急得半疯。谁知竟没有；虽说摔的是她最好的细瓷，她却止住了少校的道歉，带着几分类似笑意的神气向他担保，这点事无足轻重。“茶水不染东西；但愿没溅到您外套上；”说着从橱柜里取出一块抹布，除了瓷器损失之外，这场祸事不出两分钟便收拾停当。

七点半钟，少校同撒迦利亚一同出门。他们穿过哈顿花园（Hatton Garden）顶端，一路向前，来到红狮街。从一家小酒馆旁边一道低矮的过道进去，上了一段楼梯，面前是一扇上了锁的门。少校叩了三下，停了停；片刻之后又叩两下；里面转动锁眼，放他们进去。撒迦利亚发觉自己置身于一间宽敞的大阁楼。屋子正中纵摆着一张长桌，桌边围坐着十来个人，多半在抽烟喝啤酒。众人敲着桌子欢迎少校，他朝上首那把空椅子走去。

“你来得晚啦，主席。”有人说道。

“去接一位新同志了。”

“就是这位老兄？看着还行。祝您健康，老板，也叫那些暴君统统见鬼去。”说罢他端起啤酒灌了一大口。

===Original===
“Swear him,” said the Major.

A disagreeable-looking man with a big round nose, small red eyes, unshaven face, and slightly unsteady voice, rose, laid down his pipe, and beckoned to Zachariah, who advanced towards him.

The Secretary﻿—for he it was﻿—produced a memorandum-book, and began with a stutter:

“In the sacred name of﻿—”

“Stop!” cried Zachariah, “I don’t swear.”

===Chinese===
“让他宣誓入会。”少校说。

一个长相不大讨喜的男人站起身来——圆头大鼻，一对细小的红眼睛，脸也没刮，说话嗓音微微发飘——他放下烟斗，朝撒迦利亚招了招手，撒迦利亚便走上前去。

那人正是秘书。他掏出一本记事簿，开口便有些结巴：

“以神圣的名义——”

“且慢！”撒迦利亚喊道，“我不起誓。”

===Original===
“That will do,” shouted the Major across a hubbub which arose﻿—“religious. I’ll answer for him: let him sign; that’s enough.”

“You *are* answerable,” growled the Secretary “if he’s a d﻿—﻿—﻿d spy we’ll have his blood, that’s all, and yours too, Major.” The Major took no notice, and Zachariah put his name in the book, the roll of the Red Lion Friends of the People.

“Business, Mr. Secretary﻿—the last minutes.”

The minutes were read, and an adjourned debate was then renewed on a motion to organise public meetings to petition in favour of Parliamentary Reform. The reader must understand that politics in those days were somewhat different from the politics of fifty or sixty years later. Bread was thirteenpence a quartern loaf; the national debt, with a much smaller population, was what it is now; everything was taxed, and wages were very low. But what was most galling was the fact that the misery, the taxes, and the debt had been accumulated, not by the will of the people, but by a corrupt House of Commons, the property of borough-mongers, for the sake of supporting the Bourbons directly, but indirectly and chiefly the House of Hanover and the hated aristocracy. There was also a scandalous list of jobs and pensions. Years afterwards, when the Government was forced to look into abuses, the Reverend Thomas Thurlow, to take one example amongst others, was awarded, as compensation for the loss of his two offices, Patentee of Bankrupts and Keeper of Hanaper, the modest allowance annually until his death of £11,380 14s. 6d. The men and women of that time, although there were scarcely any newspapers, were not fools, and there was not a Nottingham weaver who put a morsel of bread in his hungry belly who did not know that two morsels might have gone there if there were no impost on foreign corn to maintain rents, and if there were no interest to pay on money borrowed to keep these sacred kings and lords safe in their palaces and parks. Opinion at the Red Lion Friends of the People Club was much divided. Some were for demonstrations and agitation, whilst others were for physical force. The discussion went on irregularly amidst much tumult.

===Chinese===
“行了，”少校隔着骤然腾起的喧嚷喊道——“他信教。我替他担保：让他签名，这就够了。”

“你担保，出了事就找你，”秘书低吼道，“他要是那号该死的奸细，我们就放他的血，就这么回事——连你的血也放，少校。”少校只当没听见。撒迦利亚在簿子上签下名字——那簿子便是红狮人民之友社（Red Lion Friends of the People）的名册。

“议事吧，秘书先生——念上次的会议记录。”

会议记录念毕，随后重启上次搁下的辩论：一项动议，主张组织公众集会，请愿支持议会改革。读者须明白，那个年月的政治，同五六十年后的政治颇不相同。四磅一条的面包卖到十三便士；人口比现在少得多，国债却同如今一般大；样样东西都上了税，工钱却极低。而最扎心的是：这般穷困、这般捐税、这般国债，并不是出于民意的积累，而是出自一个腐败的下议院——选区贩子的私产——为着直接支撑波旁王朝，间接而主要地支撑汉诺威王室和人人切齿的贵族。此外还有一长串丑闻累累的闲差与恩俸。多年以后，政府被迫清查积弊，仅举一例：托马斯·瑟洛牧师（Reverend Thomas Thurlow）因失去“破产案专利官”与“汉纳珀保管官”两项官职，获给每年 11,380 镑 14 先令 6 便士的不算丰厚的津贴，直至身故。那个时代的男男女女虽没有几份报纸可读，却并不傻；诺丁汉哪一个织工往饥肠里塞进一口面包，会不晓得倘若没有为保地租而征的外国谷物税，倘若没有为供这些神圣的国王贵人们安坐宫苑而借的债要付利息，那口里本可以塞进两口？红狮人民之友社里意见纷纭：有人主张集会鼓动，有人主张诉诸武力。讨论东一句西一句，在一片喧闹里进行。

===Original===
“How long would they have waited over the water if they had done nothing but jaw? They met together and tore down the Bastile, and that’s what we must do.”

“That may be true,” said a small white-faced man who neither smoked nor drank, “but what followed? You don’t do anything really till you’ve reasoned it out.”

“It’s my belief, parson,” retorted the other, “that you are in a d﻿—﻿—﻿d funk. This is not the place for Methodists.”

“Order, order!” shouted the chairman.

“I am not a Methodist,” quietly replied the other; “unless you mean by Methodist a man who fears God and loves his Saviour. I am not ashamed to own that, and I am none the worse for it as far as I know. As for being a coward, we shall see.”

===Chinese===
“当年海峡对岸那帮人要是光会磨嘴皮子，还得等到几时？人家聚拢起来，把巴士底狱掀翻了，咱们也得这么干。”

“这话兴许不假，”一个脸色苍白的小个子男人说——他烟酒不沾——“可后来呢？不先把道理想透，什么也真办不成。”

“依我看，牧师先生，”对方反唇相讥，“你是吓破了该死的胆。循道宗（Methodists）的人可不该来这种地方。”

“肃静，肃静！”主席喊道。

“我不是循道宗的人，”那人不动声色地答道，“除非你说的循道宗，是指一个敬畏上帝、爱戴救主的人。这一点我不耻于承认，据我所知也没有因此吃了什么亏。至于说我胆小，走着瞧吧。”

===Original===
The Secretary meanwhile had gone on with his beer. Despite his notorious failing, he had been chosen for the post because in his sober moments he was quick with his pen. He was not a working man; nay, it was said he had been at Oxford. His present profession was that of attorney’s clerk. He got up and began a harangue about Brutus.

“There’s one way of dealing with tyrants﻿—the old way, Mr. Chairman. Death to them all, say I; the shortcut; none of your palaver; what’s the use of palavering?”

He was a little shaky, took hold of the rail of his chair, and as he sat down broke his pipe.

Some slight applause followed; but the majority were either against him, or thought it better to be silent.

The discussion continued irregularly, and Zachariah noticed that about half-a-dozen of those present took no part in it. At about ten o’clock the chairman declared the meeting at an end; and it was quite time he did so, for the smoke and the drink had done their work.

===Chinese===
这当口，秘书的啤酒一直没断。虽说他的毛病尽人皆知，大家仍选他当这个差，因为他清醒的时候笔头来得快。他不是做工的人；不，据说他还上过牛津。他眼下的营生是律师行的书记。他站起身，就布鲁图斯（Brutus）发了一通宏论。

“对付暴君只有一个法子——老法子，主席先生。叫他们统统去死，我说；抄近道；少来那些废话；废话管什么用？”

他身子有点儿晃，一把抓住椅背，坐下去的时候压断了烟斗。

随后响起稀稀落落的几声喝彩；但多数人要么不赞成他，要么觉得还是不吭声为妙。

讨论仍旧东拉西扯地进行着，撒迦利亚注意到，在座约有五六个人压根儿不参与。十点钟光景，主席宣布散会；也该散了，烟气同酒力早已各自完成了使命。

===Original===
As Zachariah came out, a man stood by his side whom he had scarcely noticed during the evening. He was evidently a shoemaker. There was a smell of leather about him, and his hands and face were grimy. He had a slightly turned-up nose, smallish eyes, half hidden under very black eyebrows, and his lips were thin and straight. His voice was exceedingly high-pitched, and had something creaking in it like the sound of an ill-greased axle. He spoke with emphasis, but not quite like an Englishman, was fond of alliteration, and often, in the middle of a sentence, paused to search for a word which pleased him. Having found it, the remainder of the sentence was poised and cast from him like a dart. His style was a curious mixture of foreign imperfection and rhetoric﻿—a rhetoric, however, by no means affected. It might have been so in another person, but it was not so in him.

“Going east?” said he.

“Yes.”

“If you want company, I’ll walk with you. What do you think of the Friends?”

Zachariah, it will be borne in mind, although he was a Democrat, had never really seen the world. He belonged to a religious sect. He believed in the people, it is true, but it was a people of Cromwellian Independents. He purposely avoided the company of men who used profane language, and never in his life entered a tavern. He did not know what the masses really were; for although he worked with his hands, printers were rather a superior set of fellows, and his was an old-established shop which took the best of its class. When brought actually into contact with swearers and drunkards as patriots and reformers he was more than a little shocked.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚走出来时，身边跟定一个人——整个晚上他几乎没留意过这人。一看便知是个鞋匠：他身上有股皮革气味，手和脸都黑黢黢的。他鼻子微微上翘，眼睛不大，半掩在两道极黑的浓眉底下，嘴唇又薄又直。他的嗓门尖得出奇，还带着点吱嘎声，像缺油的车轴在响。他说话铿锵有力，路数却不大像英国人；爱用头韵，还常常一句话说到半截，停下来寻摸一个称心的字眼；找到了，后半句便凝住神，像掷标枪一样甩出来。他的谈吐是外国腔的不完满与雄辩术的古怪混合——不过那雄辩绝非做作。搁在别人身上兴许是做作，搁在他身上却不是。

“往东走？”他说。

“是。”

“要个伴儿的话，我陪你走。你觉得‘人民之友’这班人怎么样？”

要知道，撒迦利亚虽然是个民主党人，却从不曾真正见过世面。他属于一个教门。他固然信仰人民，但那人民是克伦威尔式独立派的人民。他刻意躲开满口亵渎粗话的人，平生没有进过一回酒馆。他并不知道底层大众究竟是什么样子；因为虽说也靠双手吃饭，印刷工人算是体面些的一路人，而他那家老字号铺子接的又尽是同行里最上等的活计。如今当真同满口咒骂、酩酊大醉的爱国者与改革者打上交道，他心里的震动着实不小。

===Original===
“Not much,” quoth he.

“Not worse than our virtuous substitute for a sovereign?”

“No, certainly.”

“You object to giving them votes, but is not the opinion of the silliest as good as that of Lord Sidmouth?”

“That’s no reason for giving them votes.”

===Chinese===
“不怎么样。”他说。

“总不比咱们那位德行无亏的代理君主更糟吧？”

“当然不比。”

“您反对给他们选票，可最蠢的人的见解，难道还比不上西德茅斯勋爵（Lord Sidmouth）的高见么？”

“这也算不上给他们选票的理由。”

===Original===
“I should like to behold the experiment of a new form of misgovernment. If we are to be eternally enslaved to fools and swindlers, why not a change? We have had regal misrule and aristocratic swindling long enough.”

“Seriously, my friend,” he continued, “study that immortal charter, the Declaration of the Rights of Man.”

He stopped in the street, and with an oratorical air repeated the well-known lines, “Men are born and always continue free, and equal in respect of their rights.﻿ ﻿… Every citizen has a right, either by himself or by his representative, to a free voice in determining the necessity of public contributions, the appropriation of them, and their amount, mode of assessment, and duration.” He knew them by heart. “It is the truth,” he continued: “you must come to that, unless you believe in the Divine appointment of dynasties. There is no logical repose between Lord Liverpool and the Declaration. What is the real difference between him and you? None but a question of degree. He does not believe in absolute monarchy, and stays at this point. You go a little lower. You are both alike. How dare you say, ‘My brother, I am more honest and more religious than you; pay me half-a-crown and I will spend it for your welfare’? You cannot tell me that. You know I should have a *right* to reject you. I refuse to be coerced. I prefer freedom to﻿—felicity.”

===Chinese===
“我倒想见识见识一种新式弊政的实验。横竖要永远给傻瓜和骗子当奴隶，何不换一换？君王的乱政、贵族的欺诈，咱们受得够久的了。”

“认真说吧，朋友，”他接着道，“去研读研读那份不朽的宪章——《人权宣言》（Declaration of the Rights of Man）。”

他在街心站住，摆出演说家的架势，背诵起那几行有名的话：“人生而自由，并始终自由，在权利上一律平等。……每个公民，不论亲自出面或由代表代言，都有权自由发表意见，以决定公共捐税是否必要、其用途、数额、征收方式与期限。”这些他烂熟于心。“这是真理，”他接着说，“除非你相信王朝乃上天所命，否则你迟早要走到这一步。在利物浦勋爵（Lord Liverpool）与《宣言》之间，逻辑上没有歇脚的地方。他和你究竟有什么真正的分别？不过是程度之差罢了。他不信绝对的君主制，便停在这里；你往下多走了一小步。你们俩原本一路。你怎么敢说：‘我的兄弟，我比你更诚实、更虔诚；交给我半克朗，我替你把它花在你的福祉上’？这话你对我说不出口。你知道我有权拒绝你。我拒绝受人胁迫。比起幸福来——我更爱自由。”

===Original===
Zachariah was puzzled. He was not one of those persons who can see no escape from an argument and yet are not convinced; one of those happy creatures to whom the operations of the intellect are a joke﻿—who, if they are shown that the three angles of a triangle are equal to two right angles, decline to disprove it, but act as if they were but one. To Zachariah the appeal “Where will you stop?” was generally successful. If his understanding told him he could not stop, he went on. And yet it so often happens that if we do go on we are dissatisfied; we cannot doubt each successive step, but we doubt the conclusion. We arrive serenely at the end, and lo! it is an absurdity which common sense, as we call it, demolishes with scoffs and laughter.

They had walked down to Holborn in order to avoid the rather dangerous quarter of Gray’s Inn Lane. Presently they were overtaken by the Secretary, staggering under more liquor. He did not recognise them, and rolled on. The shoemaker instantly detached himself from Zachariah and followed the drunken official. He was about to turn into a public-house, when his friend came up to him softly, abstracted a book which was sticking out of his pocket, laid hold of him by the arm, and marched off with him across the street and through Great Turnstile.

Sunday came, and Zachariah and his wife attended the services at Pike Street Meetinghouse, conducted by that worthy servant of God, the Reverend Thomas Bradshaw. He was at that time preaching a series of sermons on the Gospel Covenant, and he enlarged upon the distinction between those with whom the covenant was made and those with whom there was none, save of judgment. The poorest and the weakest, if they were sons of God, were more blessed than the strongest who were not. These were nothing: “they should go out like the smoke of a candle with an ill favour; whereas the weak and simple ones are upholden, and go from strength to strength, and increase with the increasings of God.” Zachariah was rather confused by what had happened during the week, and his mind, especially during the long prayer, wandered a good deal much to his discomfort.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚被难住了。他不是那种人——明明寻不出反驳的路，心里却并不服气；也不是那种幸运儿，把理智的运作当作一场玩笑——三角形的三个角等于两个直角，证明摆在他面前，他也不打算推翻，行事却只当它们是一个。对撒迦利亚来说，“你要在哪里停下？”这一问向来灵验：倘若理智告诉他停不下来，他便继续走下去。然而我们一路走下去，又常常心怀不满：每一步都无法怀疑，结论却叫人起疑。我们安然抵达终点，瞧！竟是一团荒谬，被我们所谓的常识报以讥笑，轰然拆毁。

他们一路走到霍尔本（Holborn），为的是绕开格雷律师学院巷（Gray’s Inn Lane）那段颇不太平的地界。不多时，秘书从后面赶了上来，又被更多的酒灌得东倒西歪。他没有认出他们，摇摇晃晃自顾自去了。鞋匠立刻撇下撒迦利亚，跟上那位醉醺醺的官员。眼看他要拐进一家酒馆，他的朋友轻步凑上前去，从他口袋里抽出一本露着半截的书，一把挽住他的胳膊，架着他穿过街道，经大转栅巷（Great Turnstile）去了。

礼拜日到了，撒迦利亚携妻子去派克街礼拜堂（Pike Street Meetinghouse）做礼拜，主持圣职的是上帝那位可敬的仆人托马斯·布拉德肖牧师（Thomas Bradshaw）。其时他正在讲一组论“福音圣约”的道，细加分野：圣约是与哪些人立的，又与哪些人无份——同后者立的无非是审判之约。最贫苦、最软弱的人，只要是上帝的儿子，也比最强壮的外人更有福气。那些人算不得什么：“他们必如烛烟带着恶味散去；软弱纯良的人却蒙扶持，力上加力，随着上帝的增添而增添。”撒迦利亚被这一星期里的事搅得心神不宁，做礼拜时——尤其在长祷期间——心思一再飘远，令他好不难受。

## XI Politics and Pauline / 十一、政治与波琳

===Original===
Soon after this visit debates arose in Zachariah’s club which afterwards ended in the famous march of the Blanketeers, as they were called. Matters were becoming very serious, and the Government was thoroughly alarmed, as well it might be, at the discontent which was manifest all over the country. The Prince Regent was insulted as he went to open Parliament, and the windows of his carriage were broken. It was thought, and with some reason, that the army could not be trusted. One thing is certain, that the reformers found their way into the barracks at Knightsbridge and had lunch there at the expense of the soldiers, who discussed Hone’s pamphlets and roared with laughter over the *Political Litany*. The Prince Regent communicated to both Houses certain papers, and recommended that they should at once be taken into consideration. They contained evidence, so the royal message asserted, of treasonable combinations “to alienate the affections of His Majesty’s subjects from His Majesty’s person and Government,” etc. Secret committees were appointed to consider them both by Lords and Commons, and in about a fortnight they made their reports. The text was the Spitalfields meeting of the preceding 2nd of December. A mob had made it an excuse to march through the city and plunder some shops. Some of the charges brought against the clubs by the Lords’ Committee do not now seem so very appalling. One was, that they were agitating for universal suffrage and annual Parliaments﻿—“projects,” say the Committee, “which evidently involve, not any qualified or partial change but a total subversion of the British constitution.” Another charge was the advocacy of “parochial partnership in land, on the principle that the landholders are not proprietors in chief; that they are but stewards of the public; that the land is the people’s farm; that landed monopoly is contrary to the spirit of Christianity and destructive of the independence and morality of mankind.” The Reform party in Parliament endeavoured to prove that the country was in no real danger, and that the singularly harsh measures proposed were altogether unnecessary. That was true. There was nothing to be feared, because there was no organisation; but nevertheless, especially in the manufacturing towns, the suffering was fearful and the hatred of the Government most bitter. What is so lamentable in the history of those times is the undisciplined wildness and feebleness of the attempts made by the people to better themselves. Nothing is more saddening than the spectacle of a huge mass of humanity goaded, writhing, starving, and yet so ignorant that it cannot choose capable leaders, cannot obey them if perchance it gets them, and does not even know how to name its wrongs. The governing classes are apt to mistake the absurdity of the manner in which a popular demand expresses itself for absurdity of the demand itself; but in truth the absurdity of the expression makes the demand more noteworthy and terrible. Bamford, when he came to London in the beginning of 1817, records the impression which the clubs made upon him. He went to several and found them all alike; “each man with his porter-pot before him and a pipe in his mouth; many speaking at once, more talkers than thinkers; more speakers than listeners. Presently ‘Order’ would be called, and comparative silence would ensue; a speaker, stranger or citizen, would be announced with much courtesy and compliment. ‘Hear, hear, hear’ would follow, with clapping of hands and knocking of knuckles on the tables, till the half-pints danced; then a speech, with compliments to some brother orator or popular statesman; next a resolution in favour of Parliamentary Reform, and a speech to second it; an amendment on some minor point would follow; a seconding of that; a breach of order by some individual of warm temperament; half a dozen would rise to set him right; a dozen to put them down; and the vociferation and gesticulation would become loud and confounding.”
The Manchester clubs had set their hearts upon an expedition to London﻿—thousands strong; each man with a blanket to protect him and a petition in his hand. The discussion on this project was long and eager. The Major, Caillaud and Zachariah steadfastly opposed it; not because of its hardihood, but because of its folly. They were outvoted; but they conceived themselves loyally bound to make it a success. Zachariah and Caillaud were not of much use in organisation, and the whole burden fell upon the Major. Externally gay, and to most persons justifying the charge of frivolity, he was really nothing of the kind when he had once settled down to the work he was born to do. His levity was the mere idle sport of a mind unattached and seeking its own proper object. He was like a cat, which will play with a ball or its own tail in the sunshine, but if a mouse or a bird crosses its path will fasten on it with sudden ferocity. He wrought like a slave during the two months before the eventful 10th March 1817, and well nigh broke his heart over the business. Everything had to be done subterraneously; for though the Habeas Corpus Act was not yet suspended, preparations for what looked like war were perilous. But this was not the greatest difficulty. He pleaded for dictatorial powers, and at once found he had made himself suspected thereby. He was told bluntly that working men did not mean to exchange one despot for another, and that they were just as good as he was. Any other man would have thrown up his commission in disgust, but not so Major Maitland. He persevered unflaggingly, although a subcommittee had been appointed to act with him and check his proceedings. The secretary of this very subcommittee, who was also treasurer, was one of the causes of the failure of the enterprise, for when the march began neither he nor the funds with which he had been entrusted could be found. After the club meetings in the evening there was often an adjournment to Caillaud’s lodgings, where the Major, Zachariah, Caillaud, and Pauline sat up till close upon midnight. One evening there was an informal conference of this kind prior to the club meeting on the following night. The Major was not present, for he was engaged in making some arrangements for the commissariat on the march. He had always insisted on it that they were indispensable, and he had been bitterly opposed the week before by some of his brethren, who were in favour of extempore foraging which looked very much like plunder. He carried his point, notwithstanding some sarcastic abuse and insinuations of half-heartedness, which had touched also Caillaud and Zachariah, who supported him. Zachariah was much depressed.

===Chinese===
此事过后不久，撒迦利亚所在的俱乐部里起了辩论，后来便演成了那场有名的「背毯队」（Blanketeers）进军——人们这样称呼他们。时局日趋严重，全国上下怨望昭彰，政府惶恐不安——这也难怪。摄政王赴议会开幕的途中遭到辱骂，马车的窗玻璃也给砸碎了。军队靠不住——当时人们有这样的想法，也不无道理。有一件事是确凿的：改革派居然摸进了骑士桥（Knightsbridge）军营，还在那里让士兵们破费，吃了一顿午饭；席间士兵们议论霍恩（Hone）的小册子，念到《政治连祷文》（Political Litany）时哄堂大笑。摄政王向两院提交了几份文件，建议立即审议。御前文告宣称，这些文件载有叛国结社的证据，其目的在「离间陛下臣民对陛下圣躬及其政府的爱戴」云云。上议院和下议院各任命了一个秘密委员会加以审议，约莫两个星期后便提出了报告。其题旨便是头年十二月二日斯皮塔菲尔兹的集会：一伙暴民借它为由，列队穿城而过，劫掠了几家铺子。上院委员会加在这些俱乐部头上的罪名，有几条如今看来并不怎么骇人。其一曰：他们正在鼓动普选权和一年一选的议会——委员会说，这些「图谋所显然涉及的，并非任何有限度的或局部的变更，而是对不列颠宪制的彻底颠覆」。另一条罪状，是提倡「土地的教区共有」，其原则是：土地持有人并非最高的所有主，而不过是公众的管家；土地是人民的农庄；土地垄断违背基督教的精神，并且毁灭人类的独立与道德。议会中的改革派竭力证明国家并无真正的危险，所提出的那些格外严酷的措施全然多余。这话不错。没有什么可怕的，因为根本没有组织；然而即便如此，尤其在那些制造业城镇，苦难骇人听闻，对政府的仇恨也最为刻毒。那个时代的历史中最可悲叹的，是民众为求出头所作的种种尝试，既失于散漫狂乱，又失于孱弱无力。再没有比这更叫人痛心的景象了：一大团人海，受着驱迫，辗转翻腾，忍饥挨饿，却愚昧得挑不出能干的领袖，侥幸挑出了也服从不了，甚至连自己所受的冤屈都叫不出名目。统治阶层往往把民间诉求在表达方式上的荒唐，错当成诉求本身的荒唐；殊不知实际上，表达的荒唐，恰恰使这诉求更加不容忽视、更加可怕。巴姆福德（Bamford）于 1817 年初来到伦敦，记下了这些俱乐部给他的印象。他走访了几家，发现家家都是一个模样：「每人面前一大杯黑啤酒，嘴里叼着烟斗；许多人同时抢着说话，空谈的多，思考的少；发话的多，倾听的少。不多时有人喊一声『肃静』，接着便算安静了下来；一位演讲人，无论是外客还是本地人，被满口的客气与恭维引介出场。随之是『好哇，好哇，好哇』，拍手的拍手，用指节敲桌子的敲桌子，敲得半品脱的小杯都在桌上跳起舞来；然后是一篇演说，向某位同道演说家或某位得人心的政治家恭维一番；接着是一项赞成议会改革的决议，外加一篇附议的演说；随后又有人就某个小节提出修正案，又有人附议；某位性子火暴的先生破坏秩序；五六个人站起来纠正他；十来个人又起来把他们压下去；末了叫嚷与比手画脚齐齐高涨，闹嚷嚷乱作一团。」
曼彻斯特各俱乐部一心要办一场进军伦敦的远征——人数以千计，每人带一条毯子御寒，手里执一份请愿书。这个计划议了很久，也争得很热。少校、凯约和撒迦利亚坚决反对；倒不是嫌它大胆，而是嫌它愚妄。表决时他们被压倒了；但他们认为自己本着忠诚，责无旁贷，仍须使它办成。组织的事，撒迦利亚和凯约帮不上多少忙，全部担子便都落在少校肩上。他外表轻快，在多数人眼里正坐实了轻浮的指摘，可一旦安下心去做他生来该做的工作，他其实全然不是那种人。他的轻浮，不过是一颗尚无所系、正自寻索本职的心灵，闲散时的一场嬉戏。他像一只猫：太阳底下会拿线团或自己的尾巴耍戏，可一旦有老鼠或鸟儿打眼前经过，便以骤然的凶猛扑将上去。在那多事的 1817 年 3 月 10 日之前的两个月里，他做得像牛马一般，为这件事险些把心都操碎。一切都得在地下进行；因为人身保护法虽尚未停废，可是为一场形同兵事的举动做筹备，终归危险。然而这还算不得最大的难处。他请求授予独断的全权，随即发觉自己反倒因此招了嫌疑。人家直截了当地对他说：做工的人无意用一个暴君去换另一个暴君，他们同他一样是人。换了别人，早就负气把差事撂下了，梅特兰少校却不。他孜孜不倦地撑持着，尽管会里指派了一个小组委员会同他共事，处处掣肘。恰恰是这个小组委员会的秘书——他还兼任司库——成了这桩事业失败的原因之一：进军开拔时，他本人连同托付给他的款项，一齐没了踪影。晚间俱乐部散会之后，众人常常移到凯约的寓所再叙，少校、撒迦利亚、凯约和波琳围坐一处，直谈到将近午夜。有一晚，趁次日夜里俱乐部开会之前，又开了这样一次非正式的小聚。少校没有到场，他正忙着为行军安排给养。他一贯坚持给养断不可少，前一个星期还为这事被几位弟兄狠狠地反对了一通——那些人主张临时就地筹粮，而那套办法同打家劫舍也相差无几。他到底把主张贯彻了下去，虽则挨了些冷嘲恶骂，还被人影射三心二意——这影射也刺着了支持他的凯约和撒迦利亚。撒迦利亚郁郁寡欢。

===Original===
“Mr. Coleman, you are dull,” said Pauline. “What is the matter?”
“Dull!﻿—that’s not exactly the word. I was thinking of tomorrow.”
“Ah! I thought so. Well?”
Zachariah hesitated a little. “Is it worth all the trouble?” at last he said, an old familiar doubt recurring to him﻿—“Is it worth all the trouble to save them? What are they?﻿—and, after all, what can we do for them? Suppose we succeed, and a hundred thousand creatures like those who blackguarded us last week get votes, and get their taxes reduced, and get all they want, what then?”

===Chinese===
「科尔曼先生，你今天好生沉闷，」波琳说。「怎么了？」
「沉闷！——这个词不大确切。我在想明天的事。」
「啊！我料到是这么回事。喏，怎么样？」
撒迦利亚略一迟疑。「这一切辛苦，值得么？」他终于开口，一缕旧日熟稔的疑虑又浮上心头——「救他们，值得费这一切辛苦么？他们算什么？——况且，归根到底，我们又能为他们做什么？就算我们成功了，十万个人——像上星期冲我们满口污言的那号人——拿到了选票，减掉了捐税，要什么有什么，往后又怎样呢？」

===Original===
Pauline broke in with all the eagerness of a woman who is struck with an idea﻿—“Stop, stop, Mr. Coleman. Here is the mistake you make. Grant it all﻿—grant your achievement is ridiculously small﻿—is it not worth the sacrifice of two or three like you and me to accomplish it? That is our error. We think ourselves of such mighty importance. The question is, whether we are of such importance, and whether the progress of the world one inch will not be cheaply purchased by the annihilation of a score of us. You believe in what you call salvation. You would struggle and die to save a soul; but in reality you can never save a man; you must be content to struggle and die to save a little bit of him﻿—to prevent one habit from descending to his children. You won’t save him wholly, but you may arrest the propagation of an evil trick, and so improve a trifle﻿—just a trifle﻿—whole generations to come. Besides, I don’t believe what you will do is nothing. ‘Give a hundred thousand blackguard creatures votes’﻿—well, that is something. You are disappointed they do not at once become converted and all go to chapel. That is not the way of the Supreme. Your hundred thousand get votes, and perhaps are none the better, and die as they were before they had votes. But the Supreme has a million, or millions, of years before Him.”

===Chinese===
波琳带着一个女子被念头击中时的全部急切，插了进来——「打住，打住，科尔曼先生。你的毛病正在这里。就算全都依你——就算你的成就小得可笑——为成全它，赔上两三个像你像我这样的人，难道不值得么？我们的错处正在这里。我们把自己看得重于千钧。问题恰恰是：我们果真有那么要紧么？世界向前挪进一寸，若拿我们二十个人的湮灭去换，这代价难道不便宜么？你信你所谓的救恩。为了救一个灵魂，你肯去拼、肯去死；可实际上，你永远救不了一个完整的人；你必须甘心：拼了、死了，也只是救他的一个小小的片段——拦住某一个恶习，别叫它传给他的儿女。你救不全他这个人，却可以截住一种恶习的繁衍，从而叫后来的世世代代都改善那么一丝——仅仅一丝。况且，我也不信你们要做的事会是徒劳。『给十万个粗坯选票』——嗯，这已经是些东西了。你失望，是因为他们没有立地皈依，没有齐齐整整都上礼拜堂去。至高者行事，不是这个路数。你那十万人得了选票，或许并不因此更好一分，到头来同没有选票时一样地死去。可是至高者面前，摆着一百万年、千百万年呢。」

===Original===
Zachariah was silent. Fond of dialectic, he generally strove to present the other side; but he felt no disposition to do so now, and he tried rather to connect what she had said with something which he already believed.
“True,” he said at last; “true, or true in part. What are we?﻿—what are we?” and so Pauline’s philosophy seemed to reconcile itself with one of his favourite dogmas, but it had not quite the same meaning which it had for him ten years ago.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚沉默了。他素来好辩，总要设法摆出事情的另一面；此刻却全无这份兴致，反倒竭力把她的话，同自己心中本已信奉的什么道理接起榫来。
「不错，」他终于说；「不错，或者说一半不错。我们算什么？——我们算什么？」就这样，波琳的这套哲理，似乎同他素来心爱的一条教义言归于好了；只是这条教义在他心里，已不全是十年前的那个意思了。

===Original===
“Besides,” said Caillaud, “we hate Liverpool and all his crew. When I think of that speech at the opening of Parliament I become violent. There it is; I have stuck it up over the mantelpiece:
> “Deeply as I lament the pressure of these evils upon the country, I am sensible that they are of a nature not to admit of an immediate remedy. But whilst I observe with peculiar satisfaction the fortitude with which so many privations have been borne, and the active benevolence which has been employed to mitigate them, I am persuaded that the great sources of our national prosperity are essentially unimpaired; and I entertain a confident expectation that the native energy of the country will at no distant period surmount all the difficulties in which we are involved.”
“My God,” continued Caillaud, “I could drive a knife into the heart of the man who thus talks!”
“No murder, Caillaud,” said Zachariah.

===Chinese===
「再说，」凯约道，「我们恨利物浦，恨他那一伙人。一想起议会开幕那篇演说，我就按捺不住。瞧，就在那儿，我把它钉在壁炉台上方：」
> 「这些祸患压在这个国家身上，我深为痛心；我也明白，它们的性质不容立即补救。但当我看到如此多的困苦都被坚忍地承受了下来，又看到人们以怎样的仁爱之心去设法纾缓，我尤觉欣慰；我确信，我国繁荣的各大源泉并未受到根本的损伤；我并且满怀信心地预期，这个国家固有的活力，必能在不久的将来，克服我们所陷入的一切困难。」
「我的天哪，」凯约接着说，「这样说话的人，我能一刀捅进他的心口！」
「凯约，不可杀人，」撒迦利亚说。

===Original===
“Well, no. What is it but a word? Let us say sacrifice. Do you call the death of your Charles a murder? No; and the reason why you do not is what? Not that it was decreed by a Court. There have been many murders decreed by Courts according to law. Was not the death of your Jesus Christ a murder? Murder means death for base, selfish ends. What said Jesus﻿—that He came to send a sword? Of course He did. Every idea is a sword. What a God He was! He was the first who ever cared for the people﻿—for the real people, the poor, the ignorant, the fools, the weak-minded, the slaves. The Greeks and Romans thought nothing of these. I salute thee, O Thou Son of the People!” and Caillaud took down a little crucifix which, strange to say, always hung in his room, and reverently inclined himself to it. “A child of the people,” he continued, “in everything, simple, foolish, wise, ragged, Divine, martyred Hero.”

===Chinese===
「呃，也是。杀人算什么？不就是一个词么。咱们换个词，说祭献。你们那位查理（Charles）的死，你们管它叫谋杀么？不叫；不叫的缘故何在？并不在于那是法庭判的。依法庭之法判定的谋杀，从来多的是。你们的耶稣基督的死，难道不是谋杀么？所谓谋杀，是为了卑鄙、自私的目的而叫人死。耶稣是怎么说的？——说祂来，是要在地上投下一柄剑？祂当然要。每一个观念都是一柄剑。祂是怎样的一位神哪！祂是有史以来头一个把人民放在心上的人——真正的人民：穷人、无知的人、愚人、心智孱弱的人、奴隶。希腊人罗马人从来不把这些放在眼里。我向你致敬，啊，你，人民之子！」说着，凯约摘下一具小小的十字架苦像——说来奇怪，它一直挂在他房里——向它恭恭敬敬地俯首一拜。「人民之子，」他接着说，「在一切方面——单纯、愚拙、智慧、褴褛、神圣——一位殉道的英雄。」

===Original===
Zachariah was not astonished at this melodramatic display, for he knew Caillaud well; and although this was a little more theatrical than anything he had ever seen before, it was not out of keeping with his friend’s character. Nor was it insincere, for Caillaud was not an Englishman. Moreover, there is often more insincerity in purposely lowering the expression beneath the thought, and denying the thought thereby, than in a little exaggeration. Zachariah, although he was a Briton, had no liking for that hypocrisy which takes a pride in reducing the extraordinary to the commonplace, and in forcing an ignoble form upon that which is highest. The conversation went no further. At last Caillaud said:
“Come, Pauline, a tune; we have not had one for a long time.”
Pauline smiled, and went into her little room. Meanwhile her father removed chairs and table, piling them one on another so as to leave a clear space. He and Zachariah crouched into the recess by the fireplace. Pauline entered in the self same short black dress trimmed with red, with the red artificial flower, wearing the same red stockings and dancing-slippers, but without the shawl. The performance this time was not quite what it was when Zachariah had seen it in London. Between herself and the corner where Zachariah and her father were seated she now had an imaginary partner, before whom she advanced, receded, bowed, displayed herself in the most exquisitely graceful attitudes, never once overstepping the mark, and yet showing every limb and line to the utmost advantage. Zachariah, as before, followed every movement with eager﻿—shall we say with hungry eyes? He was so unused to exhibitions of this kind that their grace was not, as it should have been, their only charm; for, as we before observed, in his chapel circle even ordinary dancing was a thing prohibited. The severity of manners to which he had been accustomed tended to produce an effect the very opposite to that which was designed; for it can hardly be doubted that if it were the custom in England for women to conceal the face, a glimpse of an eye or a nose would excite unpleasant thoughts.

===Chinese===
对这番传奇剧式的做派，撒迦利亚并不惊讶，他深知凯约其人；虽说这比他以往见过的任何场面都更带几分戏剧腔，却与他这位朋友的性子并无不合。这也并非虚情假意，因为凯约不是英国人。再说，故意把言辞压得低于思想、借以否认那思想，这里头常见的虚伪，往往比一点夸张里头的还多。撒迦利亚虽是个不列颠人，对那种伪善却毫无好感——那种以把非凡贬成寻常、给至高之物硬安一副卑陋形骸为荣的伪善。谈话至此而止。末了凯约说：
「来吧，波琳，来一支曲子；好久没有听你的了。」
波琳嫣然一笑，走进她的小房间去。趁这工夫，她父亲把桌椅搬开，一件摞在一件上头，腾出一方空地。他和撒迦利亚蜷进壁炉旁的凹龛里。波琳进来了，穿的还是那条镶红边的黑色短裙，簪着那朵红色绢花，同样的红长袜、跳舞鞋，只是没有披那条轻纱披巾。这一回的表演，同撒迦利亚当年在伦敦所见的不尽相同。在她自己和两人所坐的角落之间，她如今安置了一位想象中的舞伴；她朝着它趋进、退却、折腰行礼，把自己舒展成种种最为精妙优雅的姿势，从不曾一步逾了分际，却又把每一处肢体、每一道线条都呈现到极致的好处。撒迦利亚同当年一样，追随着她的每一个动作，目光急切——我们该不该说，是一双如饥似渴的眼睛？他对这一类演出实在太生疏了，以致那风姿之美，并没有像它理当的那样，仅仅作为美来打动他；因为我们前面说过，在他那个礼拜堂的圈子里，连寻常的跳舞也是禁绝之事。他所习染的那种严峻礼俗，偏生出同其本意正相反的效果；因为几乎无可怀疑：倘若在英国，妇女遮掩脸面蔚为风俗，那么偷露的一只眼睛、一个鼻尖，也会撩起不堪的念头。

===Original===
The dance came to an end, and as it was getting late Zachariah rose.
“Stop,” said Caillaud. “It is agreed that if they persist on this march, one or the other of us goes too. The Major will be sure to go. Which shall it be, you or me?”
“We will draw lots.”
“Good.” And Zachariah departed, Pauline laughingly making him one of her costume curtseys. He was very awkward. He never knew how to conduct himself becomingly, or with even good manners, on commonplace occasions. When he was excited in argument he was completely equal to the best company, and he would have held his own on level terms at a Duke’s dinner-party, provided only the conversation were interesting. But when he was not intellectually excited he was lubberly. He did not know what response to make to Pauline’s graceful adieu, and retreated sheepishly. When he got home he found his wife waiting for him. The supper was cleared away, and, as usual, she was reading, or pretending to be reading, the Bible.

===Chinese===
舞罢曲终，时候已经不早，撒迦利亚起身要走。
「慢着，」凯约说。「已经说定了：他们若执意进军，你我二人之中须去一个。少校是必去无疑的。去的是谁——你，还是我？」
「我们抽签。」
「好。」撒迦利亚于是告辞，波琳笑着向他行了一个她那舞衣行头里的屈膝礼。他窘极了。寻常场合之下，他从来不知怎样举止才算得体，甚至连像样的礼貌也拿不出来。一旦辩论得兴起，他却完全可以厕身最上等的宾客之中而毫不逊色；只要席间谈锋有趣，便是在公爵的晚宴上，他也尽可与人分庭抗礼。可是智识上一旦无兴，他便呆拙不堪。对波琳优雅的道别，他不知拿什么作答，只得讪讪而退。到家时，他见妻子还在等他。晚饭的杯盘已经撤下，她照例捧着圣经在读——或者装作在读。

===Original===
“You have had supper, of course?” There was a peculiar tone in the “of course,” as if she meant to imply not merely that it was late, but that he had preferred to have it with somebody else.
“I do not want any.”
“Then we had better have prayers.”

===Chinese===
「你晚饭当然是在外头用过了？」那句「当然」里带着一种异样的调子，仿佛她的用意不止是说时候不早了，还嫌他宁愿在别处、同别人一道用饭。
「我什么也不想吃。」
「那么，我们最好做祷告。」

## VI Tea à la Mode / 六、时髦茶会

===Original===
Sunday afternoon came. It was the strangest party. Pauline, on being introduced to Mrs. Coleman, made a profound curtsey, which Mrs. Coleman returned by an inclination of her head, as if she consented to recognise Pauline, but to go no further. Tea was served early, as chapel began at half-past six. Mrs. Coleman, although it was Sunday, was very busy. She had made hot buttered toast, and she had bought some muffins, but had appeased her conscience by telling the boy that she would not pay for them till Monday. The milk was always obtained on the same terms. She also purchased some watercresses; but the watercress man demanded prompt cash settlement, and she was in a strait. At last the desire for the watercresses prevailed, and she said:

“How much?”

“Three-halfpence.”

“Now, mind I give you twopence for yourself—mind I give it you. I do not approve of buying and selling on Sunday. We will settle about the other ha’porth another time.”

“All right, ma’am; if you like it that way, it’s no odds to me;” and Mrs. Coleman went her way upstairs really believing that she had prevented the commission of a crime.

Let those of us cast the stone who can take oath that in their own morality there is no casuistry. Probably ours is worse than hers, because hers was traditional and ours is self-manufactured.

===Chinese===
礼拜日下午到了。这真是一场再古怪不过的聚会。波琳（Pauline）被引见给科尔曼太太时，深深行了一个屈膝礼；科尔曼太太只微微颔首作答，仿佛是勉准承认波琳这个人，却再不肯多让一步。茶点开得早，因为礼拜堂六点半就要开始聚会。科尔曼太太虽说守着礼拜日，却忙得很。她烤了热黄油吐司，又买了几只松饼，只是对那送松饼的孩子说，货钱要等到礼拜一才付，以此安抚了自己的良心。牛奶向来也是照同样的条件赊来的。她还买了一把水田芥；偏那卖水田芥的非要当场现结，一时让她犯了难。到头来，想要水田芥的念头占了上风，她便说：

「多少钱？」

「一个半便士。」

「听着，这两便士是给你本人的——记着，是我给你的。我不赞成礼拜日做买卖。另外那半便士的账，改日再算。」

「成，太太；您乐意这么办，对我全是一样；」科尔曼太太转身上楼去了，心里实打实地相信，自己方才阻止了一桩罪案的发生。

咱们中间，谁敢起誓说自己立身处世的道德里没有半点诡辩，谁就扔这块石头吧。我们的道德多半比她的更糟：她那一套是祖上传下来的，我们这一套却是自己制造的。

===Original===
Everything being at last in order, Mrs. Coleman, looking rather warm, but still very neat and very charming, sat at the head of the table, with her back to the fireplace; the Major was on her right, Jean on her left, Pauline next to him, and opposite to her Zachariah. Zachariah and his wife believed in asking a blessing on their food; but, curiously enough, in 1814, even amongst the strictest sort, it had come to be the custom not to ask it at breakfast or tea, but only at dinner; although breakfast and tea in those days certainly needed a blessing as much as dinner, for they were substantial meals. An exception was made in favour of public tea-meetings. At a public tea-meeting a blessing was always asked and a hymn was always sung.

For some time nothing remarkable was said. The weather was very hot, and Mrs. Coleman complained. It had been necessary to keep up a fire for the sake of the kettle. The Major promptly responded to her confession of faintness by opening the window wider, by getting a shawl to put over the back of her chair; and these little attentions she rewarded by smiles and particular watchfulness over his plate and cup. At last he and Jean fell to talking about the jubilee which was to take place on the first of the next month to celebrate the centenary of the “accession of the illustrious family of Brunswick to the throne”—so ran the public notice. There was to be a grand display in the parks, a sham naval action on the Serpentine, and a balloon ascent.

“Are you going, Caillaud?” said the Major. “It will be a holiday.”

===Chinese===
一切终于安排停当，科尔曼太太坐了桌子的主位，背朝壁炉，脸上微有热意，人却依旧齐整，依旧动人；少校坐在她右手，让（Jean）在她左手，波琳挨着让，撒迦利亚坐在波琳对面。撒迦利亚夫妇用饭向来要求祝福；但说来也怪，到了 1814 年，连最严苛的教友中间也成了这样的规矩：早饭和茶点不求祝福，只有正餐才求——虽说那个年头的早饭和茶点实实在在同正餐一样需要祝福，因为它们都是扎实饱腹的一餐。唯公开的茶会例外。公开茶会上，祝福总归是要求的，赞美诗也总归是要唱的。

有一阵子，席间没有说什么特别的话。天气很热，科尔曼太太抱怨起来：为了烧那把水壶，不得不一直生着炉火。她一说头晕乏力，少校立刻把窗子推大些，又取来一条披肩搭在她椅背上；这些小小的殷勤，她报以微笑，外加对他的盘盏杯盏格外留心照应。到后来，他和让谈起下月一日将举行的百年庆典，庆祝「显赫的不伦瑞克（Brunswick）家族登临王位」一百周年——公告上便是这么写的。各处公园将有盛大的演出，蛇形湖（the Serpentine）上有一场模拟海战，还有气球升空。

「凯约（Caillaud），你去吗？那天可是个假日。」少校说。

===Original===
“What’s your objection to the House of Brunswick? And as for the battle of the Nile, you are no friend to Napoleon.” So replied the Major, who always took a pleasure in exciting Pauline.

“The House of Brunswick! Why should we thank God for them; thank God for the stupidest race that ever sat upon a throne; thank God for stupidity—and in a king, Major? God, the Maker of the sun and stars—to call upon the nation to bless Him for your Prince Regent. As for the Nile, I am, as you say, no friend to Napoleon, but I am French. It is horrible to me to think—I saw him the other day—that your Brunswick Prince is in London and Napoleon is in Elba.”

“God, after all,” said the Major, laughing, “is not so hostile to stupidity, then, as you suppose.”

“Ah! don’t plague me, Major; that’s what you are always trying to do. I’m not going to thank the Supreme for the Brunswicks. I don’t believe He wanted them here.”

===Chinese===
「你对不伦瑞克王室有什么可不满的？至于尼罗河海战，你又算不上拿破仑的朋友。」少校这样答道——逗激波琳，向来是他的乐事。

「不伦瑞克王室！凭什么要我们为他们感谢神；为有史以来坐上王位的最愚笨的一族感谢神；为愚笨本身感谢神——而且还是国王身上的愚笨呢，少校？神，造太阳造星辰的神——竟要号召举国上下为你们的摄政王称颂祂。至于尼罗河，不错，正如你所说，我不帮拿破仑，可我是法国人。我一想起来便觉得可怕——前几天我还亲眼见过他——你们的这位不伦瑞克亲王好端端坐在伦敦，拿破仑却被拘在厄尔巴岛上。」

「这么说，」少校笑道，「神对愚笨的仇视，毕竟不像你设想的那么深喽。」

「哎呀！少校，别拿我烦了；你一味想的就是这个。我是不肯为不伦瑞克一家去感谢那位至上者的。我不信祂乐见他们留在这儿。」

===Original===
Pauline’s religion was full of the most lamentable inconsistencies, which the Major was very fond of exposing, but without much effect, and her faith was restored after every assault with wonderful celerity. By way of excuse for her we may be permitted to say that a perfectly consistent, unassailable creed, in which conclusion follows from premise in unimpeachable order, is impossible. We cannot construct such a creed about any man or woman we know, and least of all about the universe. We acknowledge opposites which we have no power to bring together; and Pauline, although she knew nothing of philosophy, may not have been completely wrong with her Supreme who hated the Brunswicks and nevertheless sanctioned Carlton House.

Pauline surprised Mrs. Zachariah considerably. A woman, and more particularly a young woman, even supposing her to be quite orthodox, who behaved in that style amongst the members of Pike Street, would have been like a wild seagull in a farmyard of peaceful, clucking, brown-speckled fowls. All the chapel maidens and matrons, of course, were serious; but their seriousness was decent and in order. Mrs. Coleman was therefore scandalised, nervous, and dumb. Jean, as his manner was when his daughter expressed herself strongly, was also silent. His love for her was a consuming, hungry fire. It utterly extinguished all trace, not merely of selfishness, but of self, in him, and he was perfectly content, when Pauline spoke well, to remain quiet, and not allow a word of his to disturb the effect which he thought she ought to produce.

The Major, as a man of the world, thought the conversation was becoming a little too metaphysical, and asked Mrs. Coleman gaily if she would like to see the fête.

===Chinese===
波琳的信仰里满是令人叹惋的自相矛盾，少校最喜欢揭露这些矛盾，却收效甚微：每一次攻击过后，她的信仰总能以惊人的速度复原如初。容我们为她辩解一句：一副完全融贯、无懈可击、结论一板一眼从前提推演出来的信条，是造不出来的。对我们认识的任何男人女人，我们都拼不出这样一副信条，对整个宇宙就更不必说了。我们承认种种彼此对立的东西，却无力将它们合到一处；波琳虽然对哲学一无所知，可她那位既恨恶不伦瑞克、却又称许卡尔顿宫（Carlton House）的至上者，未必就全然错了。

波琳大大出乎撒迦利亚太太的意料。一个女人，尤其是年轻女人，哪怕全然正统，在派克街会众中间如此这般的做派，也活像一只野海鸥落进了农家院子，满院子安安稳稳、咯咯啼叫、褐斑点点、心平气和的家禽。礼拜堂里的闺女和主妇们，自然个个庄重严肃；只是她们的庄重合乎体统，规规矩矩。科尔曼太太于是又骇又慌，一言不发。让呢，照他素来的习惯，每逢女儿说得激烈，他也沉默。他对她的爱，是一团焚灼而饥饿的火，把他里面自私的痕迹——岂止自私，连「自己」本身——都烧得一干二净；每逢波琳说得精彩，他便甘愿安安静静地待着，不肯让自己吐出的一个字，搅扰他认为她理应造成的效果。

少校是个见过世面的人，觉得这场谈话未免变得太玄虚了些，便喜滋滋地问科尔曼太太，想不想去看看那场庆典。

===Original===
“Really, I hardly know what to say. I suppose”—and this was said with a peculiar acidity—“there is nothing wrong in it? Zachariah, my dear, would you like to go?”

Zachariah did not reply. His thoughts were elsewhere. But at last the spirit moved in him:

“Miss Pauline, your Supreme Being won’t help you very far. There is no light save in God’s Holy Word. God hath concluded them all in unbelief that He might have mercy upon all. As by one man’s disobedience many were made sinners, so by the obedience of One shall many be made righteous. That is the explanation; that is the gospel. God allows all this wickedness that His own glory may be manifested thereby, and His own love in sending Jesus Christ to save us: that, as sin hath reigned unto death, even so might grace reign through righteousness unto eternal life by Jesus Christ our Lord. Do you ask me why does God wink at the crimes of kings and murderers? What if God, willing to show His wrath, and to make His power known, endured with much long-suffering the vessels of wrath fitted to destruction, and that He might make known the riches of His glory on the vessels of mercy which He had afore prepared unto glory, even us whom He had called? Miss Pauline, the mere light of human reason will never save you or give you peace. Unless you believe God’s Word you are lost; lost here and hereafter; lost *here* even, for until you believe it you wander in a fog of ever deepening confusion. All is dark and inexplicable.”

Being very much excited, he used largely the words of St. Paul, and not his own. How clear it all seemed to him, how indisputable! Childish association and years of unquestioning repetition gave an absolute certainty to what was almost unmeaning to other people.

===Chinese===
「这个嘛，我真不知道说什么好。我想，」——这话里带着一股别样的酸味——「那总没有什么不妥之处吧？撒迦利亚，亲爱的，你想去吗？」

撒迦利亚没有回答。他的心思在别处。可是到末了，他里面的灵终于动了：

「波琳小姐，您那位至上者帮不了您多远。除了神的圣言，再没有光。神将众人都圈在不顺服之中，特意要怜恤众人。因一人的悖逆，众人成为罪人；照样，因一人的顺从，众人也成为义。这就是解释；这就是福音。神容许这一切恶事，为要藉此显明祂自己的荣耀，显明祂差耶稣基督来救我们的大爱：就如罪作王叫人死，照样，恩典也藉着义作王，叫人因我们的主耶稣基督得永生。您问我，神何以容忍君王和杀人犯的罪行？倘若神要显明祂的忿怒，彰显祂的权能，就多多忍耐宽容那可怒、预备遭毁灭的器皿，又要将祂丰盛的荣耀彰显在那蒙怜悯、早预备得荣耀的器皿上——就是我们这些祂所召来的人，那又怎样呢？波琳小姐，单凭人类理性的微光，永远救不了您，也永远给不了您平安。您若不信神的话，就灭亡了；今生来世都灭亡；甚至*今生*就已灭亡，因为您一日不信，便在一团愈陷愈深的迷雾里彷徨。一切尽是黑暗，无从索解。」

他情绪激昂，用的多半是圣保罗的话，并非他自己的话。这一切在他看来何等清晰，何等无可置疑！童年的联想，加上多年不假思索的反复诵念，给这些在旁人听来几乎全无意义的话，镀上了一层绝对的确定。

===Original===
Mrs. Zachariah, although she had expressed a strong desire for the Major’s conversion, and was the only other representative of the chapel present, was very fidgety and uncomfortable during this speech. She had an exquisite art, which she sometimes practised, of dropping her husband, or rather bringing him down. So, when there was a pause, everybody being moved at least by his earnestness, she said:

“My dear, will you take any more tea?”

He was looking on the tablecloth, with his head on his hands, and did not answer.

“Major Maitland, may I give you some more tea?”

“No, thank you.” The Major too was impressed—more impressed than the lady who sat next to him, and she felt rebuffed and annoyed. To Pauline, Zachariah had spoken Hebrew; but his passion was human, and her heart leapt out to meet him, although she knew not what answer to make. Her father was in the same position; but the Major’s case was a little different. He had certainly at some time or other read the Epistle to the Romans, and some expressions were not entirely unfamiliar to him.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚太太虽然曾热切表示巴望少校皈信，又是席上除丈夫外礼拜堂唯一的代表，这番话却听得她坐立难安。她有一手精妙的功夫，时不时施展一回：把自己的丈夫晾起来，或者不如说，把他按下去。于是趁着一个停顿——在座的人至少都被他的恳切打动了——她开口道：

「亲爱的，你再喝点茶吗？」

他正两手支着头，望着桌布出神，没有应声。

「梅特兰少校，我再给您添点茶？」

「不用了，谢谢。」少校也被打动了——比坐在他身旁的那位太太打动得更深；她于是自觉受了抢白，心中着恼。撒迦利亚这番话，在波琳听来无异于希伯来语；可他那股激情是属人的，她的心一跃而起迎上前去，尽管她不知该作何回答。她父亲也是一般的处境；只是少校的情形稍有不同。他从前总归是读过《罗马书》的，其中有些字句，他并非全然陌生。

===Original===
“ ‘Vessels of wrath fitted to destruction!’—a strong and noble phrase. Who are your vessels of wrath, Coleman?”

Caillaud and Pauline saw a little light, but it was speedily eclipsed again.

“The unregenerate.”

“Who are they?”

“Those whom God has not called.”

“Castlereagh, Liverpool, Sidmouth, and the rest of the gang, for example?”

===Chinese===
「『那可怒、预备遭毁灭的器皿！』——好一个有力而高贵的短语。你那些可怒的器皿都是些什么人，科尔曼？」

凯约和波琳隐约窥见了一点微光，可这微光很快又被掩没了。

「未重生的人。」

「他们是哪些人？」

「神所没有呼召的人。」

「比方说，卡斯尔雷、利物浦、西德茅斯，还有他们那一伙？」

===Original===
Zachariah felt that the moment had come.

“Yes, yes; but not only they. More than they. God help me if I deny the Cross of Christ—all of us into whose hearts God’s grace has not been poured—we, you, all of us, if we have not been born of the Spirit and redeemed by the sacrifice of His Son.”

Zachariah put in the “us” and the “we,” it will be observed. It was a concession to blunt the sharpness of that dreadful dividing-line.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚感到，时候到了。

「是，是；可不止他们。远不止他们。我若否认基督的十字架，愿神惩治我——我们每一个人，凡是神的恩典还没有浇灌进心里的——我们、你们、我们大家，只要还不是从圣灵而生、还没有靠祂儿子的牺牲蒙赎，便都在其中。」

诸位当会留意，撒迦利亚把「我们」二字添了进去。这是一种让步，为的是磨钝那道可怕的分割线的锋芒。

===Original===
“We? Not yourself, Caillaud, and Pauline?”

He could not face the question. Something within him said that he ought to have gone further; that he ought to have singled out the Major, Caillaud, and Pauline; held them fast, looked straight into their eyes, and told them each one there and then that they were in the bonds of iniquity, sold unto Satan, and in danger of hellfire. But, alas! he was at least a century and a half too late. He struggled, wrestled, self against self, and failed, not through want of courage, but because he wanted a deeper conviction. The system was still the same, even to its smallest details, but the application had become difficult. The application, indeed, was a good deal left to the sinner himself. That was the difference. Phrases had been invented or discovered which served to express modern hesitation to bring the accepted doctrine into actual, direct, weekday practice. It was in that way that it was gradually bled into impotence. One of these phrases came into his mind. It was from his favourite author:

“ ‘Who art thou that judgest?’ It is not for me, Major Maitland.”

Ah, but, Zachariah, do you not remember that Paul is not speaking of those who deny the Lord, but of the weak in faith; of differences in eating and drinking, and the observation of days? Whether he remembered it or not, he could say no more. Caillaud, the Major, Pauline, condemned to the everlasting consequences of the wrath of the Almighty! He could not pronounce such a sentence, and yet his conscience whispered that just for want of the last nail in a sure place what he had built would come tumbling to the ground. During the conversation the time had stolen away, and, to their horror, Zachariah and his wife discovered that it was a quarter-past six. He hastily informed his guests that he had hoped they would attend him to his chapel. Would they go? The Major consented. He had nothing particular on hand, but Caillaud and Pauline refused. Zachariah was particularly urgent that these two should accompany him, but they were steadfast, for all set religious performances were hateful to them.

===Chinese===
「我们？难道不算你自己，凯约，还有波琳？」

他无法直面这个问题。他心里有个声音说，他本该再往前一步；本该把少校、凯约和波琳一个个点出来；牢牢抓住他们，直视他们的眼睛，当场告诉他们每一个人：他们正被罪恶捆锁，卖给了撒但，命悬于地狱之火。可是，唉！他至少晚生了一个半世纪。他挣扎，角力，自己与自己摔跤，终究败下阵来——不是因为缺少勇气，而是因为缺少更深的确信。那套体系依旧原封未动，连最细微的枝节都照旧，只是应用起来变得艰难了。说实在的，这应用如今大有推给罪人自己去做的余地。分别便在这里。人们发明或发现了种种现成的说法，恰好用来表达现代人的迟疑——不肯把公认的教义落到实际的、直截了当的、平常日子里的践行上去。那教义就是这样被一点一点地放血，放到瘫软无力。其中一个说法此刻浮上他的心头，出自他最心爱的作者：

「『你是谁，竟论断人呢？』这事轮不到我，梅特兰少校。」

唉，可是撒迦利亚啊，你可记得，保罗说的并不是那些不认主的人，而是信心软弱的人；说的是吃喝上的分歧，是守日一类的事？他记得也罢，不记得也罢，反正再也说不下去了。凯约、少校、波琳，被判去承受全能者之忿怒的永劫后果！这样一句判词，他宣不出口；然而他的良心又低声提示：只因缺少最后一颗钉牢在稳妥之处的钉子，他建造起来的一切便会轰然倒塌。谈话之间，时间悄悄溜走了，撒迦利亚夫妇惊觉已是六点一刻，不由得心头一骇。他匆匆告诉客人，他本盼望他们陪他同去礼拜堂。他们去吗？少校应承了——他手头反正没有别的事；凯约和波琳却回绝了。撒迦利亚格外恳切地劝这两位同去，无奈二人心意坚定，因为一切照本宣科的宗教仪文，在他们是深恶痛绝的。

===Original===
“No, Coleman, no more; I know what it all means.”

“And I,” added Pauline, “cannot sit still with so many respectable people; I never could. I have been to church, and always felt impelled to do something peculiar in it which would have made them turn me out. I cannot, too, endure preaching. I cannot tolerate that man up in the pulpit looking down over all the people—so wise and so self-satisfied. I want to pull him out and say. ‘Here, you, sir, come here and let me see if you can tell me two or three things I want to know.’ Then, Mr. Coleman, I am never well in a great building, especially in a church; I have such a weight upon my head as if the roof were resting on it.”

He looked mournfully at her, but there was no time to remonstrate. Mrs. Zachariah was ready, in her Sunday best of sober bluish cloud-colour. Although it was her Sunday best, there was not a single thread of finery on it, and there was not a single crease nor spot. She bade Caillaud and Pauline goodbye with much cheerfulness, and tripped downstairs. The Major had preceded her, but Zachariah lingered for a moment with the other two.

“Come, my dear, make haste, we shall be so late.”

===Chinese===
「不了，科尔曼，别再说了；这一套究竟是什么意思，我心里有数。」

「还有我，」波琳接着说，「同这么多体面人坐在一起，我坐不住；从来就坐不住。我进过教堂，总觉得有一股冲动，要在里头做点出格的事，好让他们把我轰出去。还有，讲道我也受不住。我容忍不了那个人高踞讲坛之上，俯视着底下的众人——那般睿智，又那般志得意满。我真想把他拽下来，说：『喂，你，先生，到这儿来，让我瞧瞧，我想知道的两三件事，你答不答得上来。』再有，科尔曼先生，我一进高大的建筑物就浑身不对劲，教堂尤其如此；我头上像压着千斤重担，仿佛屋顶正搁在我头顶上。」

他哀愁地望着她，却已没有工夫再规劝。撒迦利亚太太已收拾停当，穿着她那身素净蓝灰、云雾色的礼拜日盛装。虽说是礼拜日盛装，上面却寻不出一缕华饰，也没有一道褶痕、一点污渍。她欢欢喜喜地向凯约和波琳道了别，脚步轻盈地下楼去了。少校已先行一步，撒迦利亚却同那两位又逗留了片刻。

「快来，亲爱的，抓紧些，咱们要迟得很了。」

===Original===
“Go on with the Major; I shall catch you in a moment; I walk faster than you. I must close the window a trifle, and take two or three of the coals off the fire.”

Caillaud and Pauline lingered too. The three were infinitely nearer to one another than they knew. Zachariah thought he was so far, and yet he was so close. The man rose up behind the Calvinist, and reached out arms to touch and embrace his friends.

“Goodbye, Caillaud; goodbye, Pauline! May God in His Mercy bless and save you. God bless you!”

Caillaud looked steadfastly at him for a moment, and then, in his half-forgotten French fashion, threw his arms round his neck, and the two remained for a moment locked together, Pauline standing by herself apart. She came forward, took Zachariah’s hand, when it was free, in both her own, held her head back a little, as if for clearness of survey, and said slowly, “God bless you, Mr. Coleman.” She then went downstairs. Her father followed her, and Zachariah went after his wife and the Major, whom, however, he did not overtake till he reached the chapel door, where they were both waiting for him.

===Chinese===
「你同少校先走；我一会儿就赶上你们；我腿脚比你们快。我得把窗户掩上一点，再从炉子里拣掉两三块煤。」

凯约和波琳也留了下来。这三个人彼此间的距离，远比他们自己知道的要贴近。撒迦利亚自以为隔着千山万水，其实近在咫尺。在那个加尔文宗信徒的身后，「人」直起了身，伸出双臂，去触碰、去拥抱他的朋友们。

「再见了，凯约；再见了，波琳！愿神以祂的怜悯祝福你们、拯救你们。神祝福你们！」

凯约定定地望了他片刻，随即，用他那半已淡忘的法兰西做派，张开双臂搂住他的脖子；两人紧紧抱在一处，僵立了片刻，波琳独自站在一旁。她走上前来，等他的手腾出来，便用双手把它握住，头微微向后仰着，像是好把对方端详得更清楚，缓缓说道：「神祝福您，科尔曼先生。」说罢她便下楼去了。她父亲随她而去，撒迦利亚也动身去追妻子和少校，不过直到礼拜堂门口才赶上，两人正在那里等他。

## V The Horizon Widens / 五、眼界渐宽

===Original===
Jean Caillaud, shoemaker, whom we have met before, commonly called John Kaylow, friend of the Major and member of the Society of the Friends of the People, was by birth a Frenchman. He had originally come to this country in 1795, bringing with him a daughter, Pauline, about four or five years old. Why he came nobody knew, nor did anybody know who was the mother of the child. He soon obtained plenty of employment, for he was an admirable workman, and learned to speak English well. Pauline naturally spoke both English and French. Her education was accomplished with some difficulty, though it was not such a task as it might have been, because Jean’s occupation kept him at home; his house being in one of the streets in that complication of little alleys and thoroughfares to most Londoners utterly unknown; within the sound of St. Bride’s nevertheless, and lying about a hundred yards north of Fleet Street. If the explorer goes up a court nearly opposite Bouverie Street, he will emerge from a covered ditch into one that is opened, about six feet wide. Presently the ditch ends in another and wider ditch running east and west. The western one turns northward, and then westward again, roofs itself over, squeezes itself till it becomes little less than a rectangular pipe, and finally discharges itself under an oil and colourman’s house in Fetter Lane. The eastern arm, strange to say, suddenly expands, and one side of it, for no earthly reason, is set back with an open space in front of it, partitioned by low palings. Immediately beyond, as if in a fit of sudden contrition for such extravagance, the passage or gutter contracts itself to its very narrowest and, diving under a printing-office shows itself in Shoe Lane. The houses in these trenches were not by any means of the worst kind. In the aforesaid expansion they were even genteel, or at any rate aspired to be so, and each had its own brass knocker and kept its front-door shut with decent sobriety and reticence. On the top floor of one of these tenements lodged Jean Caillaud and Pauline. They had three rooms between them; one was Jean’s bedchamber, one Pauline’s, and one was workroom and living-room, where Jean made ball-slippers and light goods﻿—this being his branch of the trade﻿—and Pauline helped him. The workroom faced the north, and was exactly on a level with an innumerable multitude of red chimney-pots pouring forth stinking smoke which, for the six winter months, generally darkened the air during the whole day. But occasionally Nature resumed her rights, and it was possible to feel that sky, stars, sun, and moon still existed, and were not blotted out by the obscurations of what is called civilised life. There came, occasionally, wild nights in October or November, with a gale from the southwest and then, when almost everybody had gone to bed and the fires were out, the clouds, illuminated by the moon, rushed across the heavens, and the Great Bear hung over the dismal waste of smutty tiles with the same solemnity with which it hangs over the mountains, the sea, or the desert. Early in the morning, too, in summer, between three and four o’clock in June, there were sights to be seen worth seeing. The distance was clear for miles, and the heights of Highgate were visible, proclaiming the gospel of a beyond and beyond even to Kent’s Court, and that its immediate surroundings were mercifully not infinite. The light made even the nearest bit of soot-grimed, twisted, rotten brickwork beautiful, and occasionally, but at very rare intervals, the odour of London was vanquished, and a genuine breath from the Brixton fields was able to find its way uncontaminated across the river. Jean and Pauline were, on the whole, fond of the court. They often thought they would prefer the country, and talked about it; but it is very much to be doubted, if they had been placed in Devonshire, whether they would not have turned back uneasily after a time to their garret. They both liked the excitement of the city, and the feeling that they were so near to everything that was stirring in men’s minds. The long stretch of lonely seashore is all very well, very beautiful, and, maybe, very instructive to many people; but to most persons half-an-hour’s rational conversation is much more profitable. Pauline was not a particularly beautiful girl. Her hair was black, and, although there was a great deal of it, it was coarse and untidy. Her complexion was sallow﻿—not as clear as it might be﻿—and underneath the cheekbones there were slight depressions. She had grown up without an attachment, so far as her father knew, and indeed so far as she knew. She had one redeeming virtue﻿—redeeming especially to Jean, who was with her alone so much. She had an intellect, and it was one which sought for constant expression; consequently she was never dull. If she was dull, she was ill. She had none of that horrible mental constriction which makes some English women so insupportably tedious. The last thing she read, the last thing she thought, came out with vivacity and force, and she did not need the stimulus of a great excitement to reveal what was in her. Living as she did at work side by side with her father all day, she knew all his thoughts and read all his books. Neither of them ever went to church. They were not atheists, nor had they entirely pushed aside the religious questions which torment men’s minds. They believed in what they called a Supreme Being, whom they thought to be just and good; but they went no further. They were revolutionary, and when Jean joined the Friends of the People, he and the Major and one other man became a kind of interior secret committee, which really directed the affairs of the branch. Companions they had none, except the Major and one or two compatriots; but they were drawn to Zachariah, and Zachariah was drawn to them, very soon after he became a member of the Society. The first time he went to Kent’s Court with Jean was one night after a meeting. The two walked home together, and Zachariah turned in for an hour, as it was but ten o’clock. There had been a grand thanksgiving at St. Paul’s that day. The Prince Regent had returned thanks to Almighty God for the restoration of peace. The Houses of Parliament were there, with the Foreign Ambassadors, the City Corporation, the Duke of Wellington, Field-Marshal Blucher, peeresses, and society generally. The Royal Dukes, Sussex, Kent, York, and Gloucester, were each drawn by six horses and escorted by a separate party of the Guards. It took eight horses to drag the Prince himself to divine service, and he, too, was encompassed by soldiers. Arrived at the cathedral, he was marshalled to a kind of pew surmounted by a lofty crimson-and-gold canopy. There he sat alone, worshipped his Creator, and listened to a sermon by the Bishop of Chester. Neither Jean nor Pauline troubled themselves to go out, and indeed it would not have been of much use if they had tried; for it was by no means certain that Almighty God, who had been so kind as to get rid of Napoleon, would not permit a row in the streets. Consequently, every avenue which led to the line of the procession was strictly blocked. They heard the music from a distance, and although they both hated Bonaparte, it had not a pleasant sound in their ears. It was the sound of triumph over Frenchmen, and, furthermore, with all their dislike to the tyrant, they were proud of his genius.

Walking towards Clerkenwell that evening, the streets being clear, save for a number of drunken men and women, who were testifying to the orthodoxy of their religious and political faith by rolling about the kennel in various stages of intoxication, Jean pressed Zachariah to go upstairs with him. Pauline had prepared supper for herself and her father, and a very frugal meal it was, for neither of them could drink beer nor spirits, and they could not afford wine. Pauline and Zachariah were duly introduced, and Zachariah looked around him. The room was not dirty, but it was extremely unlike his own. Shoemaking implements and unfinished jobs lay here and there without being “put away.” An old sofa served as a seat, and on it were a pair of lasts, a bit of a French newspaper, and a plateful of small onions and lettuce, which could not find a place on the little table. Zachariah, upstairs in Rosoman Street, had often felt just as if he were in his Sunday clothes and new boots. He never could make out what was the reason for it. There are some houses in which we are always uncomfortable. Our freedom is fettered, and we can no more take our ease in them than in a glass and china shop. We breathe with a sense of oppression, and the surroundings are like repellant chevaux de frise. Zachariah had no such feelings here. There was disorder, it is true; but, on the other hand, there was no polished tea-caddy to stare at him and claim equal rights against him, defying him to disturb it. He was asked to sit upon the sofa, and in so doing upset the plateful of salad upon the floor. Pauline smiled, was down upon her knees in an instant, before he could prevent her, picked up the vegetables and put them back again. To tell the truth, they were rather dirty; and she, therefore, washed them in a hand-basin. Zachariah asked her if she had been out that day.

“I?﻿—to go with the Lord Mayor and bless the good God for giving us back Louis Bourbon? No Mr. Coleman; if the good God did give us Louis back again, I wouldn’t bless Him for it, and I don’t think He had much to do with it. So there were two reasons why I didn’t go.”

===Chinese===
鞋匠让·凯约（Jean Caillaud）——此人我们先前已经见过——人们通常叫他约翰·凯洛（John Kaylow），是少校的朋友、人民之友社（Society of the Friends of the People）的社员，生来是个法国人。他最初是在 1795 年来到这个国家的，随身带着一个女儿，名叫波琳（Pauline），大约四五岁。他为什么来，谁也不知道；这孩子的母亲是谁，也没有人知道。他很快便揽下了做不完的活计，因为他是位出色的工匠，英语也学得很好。波琳自然英语法语都会说。她的教育是颇费了些周折才完成的，不过还不像原本可能的那样吃力，因为让的营生把他拴在家里。他的家坐落在一带小胡同小巷盘根错节、大多数伦敦人全然不知晓的地方，却仍在圣布莱德教堂（St. Bride's）钟声可闻的范围之内，位于舰队街（Fleet Street）以北约一百码。若有人想探个究竟，从一条几乎正对布弗里街（Bouverie Street）的巷子走进去，便会由一条加盖的沟渠钻进一条露天的沟，宽约六英尺。走不多远，这条沟到了头，汇入另一条更宽的、东西走向的沟。西边那条折向北去，再折向西，给自己盖上顶，把自身一路挤窄，直到差不多只剩一根方形管道，末了从菲特巷（Fetter Lane）一家油漆颜料商的宅子底下钻出去。东边那条臂，说来奇怪，忽然放宽了，其中一侧无缘无故地往里收，前头空出一片场子，用矮栅栏隔开。再往前去，这条通道——或者叫檐沟——仿佛为方才那番奢侈猛然悔悟似的，把自己缩到最窄处，一个猛子扎进一家印刷所底下，到肖巷（Shoe Lane）才露出身来。这些沟槽里的房屋绝不是最糟的那一等。在方才说的那处放宽的地方，它们甚至称得上体面，或者至少存着体面的想望，家家有自家的黄铜门环，前门关得端端正正、规矩而缄默。让·凯约和波琳便寄居在其中一幢楼房的顶层。他们两个合着三间屋：一间是让的卧室，一间是波琳的，还有一间做工兼起居，让在这里制做舞鞋和轻巧活计——这一支是他的专门行当——波琳从旁帮忙。工作间朝北，恰好同一大片数不清的红色烟囱管子平齐，那些烟囱喷吐着恶臭的烟，一连六个月冬天，往往整日把天空熏得昏暗。不过造化偶尔也要收回她的权利，令人还能感到天空、星辰、太阳和月亮依然存在，并没有被所谓文明生活的重重障蔽涂抹干净。十月或十一月间，偶尔会碰上几个狂野的夜晚，西南风大作；这时节，待到几乎人人都已上床、炉火尽熄，被月亮照亮的云朵便在长天上疾驰而过，大熊星座悬在乌黑瓦顶那片阴惨惨的荒野之上，其庄严，一如它悬在群山、大海或荒漠之上时。夏日清晨也一样——六月里三点到四点之间——有些景致值得一看。远处的景物清朗可辨，一望好几英里，连海格特（Highgate）的高地也望得见，它们传扬着一篇「墙外有天、天外更有天」的福音，这福音甚至一直传到了肯特巷（Kent's Court），并且宣示：这巷子四近的周遭，托天之福，并非漫无际涯。那晨光把近在咫尺、被煤烟熏黑、歪扭朽坏的一段砖墙也照得美丽起来；偶尔——虽然隔得极久——伦敦的气味也会被击溃，从布里克斯顿（Brixton）田野上吹来的一口真气息，得以不带污染地渡河而来。总的说来，让和波琳都喜欢这条巷子。他们时常想着还是乡下好，也常把这话挂在嘴边；不过很可怀疑：倘若把他们放到德文郡（Devonshire）去，过上一阵，他们会不会又心神不宁地折返回自家的阁楼。他们俩都喜欢城市里的那股振奋劲儿，喜欢那种感觉自己同人们头脑里一切翻腾着的东西近在咫尺。绵延荒凉的一片孤岸自有它的好处，很美，对许多人兴许还很有教益；但对大多数人来说，半个钟头有条有理的谈话，益处要大得多。波琳并不是个特别美的姑娘。她的头发乌黑，虽然长得浓密，却又粗又乱。她的肤色发黄——不及本可以有的那样明净——颧骨底下微微下陷。据她父亲所知，她长大至今不曾钟情过谁；据她自己所知，也确乎如此。她有一桩足以补赎诸般缺点的美德——于让尤为难得，因为他与她单独厮守的时辰实在太多。她有一副头脑，而且是时时刻刻寻求出口来表达的头脑；因此她从不乏味。她若乏味了，那便是病了。她全然没有那种可怕的心灵拘挛——正是它，叫某些英国妇人乏味得令人无法忍受。她读到的最后一件事、想到的最后一件事，都会带着生气与力量倾倒出来；并不需要什么大兴奋来刺激，才肯显出胸中之所有。她整天同父亲并肩做工，过着这样的日子，他的全部思想她都知道，他的全部书她都读过。他们父女俩从不上教堂。他们不是无神论者，也未曾把那些折磨人心的宗教问题全然推开。他们信一位他们所谓的「至高存在」，认他为公正而良善；但也仅此为止。他们是革命的一派。让加入「人民之友」之后，他同少校还有另外一个人，便结成了一种内部秘密委员会，实际操纵着分部的事务。除了少校和一两个同胞，他们没有什么伴侣；可是在撒迦利亚入社后不久，他们便亲近起他来，他也亲近起他们。他头一回跟让到肯特巷去，是在一次集会后的一个晚上。两人结伴走回去，撒迦利亚进去坐了一个钟头，因为那时才不过十点。那天，圣保罗大教堂（St. Paul's）里刚举行过一场盛大的感恩礼拜。摄政王为和平的恢复，向全能的上帝献了感恩。议会两院人士都到了，还有各国使节、伦敦市市政当局、威灵顿公爵（Duke of Wellington）、布吕歇尔元帅（Field-Marshal Blucher）、诰命贵妇，以及整个上流社会。萨塞克斯、肯特、约克、格洛斯特几位王家公爵，每一位都由六匹马拉着，各有一队卫兵护从。光是把亲王本人拖去作礼拜，就用了八匹马，他也被士兵们团团围住。到了大教堂，他被引领到一处特设的席位，席子上头罩着一顶高高的绯红镶金华盖。他在那里独自坐着，敬拜他的造物主，听切斯特主教（Bishop of Chester）讲道。让和波琳都懒得费神出门，其实就算想去，也没有多大用处；因为那位好心地替世人除掉了拿破仑的全能上帝，究竟会不会容许街头闹出一场乱子，谁也说不准。于是，凡是通往游行队伍经过路线的道路，一律严严实实地封锁了。他们远远听见了乐声；尽管他们父女都恨波拿巴（Bonaparte），那乐声在他们耳中却并不好听。那是压倒法国人的凯歌；再说，任凭他们如何憎恶那暴君，对他的天才，他们到底是引以为傲的。

那天晚上朝克拉肯韦尔走去，街道已经清静，只剩下一群群的醉汉醉妇——他们以在街沟里东倒西歪、深浅不一的醉态，见证着自家宗教信条与政治信仰的正统——让便再三拉撒迦利亚同他上楼去坐坐。波琳为父亲和自己备下了晚饭，那是极清简的一餐，因为父女俩啤酒烧酒都喝不得，葡萄酒又喝不起。波琳和撒迦利亚按礼引见了，撒迦利亚便四下里打量。这屋子不算脏，却同他自家的屋子极不相像。制鞋的家什和没做完的活计东一件西一件地摊着，从不「归置」起来。一张旧长沙发权充座位，上面搁着一双鞋楦、一角法国报纸，还有满满一盘小洋葱和生菜——小桌子上已容不下它们了。撒迦利亚在罗索曼街（Rosoman Street）自家的楼上，就常常有那样的感觉，仿佛自己穿着节日礼服、蹬着新靴子。他始终琢磨不出这是什么缘故。有些屋子，我们待在里面永远不自在。我们的自由被上了镣，就像在玻璃瓷器铺子里一样无法安闲。我们呼吸都带着压迫之感，四周的陈设活像拒人千里的拒马尖桩。在让这里，撒迦利亚却全无这些感觉。凌乱固然是凌乱；可另一面，也没有什么擦得锃亮的茶叶罐瞪着他，对他主张平等权利，公然挑衅他动一动试试。人家请他在沙发上坐，他这一坐，便把那盘沙拉掀翻在地。波琳微微一笑，不等他来得及拦阻，眨眼便跪了下去，把蔬菜一一拾起，放回盘里。说实话，那些菜已经相当脏了；于是她又用洗脸盆把它们洗了一遍。撒迦利亚问她今天可曾出门。

「我？——去给市长大人作陪，为善良的上帝把路易·波旁（Louis Bourbon）还给我们而祝福他？不，科尔曼先生（Mr. Coleman）；善良的上帝纵然真把路易还给了我们，我也不会为这个祝福他，何况我并不觉得这事跟他有多大干系。所以我不去，缘由倒有两个。」

===Original===
Zachariah was a little puzzled, a little shocked, and a little out of his element.

“I thought you might have gone to see the procession and hear the music.”

“I hate processions. Whenever I see one, and am squeezed and trampled on just because those fine people may ride by, I am humiliated and miserable. As for the music, I hate that too. It is all alike, and might as well be done by machinery. Come, you are eating nothing. What conspiracy have you and my father hatched tonight?”

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚有点发窘，有点吃惊，还有点局促失措。

「我还当你们会去看游行、听听音乐呢。」

「我恨游行。每逢撞见一回，单为着让那些贵人驱车经过，便叫人又挤又踩，我就觉得受辱，觉得悲苦。至于音乐，我也恨。全都一个腔调，索性用机器奏得了。来吧，你可什么也没吃呢。你同我父亲今晚密谋了些什么呀？」

===Original===
“Conspiracy!” said Jean. “Who are the conspirators? Not we. The conspirators are those thieves who have been to St. Paul’s.”

“To give thanks,” said Pauline. “If I were up there in the sky, shouldn’t I laugh at them. How comical it is! Did they give thanks for Austerlitz or Jena?”

“That’s about the worst of it,” replied Jean. “It is one vast plot to make the people believe lies. I shouldn’t so much mind their robbing the country of its money to keep themselves comfortable, but what is the meaning of their Te Deums? I tell you again,”﻿—and he repeated the words with much emphasis﻿—“it is a vast plot to make men believe a lie. I abhor them for that ten times more than for taking my money to replace Louis.”

“Oh,” resumed Pauline, “*if* I were only up in the sky for an hour, I would have thundered and lightened on them just as they got to the top of Ludgate Hill, and scattered a score or so of them. I wonder if they would have thanked Providence for their escape? O father, such a joke! The Major told me the other day of an old gentleman he knew who was riding along in his carriage. A fireball fell and killed the coachman. The old gentleman, talking about it afterwards, said that ‘*providentially* it struck the box-seat.’ ”

Zachariah, although a firm believer in his faith, and not a coward, was tempted to be silent. He was heavy and slow in action, and this kind of company was strange to him. Furthermore, Pauline was not an open enemy, and notwithstanding her little blasphemies, she was attractive. But then he remembered with shame that he was ordered to testify to the truth wherever he might be, and unable to find anything of his own by which he could express himself, a text of the Bible came into his mind, and, half to himself, he repeated it aloud:

===Chinese===
「密谋！」让说。「密谋的人是谁？不是我们。密谋的是那伙到圣保罗去过的贼。」

「去谢恩，」波琳说。「我要是升到天上，不笑死他们才怪。多滑稽呀！奥斯特里茨（Austerlitz）、耶拿（Jena）的时候，他们谢过恩吗？」

「最糟的就在这里，」让答道。「这是一场天大的阴谋，为的是教人民相信谎言。他们为着自己安逸，把国家的钱抢去一些，我倒不那么在意；可他们那些《感恩赞》（Te Deum）又算是什么意思？我再对你说一遍，」——他把这几个字加重了语气重说了一遍——「这是一场教人相信谎言的天大阴谋。为这个，我恨他们，十倍于恨他们拿我的钱去把路易扶上王座。」

「哦，」波琳接着说，「*要是*我能在天上待上一个钟头就好了，等他们爬上勒德盖特山（Ludgate Hill）顶的那一刻，我便朝他们打雷放电，轰散他一二十个。不知他们可会为死里逃生感谢天恩？啊，父亲，这笑话多妙啊！少校前几天同我讲过一位他熟识的老先生的事。那天他正坐着自己的马车赶路，一颗火球落下来，把车夫砸死了。老先生事后谈起，说『*幸蒙天佑*，它砸在了车夫的座位上』。」

撒迦利亚虽是自家信仰的坚定信徒，又并非懦夫，这会儿却也禁不住想要沉默。他举止滞重迟缓，这样的交际场面对他十分陌生。何况波琳并非公然为敌的人，尽管说了几句小小的渎神话，她自有动人之处。可他随即羞愧地记起，自己奉命无论身在何处都当为真理作证；一时又寻不出自家的话语来表达，脑中便浮起一节圣经，他半似自语，朗声念了出来：

===Original===
“I form the light and create darkness: I make peace and create evil; I the Lord do all these things.”

“What is that?” said Jean. “Repeat it.”

Zachariah slowly repeated it. He had intended to add to it something which might satisfy his conscience and rebuke Pauline, but he could not.

“Whence is that?” said Jean.

“From the Bible; give me one and I will show it to you.”

There was no English Bible in the house. It was a book not much used; but Pauline presently produced a French version, and Jean read the passage﻿—“*Qui forme la lumière, et qui crée les ténèbres; qui fait la paix, et qui crée l’adversité; c’est moi, l’Eternel, qui fais toutes les choses là.*”

===Chinese===
「我造光，又造暗；我施平安，又降灾祸；造作这一切的是我——耶和华。」

「那是什么？」让说。「再念一遍。」

撒迦利亚慢慢重念了一遍。他本想再补上几句，好既安抚自己的良心，又折服波琳，却终究没能做到。

「这是哪里的？」让问。

「圣经上的；给我一部，我指给你看。」

家里没有英文圣经。那不是一部常用的书；但波琳随即取出一部法文译本，让念了那一段——「*Qui forme la lumière, et qui crée les ténèbres; qui fait la paix, et qui crée l'adversité; c'est moi, l'Eternel, qui fais toutes ces choses là.*」（造光又造暗，施平安又降灾祸；做成这一切的，是我耶和华。）

===Original===
Pauline bent over her father and read it again.

“*Qui crée l’adversité*,” she said. “Do you believe that?”

“If it is there I do,” said Zachariah.

“Well, I don’t.”

===Chinese===
波琳俯在父亲肩头，又读了一遍。

「『*Qui crée l'adversité*』——『谁造灾祸』，」她说。「这话你信吗？」

「书上有的，我就信。」撒迦利亚说。

「我可不信。」

===Original===
“What’s adversity to hell fire? If He made hellfire, why not adversity? Besides, if He did not, who did?”

“Don’t know a bit, and don’t mean to bother myself about it.”

“Right!” broke in Jean﻿—“right, my child; bother﻿—that is a good word. Don’t bother yourself about anything when﻿—bothering will not benefit. There is so much in the world which will﻿—bear a botheration out of which some profit will arise. Now, then, clear the room, and let Zachariah see your art.”

The plates and dishes were all put in a heap and the table pushed aside. Pauline retired for a few moments, and presently came back in a short dress of black velvet, which reached about halfway down from the knee to the ankle. It was trimmed with red; she had stuck a red artificial flower in her hair, and had on a pair of red stockings with dancing slippers, probably of her own make. Over her shoulders was a light gauzy shawl. Her father took his station in a corner, and motioned to Zachariah to compress himself into another. By dint of some little management and piling up the chairs an unoccupied space of about twelve feet square was obtained. Pauline began dancing, her father accompanying her with an oboe. It was a very curious performance. It was nothing like ordinary opera-dancing, and equally unlike any movement ever seen at a ball. It was a series of graceful evolutions with the shawl which was flung, now on one shoulder and now on the other, each movement exquisitely resolving itself, with the most perfect ease, into the one following, and designed apparently to show the capacity of a beautiful figure for poetic expression. Wave fell into wave along every line of her body, and occasionally a posture was arrested, to pass away in an instant into some new combination. There was no definite character in the dance beyond mere beauty. It was melody for melody’s sake. A remarkable change, too, came over the face of the performer. She looked serious; but it was not a seriousness produced by any strain. It was rather the calm which is found on the face of the statue of a goddess. In none of her attitudes was there a trace of coquettishness, although some were most attractive. One in particular was so. She held a corner of the shawl high above her with her right hand, and her right foot was advanced so as to show her whole frame extended excepting the neck; the head being bent downward and sideways.

===Chinese===
「灾祸比地狱之火算得了什么？他既然造了地狱之火，为何不造灾祸？再说，倘若不是他造的，又是谁造的？」

「一点也不晓得，也不打算为这个伤脑筋。」

「对！」让插进来道——「对，我的孩子；伤脑筋——这个词儿用得好。无论什么事都别去伤脑筋——只要伤脑筋没有益处。世上伤得起脑筋的事多着呢——伤上一伤，倒能生出几分好处来。好了，把屋里腾一腾，让撒迦利亚看看你的舞艺。」

盘碟摞作一堆，桌子推到了一旁。波琳退出去片刻，很快便回来了，穿一条黑天鹅绒短裙，裙摆垂到膝踝之间约莫一半的地方。裙上镶着红边；她头发上插着一朵红色绢花，脚上是一双红长袜，配着跳舞鞋——鞋多半是她自己做的。肩上披一条轻纱披巾。她父亲在一个角落里站定，又打手势叫撒迦利亚把自己收拢进另一个角落。经过一点小小的调度，再把椅子摞起来，总算腾出一块约莫十二英尺见方的空地。波琳开始起舞，父亲用一支双簧管为她伴奏。那是一场非常奇特的表演。它一点也不像寻常的歌剧舞蹈，也同样不像舞会上见过的任何动作。那是一连串同披巾相配合的曼妙回旋——披巾忽而甩上这边的肩，忽而甩上那边的肩——每一个动作都以最从容不迫的姿态，精妙地化入紧随其后的那一个；看那用意，显然是要显示一副美丽身段所具有的诗意表现力。她身体的每一道线条上都波逐着波；偶或一个姿势凝住不动，转瞬间又消融于某个新的组合之中。这支舞除了单纯的美，别无确定的性格。它是为旋律而旋律。表演者的面容也起了一种显著的变化。她神情庄重；但那不是用力绷出来的庄重，倒近于女神石像面上所有的静穆。她的种种姿势里没有一丝卖弄风情的痕迹，虽然其中几个极其动人。有一个尤其如此。她右手把披巾一角高高举过头顶，右脚向前迈出，好教全身显出舒展的姿态——只颈项除外；头则向下、向侧俯着。

===Original===
Suddenly Jean ceased; Pauline threw the shawl over both her shoulders, made a profound curtsey, and retired; but in five minutes she was back again in her ordinary clothes. Zachariah was in sore confusion. He had never seen anything of the kind before.

He had been brought up in a school which would have considered such an exhibition as the work of the devil. He was distressed too to find that the old Adam was still so strong within him that he detected a secret pleasure in what he had seen. He would have liked to have got up and denounced Jean and Pauline, but somehow he could not. His great great grandfather would have done it, beyond a doubt, but Zachariah sat still.

“Did you ever perform in public?” he asked.

“No. I was taught when I was very young; but I have never danced except to please father and his friends.”

This was a relief, and some kind of an excuse. He felt not quite such a reprobate; but again he reflected that when he was looking at her he did not know that she was not in a theatre every night in the week. He expected that Jean would offer some further explanation of the unusual accomplishment which his daughter had acquired; but he was silent, and Zachariah rose to depart, for it was eleven o’clock. Jean apparently was a little restless at the absence of approval on Zachariah’s part, and at last he said abruptly:

===Chinese===
让的乐声戛然而止；波琳把披巾披上双肩，深深一屈膝，退了下去；不出五分钟，她又换回家常衣裳回来了。撒迦利亚窘迫万状。这样的场面，他有生以来从未见过。

他自幼所受的那一套教养，会把这样一场表演看作魔鬼的作为。叫他苦恼的是，他还发觉体内的老亚当依旧如此强壮，竟从方才所见之中察觉出一缕隐秘的快意。他本想站起身来，痛斥让和波琳一顿，可不知怎么，他就是做不到。换了他的高曾祖父，毫无疑问是会那样做的，撒迦利亚却坐着没动。

「你可曾在人前表演过？」他问。

「没有。我很小的时候学的；可我从来不跳舞，除非为了让父亲和他的朋友们高兴。」

这话令人一宽，也算得一种辩白。他觉得自己不那么像个堕落遭弃的人了；可转念又想，自己方才望着她的时候，并不知道她并非一星期每晚都在戏院里呢。他原以为让会就女儿习得的这门不寻常的才艺再解释几句；让却一言不发，撒迦利亚便起身告辞，因为已经十一点了。让见撒迦利亚没有一点称许之意，显然有些坐立不安，末了突然说道：

===Original===
“What do you think of her?”

Zachariah hesitated, and Pauline came to the rescue. “Father, what a shame! Don’t put him in such an awkward position.”

“It was very wonderful,” stammered Zachariah, “but we are not used to that kind of thing.”

“Who are the ‘we’?” said Pauline. “Ah, of course you are Puritans. I am a﻿—what do you call it?﻿—a daughter, no, that isn’t it﻿—a child of the devil. I won’t have that though. My father isn’t the devil. Even *you* wouldn’t say that, Mr. Coleman. Ah, I have no business to joke, you look so solemn; you think my tricks are satanic; but what was it in your book, ‘*C’est moi, l’Eternel, qui fais toutes les choses là*’?” and as Zachariah advanced to the doors she made him a bow with a grace which no lady of quality could have surpassed.

He walked home with many unusual thoughts. It was the first time he had ever been in the company of a woman of any liveliness of temperament, and with an intellect which was on equal terms with that of a man. In his own Calvinistic Dissenting society the pious women who were members of the church took little or no interest in the mental life of their husband. They read no books, knew nothing of politics, were astonishingly ignorant, and lived in their household duties. To be with a woman who could stand up against him was a new experience. Here was a girl to whom every thought her father possessed was familiar!

===Chinese===
「你觉得她怎么样？」

撒迦利亚迟疑着，波琳出来解了围。「父亲，多不害臊！别把人家搁在这么个尴尬位置上。」

「真是奇妙极了，」撒迦利亚嗫嚅道，「只是我们不惯看那样的东西。」

「『我们』是谁呀？」波琳说。「啊，当然，你们是清教徒。我是个——你们管那叫什么来着？——是个女儿……不对，不是这个字——是个魔鬼的孩子。不过这个名目我可不领。我父亲又不是魔鬼。连*你*也不会这么说的，科尔曼先生。啊，我不该拿这个开玩笑，瞧你神色这样郑重；你以为我这些把戏出自撒旦；可你那本书上是怎么说的？『C'est moi, l'Eternel, qui fais toutes ces choses là』——『做成了这一切的，是我耶和华』。」撒迦利亚走向门口时，她向他行了一礼，那份优雅，任何一位名门贵妇也无法胜过。
