他一路走回家去，怀着许多平素未有的念头。这是他头一回同一位性情活泼、才智堪与男子分庭抗礼的女子相处。在他自己那个加尔文宗的不信国教团体里，身为教会成员的虔敬妇人，对丈夫的精神生活少有兴趣，甚或全无兴趣。她们不读书，不知政治，无知得惊人，一门心思过活在家务里。同一个顶得住他的女子相处，是一番全新的经验。眼前便是一个姑娘，父亲胸中的每一个念头，她无不了然！

===Original===
But there was another experience. From his youth upwards he had been trained with every weapon in the chapel armoury, and yet he now found himself as powerless as the merest novice to prevent the very sinful occupation of dwelling upon every attitude of Pauline, and outlining every one of her limbs. Do what he might, her image was forever before his eyes, and reconstructed itself after every attempt to abolish it, just as a reflected image in a pool slowly but inevitably gathers itself together again after each disturbance of the water. When he got home, he found, to his surprise, that his wife was still sitting up. She had been to the weekly prayer-meeting, and was not in a very pleasant temper. She was not spiteful, but unusually frigid. She felt herself to be better than her husband, and she asked him if he could not arrange in future that his political meetings might not interfere with his religious duties.

“Your absence, too, was noticed, and Mrs. Carver asked me how it was that Mr. Coleman could let me go home alone. She offered to tell Mr. Carver to come home with me; but I refused.”

Delightfully generous of Mrs. Carver! That was the sort of kindness for which she and many of her Pike Street friends were so distinguished; and Mrs. Coleman not only felt it deeply, but was glad of the opportunity of letting Mr. Coleman know how good the Carvers were.

===Chinese===
可还有另一种经验。他从少年时代起，便受遍了礼拜堂军械库里每一件兵器的操练，此刻却发现自己同最稚嫩的新手一样无能为力，拦不住那桩罪孽深重的营生：一味回味波琳的每一个姿势，勾描她四肢的每一根线条。任凭他怎样挣扎，她的身影永远悬在眼前，每一回想将它驱散，它便重新聚合起来，恰如池水中的倒影，每被搅动一回，又缓慢而无可避免地自行聚拢回来。到家时他诧异地发现，妻子竟还没有就寝。她去过了每周的祷告会，心绪不甚和悦。她不含恶意，只是异乎寻常地冷。她自觉比丈夫高出一头，便问他，往后能不能安排安排，别叫他的政治集会妨碍了他的宗教本分。

「你今晚不在场，人家也留意到了。卡弗太太（Mrs. Carver）还问我，科尔曼先生怎么竟放心让我独自回家。她说要吩咐卡弗先生陪我一道回去；我谢绝了。」

卡弗太太真是慷慨得可感！她同她派克街的许多朋友，素以这一类的好意见称；科尔曼太太不但深深受了感动，还庆幸得了这个机会，好教科尔曼先生知道卡弗一家是何等的好人。

===Original===
It was late, but Mrs. Coleman produced the Bible. Zachariah opened it rather mechanically. They were going regularly through it at family worship, and had got into Numbers. The portion for that evening was part of the 26th chapter: “And these are they that were numbered of the Levites after their families: of Gershon, the family of the Gershonites: of Kohath, the family of the Kohathites: of Merari, the family of the Merarites,” etc., etc. Zachariah, having read about a dozen verses, knelt down and prayed; but, alas, even in his prayer he saw Pauline’s red stockings.

The next morning his wife was more pleasant, and even talkative﻿—talkative, that is to say, for her. Something had struck her.

“My dear,” quoth she, as they sat at breakfast, “what a pity it is that the Major is not a converted character!”

Zachariah could not but think so too.

===Chinese===
时候已经不早，科尔曼太太还是捧出了圣经。撒迦利亚颇为机械地翻开。家庭礼拜上他们正按部就班地通读全书，已经读到了《民数记》。那晚诵读的是第二十六章的一部分：「利未人，按着他们的各家被数的：属革顺的，有革顺族；属哥辖的，有哥辖族；属米拉利的，有米拉利族」，云云。撒迦利亚念了约莫十几节，跪下祷告；可是唉，连在祷告里，他看见的也是波琳那双红袜子。

第二天早晨，妻子和悦了些，甚至健谈起来——所谓健谈，是对她而言。有什么事触动了她。

「亲爱的，」早餐桌前坐下时她开了口，「少校竟不是一个归信了主的人，多么可惜呀！」

撒迦利亚也不由得这般想。

===Original===
“I have been wondering if we could get him to attend our chapel. Who knows?﻿—some word might go to his heart which might be as the seed sown on good ground.”

“Have you tried to convert him yourself?”

“Oh no, Zachariah! I don’t think that would be quite proper.”

She screwed up her lips a little, and then, looking down at her knees very demurely, smoothed her apron.

===Chinese===
「我一直在盘算，我们能不能请他来我们的礼拜堂。谁知道呢？——说不定有哪句话会进到他心里去，如同撒在好土里的种子。」

「你自己可曾试着劝化过他？」

「哦，不，撒迦利亚！我看那样未免不大合适。」

她微微抿了抿嘴唇，随后极其端庄地垂眼望着膝头，抚平围裙。

===Original===
“Why not, my dear? Surely it is our duty to testify to the belief that is in us. Poor Christiana, left alone, says, as you will remember, ‘O neighbour, knew you but as much as I do, I doubt not but that you would go with me.’ ”

“Ah, yes, that was all very well then.” She again smoothed her apron. “Besides, you know,” she added suddenly, “there were no public means of grace in the City of Destruction. Have *you* said anything to the Major?”

“No.”

She did not push her advantage, and the unpleasant fact again stood before Zachariah’s eyes, as it had stood a hundred times before them lately, that when he had been with sinners he had been just what they were, barring the use of profane language. What had he done for his master with the Major, with Jean, and with Pauline?﻿—and the awful figure of the Crucified seemed to rise before him and rebuke him. He was wretched: he had resolved over and over again to break out against those who belonged to the world, to abjure them and all their works. Somehow or other, though, he had not done it.

===Chinese===
「为什么不合适呢，亲爱的？为我们心中所信的作见证，总是我们的本分呀。可怜的女基督徒（Christiana）落得孤身一人时，你总记得，说过：『哦，邻舍，你若知道我所知道的，我毫不怀疑，你必与我同去。』」

「啊，是呀，那时候这样自然很好。」她又抚了抚围裙。「况且，你知道，」她突然添了一句，「将亡城里可没有公开的蒙恩之道。*你*同少校说过什么吗？」

「没有。」

她没有再乘势进逼，而那桩不痛快的事实又立在撒迦利亚眼前——近来它已在他眼前立过上百回了——那就是：每当他与罪人同处，他自己便与他们一模一样，只除了不曾口出渎神的话。他为主人向少校、向让、向波琳，做成过什么呢？——那钉十字架者的可畏身形仿佛在他面前升起，责备他。他愁苦极了：他一遍又一遍下定决心，要向那些属于世界的人发作出来，弃绝他们和他们的一切作为。可是不知怎么，他终究没有做到。

===Original===
“Suppose,” said Mrs. Zachariah, “we were to ask the Major here on Sunday afternoon to tea, and to chapel afterwards.”

“Certainly.” He was rather pleased with the proposition. He would be able to bear witness in this way at any rate to the truth.

“Perhaps we might at the same time ask Jean Caillaud, his friend. Would to God”﻿—his wife started﻿—“would to God,” he exclaimed fervently, “that these men could be brought into the Church of Christ!”

“To be sure. Ask Mr. Caillaud, then, too.”

“If we do, we must ask his daughter also; he would not go out without her.”

===Chinese===
「我倒想着，」撒迦利亚太太说，「星期日下午把少校请到家里喝个茶，随后再去做礼拜。」

「好啊。」这个提议倒叫他相当欢喜。这样，他至少还能以某种方式为真理作一回见证。

「或者还可以同时请他的朋友让·凯约。愿上帝——」他妻子吓了一跳——「愿上帝，」他热切地喊道，「叫这几个人能被领进基督的教会！」

「可不是。那就把凯约先生也请上吧。」

「要请他，就得连他女儿一并请；没有女儿，他是不出门的。」

===Original===
“I was not aware he had a daughter. You never told me anything about her.”

“I never saw her till the other evening.”

“I don’t know anything of her. She is a foreigner too. I hope she is a respectable young person.”

“I know very little; but she is more English than foreign. Jean has been here a good many years, and she came over when she was quite young. I think she must come.”

“Very well.” And so it was settled.

Zachariah that night vowed to his Redeemer that, come what might, he would never again give Him occasion to look at him with averted face and ask if he was ashamed of Him. The text ran in his ears: “Whosoever therefore shall be ashamed of me and of My words in this adulterous and sinful generation, of him also shall the Son of man be ashamed, when He cometh in the glory of His Father with the holy angels.”

===Chinese===
「我原先并不知道他有个女儿。你从没同我讲过她。」

「到前两天晚上以前，我自己也没见过她。」

「她的事我一概不知。她还是个外国人呢。但愿她是个端正的年轻女子。」

「我知之甚少；不过她与其说是外国人，不如说更像英国人。让来此地已好多年，她过来的时候年纪还很小。我想她非请不可。」

「很好。」事情就这样定了。

撒迦利亚当夜向他的救赎主起誓：无论将来出什么事，他再也不给祂任何因由，教祂别转脸来看他、问他是否以祂为耻。那节经文在他耳中回响：「凡在这淫乱罪恶的世代，把我和我的道当作可耻的，人子在他父的荣耀里、同圣天使降临的时候，也要把那人当作可耻的。」

## XIX “The Kingdom of Heaven Is Like Unto Leaven” / 十九、「天国好像面酵」

===Original===
Mr. Isaac Allen, Fanny’s father, was an ardent Whig in politics—what in later years would have been called a Radical. He had been apprenticed in London, and had attended Mr. Bradshaw’s ministrations there. He was the chosen friend of Zachariah Coleman; but although he loved Zachariah, he had held but little intercourse with him during his first marriage. There were family reasons for the estrangement, due principally to a quarrel between Mrs. Isaac and the first Mrs. Zachariah. But after Mrs. Zachariah had died and her husband suffered so much Isaac was drawn to him again. He was proud of him as a martyr for a good cause, and he often saw him when he went to London on business.

It was in consequence of these London visits that books appeared on the little bookshelf in Cowfold Square which were to be found nowhere else in the town, at any rate not in the Dissenting portion of it. It was a little bookcase, it is true, for people in country places were not great readers in those days; but Sir Walter Scott was there, and upstairs in Mr. Allen’s room there was Byron—not an uncut copy, but one well used both by husband and wife. Mrs. Allen was not a particularly robust woman, although she was energetic. Often without warning, she would not make her appearance till twelve or one o’clock in the day, and would have her fire alight in her bedroom and take her breakfast in bed. It was well understood when she was not at the table with the others that the house was to be kept quiet. After a cup of tea—nothing more—she rose and sat reading for a good two hours. It was not that she was particularly unwell—she simply needed rest. Every now and then retreat from the world and perfect isolation were a necessity to her. If she forced herself to come downstairs when she ought to be by herself she became really ill. Occasionally the fire was alight in the evening, too, and she would be off the moment tea was over, Isaac frequently joining her then, although he never remained with her in the morning. She was almost sure to escape on the day following any excitement or undue worry about household affairs. She knew Sir Walter Scott from end to end, and as few people knew him. He had been to her, and to her husband too, what he can only be to people leading a dull life far from the world. He had broken up its monotony and created a new universe! He had introduced them into a royal society of noble friends. He had added to the ordinary motives which prompted Cowfold action a thousand higher motives. Then there was the charm of the magician, so sanative, so blessed, felt directly any volume of that glorious number was opened. *Kenilworth* or *Redgauntlet* was taken down, and the reader was at once in another country and in another age, transported as if by some Arabian charm away from Cowfold cares. If anywhere in another world the blessings which men have conferred here are taken into account in distributing reward, surely the choicest in the store of the Most High will be reserved for His servant Scott! It may be said of others that they have made the world wise or rich, but of him it must be said that *he, more than all, has made the world happier*—wiser too, wiser through its happiness.

Of the influence of Byron nothing more need be said here, because so much has been said before. It may seem strange that the deacon of a Dissenting chapel and his wife could read him, and could continue to wait upon the ministrations of the Reverend John Broad; but I am only stating a fact. Mrs. Allen could repeat page after page of *Childe Harold*, and yet she went diligently to Tanner’s Lane. Part of what was read exhaled in the almost republican politics of the Allen household; but it had also its effect in another direction, and it was always felt by the Broads that the Allens were questionable members of the flock. They were gathered into the fold on Sunday, and had the genuine J. B. on their wool, but there was a cross in them. There was nothing which could be urged against them. No word of heresy ever escaped them, no symptom of disbelief was ever seen and yet Mr. Broad often desired exceedingly that they were different, was never at ease with them, and in his heart of hearts bitterly hated them. After all that can be said by way of explanation, there was much in this concealed animosity of Mr. Broad which was unaccountable. It was concealed because he was far too worldly-wise to show it openly; but it was none the less intense. Indeed, it was so intense as to be almost inconsistent with Mr. Broad’s cast of character, and his biographer is at a loss to find the precise point where it naturally connects itself with the main stem from which branch off the rest of his virtues and vices. However, there it was, and perhaps some shrewder psychologist may be able to explain how such a passion could be begotten in a nature otherwise so somnolent.

===Chinese===
范妮的父亲艾萨克·艾伦先生（Isaac Allen）在政治上是个热忱的辉格党人——搁到往后的年月，就得叫激进派了。他早年在伦敦当学徒，在那里常去听布拉德肖先生的讲道事奉。他是撒迦利亚·科尔曼择定的挚友；只是他虽爱重撒迦利亚，在对方头一段婚姻期间，却与他往来甚疏。两家所以疏远，自有家务上的缘由，主因是艾萨克太太与第一任撒迦利亚太太拌过一场大嘴。待撒迦利亚太太亡故、她丈夫又受尽苦楚之后，艾萨克重又向他亲近过来。他以这位为正义事业受难的殉道者为荣，自己因买卖去伦敦时，常与他相见。

正是缘着这些伦敦之行，考福尔德广场（Cowfold Square）那座小书架上，才出现了全镇别处寻不见的书——至少在镇上不信国教的那一隅寻不见。那确实只是个小书橱，那年月乡下地方的人本不大读书；可沃尔特·司各特爵士（Sir Walter Scott）立在那里，艾伦先生楼上的房间里还有一部拜伦——不是连页都未曾裁开的毛边本，而是夫妇俩都翻熟了的一部。艾伦太太算不得身子骨特别结实，虽然人很有劲头。她常常不打招呼，到晌午十二点或一点才露面，叫人在卧室里生上火，在床上用早饭。她若不同众人一桌用饭，全家自然心照不宣：屋里须得保持安静。喝过一杯茶——仅仅一杯——她便起身，坐下足足读上两个钟头的书。这倒不是她哪里格外不舒服——她只是需要歇息。隔三差五地遁世离群、全然独处，于她是必需。倘若她本该独自静养，却强撑着下楼，当真会病倒。有时傍晚卧室里也生着火，一用完茶她立刻便上楼去，艾萨克那时常跟上去陪她，虽然早晨他从不去她房里。但凡前一日有过什么兴奋劳神，或为家务操了过度的心，第二天她几乎必定要躲起来。司各特爵士，她从头至尾烂熟于胸，少有人及得上她。对于她、也对于她丈夫，司各特是唯有对远离尘世、过着单调日子的人才可能成为的东西。他打碎了那单调，造出了一个新宇宙！他引他们进入一个高贵朋友组成的皇家社交圈。他在驱使考福尔德人做事的寻常动机之外，又添上一千种更高的动机。还有那魔法师一般的魅力，那样疗愈，那样蒙福——那煌煌一列书卷，随便翻开哪一册，都即刻能感到。取下《肯尼尔沃思》（Kenilworth）或《雷德冈特利特》（Redgauntlet），读书人登时便置身另一国土、另一时代，仿佛被一道阿拉伯符咒摄走，脱离了考福尔德的种种烦忧。倘若在另一个世界，分派赏赐时当真会计较人们在此世所施与的福泽，那么至高者库藏中最精选的那一份，必定是留给祂的仆人司各特的！论旁人，可以说他们使世界更聪明了、更富足了；论他，却必须说：*他比一切人都更使世界幸福*——也更聪明，因着那幸福而聪明。

至于拜伦的影响，此处毋庸赘言，因为前文已经说得够多了。一位不信国教礼拜堂的执事和他的太太，居然读得进拜伦，居然还能照旧恭听约翰·布罗德牧师的讲道事奉，这看上去未免奇怪；可我不过陈述一桩事实。艾伦太太能把《恰尔德·哈罗尔德游记》（Childe Harold）一页接一页地背出来，却依旧勤勤恳恳地上坦纳斯巷去聚会。读进去的东西，有一部分化作艾伦家那种近乎共和派的政治气味，散发了出来；可它也朝另一个方向起了作用：布罗德一家总觉得，艾伦一家是羊群里靠不住的成员。礼拜日他们也被收进羊圈，羊毛上烙着货真价实的「J. B.」（约翰·布罗德）印记，可他们血里带着一道杂交。要指责他们，却什么把柄也拿不出来。异端的话，他们口中从未漏出一句；不信的形迹，也从未让人见过一分；然而布罗德先生屡屡巴不得他们是另一副模样，同他们相处从来没有自在过，心底深处更是恨毒了他们。任凭怎样解说，布罗德先生这份藏而不露的敌意里，总有许多无从索解的地方。他把它藏着，是因为他深谙世故，断不肯摆到明处；可它丝毫不因此减却炽烈。说实在的，它炽烈得几乎与布罗德先生的性情底子不相称，替他作传的人实在找不到那个确切的接榫之处，好把这情念自然而然地连上主干——他其余的种种美德与恶行，都是从那主干上岔出来的枝条。然而它偏偏就在那里；兴许有哪位更高明的心理学家，能够解释这样一桩情念，怎么会在如此一副昏沉的天性里生出来。

===Original===
For this literary leaven in the Allen’s household, as we have said, Zachariah was answerable. Mrs. Allen loved him as she loved her father, and he wrote to her long letters, through which travelled into Cowfold Square all the thought of the Revolution. He never went to Cowfold himself, nor could he ever be persuaded to let little Pauline go. She had been frequently invited, but he always declined the invitation courteously on the ground that he could not spare her. The fame of her beauty and abilities had, however, reached Cowfold, and so it came to pass that when Mr. Thomas Broad, junior, being duly instructed in the doctrine of the Comforter, entered the Dissenting College in London, he determined that at the first opportunity he would call and see her. He had been privately warned both by his father and mother that he was on no account to visit this particular friend of the Allens, firstly, because Zachariah was reputed to be, “inclined towards infidelity,” and secondly, because, summing up the whole argument, he was not “considered respectable.”

“Of course, my dear, you know his history,” quoth Mrs. Broad, “and it would very much interfere with your usefulness if you were to be intimate with him.”

Little Pauline had by this time grown to be a woman, or very nearly one. She had, as in nine times, perhaps, out of ten is the case, inherited her temperament from her mother. She had also inherited something more, for she was like her in face. She had the same luxuriantly dark hair—a wonder to behold when it was let down over her shoulders—the same grey eyes, the same singularly erect attitude, and lips which, although they were not tight and screwed up, were always set with decision. But her distinguishing peculiarity was her inherited vivacity, which was perfectly natural, but frequently exposed her—just as it did her mother—to the charge of being theatrical. The criticism was as unjust in her case as in that of her mother, if by being theatrical we mean being unreal. The unreal person is the half-alive languid person. Pauline felt what she said, and acted it in every gesture. Her precious promptitude of expression made her invaluable as a companion to her father. He was English all over and all through; hypochondriacal, with a strong tendency to self-involution and self-absorption. She was only half English, or rather altogether French, and when he came home in the evening he often felt as if some heavy obstruction in his brain and about his heart were suddenly dissolved. She and her mother were like Hercules in the house of Admetus. Before Hercules has promised to rescue Alcestis we feel that the darkness has disappeared. Pauline was loved by her father with intense passion. When she was a little child, and he was left alone with a bitter sense of wrong, a feeling that he had more than his proper share of life’s misery, his heart was closed, and he cared for no friendship. But the man’s nature could not be thus thwarted, and gradually it poured itself out in full flood—denied exit elsewhere—at this one small point. He rejoiced to find that he had not stiffened into death, and he often went up to her bedside as she lay asleep, and the tears came, and he thanked God, not only for her but for his tears. He could not afford to bring her up like a lady, but he did his best to give her a good education. He was very anxious that she should learn French, and as she was wonderfully quick at languages, she managed in a very short time to speak it fluently.

===Chinese===
如前文所说，艾伦家中的这团文学面酵，是要归到撒迦利亚账上的。艾伦太太爱他，如同爱自己的父亲；他给她写来一封封长信，大革命的全部思想，便顺着这些信流进了考福尔德广场。他自己从不来考福尔德，人家也休想劝得动他放小波琳来。人家屡屡相邀，他总客客气气地辞谢，理由是他离不开她。然而她那美貌与才艺的名声，早已传到了考福尔德；于是事情便这样成了：小托马斯·布罗德先生既已在保惠师的教义上受过正当的教诲，进了伦敦的不信国教学院，便拿定主意，一有机会就去登门看她。他的父亲和母亲都私下告诫过他，无论如何不得去拜访艾伦家这位特别的朋友：第一，因为据说撒迦利亚「倾向于不信」；第二，把全部道理总括起来——他「算不得体面人」。

「当然啰，亲爱的，他的来历你是知道的，」布罗德太太说道，「你若同他过从亲密，那你将来的用处，可就要大受妨害了。」

小波琳这时已经长成一个女子，或者说几乎是了。她的性情得自母亲——世间十之八九，怕大抵如此。她承袭下来的还不止这个，因为论容貌她也像母亲。同样一头浓密的深色头发——披散下来垂在肩头时，端的叫人惊叹——同样一双灰眼睛，同样挺拔得出奇的身姿，还有那两片嘴唇：虽不紧紧抿起、拧作一团，却总带着决断的神气。但她与众不同的特别之处，是那份承自血统的活泼。这活泼纯出天然，却常常使她——正如当年使她母亲一样——落下个「做戏」的讥评。这讥评加在她身上，同加在她母亲身上一样不公道——倘若所谓「做戏」，指的便是不真诚。不真诚的人，是那种半死不活、恹恹无力的人。波琳的话，无一不是心有所感，而且一颦一动都把它演了出来。她那出口即言、敏于表达的可贵天资，使她作为父亲的伴侣，宝贵得无可替代。他是彻头彻尾、通体里外的英国人，忧郁成性，一味自我缠绕、自我沉溺。她只有一半英国血统——或者索性说，整个儿是个法国女人。晚间他回到家里，常觉得仿佛脑际、心头某一重滞碍，霎时间消融了。她和她母亲，正像阿德墨托斯（Admetus）家中的赫拉克勒斯（Hercules）：赫拉克勒斯还未曾应承去救阿尔刻斯提斯（Alcestis），我们便已觉得那重重昏黑散了。波琳被父亲以炽烈的深情爱着。当她还是个小孩子的时候，他孤身守着满腹含冤负屈的苦涩，觉得人生的不幸，分派到自己头上的多过了本分；他的心是关闭着的，什么友谊也不放在心上。可这个男人的天性受不住这般窒塞，渐渐地，别处既无出口，便以满川的洪流，向这一个小小的豁口倾泻而出。他欢喜地发现自己还不曾僵冷成死灰；他常趁她睡熟走到她床边，眼泪涌上来，他便感谢上帝——不仅为她，也为自己的眼泪。要把女儿当大家闺秀那样养大，他的财力是不逮的，但他竭尽所能，给她良好的教育。他一心盼望她学会法语；她学语言又快得出奇，不多时，便说得一口流利的法语了。

## XXII The Oracle Warns﻿—After the Event / 二十二、神谕示警——总在事后

===Original===

It is no part of my business to tell the story of the lovemaking between George and Priscilla. Such stories have been told too often. Every weakness in her was translated by George into some particularly attractive virtue. He saw nothing, heard nothing, which was not to her advantage. Once, indeed, when he was writing the letter that was forever to decide his destiny, it crossed his mind that this was an epoch﻿—a parting of the ways﻿—and he hesitated as he folded it up. But no warning voice was heard; nothing smote him; he was doing what he believed to be the best; he was allowed to go on without a single remonstrant sign. The messenger was despatched, and his fate was sealed. His mother and father had held anxious debate. They believed Priscilla to be silly, and the question was whether they should tell George so. The more they reflected on the affair the less they liked it; but it was agreed that they could do nothing, and that to dissuade their son would only embitter him against them.

“Perhaps,” said Mrs. Allen, “when she has a family she will be better.”

Mrs. Allen had a belief that children cured a woman of many follies.

Nevertheless the mother could not refrain, when she had to talk to George about his engagement, from “letting out” just a word.

“I hope you will be happy, my dear boy. The great thing is not to have a fool for a wife. There has never, to my knowledge, been a woman amongst the Burtons or the Allens who was a fool.”

George felt nothing at the time, for he suspected nothing; but the words somehow remained with him, and reappeared later on in black intensity like invisible writing under heat.

===Chinese===

乔治同普丽西拉的恋爱经过，不归我来讲。这类故事讲得太多了。她身上每一点短处，经乔治一转译，都成了某种格外动人的美德。他眼中所见、耳中所闻，没有一样不是于她有利的。的确，有一回，他正写那封从此一锤定音、永远决定他命运的信，心头掠过一个念头：这是一个纪元，是人生的岔路口——折信的时候，他迟疑了片刻。然而没有听见任何警告的声音；没有什么击中他；他做的是他深信最好的事；没有一丝规诫的迹象拦他，任他一路走下去。信差被打发走了，他的命运就此铸定。他的父母为此焦灼地商议过。他们相信普丽西拉是个傻姑娘，问题只在于要不要照直告诉乔治。这件事他们越琢磨越不合意；但两人商定：他们无能为力，劝阻儿子只会招他怨恨他们。

「也许，」艾伦太太说，「等她有了儿女，就会好些。」

艾伦太太素来有个信念：生儿育女能治好一个女人身上的许多糊涂。

然而当母亲的，等到不得不与乔治谈他的婚约时，还是忍不住「漏出」了那么一句。

「我盼你幸福，我亲爱的孩子。顶要紧的是，别娶个傻姑娘做老婆。据我所知，伯顿家也好，艾伦家也好，从来没有出过一个傻女人。」

乔治当时毫无所感，因为他毫无猜疑；可这几句话不知怎么留在了他心里，日后显出形来，黑得吓人，像受了热的隐形墨水字迹。

===Original===

So they were married, and went to live in a cottage, small, but very respectable, in the Shott Road. For the first six months both were in bliss. Priscilla was constantly backwards and forwards to her mother, who took upon herself at once the whole direction of her affairs; but there was no rupture with the Allens, for, whatever her other faults might be, Priscilla was not given to making quarrels, and there was little or no bitterness or evil temper in her. George came home after his work was over at the shop, and sometimes went out to supper with his wife, or read to her the newspaper, which came once a week. Like his father, he was an ardent politician, and, from the very beginning of the struggle, an enthusiastic Free Trader. The Free Trade creed was, indeed, the cause of serious embarrassment, for not only were the customers agricultural and Protectionist, but the deacons at Tanner’s Lane, being nearly all either farmers or connected with the land, were also Protectionist, and Mr. Broad had a hard time of it. For himself, he expressed no opinion; but once, at a deacons’ meeting, when it looked as if some controversy would arise, he begged Brother Allen to remember that, though we might be wise as serpents, we were also commanded to be harmless as doves. There was a small charity connected with the chapel, which was distributed, not in money, but in bread, and Brother Allen, not being able to contain himself, had let fall a word or two about the price of bread which would have raised a storm if Mr. Broad had not poured on the troubled waters that oil of which he was a perfect reservoir.

George did his best to instruct his wife in the merits of the controversy, and when he found anything in his newspaper read it aloud to her.

“You see, Priscilla,” he said one evening, “it stands to reason that if foreign corn pays a duty, the price of every quarter grown here is raised, and this increased price goes into the farmer’s or landlord’s pocket: Why should I, or why should my men, pay twopence more for every loaf to buy Miss Wootton a piano?”

===Chinese===

于是他们结了婚，搬进肖特路（Shott Road）上一所小屋去住——屋子不大，却十分体面。头六个月，两人都沉浸在福乐里。普丽西拉三天两头往娘家跑，她母亲当即把她的家事整个儿揽到手上；不过同艾伦家并没有闹翻，因为普丽西拉纵有百般不是，却不爱寻衅拌嘴，她身上几乎没有怨毒，也没有坏脾气。乔治在铺子里做完工回家，有时携妻子出去吃晚饭，有时把报纸读给她听——报纸一星期来一份。他同父亲一样是个热衷政治的人，而且从这场争端发端之日起，便是个热烈的自由贸易派。这自由贸易的信条，实在叫他大大的为难：不但主顾们都是庄稼人、都是保护贸易派，坦纳斯巷礼拜堂的执事们也几乎个个不是种地的便是同土地沾亲带故，同样是保护贸易派，布罗德先生的日子很不好过。他本人不置一词；但有一回执事聚会，眼见要起一场争执，他求艾伦弟兄（Brother Allen）记住：我们固然可以灵巧像蛇，却也受了命令要驯良像鸽子。礼拜堂附设一桩小小的慈善，散的不是钱，是面包；艾伦弟兄按捺不住，就面包价钱漏出了一两句话，若不是布罗德先生把油浇上这片起了风浪的水面，将它平息——论这种油，他正是一口现成的大油库——一场风暴眼看便要掀起。

乔治尽了最大的努力，把这场争端的是非曲直讲给妻子听，在报上寻着什么，便念给她听。

「你瞧，普丽西拉，」一天晚上他说，「道理明摆着：外国谷物既要纳关税，本地出产的每一夸特粮价便都抬高了，抬出来的这笔钱进了庄稼人或是地主的口袋。凭什么我，或是凭什么我铺子里的伙计，要每只面包多付两便士，去给伍顿小姐（Miss Wootton）买钢琴？」

===Original===

“Really, George, do you mean to say that they are going to buy Miss Wootton a piano?”

“My dear, I said that when they buy a loaf of bread twopence out of it goes to buy Miss Wootton’s piano!” repeated George, laying an emphasis on every word. “I did not mean, of course, that they put their twopences in her pocket. The point is, that the duty enables Wootton to get more for his corn.”

“Well,” said Priscilla triumphantly, “I can tell you she is *not* going to have a piano. She’s going to have a little organ instead, because she can play tunes better on an organ, and it’s more suitable for her; so there’s an end of that.”

“It doesn’t matter whether it is an organ or piano,” said George, “the principle is the same.”

“Well, but you said a piano; I don’t think the principle is the same. If I were she I would sooner have the piano.”

A shade of perplexed trouble crossed George’s face, and some creases appeared in his forehead; but he smoothed them away and laid down his paper.

===Chinese===

「真的么，乔治，你是说，他们要给伍顿小姐买一架钢琴？」

「亲爱的，我是说，他们每买一只面包，里头便有两便士是拿去买伍顿小姐的钢琴！」乔治重说一遍，一字一顿。「我当然不是讲，他们把两便士直接放进她口袋。要害在于：这笔关税叫伍顿的粮食能多卖钱。」

「好哇，」普丽西拉得意洋洋地说，「我倒可以告诉你，她*不*是要钢琴。她要的是一架小风琴，因为风琴上曲子她弹得更好，对她也更相宜；这下子没得说了吧。」

「风琴也好，钢琴也好，横竖没要紧，」乔治说，「道理是一样的。」

「话虽这样说，你方才讲的可是钢琴；我看道理就不一样。我要是她，宁可要钢琴。」

一层困惑作难的阴影掠过乔治的脸，额上起了几道皱纹；但他把它们抹平了，放下报纸。

===Original===

“Priscilla, put away your work for a moment and just listen.”

Priscilla was making something in the shape of netting by means of pins and a long loop which was fastened under her foot.

“I can listen, George; there is no occasion to put it away.”

“Well then,” he answered, placing both his elbows on the table, and resting his face upon them, “all corn which comes into this country pays a duty﻿—that you understand. Consequently it cannot be sold here for less than sixty shillings a quarter. Of course, if that is the case, English wheat is kept up to a higher price than it would fetch it there was no duty. Therefore bread is, as I calculate, about twopence a loaf dearer than it ought to be. And why should it be? That’s what I want to know.”

“I believe,” said Priscilla, “we might save a good bit by baking at home.”

“Yes, yes; but never mind that now. You know that foreign corn pays a duty. You do know that?”

===Chinese===

「普丽西拉，把手里的活计搁一搁，只管听我说。」

普丽西拉正用几枚别针和一条长线环做一件网状的活计，线环一头拴在她脚底下。

「我可以一边做一边听，乔治；用不着搁下。」

「那么好，」他答道，两肘支在桌上，把脸伏在臂上，「凡进这个国家的谷物都要纳关税——这个你明白。所以它在这里便卖不得低于每夸特六十先令。当然，事情既是这样，英国小麦的价钱便被托住了，比无关税时它所能卖得的还高。因此，照我算来，面包每只大约贵了两便士，本不该这样的。凭什么就该如此？这正是我要知道的。」

「我想，」普丽西拉说，「我们自家烘面包，倒能省出好一笔呢。」

「是，是；这会儿先别说这个。你知道外国谷物要纳关税。你当真知道？」

===Original===

“Yes,” said Priscilla, because there was nothing else to be said.

“Well, then, you must see that, if that be so, farmers can obtain a higher price for English corn.”

Poor Priscilla really did her best to comprehend. She stopped her knitting for a moment, put her knitting-pin to her lips, and answered very slowly and solemnly “Ye‑es.”

“Ah; but I know when you say ‘Ye‑es’ like that you do not understand.”

“I do understand,” she retorted, with a little asperity.

“Well then, repeat it, and let us see.”

===Chinese===

「知道，」普丽西拉说，因为除了这个再没有别的话可说。

「那么，你必定看得出：既然如此，英国谷物庄稼人便能卖个高些的价钱。」

可怜的普丽西拉当真尽了最大的努力去领会。她把手里的针线停了停，一根织针抵在唇边，极慢、极郑重地答道：「是——的。」

「啊；可你这样答『是——的』，我就知道你并没有懂。」

「我懂了，」她回嘴道，带一点火气。

「那么好，你把它复述一遍，我们瞧瞧。」

===Original===

“No, I shall not.”

“Dear Priscilla, I am not vexed: but I only wanted to make it quite plain to you. The duty on foreign corn is a tax in favour of the farmer, or perhaps the landlord, just as distinctly as if the tax-collector carried the coin from our till and gave it them.”

“Of course it is quite plain,” she responded, making a bold stroke for her life. “Of course it is quite plain we are taxed”﻿—George’s face grew bright, for he thought the truth had dawned upon her﻿—“because the farmers have to pay the duty on foreign corn.”

He took up his newspaper, held it open so as to cover his face, was silent for a few minutes, and then, pulling out his watch, declared it was time to go to bed. She gathered up her netting, looked at him doubtfully as she passed, and went upstairs.

===Chinese===

「不，我不复述。」

「亲爱的普丽西拉，我不是恼了：我不过想叫你把这件事看得明明白白。外国谷物的关税，是一笔偏帮庄稼人、或许偏帮地主的税，明明白白，就像税吏从我们钱柜里把钱拿走、递到他们手里一样。」

「当然明明白白，」她应道，孤注一掷地豁出去。「当然明明白白，我们是在纳税」——乔治的脸亮起来，他以为真理总算照进她心里了——「因为庄稼人不得不替外国谷物缴关税呀。」

他拿起报纸，摊开着遮住自己的脸，沉默了几分钟，随后掏出怀表，说该睡了。她收拾起网状的活计，走过他身边时将信将疑地望了他一眼，上楼去了。

===Original===

The roof of George’s house had a kind of depression or well in the middle of it, whence ran a rainwater pipe, which passed down inside, and so, under the floor, to the soft-water cistern. A bad piece of construction, thought he, and he wished, if he could have done so, to improve it; but there was no way of altering it without pulling the whole place to pieces. One day, a very short time after the talk about Free Trade, a fearful storm of rain broke over Cowfold, and he was startled by Ellen, his servant, running into the shop and telling him that the staircase was flooded, and missis wanted him at once. He put on his coat and was off in a moment. When he got there Priscilla met him at the door crying, and in a great fright. The well up aloft was full of water, and it was pouring in torrents through the little window. It had gone through the floor of the bedroom and into the dining-room, pulling down with it about half the ceiling, which lay in a horrid mess upon the dining table and the carpet, George saw in an instant what was the matter. He ran up the steps to the well, pulled out a quantity of straw and dirt which blocked up the entrance to the pipe; the water disappeared in two minutes, and all further danger was arrested.

“Why on earth,” he cried in half a passion, “did not you think to clear away the rubbish, instead of wasting your time in sending for me? It ought to have entered into anybody’s head to do such a simple thing as that.”

“How was I to know?” replied Mrs. George. “I am not an ironmonger. What have I to do with pipes? You shouldn’t have had such a thing.”

===Chinese===

乔治家的屋顶正中有一处凹陷，或者说一口天井，从那里引下一根雨水管，打屋子里面穿下去，再从地板底下通到蓄雨水的软水窖。工程上的一处败笔，他心里想；倘若做得到，他真想把它改一改；可要改它，除非把整座房子拆散。自由贸易那番谈话过后没几天，一天，一场可怕的暴雨倾泻在考福尔德上空，女仆埃伦（Ellen）跑进铺子，说楼梯间淹了水，太太叫他立刻回去，把他吓了一跳。他抓起外套，转身就走。到家时普丽西拉哭着迎在门口，吓得不轻。屋顶那口天井灌满了水，正从小窗里倾盆灌下来。水穿透了卧室的地板，流进楼下的餐室，带下来约莫半个天花板，一塌糊涂地摊在餐桌和地毯上。乔治一眼便看出了毛病所在。他奔上台阶到天井口，掏出一把堵住管口的草秸污泥；水两分钟便退尽了，一切后患就此止住。

「你究竟为什么，」他半怒地喊道，「就想不到把那些堵着的东西掏开，倒白白糟蹋工夫打发人去叫我？这么简单的一件事，谁也该想得到的呀。」

「我怎么知道？」乔治太太答道。「我又不是五金商。管道同我什么相干？你原就不该安这么个东西。」

===Original===

Ellen stood looking at the wreck.

“We don’t want you;” said George savagely; “go into the kitchen,” and he shut the dining-room door. There the husband and wife stood face to face with one another, with the drip, drip, drip still proceeding, the ruined plaster, and the spoilt furniture.

“I don’t care,” he broke out, “one brass farthing for it all; but what I do care for is that you should not have had the sense to unstop that pipe.”

She said nothing, but cried bitterly. At last she sat down and sobbed out: “O George, George, you are in a rage with me; you are tired of me; you are disappointed with me. Oh! what shall I do, what *shall* I do?” Poor child! her pretty curls fell over her face as she covered it with her long white hands. George was touched with pity in an instant, and his arms were round her neck. He kissed her fervently, and besought her not to think anything of what he had said. He took out his handkerchief, wiped her eyes tenderly, lifted one of her arms and put it round his neck as he pulled a chair towards him and sat down beside her. Nothing she loved like caresses! She knew what *their* import was, though she could not follow his economical logic, and she clung to him, and buried her face on his shoulder. At that moment, as he drew her heavy brown tresses over him, smothered his eyes and mouth in them, and then looked down through them on the white, sweet beauty they shadowed, he forgot or overlooked everything, and was once more completely happy.

Suddenly she released herself. “What shall we do tonight, George, the bedroom will be so damp?”

===Chinese===

埃伦站在那里，望着这片残局。

「这儿用不着你，」乔治狠狠地说，「到厨房去。」说着关上了餐室的门。

「这一切，」他迸出一句，「我半个铜法新也不心疼；我心疼的只是，你竟连捅开那根管子的头脑都没有。」

她一言不发，只是苦苦地哭。末了她坐下来，抽抽噎噎地说：「噢，乔治，乔治，你在冲我发火；你厌烦我了；你对我失望了。哎呀！我可怎么办，我可*怎么*办哪？」可怜的孩子！她抬起修长白皙的双手捂住脸，好看的鬈发披散在脸上。乔治霎时动了怜惜，两条胳膊已经搂上她的脖颈。他热切地吻她，求她千万别把他方才的话放在心上。他掏出手帕，轻轻替她拭泪，抬起她一条胳膊环上自己的脖颈，一面把椅子拖过来，在她身旁坐下。再没有什么像温存这样叫她受用的了！他的经济学逻辑她虽然跟不上，这温存里的意思她却懂得；她偎着他，把脸埋在他肩窝里。那一刻，他把她的棕色浓发拢过来罩住自己，把眼睛和嘴都埋进发间，又隔着发丝俯望底下那张掩在阴影里的白皙甜美的脸——他把一切都忘了，或者说放过了一切，重新变得完完全全地快活。

她忽然挣脱出来。「今儿夜里我们怎么办呢，乔治，卧室会这样潮？」

===Original===

He recovered himself, and admitted that they could not sleep there. There was the spare bedroom; but the wet had come in there too.

“I will sleep at father’s, and you sleep at home too. We will have fires alight, and we shall be dry enough tomorrow. You be off now, my dear; I will see about it all.”

So George had the fires alight, got in a man to help him, and they swept and scoured and aired till it was dark. In a day or two the plasterer could mend the ceiling.

Priscilla had left, and, excepting the servant, who was upstairs, George was alone. He looked round, walked about﻿—what was it? Was he tired? It could not be that; he was never tired. He left as soon as he could and went back to the shop. After telling the tale of the calamity which had befallen him he announced﻿—it was now suppertime﻿—that he was going to stay all night. Mother, father, and sister were delighted to have him﻿—“It looked like old times again;” but George was not in much of a mood for talking, and at ten o’clock went upstairs; his early departure being, of course, set down to the worry he had gone through. He turned into bed. Generally speaking he thought no more of sleep than he did of breathing; it came as naturally as the air into his lungs; but what was this new experience? Half an hour, an hour, after he had laid down he was still awake, and worse than awake; for his thoughts were of a different cast from his waking thoughts; fearful forebodings; a horror of great darkness. He rose and bathed his head in cold water, and lay down again; but it was of no use, and he walked about his room. What an epoch is the first sleepless night﻿—the night when the first wrench has been given us by the Destinies to loosen us from the love of life; when we have first said to ourselves that there are worse things than death!

George’s father always slept well, but the mother stirred at the slightest sound. She heard her boy on the other side of the wall pacing to and fro, and she slipped out of bed, put on her dressing-gown, and went to listen. Presently she knocked gently.

===Chinese===

他回过神来，也承认那屋里睡不得了。本还有一间客房；可那边也进了水。

「我到父亲那边去睡，你也睡在家里。咱们把火都生起来，到明天就干得差不多了。你这就先走吧，亲爱的；这里的一切都归我打点。」

于是乔治生起了火，又雇了个人来帮忙，两人扫的扫、刷的刷、敞窗晾屋，一直弄到天黑。过一两天，泥水匠便可以来修天花板了。

普丽西拉走了，家里只剩乔治一个人——女仆在楼上。他四下里张望，踱来踱去——这是怎么了？是累了？不可能是；他从来不曾累过。他尽早脱身，回了铺子。把遭的这场灾祸讲说了一遍之后，他宣布——这时已到晚饭时分——今夜要住在这里。母亲、父亲和妹妹都高兴留他——「又像从前的日子了」；可乔治没有多少谈兴，十点钟便上了楼；他这早早的离席，自然都归到白天受的那番惊劳上去。他上床躺下。平日里，他对睡觉想也不想，同对呼吸一样；睡意之来，像空气进肺一样自然；可如今这是怎样新的一场经历？躺下半个钟头、一个钟头过去，他还醒着，而且比醒着更糟；因为他的念头换了另一副铸造，同白日清醒时的念头截然两样：种种骇人的预感；惊人的大黑暗落了下来。他起来用冷水浸了头，再躺下；可是无用，他便在屋里走来走去。头一个不眠之夜是何等的纪元啊——那一夜，命运之神头一回猛拧我们一把，把我们从对生命的热爱里拧松下来；那一夜，我们头一回对自己说：世上还有比死更坏的事！

乔治的父亲一向睡得沉，母亲却连最轻的声响也会惊醒。她听见儿子在墙那边来回踱步，便悄悄溜下床，披上晨衣，过去听。不多时，她轻轻叩门。

===Original===

“George, my dear, aren’t you well?”

“Yes, mother; nothing the matter.”

“Let me in.”

He let her in, and sat down. The moon shone brightly, and there was no need for any other light.

The mother came and sat beside her child.

===Chinese===

「乔治，亲爱的，你不舒服么？」

「没有，妈妈；没什么。」

「让我进来。」

他放她进来，坐下了。月光正亮，用不着别的灯火。

母亲走过来，坐在她的孩子身旁。

===Original===

“George, my dear, there is something on you mind? What is it?﻿—tell me.”

“Nothing, mother; nothing indeed.”

She answered by taking his cold hand in both her own and putting it on her lap. Presently he disengaged himself and went to the window. She sat still for a moment, and followed him. She looked up in his face; the moonlight was full upon it; there was no moisture in his eyes, but his lips quivered. She led him away, and got him to sit down again, taking his hand as before, but speaking no word. Suddenly, without warning, his head was on his mother’s bosom, and he was weeping as if his heart would break. Another first experience to him and to her; the first time he had ever wept since he was a child and cried over a fall or because it was dark. She supported that heavy head with the arm which had carried him before he could walk alone; she kissed him, and her tears flowed with his; but still she was silent. There was no reason why she should make further inquiry; she knew it all. By themselves there they remained till he became a little calmer, and then he begged her to leave him. She wished to stay, but he would not permit it, and she withdrew. When she reached her bedroom her husband was still asleep, and although she feared to wake him, she could no longer contain herself, and falling on her knees with her face in the bedclothes, so that she might not be heard, she cried to her Maker to have mercy on her child. She was not a woman much given to religious exercises, but she prayed that night such a prayer as had not been prayed in Tanner’s Lane since its foundation was laid. For this cause shall a man leave father and mother and cleave to his wife? Ah, yes! he does leave them; but in his heart does he never go back? And if he never does, does his mother ever leave him?

===Chinese===

「乔治，亲爱的，你心上压着事？是什么？——告诉我。」

「没什么，妈妈；真没什么。」

她没有作声，只把他冰凉的手握进自己双手，搁在膝上。过了一会儿，他抽出手，走到窗前。她静坐了片刻，跟了过去。她仰脸望他；月光满满地照在他脸上；他眼里没有一点湿意，嘴唇却在颤动。她把他引开，扶他重新坐下，照先前那样握着他的手，却不说一句话。突然，毫无预兆，他的头伏在了母亲胸口，哭得仿佛心都要碎了。这于他、于她，又是一件平生的初历；自打他还是个幼儿、摔一跤要哭、天黑了要哭的那些日子以来，这是他头一回落泪。她用那条在他还不会独自走路时抱过他的胳膊，撑住那颗沉重的头颅；她吻他，自己的泪同他的泪一道流；可她仍旧沉默。她无须再问；这一切她全知道。两人就这样单独待着，待到他平静了些，他便求她走开。她想要留下，他不肯，她只得退出。回到自己卧房，丈夫还睡着；她虽怕惊醒他，却再也不能自持，双膝跪下，把脸埋进被褥里，免得出声，向造她的主呼求，求他怜悯她的孩子。她本不是个多作宗教操练的女人，可那一夜她作的祷告，是坦纳斯巷自奠基以来不曾有过的祷告。「因此，人要离开父母，与妻子连合」，是这样的么？啊，是的！人是离开了；可他心里就再也不回头么？倘若他果真从不回头，做母亲的，可曾离开过他么？

===Original===

In the morning Mrs. Allen was a little pale, and was asked by her husband if she was unwell, but she held her peace. George, too, rose, went about his work, and in the afternoon walked up to the cottage to meet his wife there. She was bright and smiling, and had a thousand things to tell him about what her mamma said, and how mamma hoped that the nasty pipe would be altered and never ought to have been there; and how she was coming after tea to talk to him, and how she herself, Priscilla, had got a plan.

“What is it?” said George.

“Why, I would put a grating, or something, over the pipe, so that it shouldn’t get stopped up.”

===Chinese===

早晨，艾伦太太脸色略有些苍白，丈夫问她是不是身上不好，她一句话不说。乔治也起了身，照旧去做工，下午步行到小屋那边去会妻子。她喜盈盈笑眯眯的，有一千件事要告诉他：妈妈说了什么呀；妈妈怎样盼着那根可恶的管子改一改、本来就不该安在那里呀；妈妈吃过茶点要过来同他谈一谈呀；还有她普丽西拉自己，想出了一个主意呀。

「什么主意？」乔治说。

「嗐，我要在管口上安一个格栅，还是什么的，它就堵不住了。」

===Original===

“But if the grating got stopped up that would be just as bad.”

“Well then, I wouldn’t have a well there at all. Why don’t you cover it over?”

“But what are you to do with the window? You cannot block out the light.”

So Priscilla’s “plans,” as she called them, were nothing. And though George had a plan which he thought might answer, he did not consult her about it.

===Chinese===

「可要是格栅堵住了，还不是一样糟。」

「那么干脆就别要那口天井。你怎么不把它整个儿盖掉呢？」

「可那扇小窗怎么办？总不能把光堵死吧。」

于是普丽西拉所谓的「主意」，不过如此。乔治自己倒有一个主意，觉得或许行得通，只是他没有同她商量。

## XIV The School of Adversity: The Sixth Form Thereof / 十四、苦难的学校：及其最高年级

===Original===
When Zachariah came to himself he was in a large, long, whitewashed room, with twenty beds or more in it. A woman in a greyish check dress was standing near him.

“Where am I?” he said.

“Where are yer?” she said; “why, in the workus infirmary, to be sure, with me a-looking after yer. Where would yer be?”

Zachariah relapsed and was still. The next time he opened his eyes the woman had left him. It was true he was in the workhouse, and a workhouse then was not what it is now. Who can possibly describe what it was? Who can possibly convey to anybody who has not known what it was by actual imprisonment in it any adequate sense of its gloom; of the utter, callous, brutal indifference of the so-called nurses; of the neglect of the poor patients by those who were paid to attend to them; of the absence of even common decency; of the desperate persistent attempts made by everybody concerned to impress upon the wretched mortals who were brought there that they were chargeable to the parish and put there for form’s sake, prior to being shovelled into a hole in the adjoining churchyard? The infirmary nurses were taken from the other side of the building﻿—sometimes for very strange reasons. The master appointed them, and was not bound to account to anybody for his preferences. One woman had given him much trouble. She was a stout, lazy brute, who had no business in the House, and who went in and out just as she liked. One day something displeased her, and she attacked him with such fury and suddenness that he would have been a dead man in a few minutes if she had not been pulled off. But he dared not report her. She knew too much about him, and she was moved a few days afterwards to look after the sick. She it was who spoke to Zachariah. She, however, was not by any means the worst. Worse than her were the old, degraded, sodden, gin-drinking hags, who had all their lives breathed pauper air and pauper contamination; women with not one single vestige of their Maker’s hand left upon them, and incapable, even under the greatest provocation, of any human emotion; who would see a dying mother call upon Christ, or cry for her husband and children, and would swear at her and try to smother her into silence. As for the doctor, he was hired at the lowest possible rate, and was allowed a certain sum for drugs. It was utterly insufficient to provide anything except the very commonest physic; and what could he do in the midst of such a system, even if he had been inclined to do anything? He accordingly did next to nothing; walked through the wards, and left his patients pretty much to Providence. They were robbed even of their food. They were not much to be pitied for being robbed of the stimulants, for every drop, including the “port wine,” was obtained by the directors from those of their number or from their friends who were in the trade, and it was mostly poisonous. Death is always terrible﻿—terrible on the battlefield; terrible in a sinking ship; terrible to the exile﻿—but the present writer, who has seen Death in the “House” of years gone by, cannot imagine that he can ever be so distinctively the King of Terrors as he was there. The thought that thousands and thousands of human beings, some of them tenderhearted, have had to face him there is more horrifying than the thought of French soldiers freezing in their blood on the Borodino, or of Inquisitional tortures. It is one of those thoughts which ought not to be thought﻿—a thought to be suppressed, for it leads to atheism, or even something worse than mere denial of a God. Thank Heaven that the present generation of the poor has been relieved at least of one argument in favour of the creed that the world is governed by the Devil! Thank Heaven that the modern hospital, with its sisters gently nurtured, devoted to their duty with that pious earnestness which is a true religion, has supplied some evidence of a Theocracy.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚恢复知觉的时候，发现自己躺在一间狭长的白灰墙大屋子里，屋里摆着二十来张病床。一个穿灰格子布衣裙的女人站在他近旁。

「我这是在哪儿？」他说。

「你在哪儿？哟，这不就是在济贫院的病房里嘛，还由我来照看着你。不然你还能在哪儿？」

撒迦利亚又昏沉过去，安安静静躺着。待他再次睁开眼，那女人已经走了。他确实是在济贫院里，而那时的济贫院，可不是如今这副模样。谁能描摹得出它当年是什么样子？谁又能让一个不曾身陷其中、亲历其境的人，对它的阴惨有一点真切的体会——那些所谓护士全然冷酷无情的漠不关心；那些领了钱该看护病人的家伙对可怜病患的弃置不顾；连起码的体面都荡然无存；还有院内上上下下每一个人处心积虑、锲而不舍地向那些被送进来的不幸生灵灌输一个念头：他们是教区的累赘，收留进来不过是走个过场，只等哪天往隔壁教堂墓地的土坑里一铲了事？病房的护士是从院子另一头挑来的——有时缘由极其古怪。院长要派谁就派谁，不必向任何人解释自己看中了谁。有个女人曾给他惹过不少麻烦。她是个粗壮懒惰的蠢妇，本不该留在院里，却由着性子进进出出。有一天不知什么事惹恼了她，她扑将上去，又凶又猛，若不是被人死死拉开，不出几分钟院长就没命了。可他不敢告发她——她知道他的底细太多。没过几天，她就被调去照看病人了。同撒迦利亚说话的，正是这个女人。然而她还远不是最坏的。比她更坏的是那些老迈、堕落、浮肿虚胖、泡在杜松子酒里的丑婆子，她们一辈子呼吸的都是贫民的空气、贫民的腌臜；这些女人身上，造物主的手笔已不留一丝痕迹，哪怕受了天大的刺激，也生不出半点常人的情感；眼见一个垂死的母亲呼求基督，哭喊丈夫儿女，她们会冲她破口大骂，还要捂住她的嘴，逼她噤声。至于医生，是按最低的价钱雇来的，另领一笔固定的药钱。那点钱除了最普通的药剂什么也置办不起；何况处在这般体制之中，就算他有心想做点什么，又能做成什么？于是他索性几乎什么都不做：到各病房走一遭，便把病人统统交给老天爷照管。病人连口粮都要遭人克扣。至于酒类补助被侵吞，倒不大值得同情，因为每一滴——连那「波特酒」也在内——都是董事们从自家同行或朋友手里弄来的货色，多半是毒药。死亡总是可怕的——战场上可怕，沉船里可怕，流亡途中可怕——可是笔者亲眼见过往昔岁月「院里」的死亡，实在无法想象，死亡还能在哪里像在那里一样，当得起「恐怖之王」的名号。一想到成千上万的人——其中不乏心肠柔软之辈——竟不得不在那里与死亡照面，这比想到法国士兵在博罗季诺（Borodino）的战场上僵卧血泊之中冻毙，或想到宗教裁判所的种种酷刑，更叫人毛骨悚然。这种念头属于那类不该去想的念头——一个应当压下去的念头，因为它通向无神论，甚至通向比单纯否认上帝更糟的东西。感谢上苍，如今的贫民至少已经少了一个支持「世界由魔鬼统治」这条信条的论据！感谢上苍，现代的医院，连同它那些教养温柔、以虔诚的认真——那才是一种真正的宗教——恪尽职守的修女，总算为神权统治的存在拿出了一点证据。

===Original===
Zachariah looked round again. There was an old male attendant near him. He had on a brown rough coat with brass buttons, and shoes which were much too big for him. They were supplied in sizes, and never fitted. The old men always took those that were too large. They had as their place of exercise a paved courtyard surrounded by high brick walls, and they all collected on the sunny side, and walked up and down there, making a clapping noise with their feet as the shoes slipped off their heels. This sound was characteristic of the whole building. It was to be heard everywhere.

“You’ve been very bad,” said the old man, “but you’ll get better now; it a’nt many as get better here.”

He was a poor-looking, half-fed creature, with a cadaverous face. He had the special, workhouse, bloodless aspect﻿—just as if he had lived on nothing stronger than gruel and had never smelt fresh air. The air, by the way, of those wards was something peculiar. It had no distinctive odour﻿—that is to say, no odour which was specially this or that; but it had one that bore the same relation to ordinary odours which well-ground London mud bears to ordinary colours. The old man’s face, too, had nothing distinctive in it. The only thing certainly predicable of him was, that nothing could be predicated of him. He was neither selfish nor generous; neither a liar nor truthful; neither believed anything, nor disbelieved anything; was neither good nor bad; had no hope hereafter, nor any doubt.

“Who are you?” said Zachariah.

“Well, that ain’t easy to say. I does odd jobs here as the nurses don’t do, and I gets a little extra ration.”

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚又四下里张望。他身旁有个上了年纪的男看役。那人穿一件钉着铜扣的棕色粗呢外衣，脚上的鞋却大得出奇。院里的鞋是按尺码统一发放的，从来没有合脚过，老人们总挑那些过大的。他们活动的场所是一方砖墙高垒的铺石院子，大家都聚在向阳的一面来回踱步，鞋跟一滑一滑，脚下便发出啪嗒啪嗒的响声。这声响是全院的一桩特色，走到哪儿都能听见。

「你病得不轻哇，」老人说，「不过眼下该好起来了；在这儿能好起来的，可没有几个。」

他是个面带菜色、吃不饱肚子的可怜生灵，一张死灰的面孔，一身济贫院特有的无血色的形容——仿佛一辈子只靠稀粥活命，从没闻过一口新鲜空气。顺便说一句，那些病房里的空气也是一桩奇物。它没有特别的气味——就是说，没有哪种气味能确切说成是这个或是那个；但它自有一股气息，与寻常气味的关系，恰似研得极细的伦敦街道泥浆之于寻常颜色。老人的脸也同样毫无特点。唯一能断言于他的一点是：他这个人身上断言不出任何东西。他不自私，也不慷慨；不说谎，也不诚实；什么都不信，也什么都不疑；无所谓好，也无所谓坏；对身后之事，既没有盼望，也没有疑惑。

「你是谁？」撒迦利亚问。

「嗐，这可不好说。护士们不干的杂活儿都归我干，我就多领一点口粮。」

===Original===
“How long have I been here?”

“About a fortnight.”

Zachariah was too weak to say anything more, and fell asleep again. Next day he was better, and he then thought of his wife; he thought of Caillaud, the Major, and Pauline; but he had no power to reflect connectedly. He was in that miserable condition in which objects present themselves in a tumbling crowd, one following the other with inconceivable rapidity, the brain possessing no power to disentangle the chaos. He could not detach the condition of his wife, for example, and determine what ought to be done; he could not even bring himself to decide if it would be best to let her know where he was. No sooner did he try to turn his attention to her, even for a moment, than the Major came before him, and then his other friends, and then the workhouse and the dread of death there. Mercifully he went to sleep again, and after another long night’s rest he was much stronger. He was able now﻿—first sign of restored power﻿—to settle that he ought before everything to communicate with Mrs. Carter, and he inquired of the old man if he could write.

===Chinese===
「我来这儿多久了？」

「差不多两个礼拜了。」

撒迦利亚虚弱得说不出更多的话，又睡着了。第二天他好些了，这才想起妻子；想起凯约、少校和波琳；可他已经没有力气把思绪连贯起来。他正处在那种可怜的境地：万事万物乱纷纷一拥而上，一件赶着一件，快得不可思议，脑子却全然没有本领把这一团乱麻解开。譬如说，他没法把妻子的境况单独拎出来，想一想该怎么办；他甚至拿不定主意，究竟要不要让她知道自己在哪里。他刚想把心思转到她身上，哪怕只是一瞬，少校就冒了出来，接着是别的朋友，接着又是这济贫院和死在这里的恐惧。所幸他又睡着了，又酣睡了一整夜之后，他强壮多了。这时他已经能够——恢复气力的头一个征候——拿定主意：当务之急，是先与卡特太太（Mrs. Carter）取得联系，于是他问那老人会不会写字。

===Original===
“Oh, yes, I can write,” said he, and something like a gleam of light passed over his countenance at being asked to practise an art almost forgotten in those walls.

A letter was accordingly written to Mrs. Carter, at her sister’s address, telling her briefly what had happened, but that she was not to be alarmed, as the writer was rapidly recovering. He was able to sign his name; but when the letter was finished, he reflected that he had not got a coin in his pocket with which to pay the postage. One of the institutions of the workhouse was, however, a kind of pawnshop kept by one of the under-masters, as they were called, and Zachariah got a shilling advanced on a pocketknife. The letter, therefore, was duly despatched, and he gave his secretary a penny for his trouble. This led to a little further intimacy, and Zachariah asked him how he came there.

“I don’t know,” he replied. “I was born in the country, and when I was fourteen, my father apprenticed me to the watchmaking. He was well off﻿—my father was﻿—and when I was out of my time he set me up in business in Liverpool. It was a business as had been established some time﻿—a fairish business it was. But when I came to Liverpool I felt dull.”

“What do you mean by dull? Stupid?”

“No, not exactly that. You know what dull means, don’t you?﻿—low-spirited like﻿—got nothing to talk about. Well, I can’t tell how it come about, but I was always dull, and have been so ever since. I got married soon after I was settled. My wife was a good sort of woman, but she wasn’t cheerful, and she wasn’t very strong. Somehow the business fell off. Customers as used to come didn’t come, and I got no new ones. I did my work pretty well; but still, for all that, things went down and down by degrees. I never could make out why, except that people liked to be talked to, and I had nothing particular to say to any of them when they came in. The shop, too, ought to have been painted more often, and I ought to have had something in the window, but, as I say, I was always dull, and my wife wasn’t strong. At last I was obliged to give up and go to journey-work; but when I got old I couldn’t see, and was put in here.”

===Chinese===
「哦，会，我会写字，」他说；有人请他去施展一种在这四堵墙里几乎被人遗忘了的手艺，他脸上仿佛掠过一丝光亮。

于是给卡特太太写了一封信，寄到她姐姐的地址，简要告诉她出了什么事，又说不必惊慌，写信人正在迅速康复。他还能亲手签上名字；可是信写完了，他才想起口袋里连一枚付邮资的硬币也没有。好在济贫院里有一项「院制」，是一个被唤作「下级管事」的人开的小当铺，撒迦利亚当掉一把小刀，预支了一先令。信总算如期发出，他付给这位「秘书」一便士，酬谢他的辛苦。这一来两人又亲近了几分，撒迦利亚问他是怎么进到这里来的。

「说不清，」他答道。「我生在乡下，十四岁那年，爹送我去当学徒，学的钟表手艺。爹家境不坏——我爹——我满师以后，他出钱叫我在利物浦（Liverpool）开了间铺子。那是个开了有些年头的买卖，生意也还过得去。可我一到利物浦，心里就觉得发闷。」

「你说的发闷是什么意思？是脑子笨吗？」

「不，倒不是那个意思。闷是什么意思，你还不知道吗？——就是心里没劲，没话可说。唉，我也说不清是怎么闹的，反正我老是闷着，打那儿以后就一直这样。安顿下来不久我就成了亲。我那口子是个好人，可她不痛快，身子也不大结实。不知怎么的，生意就淡了。从前来往的老主顾不来了，新的又招不来。活儿我干得不坏；可就是这么着，日子还是一天不如一天。我始终闹不明白为什么，只想到人家喜欢有人陪着说话，可他们进得店来，我偏偏没什么特别要同他们讲的。铺子也该多刷几遍漆，橱窗里也该摆点货色，可像我说的，我老是闷着，我那口子身子又不结实。到后来没法子，只好收了生意出去帮工；上了年纪，眼睛不行了，就给送进这儿来了。」

===Original===
“But,” said Zachariah, “is that all? Why, you are nearly seventy years old. You must have something more to tell me.”

“No. I don’t know as I have; that seems about all.”

“But what became of your father? He was well off. What became of his money when he died?”

“I’d had my share.”

“Had you no brothers nor sisters to help you?”

“Yes, I had some.”

===Chinese===
「可是，」撒迦利亚说，「就这些了吗？你都快七十的人了，总还有点别的可讲吧。」

「没有了。我不觉得还有；差不多就这些了。」

「那你父亲后来怎样？他家境不坏，他去世以后，那些钱到哪儿去了？」

「我那一份早拿了。」

「你没有兄弟姐妹帮衬一把吗？」

「有，有几个。」

===Original===
“Did they let you come here?”

“Why, you see, as I’ve told you before, I was dull, and my wife wasn’t strong. They never came much to see me. It was my fault; I never had nothing to say to them.”

“Had you no children?”

“Yes, I had a son and daughter.”

“Are they alive now?”

“Yes﻿—both of them; at least I haven’t heard as they are dead.”

===Chinese===
「他们就由着你到这儿来？」

「唉，你瞧，我先前说过了，我这个人闷，我那口子身子又不结实。他们从来不常来看我。那得怪我；我跟他们从来就没话说。」

「你没有孩子吗？」

「有，一儿一女。」

「如今都还在世吗？」

「在——都在；至少没听说他们死了。」

===Original===
“And able to keep themselves?”

“They used to be.”

“And do you mean that your son and daughter let you go to the workhouse?”

The old man was a little disturbed, and for a moment some slight sign of nervous excitement revealed itself in his lustreless eyes.

“I haven’t see anything of ’em for years.”

“Did you quarrel?”

===Chinese===
「自己养活得了自己？」

「早先是养活得了的。」

「这么说，你那一儿一女，就眼睁睁让你进济贫院？」

老人有点不安了，一时之间，那双没有神采的眼睛里露出一点神经质的激动。

「我好些年没见过他们的人影儿了。」

「你们吵过架？」

===Original===
“No, we didn’t quarrel; but they left off visiting us. They both of them married, and went out a good bit, and were gayer than we were. We used to ask them, and then they’d look in sometimes: but never except when they were asked, and always seemed to wish to get away. We never had nothing to show anybody, nor nothing to give anybody; for we didn’t drink and I never smoked. They went away too, both of them, from Liverpool, somewhere towards London.”

“But when you broke down didn’t you inform them?”

“No. I hadn’t heard anything of them for so long. I thought I might as well get into the House. It will do very well.”

“Didn’t you know anybody belonging to your church or chapel?”

“Well, we went to church; but when the business dropped we left off going, for nothing much seemed to come of it, and nobody ever spoke to us.”

“Wouldn’t you like to get out of this place?”

===Chinese===
「没有，我们没吵过架；只是他们后来不来了。他们俩都成了亲，出门应酬多，比我们会玩乐。我们也常请他们来，他们偶尔也来坐坐：可从不请不来，来了也总像急着要走。我们从来没有什么可以给人看的东西，也没有什么可以给人的东西；因为我们不喝酒，我又从不抽烟。后来他们俩都离开了利物浦，搬到伦敦那头什么地方去了。」

「可你垮下来的时候，没捎信告诉他们？」

「没有。这么久没他们的音信了。我想不如干脆进院里来。这儿也挺好。」

「你就没认识个熟人——你们教堂或是礼拜堂里的？」

「唉，我们是上教堂的；可生意一垮我们就不去了，反正去不去也没多大两样，也没人搭理过我们。」

「你不想离开这个地方吗？」

===Original===
“No﻿—I don’t know as I should now; I shouldn’t know what to do, and it won’t last long.”

“How old are you?”

“Sixty-five.”

It puzzled Zachariah that the man’s story of his life was so short﻿—all told in five minutes.

“But did you never have any adventures? Did you never hear about anything, or see anybody worth remembering? Tell me all about yourself. We’ve got nothing to do.”

“I don’t recollect anything particular after I came to Liverpool. Things seemed to go on pretty much in the same way.”

===Chinese===
「不想——如今真出去了，我也不知道该做什么；反正也熬不长了。」

「你多大年纪了？」

「六十五。」

这个人的一生讲出来竟这样短——前后五分钟就讲完了——撒迦利亚觉得费解。

「可你就从没经历过什么奇事？就没听说过什么值得记住的事，见过什么值得记住的人？把你自己的事统统讲给我听吧。咱们闲着也是闲着。」

「来了利物浦以后，我想不起有什么特别的事。日子好像都是差不多一个样过来的。」

===Original===
“But you got married, and your wife died?”

“Yes﻿—I got married, and she died.”

“What was your wife’s name?”

“Her name was Jenkins; she was the daughter of the saddler that lived next door.”

“Couldn’t her friends have helped you?”

“After she died they had nothing more to do with me.”

===Chinese===
「可你成了亲，后来妻子死了？」

「是——我成了亲，她死了。」

「你妻子叫什么名字？」

「她姓詹金斯（Jenkins）；是隔壁住的那个马具匠的女儿。」

「她那边的亲友就不能帮帮你？」

「她一死，他们就不跟我来往了。」

===Original===
“And you really cannot tell me any more?”

“No﻿—how can I? What more is there to tell? It’s all alike.”

The old pauper was called away, and went shuffling along to the door, leaving Zachariah to his meditations.

Another day passed, and he was lying half asleep when a visitor was announced, and close upon the announcement stood before him﻿—who should it be?﻿—no other than Mrs. Carter, out of breath, radiant, healthy, impetuous.

“God bless the poor dear man!” she burst out; “to think of finding you here, and not to have told us before. But I suppose you couldn’t. Directly as I got your letter off I came, and here I am, you see.”

===Chinese===
「你真的再没什么可跟我讲的了？」

「没了——叫我讲什么呢？还有什么可讲的？都是一样的。」

老贫民被人叫走了，趿拉着鞋挪到门口去了，撇下撒迦利亚独自沉思。

又过了一天，他正半睡半醒，有人通报来了客，话音未落，客已站到他跟前——你道是谁？——不是别人，正是卡特太太，气喘吁吁，红光满面，健旺泼辣。

「上帝保佑这可怜的好人儿哪！」她脱口嚷道。「想想看，竟叫我在这么个地方找着了你，先前也不给我们捎个信儿。不过我琢磨你也是没法子。你那封信一寄出去我就动身来了，这不，我到了。」

===Original===
Her presence was like the southwest wind and sunlight after long northeasterly gloom and frost. Astonishing is that happy power which some people possess which enables them at once to dispel depression and even disease. A woman like Mrs. Carter comes into a house where there is misery and darkness; where the sufferer is possessed by demons; unnameable apprehensions, which thicken his blood and make him cry for death, and they retreat precipitately, as their brethren were fabled to retreat at the sign of the cross. No man who is so blessed as to have a friend with that magnetic force in him need disbelieve in much of what is recorded as miraculous. Zachariah felt as if a draught of good wine had been poured down his throat. But he instantly asked:

“How is my wife?”

“She is all right; but you mustn’t bother about her. You must come out at once. You mustn’t go back to Manchester just yet﻿—not as they’d care much about you now; Nadin’s got plenty of work to do, and wouldn’t concern himself about you﻿—but you aren’t well enough and are better away. Now, look here﻿—I’ll tell you what I’ve been and done. I’ve got a cousin living here in Liverpool, as good a soul as ever lived. I goes to her and tells her you must stay there.”

“But how can I? Just think of the trouble and expense. I don’t know her.”

“Lord a mercy, there you are again﻿—trouble and expense! What trouble will you be? And as for expense, one would think you’d been living like a Lord Mayor to hear you talk. What are we made for if not to help one another?”

===Chinese===
她一来，就像漫长的东北阴寒霜冻之后刮起了西南风、放出了太阳。有些人身上自有一种快活的力量，一到场便能把愁闷甚至疾病驱散，真是不可思议。像卡特太太这样的女人走进一座愁云惨雾、昏天黑地的宅子；屋里受苦的人正被鬼魅缠身，被种种叫不出名目的恐惧攫住，恐惧凝滞他的血液，逼得他只求一死——而她一进门，那些鬼魅便抱头鼠窜，恰如传说中它们的同类一见到十字架便仓皇败退。一个人有福气交上这样的朋友，朋友身上带着那股磁石般的力量，那他尽可以相信，传闻中的许多神迹大半不假。撒迦利亚只觉仿佛有一盏上好的葡萄酒灌进了喉咙。但他当即问道：

「我妻子怎么样？」

「她好着呢；你可不许为她操心。你得马上搬出去。眼下先别回曼彻斯特——倒不是他们如今还把你放在心上；纳丁（Nadin）手头有的是事要忙，顾不上理会你——只是你身子还没好，离开那边更好。听我说——我告诉你我都办了些什么。我有个表妹就住在这利物浦，天底下再没有比她心眼更好的人。我这就去找她，跟她说你必须住到她那儿去。」

「可我怎么能呢？想想这要给人添多少麻烦、多少花费。我连认都不认识她。」

「老天爷，你又来了——麻烦、花费！你能给人添什么麻烦？至于花费，听你说话的口气，人家还当你一直过着市长大人一样的日子呢。我们生下来若不是为了你帮我、我帮你，那是为了什么？」

===Original===
“I can’t walk; and shouldn’t I be obliged to get the doctor’s permission?”

“Walk! Of course you can’t. And what did my husband say to me before I started? Says he, ‘You’ll have to get a conveyance to take him.’ ‘Leave me alone for that,’ says I; ‘although right you are.’ And I says to my cousin’s husband, who drives a hackney coach, ‘Just you drop down and carry him home. It won’t be ten minutes out of your time.’ So he’ll be here in about a quarter of an hour. As for the doctor, I understand as much about you as he does, and, doctor or no doctor, you won’t sleep in this bed tonight. I’ll go and tell the head nurse or master, or somebody or the other, that you are off. You just put on your clothes.”

In a short time she returned, found Zachariah dressed, wrapped him round in shawls and rugs, helped him downstairs, put him into the coach, and brought him to her cousin’s. It was a little house, in a long uniform street; but a good deal of pains had been taken with it to make it something special. There were two bedroom windows in front, on the upper storey, and each one had flowers outside. The flowerpots were prevented from falling off the ledge by a latticework wrought in the centre into a little gate﻿—an actual little gate. What purpose it was intended to answer is a mystery; but being there the owner of the flowerpots unfastened it every morning when the sill was dusted, and removed them through it, although lifting them would have been a much simpler operation. There were flowers in the sitting-room downstairs too; but they were inside, as the window was flush with the pavement. This sitting-room was never used except on Sundays. It was about nine feet square, and it had in it a cupboard on either side of the fireplace, a black horsehair sofa alongside the wall on the right-hand side of the door, red curtains, a black horsehair armchair, three other chairs to match, a little round table, two large shells, a framed sampler on the wall representing first the letters of the alphabet, then the figures 1, 2, 3, etc., and, finally, a very blue Jesus talking to a very red woman of Samaria on a very yellow well﻿—underneath, the inscription and date, “Margaret Curtin, 10th March 1785.” The only other decorations﻿—for pictures were dear in those days﻿—were two silhouettes, male, and female, one at each corner of the mantelpiece, and two earthenware dogs which sat eternally looking at one another on the top of one of the cupboards. On the cupboard farthest away from the window was a large Bible with pictures in it and notes, and, strange to say, a copy of Ferguson’s *Astronomy* and a handsome quarto edition in three volumes of Cook’s *First Voyage*. Everything was as neat and clean as it could possibly be; but Mr. and Mrs. Hocking had no children, and had saved a little money.

===Chinese===
「我路都走不动；再说我不是还得经医生准许才行吗？」

「走路！你当然走不动。我动身之前，我丈夫是怎么跟我说的？他说：『你得雇辆车去接他。』『这个你甭管，』我说；『不过你说得对。』我又跟表妹夫说——他是赶出租马车的——『你顺道跑一趟，把人接回家。耽误不了你十分钟。』他大概一刻钟之后就到。至于医生，我对你懂的不比他少，管他医生不医生，今晚你甭想再睡这张床。我去跟护士长还是院长说一声，反正跟哪一个当差的说一声，就讲你要走了。你只管穿上衣裳。」

不多时她回来了，见撒迦利亚已穿好衣裳，便拿披肩和绒毯把他裹了个严实，扶他下楼，安置进马车，把他送到她表妹家。那是一所小屋，坐落在一条绵长齐整的街上；可是主人家着实花了一番心思，要让它与众不同。楼上一层临街有两扇卧室的窗，每扇窗外都摆着花。窗台上有一道格子的护栏，正中做成一扇小门的模样——一扇真正的小门。它究竟派什么用场，是个谜；不过既然有了它，花盆的主人每天早晨掸窗台的时候，都要把那小门打开，把花盆从门里挪出去，虽说直接把它们端起来本要省事得多。楼下的起居室里也有花；不过那些花摆在屋里，因为窗子是同街面齐平的。这间起居室除了礼拜天从不开用。它约有九英尺见方，壁炉两侧各有一个柜子，房门右手靠墙放着一张黑马鬃沙发，红窗帘，一把黑马鬃扶手椅，另配三把同样式样的椅子，一张小圆桌，两只大海贝，墙上一幅装了框的刺绣样本：先是字母表上的字母，接着是 1、2、3 这些数字，末了是一位湛蓝的耶稣，正同一位通红的撒玛利亚妇人，在一口焦黄的水井旁谈话——底下绣着姓名和日期：「玛格丽特·柯廷（Margaret Curtin），1785 年 3 月 10 日」。其余的陈设——那年头画片是贵东西——就只有两幅剪影，一男一女，各占壁炉架的一角，还有两只陶狗，蹲在一只柜子顶上，永远你望着我、我望着你。离窗最远的那只柜子上摆着一部带插图和注释的大开本圣经，说来奇怪，还有一部弗格森（Ferguson）的《天文学》，以及一部装帧考究的三卷四开本科克（Cook）船长的《首航记》。屋里的一切都收拾得不能再整洁光鲜了——只是霍金（Hocking）先生和霍金太太没有儿女，攒下了一点钱。

===Original===
Into this apartment Zachariah was brought. There was a fire burning, though it was not cold, and on the table, covered with a perfectly white cloth, stood a basin of broth, with some toast, a little brandy in a wineglass, a jug of water, and a tumbler. The books, including the Bible, had apparently not been read much, and were probably an heirloom. As Zachariah began to recover strength he read the Ferguson. It was the first time he had ever thought seriously of Astronomy, and it opened a new world to him. His religion had centred all his thoughts upon the earth as the theatre of the history of the universe, and although he knew theoretically that it was but a subordinate planet, he had not realised that it was so. For him, practically, this little globe had been the principal object of the Creator’s attention. Ferguson told him also, to his amazement, that the earth moved in a resisting medium, and that one day it would surely fall into the sun. That day would be the end of the world, and of everything in it. He learned something about the magnitude of the planets and the distances of the fixed stars, and noted that his author, pious as he is, cannot admit that planets or stars were created for the sake of man. He dwelt upon these facts, more especially upon the first till the ground seemed to disappear under his feet, and he fell into that strange condition in which people in earthquake countries are said to be when their houses begin to tremble. We may laugh, and call him a fool to be disturbed by a forecast of what is not going to happen for millions of years; but he was not a fool. He was one of those unhappy creatures whom an idea has power to shake, and almost to overmaster. Ferguson was a Christian, and the thought of the destruction of our present dwelling-place, with every particle of life on it, did not trouble him. He had his refuge in Revelation. Zachariah too was a Christian, but the muscles of his Christianity were﻿—now at any rate, whatever they may once have been﻿—not firm enough to strangle this new terror. His supernatural heaven had receded into shadow; he was giddy, and did not know where he was. He did not feel to their full extent the tremendous consequences of this new doctrine, and the shock which it has given to so much philosophy and so many theories, but he felt quite enough, and wished he had never opened the volume. There are many truths, no doubt, which we are not robust enough to bear. In the main it is correct that the only way to conquer is boldly to face every fact, however horrible it may seem to be, and think, and think, till we pass it and come to a higher fact; but often we are too weak, and perish in the attempt. As we lie prostrate, we curse the day on which our eyes were opened, and we cry in despair that it would have been better for us to have been born oxen or swine than men. It is an experience, I suppose, not new that in certain diseased conditions some single fear may fasten on the wretched victim so that he is almost beside himself. He is unaware that this fear in itself is of no importance, for it is nothing but an index of ill-health, which might find expression in a hundred other ways. He is unconscious of the ill-health except through his fancy, and regards it as an intellectual result. It is an affliction worse ten thousand times than any direct physical pain which ends in pain. Zachariah could not but admit that he was still physically weak; he had every reason, therefore, for supposing that his mental agony was connected with his sickness, but he could not bring himself to believe that it was so, and he wrestled with his nightmares and argued with them as though they were mere logical inferences. However, he began to get better, and forthwith other matters occupied his mind. His difficulty was not fairly slain, pierced through the midst by some heaven-directed arrow, but it was evaded and forgotten. Health, sweet blood, unimpeded action of the heart, are the divine narcotics which put to sleep these enemies to our peace and enable us to pass happily through life. Without these blessings a man need not stir three steps without finding a foe able to give him his death-stroke.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚就被安置进了这间屋子。炉火烧着，虽然天气并不冷；桌上铺着雪白的桌布，摆着一盆肉汤、几片吐司，一盅葡萄酒杯里斟着少许白兰地，还有一壶水、一只平底大玻璃杯。那些书，连圣经在内，看得出不大有人翻阅，多半是传家的物件。撒迦利亚渐渐恢复气力，便读起那部弗格森来。这是他头一回认真想到天文学，书为他打开了一个新世界。他的宗教把他全部的思想都拴在地球上，把地球当作宇宙历史演出的大戏台；他虽然在道理上知道这不过是一颗从属的行星，却从未真切地意识到它是这样。在他，实际上，这小小的圆球一直是造物主眷顾的头等对象。弗格森还告诉他——他听了惊骇不已——地球是在一种有阻力的介质中运行的，终有一日必定坠入太阳。那一天将是世界的末日，是世上万物的末日。他知道了行星何其巨大、恒星何其遥远，并且注意到，这部书的作者虽是虔诚之人，也不能承认行星或恒星的创造是为了人的缘故。他反复咀嚼这些事实，尤其是头一条，直嚼得脚下的大地仿佛就要消散，他陷入一种奇异的境地——据说地震地带的人们，在屋子开始摇晃时，便是那副光景。我们可以笑他，骂他是个傻瓜，为一件几百万年后都不会发生的事的预言搅得心神不宁；可他不是傻瓜。他是那类不幸的生灵：一个念头便有力量摇撼他，几乎压倒他。弗格森是个基督徒，一想到我们如今这个居所、连同其上生命的每一粒微尘都将毁灭，他并不为之所动。他在《启示录》里有他的避难所。撒迦利亚也是个基督徒，可他那份基督教的筋骨——至少眼下是这样，从前如何且不说——还不够结实，掐不死这个新的恐怖。他那超自然的乐园已退入阴影；他头晕目眩，不知道自己身在何处。这一新学说的骇人后果，它对那么多哲学、那么多理论所造成的冲击，他没有体会到十成，可他体会到的已经足够，只后悔自己不该打开这部书。毫无疑问，有许多真理，我们的体格还不够强健，受用不起。大体上说，唯一的征服之道，是勇敢直面每一个事实，不管它看起来多么可怕，想了再想，直到越过它、抵达一个更高的事实——这话原也不错；可是我们往往太软弱，在半路上就送了命。我们倒卧在地，咒骂自己睁眼的那一天，绝望地哭喊，说自己生为牛、生为猪，倒比生而为人更好。我想这并不是什么新鲜的体验：在某些病态的境况下，某一种单独的恐惧会死死咬住那可怜的受难者，弄得他几乎魂不守舍。他不知道这恐惧本身无足轻重，因为它不过是疾病的一个指征，换成一百种别的方式发作出来也说不定。他除了通过自己的胡思乱想，感觉不到那病，反倒把恐惧当作一个智识上的结论。这种折磨，比起直接的肉体疼痛——那种疼到底也不过是疼的东西——还要苦上一万倍。撒迦利亚不能不承认自己身子还虚；因此他满有理由设想，他精神上的苦痛同疾病相关；可他怎么也信不下去，只管同他的梦魇角力，同它们讲道理，仿佛它们不过是些逻辑的推论。不过他渐渐好起来，别的事情随即又占满了他的心。他的难题并没有被正当地杀死、被哪支天引的箭当胸射穿，它只是被绕开了、被遗忘了。健康、甘美的血液、搏动无碍的心脏，是神圣的麻醉剂，能叫这些和平的仇敌昏昏睡去，使我们快乐地度过一生。没有这些福分，一个人不必迈出三步，就会撞上一个足以给他致命一击的仇敌。

===Original===
Zachariah longed to see his wife again; but he could not bring her to Liverpool until he had some work to do. At last the day came when he was able to say that he was once more earning his living, and one evening when he reached home she was there too. Mrs. Carter had herself brought her to Liverpool, but had gone back again to Manchester at once, as she could not stay the night. When he first set eyes on his wife he was astonished at the change in her. She was whiter, if possible, than ever, thin in the face, dark-ringed about the eyes, and very weak. But otherwise she was what she had always been. The hair was just as smooth, everything about her just as spotlessly clean and unruffled, and she sat as she always did, rather upright and straight, as if she preferred the discomfort of a somewhat rigid position to the greater discomfort of disarranging her gown.

Zachariah had much to say. During the whole of the four hours before bedtime he did not once feel that drying-up of conversation which used to be so painful to him. It is true she herself said little or nothing, but that was of no moment. She was strengthless, and he did not expect her to talk. So long as he could speak he was happy. The next morning came, and with it came Hope, as it usually came to him in the morning, and he kissed her with passionate fervour as he went out, rejoicing to think that, although she was so feeble, she was recovering; that he could once more look forward as in earlier days, to the evening, and forgetting every cloud which had ever come between them. Alas, when the night of that very day came he found his little store exhausted, and he and the companion of his life sat together for a quarter of an hour or more without speaking a word. He proposed reading a book, and took up the Ferguson, thinking he could extract from it something which might interest her; but she was so irresponsive, and evidently cared so little for it, that he ceased. It was but eight o’clock, and how to fill up the time he did not know. At last he said he would just take a turn outside and look at the weather. He went out and stood under the stars of which he had been reading. The meeting, after such a separation, was scarcely twenty-four hours old, and yet he felt once more the old weariness and the old inability to profit by her society or care for it. He wished, or half wished, that there might have existed such differences between them that they could have totally disregarded or even hated one another. The futility, however, of any raving was soon perfectly clear to him. He might as well have strained at a chain which held him fast by the leg, and he therefore strove to quiet himself. He came back, after being absent longer than he intended and found she was upstairs. He sat down and meditated again, but came to no conclusion, for no conclusion was possible.

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚盼望再见到妻子；可是不先寻到活计，他不能把她接来利物浦。终于到了这么一天，他可以说自己又挣得起饭碗了；一天傍晚他回到家，她也已经在那儿了。是卡特太太亲自把她带到利物浦来的，只是当夜不能留宿，便即刻回曼彻斯特去了。乍一见妻子，他吃了一惊：她变了。她的脸色比先前更白——白得不能再白，脸颊消瘦，眼圈发黑，身子极弱。可是除却这些，她还是向来那个她。头发依旧梳得那样平整，周身上下依旧那样一尘不染、纹丝不乱，坐姿也一如往常，相当端正笔直，仿佛她宁可受一点僵坐的不自在，也不愿受扯乱衣裙的更大的不自在。

撒迦利亚有许多话要说。睡前整整四个钟头，他一次也不曾感到那种曾叫他如此痛苦的谈话干涸。她本人确实少言寡语，或者一言不发，可这无关紧要。她身上没有力气，他本就不指望她开口。只要他自己能说，他就是快乐的。第二天早晨来了，希望也随之一同来了——希望总是在早晨来到他这里——他出门时怀着火热的深情吻了她，欢欢喜喜地想：她虽然这样虚弱，却在一天天好起来；他又能像早年的日子那样盼望傍晚回家了，横在两人之间的每一片阴云也都忘掉了。唉，偏偏这一天的夜晚来到时，他发现他那一小份储存已经告罄，他和他这终身伴侣对坐了一刻钟还多，谁也没说一句话。他提议读点什么，拿起那部弗格森，想从里面拣出些或许她会有兴趣的东西；可她那样毫无回应，分明毫不在意，他也就停了。才不过八点钟，这段时间怎么打发，他不知道。末了他说，他出去转一圈，看看天气。他走出去，站在他读到过的那些星星底下。这样一场久别之后的重逢，还不满二十四个钟头，可他又感到了那种旧的倦怠，那种旧的无能：既不能从她的陪伴里得益，也不去在意它。他希望——或者半希望——两人之间当初若真存在那样大的分歧，以致可以全然不理会对方、甚至彼此仇恨，倒也罢了。然而一切怨愤狂言的徒劳，他很快就看得一清二楚。那无异于去挣一条牢牢拴住他腿的锁链，于是他竭力让自己平静下来。他在外面待得比原打算的久，回来时发现她已经上楼去了。他坐下又冥想了一阵，却得不出任何结论，因为本来就不可能有结论。

===Original===
“I am not well, as you know. You yourself don’t seem well.”

He felt suddenly as if he would have liked to throw himself on his knees before her, and to have it all out with her; to say to her all he had said to himself; to expose all his misery to her; to try to find out whether she still loved him; to break or thaw the shell of ice which seemed to have frozen round her. But he could not do it. He was on the point of doing it, when he looked at her face, and there was something in it which stopped him. No such confidence was possible, and he went back into himself again.

“Shall I read to you?”

“Yes, if you like.”

“What shall I read?”

“I don’t care; anything you please.”

===Chinese===
「我不好，你是知道的。你自己看上去也不好。」

他忽然觉得，真想跪倒在她面前，把心里的话统统倒给她；把对自己说过的话都对她说；把自己的全部苦楚摊开给她看；试探一下她是否还爱他；把那层仿佛在她周身冻结起来的冰壳敲开，或者焐化。可是他做不到。他正要做的时候，看了看她的脸，脸上有一种什么东西拦住了他。这样的倾诉是不可能的，于是他又缩回了自己心里。

「我念点什么给你听好吗？」

「好，随你。」

「念什么呢？」

「无所谓；你随便。」

===Original===
“Shall it be Cook’s Voyages?”

“I have just said I really do not care.”

He took down the Cook’s Voyages; but after about ten minutes he could not go on and he put it back in its place.

“Caillaud’s trial is to take place next week,” he observed after a long pause.

“Horrible man!” she exclaimed, with a sudden increase of energy. “I understand that it was in defending him Major Maitland lost his life.”

“My dear, you are quite wrong. He was defending Major Maitland, and shot the soldier who killed him.”

===Chinese===
「念库克船长的航行记好不好？」

「我刚说了，我真的无所谓。」

他把那部库克航行记取下来；可是念了约莫十分钟，他就念不下去了，又把它放回原处。

「凯约的案子下礼拜开庭，」隔了半晌，他说。

「可怕的人！」她提高了声音，蓦地来了劲。「我听说，梅特兰少校就是为了护着他，才丢了性命。」

「亲爱的，你全弄错了。是他在护着梅特兰少校，他开枪打死了那个杀害少校的士兵。」

===Original===
“Quite wrong, am I? Of course I am quite wrong!”

“I was at headquarters, you remember.”

“Yes, you were; but you were not near Major Maitland.”

Zachariah raised his eyes; he thought he detected, he was sure he detected, in the tone of this sentence a distinct sneer.

“I was not with Major Maitland; my duties called me elsewhere; but I am more likely to know what happened than any gossiping outsider.”

“I don’t believe in your foreign infidels.”

===Chinese===
「我全弄错了，是吗？我当然全弄错了！」

「我当时在总部，你是记得的。」

「是，你是在总部；可你并不在梅特兰少校身边。」

撒迦利亚抬起了眼睛；他觉得——他敢肯定——自己在这句话的腔调里，听出了一丝明白无误的讥诮。

「我是不在梅特兰少校身边；我的职守把我调到了别处；可比起任何嚼舌头的外人，我总更有资格知道当时出了什么事。」

「你那些外国异教徒，我一个也不信。」

===Original===
“*My* foreign infidels! You have no right to call them my infidels, if you mean that, I am one. But let me tell you again you are mistaken. Besides, supposing you are right, I don’t see why he should be a horrible man. He will probably be executed for what he did.”

“It was he and that daughter of his who dragged you and the Major into all this trouble.”

“On the contrary, Caillaud, as well as myself and the Major, did all we could to prevent the march. You must admit I understand what I am talking about. I was at every meeting.”

“As usual, nothing I say is right. It was to be expected that you would take the part of the Caillauds.”

Zachariah did not reply. It was suppertime; the chapter from the Bible was duly read, the prayer duly prayed, and husband and wife afterwards once more, each in turn, silently at the bedside, with more or less of sincerity or pathos, sought Him who was the Maker of both. It struck Zachariah during his devotions﻿—a rather unwelcome interruption﻿—that his wife as well as himself was in close communication with the Almighty.

===Chinese===
「*我*那些外国异教徒！你无权管他们叫我的异教徒——你要当真是那个意思，我自己就算一个。可我还是那句话，你弄错了。再说，就算你对，我也不明白，他为什么就算个可怕的人。他多半要为他做的事被处死了。」

「正是他，还有他那个女儿，把你和少校拖进了这一场是非。」

「恰恰相反，凯约同我和少校一样，尽了全力阻止那次游行。你得承认，我说的事我自己清楚。每一次集会我都在场。」

「照例，我说什么都是错的。你偏袒凯约父女，本就在意料之中。」

撒迦利亚没有回答。到吃晚饭的时候了；圣经的经文照例读了，祷告照例做了，随后夫妻俩又各自轮流、默默地在床边，怀着或多或少的诚意或哀恳，去寻求那位造化了他们两个的主。撒迦利亚在灵修中忽然想到——这个念头来得颇不受欢迎——原来他的妻子同他自己一样，也正与全能者亲密相通着。

## III The Theatre / 三、剧院

===Original===
Major Maitland was very fond of the theatre, and as he had grown fond of Zachariah, and frequently called at his house, sometimes on business and sometimes for pleasure, he often asked his friend to accompany him. But for a long time he held out. The theatre and dancing in 1814 were an abomination to the Independents. Since 1814 they have advanced, and consequently they not only go to plays and dance like other Christians, but the freer, less prejudiced, and more enlightened encourage the ballet, spend their holidays in Paris, and study French character there. Zachariah, however, had a side open to literature, and though he had never seen a play acted, he read plays. He read Shakespeare, and had often thought how wonderful one of his dramas must be on the stage. So it fell out that at last he yielded, and it was arranged that Mrs. and Mr. Coleman should go with the Major to Drury Lane to see the great Edmund Kean in “Othello.” The day was fixed, and Mrs. Coleman was busy for a long time beforehand in furbishing up and altering her wedding-dress, so that she might make a decent figure. She was all excitement, and as happy as she could well be. For months Zachariah had not known her to be so communicative. She seemed to take an interest in politics; she discussed with him the report that Bonaparte was mad, and Zachariah, on his part, told her what had happened to him during the day, and what he had read in the newspapers. The Prince Regent had been to Oxford, and verses had been composed in his honour. Mr. Bosanquet had recited to the Prince an ode, or something of the kind, and had ventured, after dilating on the enormous services rendered by kings in general to the community during the last twenty years, to warn them—

> “But ye yourselves must bow: your praise be given
>
>  To Him, the Lord of lords, your King in heaven.”

And Mrs. Zachariah, with a smile and unwonted wit, wondered whether Mr. Bosanquet would not be prosecuted for such treasonable sentiments. Zachariah hardly knew what to make of his wife’s gaiety, but he was glad. He thought that perhaps he was answerable for her silence and coldness, and he determined at all costs to try and amend, and, however weary he might be when he came home at night, that he would speak and get her to speak too.

===Chinese===
梅特兰少校（Major Maitland）极其爱看戏，他既然又渐渐喜欢上了撒迦利亚，时常登门相访——有时为着买卖，有时只为消遣——便屡屡邀这位朋友同去。可撒迦利亚撑了好久，不肯答应。在 1814 年，戏院和跳舞在独立派（Independents）眼里乃是可憎之物。自 1814 年以来，他们已经长进了，于是如今他们不但像别的基督徒一样看戏跳舞，那些更自由、偏见更少、更加开通的人还捧芭蕾舞的场，去巴黎消度假期，在当地研究法国人的品性。不过撒迦利亚身上自有一面朝着文学敞开的窗，他虽然从未看过一出戏搬演，却读剧本。他读莎士比亚，屡屡想，莎氏的一出戏上了舞台该是怎样的奇观。事情就这样落定：他终于让了步，商定由科尔曼夫妇随少校同赴德鲁里巷（Drury Lane），去看伟大的埃德蒙·基恩（Edmund Kean）演《奥赛罗》（Othello）。日子定了，科尔曼太太早早便忙了好一阵子，翻修改制她的结婚礼服，好让自己出场时不失体面。她整个人都浸在兴奋里，快活得无以复加。有好几个月了，撒迦利亚不曾见她这般健谈。她仿佛对时政也上了心；她同他讨论波拿巴（Bonaparte）疯了的传闻，撒迦利亚这边，也把白天遇到的事、报上读到的事讲给她听。摄政王驾临了牛津，有人作诗向他致敬。博赞基特先生（Mr. Bosanquet）向王储朗诵了一首颂歌之类的作品，在铺陈了历朝君王近二十年来对黎民百姓所建树的莫大功勋之后，斗胆告诫他们——

> 「但你们自己也当俯首：你们的颂赞应献与
>
>  祂——万主之主，你们在天上的君王。」

撒迦利亚太太含着笑，用一种难得一见的机锋，纳闷博赞基特先生说了这样大逆不道的言辞，会不会被拿问治罪。撒迦利亚简直摸不透妻子这份欢快，可他心里高兴。他想，她的沉默冷淡，或许正该由自己负责，于是打定主意，无论如何也要试着弥补，往后夜里到家，哪怕再怎么疲乏，也要开口，也要引她开口。

===Original===
The eventful evening arrived. Zachariah was to get away as early as he could; the Major was to call at about six. After Zachariah had washed and dressed, they were to take a hackney coach together. At the appointed hour the Major appeared, and found Mrs. Zachariah already in her best clothes and tea ready. She was charming—finished from the uttermost hair on her head to the sole of her slipper—and the dove-coloured, somewhat Quakerish tint of her wedding-gown suited her admirably. Quarter-past six came, but there was no Zachariah, and she thought she would make the tea, as he was never long over his meals. Half-past six, and he was not there. The two now sat down, and began to listen to every sound. The coach was ordered at a quarter to seven.

“What shall we do?” said the Major. “I cannot send you on and wait for him.”

“No. How vexing it is! It is just like—” and she stopped.

“We must stay where we are, I suppose; it is rather a pity to miss being there when Kean first comes on.”

She was in a fretful agony of impatience. She rose and looked out of the window, thought she heard somebody on the stairs, went outside on the landing, returned, walked up and down, and mentally cursed her husband, not profanely—she dared not do that—but with curses none the less intense. Poor man! he had been kept by a job he had to finish. She might have thought this possible, and, in fact, did think it possible; but it made no difference in the hatred which she permitted to rise against him. At last her animosity relaxed, and she began to regard him with more composure, and even with pleasure.

===Chinese===
这个紧要的夜晚终于到了。撒迦利亚要尽早脱身；少校约定六点钟左右来。待撒迦利亚洗漱更衣完毕，他们再一同乘出租马车去。约定的钟点少校到了，只见撒迦利亚太太早已一身盛装，茶也备好了。她楚楚动人——从头顶最末一根发丝到拖鞋底，无一处不修饰完备——那件鸽灰色的、颇有几分贵格派素净的结婚礼服，穿在她身上再相称不过。六点一刻到了，撒迦利亚还不见人影，她想不如先把茶沏了，好在他用茶点从不耽搁。六点半，人还是没有来。两人这时坐下来，开始留神每一种声响。马车订的是六点三刻。

「我们怎么办？」少校说。「我没法让您一个人先去，自己留在这儿等他。」

「可不是。真叫人恼！他这脾气就是——」话到此处，她收住了。

「我看得原地等着；要是赶不上基恩头一回出场，未免可惜。」

她陷在焦躁难耐的煎熬里。她起身望向窗外，恍惚听见楼梯上有人，便走到门外楼梯口，又折回来，来回踱步，在心里咒骂丈夫——不是渎神的那种咒骂，那她不敢——可咒骂得一样狠。可怜的人！他是叫一件不得不完工的活计绊住了。她本可以想到有这种可能，事实上也想到了；但这丝毫不能减轻她任由自己心里生出的那股怨憎。到后来，她的敌意松了劲，她开始能更平静地看待他，甚至带着几分欢喜。

===Original===
“Had you not better go and leave me here, so that we may follow? I do not know what has happened, and I am sure he would be so sorry if you were to be disappointed.”

She turned her eyes anxiously towards the Major.

“That will never do. You know nothing about the theatre. No! no!”
