「行了，得让我们知道你在哪儿；可信别往这儿寄。你写信给我妹妹；喏，这是她的地址。你不用上楼了；你太太睡熟了。我替你跟她道别。听我一句劝——逃出这个郡去，随便什么地方，今夜就逃出曼彻斯特。」

===Original===
“I must go upstairs to get some money,” and Zachariah stole into his bedroom to take half a little hoard which was in a desk there. His wife, as Mrs. Carter had said, was asleep. He went to her bedside and looked at her. She was pale and worn. Lying there unconscious, all the defects which had separated him from her vanished. In sleep and death the divine element of which we are compounded reappears, and we cease to hate or criticise; we can only weep or pray. He looked and looked again. The hours of first love and courtship passed before him; he remembered what she was to him then, and he thought that perhaps the fault, after all, might have been on his side, and that he had perhaps not tried to understand her. He thought of her loneliness﻿—taken away by him into a land of strangers﻿—and now he was about to desert her; he thought, too, that she also was one of God’s children just as much as he was; perhaps more so. The tears filled his eyes, although he was a hard, strong man not used to tears, and something rose in his throat and almost choked him. He was about to embrace her; but he dared not disturb her. He knelt down at the foot of the bed, and in an agony besought his God to have mercy on him. “God have mercy on me! God have mercy on her!” That was all he could say﻿—nothing else, although he had been used to praying habitually. His face was upon her feet, as she lay stretched out there, and he softly uncovered one of them, so gently that she could not perceive it. Spotlessly white it was, and once upon a time she was so attractive to him because she was so exquisitely scrupulous! He bent his lips over it, kissed it﻿—she stirred, but did not wake; a great cry almost broke from him, but he stifled it and rose. There was a knock at the door, and he started. It was Mrs. Carter.

“Come,” she said as he went out, “you have been here long enough. Poor dear man!﻿—there, there﻿—of course it’s hard to bear﻿—poor dear man!”﻿—and the good creature put her hand affectionately on his shoulder.

“I don’t know how it is,” she continued, wiping her eyes with her apron, “I can’t a-bear to see a man cry. It always upsets me. My husband ain’t done it above once or twice in his life, and, Lord, I’d sooner a cried myself all night long. Goodbye, my dear, goodbye, goodbye; God bless you! It will all come right.”

In another minute Zachariah was out of doors. It was dark, and getting late. The cold air revived him but he could not for some time come to any determination as to what he ought to do next. He was not well acquainted with the country round Manchester, and he could not decide to what point of the compass it would be safest to bend his steps. At last he remembered that at any rate he must escape from the town boundaries, and get a night’s lodging somewhere outside them. With the morning some light would possibly dawn upon him.

===Chinese===
「我得上楼去拿点钱。」撒迦利亚蹑手蹑脚进了卧室，去取书桌里那一小笔积蓄的一半。正如卡特太太所说，妻子睡着了。他走到床边望她。她苍白而憔悴。她无知无觉地躺在那里，从前隔开他们的一切缺憾，此刻尽皆消散。在睡眠与死亡里，掺在我们身中的那一点神性重又显现，我们不再恨、不再挑剔，只会落泪，或祈祷。他望了又望。初恋与求婚的时光从眼前一一走过；他记起她当年于他是怎样一个人，又想这错处说不定终究在自己身上，自己或许不曾费心去体谅她。他想到她的孤单——被他带进一片举目无亲的人地——如今自己却要抛下她；他又想到，她同他自己一样，也是神的孩子，说不定还更像样些。泪水涌上他的眼眶——他本是个又硬又强的汉子，不惯流泪——有什么东西涌上喉头，险些把他噎住。他正要俯身去拥抱她，却不敢惊动她。他在床脚跪下，痛楚至极地求他的神垂怜于他。「神啊，怜悯我！神啊，怜悯她！」他只说得出这一句——再没有别的话，虽说素日祷告早已是他的惯常。她直挺挺躺在床上，他的脸伏在她脚上；他轻轻揭开她的一只脚，轻得叫她无从觉察。那只脚洁白无瑕——而从前有一度，她之所以那般吸引他，正因为她凡事洁净得一丝不苟！他俯下唇去，吻了它——她动了动，却没有醒；一声大喊险些从他胸中迸出，他硬生生压住，站起身来。有人敲门，他吓了一跳。是卡特太太。

「来吧，」他往外走时她说，「你在这儿待得够久了。可怜的亲人儿！——好了，好了——这自然难熬——可怜的亲人儿！」这位好心肠的女人把手亲热地搭在他肩上。

「不知是怎么回事，」她接着说，一面拿围裙擦着眼睛，「我就是见不得一个男人哭，一见心里就发翻。我男人活了这辈子，也不过哭过一两回；哎呀，倒不如让我自己哭上一整夜。再会了，亲爱的，再会，再会；神祝福你！一切都会好起来的。」

转眼间撒迦利亚已到了户外。天黑着，时候也不早了。冷空气叫他精神一振，可他一时决不定下一步该怎么办。曼彻斯特四乡他不熟悉，也断不定朝哪个方向走最稳妥。末了他想起，无论如何总得先逃出市区地界，到界外什么地方过上一夜。到了早晨，或许会有一线光照到他身上。

===Original===
Pauline’s warning was well-timed, for the constables made a descent upon Caillaud’s lodgings as soon as they got him into jail, and thence proceeded to Coleman’s. They insisted on a search, and Mrs. Carter gave them a bit of her mind, for they went into every room of the house, and even into Mrs. Coleman’s bedroom.

“I’ll tell you what it is, Mr. Nadin,” she said, turning towards the notorious chief constable, “if God A’mighty had to settle who was to be hung in Manchester, it wouldn’t be any of them poor Blanketeers. Wouldn’t you like to strip the clothes off the bed? That would be just in your line.”

“Hold your damned tongue!” quoth Mr. Nadin; but, nevertheless, seeing his men grinning and a little ashamed of themselves, he ordered them back.

Meanwhile Zachariah pursued his way northwestward unchallenged, and at last came to a roadside inn, which he thought looked safe. He walked in, and found half a dozen decent-looking men sitting round a fire and smoking. One of them was a parson, and another was one of the parish overseers. It was about half-past ten, and they were not merry, but a trifle boozy and stupid. Zachariah called for a pint of beer and some bread and cheese, and asked if he could have a bed. The man who served him didn’t know; but would go and see. Presently the overseer was beckoned out of the room, and the man came back again and informed Zachariah that there was no bed for him, and that he had better make haste with his supper, as the house would close at eleven. In a minute or two the door opened again, and a poor, emaciated weaver entered and asked the overseer for some help. His wife, he said, was down with the fever; he had no work; he had had no victuals all day, and he and his family were starving. He was evidently known to the company.

===Chinese===
波琳的警告来得正是时候：警察一俟把凯约押进牢里，便突袭了凯约的住所，又从那里直奔科尔曼家。他们执意要搜，卡特太太把他们抢白了一顿，因为他们把屋里每间房都闯了个遍，连科尔曼太太的卧室也不肯放过。

「我把话撂在这儿，纳丁先生，」她说着，转向那个声名狼藉的警察头子，「倘若得由全能的神来定曼彻斯特谁该上绞架，怎么也轮不着那帮可怜的背毯队。要不要把床上的被单也剥了去？那才正对你的路子呢。」

「闭上你那张臭嘴！」纳丁先生喝道；话虽如此，他见手下人咧着嘴笑、自己也有些讪讪的，便吩咐他们退了出去。

与此同时，撒迦利亚一路朝西北走去，无人盘问，末了来到一家路边酒馆，看模样还算安稳。他走进去，见六个体面模样的男人围着炉火吸烟。其中一位是个牧师，另一位是教区济贫员（parish overseer）。时候约莫十点半，他们谈不上欢洽，只是微带酒意、昏昏沉沉。撒迦利亚要了一品脱啤酒、一份面包干酪，问能不能给他一张床。招呼他的那人拿不准，说要进去问问。不多时，济贫员被唤出屋去，那人回来说，没有床给他，还劝他快些用晚饭，因为这家店十一点就要闭门。又过了一两分钟，门再开处，进来一个瘦骨嶙峋的穷织工，向济贫员讨一点救济。他说，妻子害热病躺倒了；他没有活计；一整天没进过一口吃食，一家老小都在挨饿。满座的人显然都认得他。

===Original===
“Ah,” said the overseer, “no work, and the fever and starving; that’s what they always say. I’ll bet a sovereign you’ve been after them Blanketeers.”

“It’s a judgment on you,” observed the parson. “You and your like go setting class against class; you never come near the church, and then you wonder God Almighty punishes you.”

“You can come on your knees to us when it suits you, and you’d burn my rick tomorrow,” said a third.

“There’s a lot of fever amongst ’em down my way,” said another, whose voice was rather thick, “and a damned lot of expense they are, too, for physic and funerals. It’s my belief that they catch it out of spite.”

“Aren’t you going to give me nothing?” said the man. “There isn’t a mouthful of food in the place, and the wife may be dead before the morning.”

“Well, what do you say, parson?” said the overseer.

===Chinese===
「啊，」济贫员说，「没活计，热病，挨饿——他们张嘴永远是这一套。我赌一枚金镑，你准去追过那帮背毯队。」

「这是降给你的报应，」牧师说道。「就仗着你和你那路人，挑得阶级跟阶级相斗；你们从来不挨近教堂，回过头来还奇怪全能的神怎么降罚给你们。」

「等用得着我们的时候，你们倒会跪着爬过来；明天你们就恨不得烧掉我的草垛。」第三个人说。

「我那一带他们那伙人里热病闹得凶，」另一个说，嗓音颇为含混，「他们还惹出一大笔该死的开销，药钱啦，丧葬钱啦。依我看，那病是他们存心招来怄人的。」

「你们就一点也不打算给我？」那人说。「家里一口吃食也没有，我那口子兴许活不到天亮。」

「那么，牧师，你说呢？」济贫员说。

===Original===
“I say we’ve got quite enough to do to help those who deserve help,” he replied, “and that it’s flying in the face of Providence to interfere with its judgment.” With that he knocked the ashes out of his pipe, and took a great gulp of his brandy-and-water.

There was an echo of assent.

“God have mercy on me!” said the man, as he sat down on the form by the table. Zachariah touched him gently, and pushed the plate and jug to him. He looked at Zachariah, and without saying a word, devoured it greedily. He just had time to finish, for the landlord, entering the room, roughly ordered them to turn out. Out they went accordingly.

“The Lord in heaven curse them!” exclaimed Zachariah’s companion when they were in the road. “I could have ripped ’em up, every one of ’em. My wife is in bed with her wits a-wandering, and there a’nt a lump of coal, nor a crumb of bread, nor a farthing in the house.”

===Chinese===
「我说，帮那些当得起帮的人，我们的事已经够多了；去插手上天的审判，就是迎面冲撞天意。」说罢，他磕掉烟斗里的灰，把掺水白兰地灌下一大口。

座中响起一片附和之声。

「神啊，怜悯我吧！」那人说着，在桌边的长凳上坐下来。撒迦利亚轻轻碰了碰他，把盘子和酒壶推到他跟前。他望了望撒迦利亚，一言不发，狼吞虎咽地吃了个精光。他刚好来得及吃完，因为店主进了屋，粗暴地撵他们出去。两人也就出了门。

「愿天上的主诅咒他们！」到了路上，撒迦利亚的同伴喊道。「我真能把他们一个个开膛破肚。我老婆躺在床上，烧得神志都散了，家里没有一块煤，没有一口面包，也没有一个法新（farthing）。」

===Original===
“Hush, my friend, cursing is of no use.”

“Ah! it’s all very well to talk; you’ve got money maybe.”

“Not much. I too have no work, no lodging, and I’m driven away from home. Here’s half of what’s left.”

“What a sinner I am!” said the other. “You wouldn’t think it, to hear me go on as I did, but I am a Methodist. The last two or three days, though, I’ve been like a raving madman. That’s the worst of it. Starvation has brought the devil into me. I’m not a-going to take all that though, master; I’ll take some of it; and if ever I prayed to the Throne of Grace in my life, I’ll pray for you. Who are you? Where are you going?”

Zachariah felt that he could safely trust him, and told him what had happened.

===Chinese===
「嘘，朋友，咒骂没有用处。」

「唉！话说得轻巧；兴许你是有钱人。」

「不多。我也没了活计，没了住处，还被从家里赶了出来。这是我剩下的钱，你拿一半去。」

「我这罪人哪！」那人说。「听我方才那副腔调，你准想不到，我是个循道宗信徒。可这两三天，我一直像个发狂的疯子。这最是要命：饥饿把魔鬼放进了我的身子。不过那钱我不能全拿，师傅；我拿一点儿就好；我这辈子若真在恩典宝座（Throne of Grace）前祷告过，往后也要为你祷告。你是谁？要往哪儿去？」

撒迦利亚觉得这人尽可放心信托，便把事情的经过原原本本对他说了。

===Original===
“I haven’t got a bit of straw myself on which to put you; but you come along with me.”

They walked together for about half a mile, till they came to a barn. There was a haystack close by, and they dragged some of the dry hay into it.

“You’d better be away from these parts afore it’s light, and, if you take my advice, Liverpool is the best place for you.”

He was right. Liverpool was a large town, and, what was of more consequence, it was not so revolutionary as Manchester, and the search there for the suspected was not so strict. The road was explained, so far as Zachariah’s friend knew it, and they parted.

===Chinese===
「我自己家里连一束给你垫身的稻草也没有；可你跟我来吧。」

两人一同走了约莫半英里，来到一座谷仓前。近旁有一个干草垛，他们便拖了些干草进仓去。

「天亮之前，你最好离了这一带；若肯听我一句，利物浦对你最是稳妥。」

他说得对。利物浦是座大城，更要紧的是，它不像曼彻斯特那样满城革命气，对嫌疑之人的搜捕也不那么严。那位朋友把认得的路指点给他，两人便分手了。

===Original===
Zachariah slept but little, and at four o’clock, with a bright moon, he started. He met with no particular adventure, and in the evening found himself once more in a wilderness of strange streets, with no outlook, face to face with the Red Sea. Happy is the man who, if he is to have an experience of this kind, is trained to it when young, and is not suddenly brought to it after a life of security. Zachariah, although he was desponding, could now say he had been in the same straits before, and had survived. That is the consolation of all consolations to us. We have actually touched and handled the skeleton, and after all we have not been struck dead.1

===Chinese===
撒迦利亚睡得极少，四点钟便趁着一片皓月上了路。一路没有遇上什么特别的波折，到得傍晚，他又置身在一片陌生街巷的荒野之中，四顾无门，与红海迎面相对。人若注定要有这样一场经历，幸亏他年轻时便受过这番操练，而不是在安稳了半生之后才被骤然推到这一步。撒迦利亚虽然沮丧，如今却说得出口：他从前也陷过同样的绝境，而且活了下来。这对我们乃是安慰中的安慰：我们实实在在摸过、握过那具骷髅，到头来并没有被击杀。1

===Original===
> “O socii, neque enim ignari sumus ante malorum,
 

 O passi graviora, dabit Deus his quoque finem.
 

 Vos et Scyllaeam rabiem penitusque sonantes
 

 Accestis scopulos; vos et Cyclopia saxa
 

 Experti. Revocate animos, moestumque timorem
 

 Mittite; forsan et haec olim meminisse juvabit.”

===Chinese===
> 「同伴们啊，我们并非不曾亲历先前的灾殃，

 忍受过更重的苦难之后，神也终将给这一场苦难一个尽头。

 你们经历过斯库拉的狂暴，驶过幽深轰鸣的礁岩，

 也领教过库克洛佩斯的磐石。

 振起精神，抛开凄惨的恐惧；

 或许有朝一日，连回忆这些也是一桩欣慰。」

===Original===
He wandered down to the water and saw a ship cleared for some port across the Atlantic. A longing seized him to go with her. Over the sea﻿—he thought there he would be at rest. So we all think, and as we watch the vessels dropping below the horizon in the sunset cloud, we imagine them bound with a happy crew to islands of the blest, the truth being that the cloud is a storm, and the destined port is as commonplace and full of misery as the one they have left. Zachariah, however, did not suffer himself to dream. He went diligently and systematically to work; but this time all his efforts were fruitless. He called on every printing-office he could find, and there was not one which wanted a hand, or saw any prospect of wanting one. He thought of trying the riverside; but he stood no chance there, as he had never been accustomed to carry heavy weights. His money was running short, and at last, when evening came on the third day, and he was faint with fatigue, his heart sank. He was ill, too, and sickness began to cloud his brain. As the power of internal resistance diminishes, the circumstance of the external world presses on us like the air upon an exhausted glass ball, and finally crushes us. It saddened him, too, to think, as it has saddened thousands before him, that the fight which he fought, and the death which, perhaps, was in front of him, were so mean. Ophelia dies; Juliet dies, and we fancy that their fate, although terrible, is more enviable than that of a pauper who drops undramatically on London stones. He came to his lodging at the close of the third day, wet, tired, hungry, and with a headache. There was nobody to suggest anything to him or offer him anything. He went to bed, and a thousand images, uncontrolled, rushed backwards and forwards before him. He became excited, so that he could not rest, and after walking about his room till nearly daylight, turned into bed again. When morning had fairly arrived he tried to rise, but he was beaten. He lay still till about eleven, and then the woman who kept the lodging-house appeared and asked him if he was going to stay all day where he was. He told her he was very bad; but she went away without a word, and he saw nothing more of her. Towards night he became worse and finally delirious.

===Chinese===
他信步走到水边，望见一艘船已办清了出港手续，要横渡大西洋去某个口岸。一股渴望攫住了他，想随船同去。海的那一边——他想，到了那里总可以安息了。我们人人都是这样想的；我们望着船只在落日云霭中没入地平线，便想象它们载着快乐的一船人驶向极乐仙岛，殊不知那云霭原是一场风暴，那命定的口岸也同他们驶离的口岸一样平凡、一样满是苦难。然而撒迦利亚不容自己做这样的梦。他勤勤恳恳、有条不紊地奔走找活；可这一回，他一切努力都落了空。他跑遍能找到的每一家印刷所，没有一家要人手，也看不出哪一家有要人手的指望。他想到码头河岸去试试；可他在那里毫无机会，因为他从来不惯扛负重物。他的钱渐渐见了底；末了，到第三天傍晚，他累得发晕，心也沉了下去。他还病着，病开始遮暗他的脑子。内心的抵抗力一弱，外界的境况便压上身来，如同空气压在抽空了的玻璃球上，终于把我们压碎。还有一桩叫他伤心——正如叫过他之前的千千万万人——他打的这一仗，也许还在前头等着他的这一死，竟是这样寒碜。奥菲利娅（Ophelia）死了；朱丽叶（Juliet）死了，我们总以为她们的命运虽然可怕，却比一个穷汉毫无戏文意味地倒在伦敦街石上的命运更令人羡慕。第三天将尽时他回到寓所，浑身湿透，疲惫，饥饿，还带着头痛。没有人替他出个主意，也没有人给他一点什么。他上了床，千百种幻象不受管束，在他面前冲来撞去。他亢奋起来，怎么也安息不了，在屋里走来走去直到天近破晓，才又躺回床上。天色大亮之后他想起身，却起不来了。他一动不动躺到十一点上下，开寄宿屋的那个女人才露面，问他是不是打算就在那儿躺一整天。他说自己病得很重；她一言不发地走开，再没有露面。入夜他病势更重，终于谵妄起来。

## VIII Unconventional Justice / 八、法外的正义

===Original===
In December, 1814, a steamboat was set in motion on the Limehouse Canal, the Lord Mayor and other distinguished persons being on board. In the same month Joanna Southcott died. She had announced that on the 19th October she was to be delivered of the Prince of Peace, although she was then sixty years old. Thousands of persons believed her, and a cradle was made. The Prince of Peace did not arrive, and in a little more than two months poor Joanna had departed, the cause of her departure having being certified as dropsy. Death did not diminish the number of her disciples, for they took refuge in the hope of her resurrection. “The arm of the Lord is not shortened,” they truly affirmed; and even to this day there are people who are waiting for the fulfilment of Joanna’s prophecies and the appearance of the “second Shiloh.” Zachariah had been frequently twitted in joke by his profane companions in the printing-office upon his supposed belief in the delusion. It was their delight to assume that all the “pious ones,” as they called them, were alike; and on the morning of the 30th of December, the day after Joanna expired, they were more than usually tormenting. Zachariah did not remonstrate. In his conscientious eagerness to bear witness for his Master, he had often tried his hand upon his mates; but he had never had the smallest success, and had now desisted. Moreover, his thoughts were that morning with his comrades, the Friends of the People. He hummed to himself the lines from *Lara*—

> “Within that land was many a malcontent,
>
> Who cursed the tyranny to which he bent;
>
> That soil full many a wringing despot saw,
>
> Who worked his wantonness in form of law:
>
> Long war without and frequent broil within
>
> Had made a path for blood and giant sin.”

The last meeting had been unusually exciting. Differences of opinion had arisen as to future procedure, many of the members, the Secretary included, advocating action; but what they understood by it is very difficult to say. A special call had been made for that night, and Zachariah was in a difficulty. His native sternness and detestation of kings and their ministers would have led him almost to any length; but he had a sober head on his shoulders. So had the Major, and so had Caillaud. Consequently they held back, and insisted, before stirring a step towards actual revolution, that there should be some fair chance of support and success. The Major in particular warned them of the necessity of drill; and plainly told them also that, not only were the middle classes all against them, but their own class was hostile. This was perfectly true, although it was a truth so unpleasant that he had to endure some very strong language, and even hints of treason. No wonder: for it is undoubtedly very bitter to be obliged to believe that the men whom we want to help do not themselves wish to be helped. To work hard for those who will thank us, to head a majority against oppressors, is a brave thing; but far more honour is due to the Maitlands, Caillauds, Colemans, and others of that stamp who strove for thirty years from the outbreak of the French revolution onwards, not merely to rend the chains of the prisoners, but had to achieve the more difficult task of convincing them that they would be happier if they were free. These heroes are forgotten, or nearly so. Who remembers the poor creatures who met in the early mornings on the Lancashire moors or were shot by the yeomanry? They sleep in graves over which stands no tombstone, or probably their bodies have been carted away to make room for a railway which has been driven through their resting-place. They saw the truth before those whom the world delights to honour as its political redeemers; but they have perished utterly from our recollection, and will never be mentioned in history. Will there ever be a great Day of Assize when a just judgment shall be pronounced; when all the impostors who have been crowned for what they did not deserve will be stripped, and the Divine word will be heard calling upon the faithful to inherit the kingdom—who, when “I was an hungered gave me meat, when I was thirsty gave me drink; when I was a stranger took me in; when I was naked visited me; when I was in prison came unto me?” Never! It was a dream of an enthusiastic Galilean youth, and let us not desire that it may ever come true. Let us rather gladly consent to be crushed into indistinguishable dust, with no hope of record: rejoicing only if some infinitesimal portion of the good work may be achieved by our obliteration, and content to be remembered only in that anthem which in the future it will be ordained shall be sung in our religious services in honour of all holy apostles and martyrs who have left no name.

===Chinese===
1814 年 12 月，一艘汽船在莱姆豪斯运河（Limehouse Canal）上开动起来，船上坐着市长大人与各路显贵。同月，乔安娜·索斯科特（Joanna Southcott）死了。她曾预告，10 月 19 日那一天她将诞下「和平之君」，尽管她当时已年届六十。成千上万的人信了她，连摇篮都备下了。和平之君并没有降临，不出两个多月，可怜的乔安娜自己也去了，死因经查验乃是水肿。死亡并未使她门徒的人数减损，因为他们退避到她必将复活的指望里去了。「主的膀臂并非缩短，」他们如此坚称，倒也并非虚言；甚至直到今天，还有人在等候乔安娜预言的应验，等候「第二个示罗」的现身。在印刷所里，撒迦利亚常因旁人认定他信这套虚妄之事，被那些不敬神的工友们拿来打趣。他们顶开心的，便是把他们口中的「虔敬之辈」一概想成一路货；到了 12 月 30 日早晨，就是乔安娜断气的第二天，他们闹腾得比平日更凶。撒迦利亚没有分辩。他出于良心上的热切，一心要为他的主作见证，曾在工友们身上屡屡试手；却从未博得丝毫成效，如今早已歇手。况且那天早晨，他的心全在他的同志们——人民之友——身上。他自顾自哼起《莱拉》（Lara）里的句子——

> 「那片国土上有的是心怀怨愤之人，
>
> 咒诅着自己俯首屈从的暴虐；
>
> 那方土壤见过多少敲骨吸髓的暴君，
>
> 将恣睢之欲妆点成法律的形式；
>
> 外则连年鏖战，内则纷争迭起，
>
> 早已为血泊与滔天罪孽踏出路来。」

上一次集会开得异乎寻常地激烈。关于往后的步骤，意见起了分歧，许多会员——秘书也在其内——主张行动；只是他们所理解的「行动」究竟何指，实在很难说。当晚又特别召集了一次会议，撒迦利亚正左右为难。他生性刚烈，又痛恨君王和他们的臣仆，这份性子几乎能把他驱到任何地步；好在他肩上还顶着一颗清醒的脑袋。少校有一颗，凯约也有一颗。于是他们按兵不动，并且坚持：在朝真正的革命挪出一步之前，总得有几分像样的胜算——有人响应，事有可为。少校尤其告诫他们，操练必不可少；他还明白直言：不但中产阶级个个与他们为敌，就连他们本阶级也怀敌意。这话千真万确，只是这真相太不中听，害他挨了不少狠话，甚至有人骂他居心叵测、意欲叛卖。这也难怪：眼睁睁不得不相信，我们想要帮的那些人自己并不愿意受帮，这滋味无疑苦涩非常。为知恩图报的人出力流汗，率领多数人去对抗压迫者，固然是勇敢的事业；然而更大的荣耀，理当归于那些梅特兰、凯约、科尔曼一流的人物——从法国革命爆发之日起，他们苦斗了三十年，不但要挣断囚徒们的锁链，还得完成一桩更难的功业：叫囚徒们相信，得了自由，他们才会更幸福。这些英雄已被忘却，或者说几乎被忘却。谁还记得那些可怜人——清早里聚在兰开夏荒原上的，或被义勇骑兵开枪打死的？他们长眠于无碑的坟茔，说不定尸骨早已被大车运走，给一条恰好劈过他们安息之地的铁路让了地方。他们比世人乐于尊奉为政治救主的那班人更早看清了真理；却已从我们的记忆里湮灭净尽，历史上永远不会提起他们。将来可会有一个大开庭的日子，公义的判决在那日得以宣示；一切无功受冕的骗子尽数被剥去冠冕，神圣的声音清晰可闻，呼召那忠信的人承受国度——可不就是那班「我饿了给我吃、渴了给我喝、作客旅时留我住、赤身露体时看顾我、下在监里时来看我」的人么？永远不会！那不过是一位热忱的加利利青年的一场梦，我们也别巴望它有成真的一天。我们毋宁欣然甘愿被碾作辨识不出的尘埃，全不存载诸记录的指望：只要我们的湮灭能促成那善工的一星半点，便自欢喜；也甘愿只在那一首颂歌里被记念——将来必要明定，在我们的礼拜仪式中要唱这首歌，以敬奉一切未留姓名的圣使徒与殉道者。

===Original===
The night before the special meeting a gentleman in a blue cloak, and with a cigar in his mouth, sauntered past the entrance to Carter’s Rents, where Mr. Secretary lived. It was getting late, but he was evidently not in a hurry, and seemed to enjoy the coolness of the air, for presently he turned and walked past the entrance again. He took out his watch—it was a quarter to eleven o’clock—and he cursed Mr. Secretary and the beer-shops which had probably detained him. A constable came by, but never showed himself in the least degree inquisitive; although it was odd that anybody should select Carter’s Rents for a stroll. Presently Mr. Secretary came in sight, a trifle, but not much, the worse for liquor. It was odd, also, that he took no notice of the blue cloak and cigar, but went straight to his own lodging. The other, after a few moments followed; and it was a third time odd that he should find the door unbolted and go upstairs. All this, we say, would have been strange to a spectator, but it was not so to these three persons. Presently the one first named found himself in Mr. Secretary’s somewhat squalid room. He then stood disclosed as the assistant whom the Secretary had first seen at Whitehall sitting in the Commissioner’s Office. This was not the second nor third interview which had taken place since then.

“Well, Mr. Hardy, what do you want here tonight?”

“Well, my friend, you know, I suppose. How goes the game?”

“D—m me if I *do* know. If you think I am going to split, you are very much mistaken.”

“Split! Who wants you to split? Why, there’s nothing to split about. I can tell you just as much as you can tell me.”

“Why do you come here then?”

===Chinese===
特别会议的前一夜，一位身披蓝色斗篷、嘴里叼着雪茄的绅士，慢悠悠地踱过卡特租屋巷（Carter’s Rents）的巷口——秘书先生就住在那条巷里。夜渐深了，他却分明不慌不忙，像是颇享受夜气的清凉，不多时又折转身来，再打巷口踱过一趟。他掏出怀表——十点三刻——心里把秘书先生咒骂了一通，连那些八成把他绊住了的啤酒店也一并骂了进去。一名巡警打旁边经过，却分毫也不显好奇；虽说世上竟有人挑中卡特租屋巷来散步，也真是桩怪事。不多时，秘书先生进了视野，带着几分酒意，倒也不算多。同样古怪的是，他对那蓝斗篷和雪茄竟毫不理会，径直回自己的住处去了。那一位隔了片刻跟了上去；而第三桩怪事是，他发觉门没有上闩，便径自上了楼。这一切，我们说，落在旁观者眼里未免离奇，可对这三位来说，却丝毫不奇。不多时，方才提到的那头一位，已经置身于秘书先生那间颇为腌臜的屋子里了。至此才见分晓——他正是秘书当初在白厅坐在专员办公室里见到过的那位助理。自那以后，这样的会面早非一回两回；这一回，既不是第二回，也不是第三回。

「那么，哈迪先生（Mr. Hardy），您今晚到这儿来，是想讨什么？」

「这个嘛，我的朋友，我想您是知道的。牌局进行得怎么样啦？」

「我要是知道才他妈的见鬼。您要是以为我会往外泄底，那可就大错特错了。」

「泄底！谁要您泄底？嗐，根本没什么可泄的。我能告诉您的，跟您能告诉我的一样多。」

「那您上这儿来干什么？」

===Original===
“For the pleasure of seeing you, and to—” Mr. Hardy put his hand carelessly in his pocket, a movement which was followed by a metallic jingle—“and just to—to—explain one or two little matters.”

The Secretary observed that he was very tired.

“Are you? I believe I am tired too.”

Mr. Hardy took out a little case-bottle with brandy in it, and the Secretary, without saying a word, produced two mugs and a jug of water. The brandy was mixed by Mr. Hardy; but his share of the spirit differed from that assigned to his friend.

“Split!” he continued; “no, I should think not. But we want you to help us. The Major and one or two more had better be kept out of harm’s way for a little while; and we propose not to hurt them, but to take care of them a bit, you understand? And if, the next time, he and the others will be there—we have been looking for the Major for three or four days, but he is not to be found in his old quarters—we will just give them a call. When will you have your next meeting? They will be all handy then.”

“You can find that out without my help. It’s tomorrow.”

===Chinese===
「为着见您一面，图个快活，再就是——」哈迪先生漫不经心地把一只手插进衣袋，这一动引出一阵金铁的叮当——「再就是——就是——解释一两件小事。」

秘书先生说自己很是疲乏。

「是么？我想我也乏了。」

哈迪先生掏出一只随身扁瓶，里面盛着白兰地；秘书先生一声不响，取来两只大酒杯和一壶清水。白兰地由哈迪先生来调；只是他给自己调的那一份，同斟给朋友的那一份，并不一样。

「泄底！」他接着说；「不，我想也不必。不过，我们要您帮个忙。少校，还有另外一两位，最好先叫他们避开一阵子风头；我们的主意不是伤他们分毫，而是把他们照看一时，您明白么？下回他要是同那几位都在场——少校这人，我们已经找了他三四天，老住处却总寻他不着——我们就去登门拜访一趟。你们下一回集会定在何时？到那时他们一个个都凑在手边，岂不方便。」

「这事不消我帮，您也打听得出。就在明天。」

===Original===
“Ah! I suppose you’ve had a stormy discussion. I hope your moderate counsels prevailed.”

Mr. Secretary winked and gave his head a twist on one side, as if he meant thereby to say: “You don’t catch me.”

“It’s a pity,” continued Mr. Hardy, taking no notice, “that some men are always for rushing into extremities. Why don’t they try and redress their grievances, if they have any, in the legitimate way which you yourself propose—by petition?”

It so happened that a couple of hours before, Mr. Secretary having been somewhat noisy and insubordinate, the Major had been obliged to rule him out of order and request his silence. The insult—for so he considered it—was rankling in him.

“Because,” he replied, “we have amongst us two or three d——d conceited, stuck-up fools, who think they are going to ride over us. By God, they are mistaken though! They are the chaps who do all the mischief. Not that I’d say anything against them—no, notwithstanding I stand up against them.”

“Do all the mischief—yes, you’ve just hit it. I do believe that if it were not for these fellows the others would be quiet enough.”

===Chinese===
「啊！我猜你们准是吵了个天翻地覆。但愿持重的意见占了上风。」

秘书先生眨了眨眼，脑袋往一边一歪，仿佛在说：「您可逮不着我。」

「可惜啊，」哈迪先生只作不觉，自顾自说下去，「有些人总是一味要往极端里冲。他们当真有什么冤屈，何不循着您自己也主张的那条正路——请愿——去申雪呢？」

说来也巧，就在两个钟头之前，秘书先生喧哗无状、不守规矩，少校不得已裁定他破坏秩序，请他肃静。这份羞辱——在他看来这就是羞辱——正扎在心里发痛。

「因为，」他答道，「我们中间有两三个该死的、自命不凡、眼高于顶的蠢货，以为能骑到我们头上来。老天在上，他们可想错了！坏事全坏在这号人手里。我倒不是说他们的坏话——不，尽管我自己挺身出来同他们对顶。」

「坏事全坏在这号人手里——对啊，您真是一语中的。我实在相信，若没有这几个人，旁的人都够安分的。」

===Original===
The Secretary took a little more brandy and water. The sense of wrong within him was like an open wound, and the brandy inflamed it. He also began to think that it would not be a bad thing for him if he could seclude the Major, Caillaud, and Zachariah for a season. Zachariah in particular he mortally hated.

“What some of these fine folks would like to do, you see, Mr. Hardy, is to persuade us poor devils to get up the row, while they *direct* it. *Direct* it, that’s their word; but we’re not going to be humbugged.”

“Too wide awake, I should say.”

“I should say so too. We are to be told off for the Bank of England, and they are to show it to us at the other end of Cheapside.”

“Bank of England,” said Mr. Hardy, laughing; “that’s a joke. You might run your heads a long while against that before you get in. You don’t drink your brandy and water.”

The Secretary took another gulp. “And he’s a military man—a military man—a military man.” He was getting rather stupid now, and repeated the phrase each of the three times with increasing unsteadiness, but also with increasing contempt.

===Chinese===
秘书先生又添了些掺水的白兰地。他心里那份冤屈像一道敞开的伤口，白兰地把这伤口烧得更旺了。他也渐渐盘算起来：若能把少校、凯约和撒迦利亚幽禁上些时日，于他未尝不是件好事。尤其是撒迦利亚，他恨之入骨。

「您瞧，哈迪先生，那班体面人想干的就是这个：劝我们这些穷鬼去起哄闹事，他们只管『指挥』。『指挥』——他们就是这个字眼；可我们不受这份哄骗。」

「照我看，你们清醒得很，不上这个当。」

「我也这么看。给我们派下的差事是去攻英格兰银行（Bank of England），他们呢，只消站在奇普赛德街（Cheapside）那一头，远远指给我们瞧。」

「英格兰银行，」哈迪先生哈哈一笑；「这可是一句玩笑话。拿脑袋往那上头撞，撞上老半天也进不去。您的掺水白兰地怎么不喝呀。」

秘书先生又灌下一大口。「再说，他是个行伍出身——行伍出身——行伍出身。」他这会儿已经有些糊涂了，同一句话前后说了三遍，一遍比一遍含糊，却也一遍比一遍轻蔑。

===Original===
Mr. Hardy took our his watch. It was getting on towards midnight. “Goodbye; glad to see you all right,” and he turned to leave. There was a jingling of coin again, and when he had left Mr. Secretary took up the five sovereigns which had found their way to the table and put them in his pocket. His visitor picked his way downstairs. The constable was still pacing up and down Carter’s Rents, but again did not seem to observe him, and he walked meditatively to Jermyn Street. He was at his office by half-past nine, and his chief was only half-an-hour later.

The Major had thought it prudent to change his address; and, furthermore, it was the object of the Government to make his arrest, with that of his colleagues, at the place of meeting, not only to save trouble, but because it would look better. Mr. Hardy had found out, therefore, all he wanted to know, and was enabled to confirm his opinion that the Major was the head of the conspiracy.

But underneath Mr. Secretary’s mine was a deeper mine; for as the Major sat at breakfast the next morning a note came for him, the messenger leaving directly he delivered it to the servant. It was very brief:—“No meeting tonight. Warn all except the Secretary, who has already been acquainted.” There was no signature, and he did not know the handwriting. He reflected for a little while, and then determined to consult Caillaud and Coleman, who were his informal Cabinet. He had no difficulty in finding Coleman, but the Caillauds were not at home, and it was agreed that postponement could do no harm. A message was therefore left at Caillaud’s house, and one was sent to every one of the members, but two or three could not be discovered.

===Chinese===
哈迪先生掏出怀表。时候已近午夜。「再会；见您安然无恙，很高兴。」说罢转身要走。又是一声钱币的叮当，客人去后，秘书先生拾起那不知几时落到桌上的五枚金镑，揣进了口袋。来客摸着黑、择着路下了楼。那巡警还在卡特租屋巷里来回踱步，却又像全然没有见他；他一路沉思着，信步走去杰明街（Jermyn Street）。九点半他已在办公处坐定，他的顶头上司也只比他晚到半个钟头。

原来少校虑事稳重，早已换了住处；再者，政府的意图，本就是要连他的同僚一并算上，在集会之处将他逮捕归案——一则省事，二则也体面些。如此说来，哈迪先生要探听的早已探听齐全，足以坐实他心中的判断：少校正是这场密谋的首脑。

然而在秘书先生这一记暗算底下，还埋着一手更深的暗算；第二天早晨，少校正坐着用早餐，有人送来一封短柬，信一交到仆人手上，来人掉头就走。信极简短：「今晚不集会。通知众人，惟独秘书除外，他已知晓。」没有署名，笔迹也认不得。他寻思片刻，决定找凯约和科尔曼商量——这两位便是他那个非正式的内阁。科尔曼一寻便着，凯约父女却不在家；众人都觉得延期不致有碍。于是有人在凯约家留了话，又给每一位会员都送了信去，只有两三位怎么也寻不着。

===Original===
Meanwhile Mr. Secretary, who, strange to say, had *not* been acquainted, had been a little overcome by Mr. Hardy’s brandy on the top of the beer he had taken beforehand, and woke in the morning very miserable. Finding the five guineas in his pocket, he was tempted to a public-house hard by, in order that he might cool his stomach and raise his spirits with a draught or two of ale. He remained there a little too long, and on reaching home was obliged to go to bed again. He awoke about six, and then it came into his still somewhat confused brain that he had to attend the meeting. At half-past seven he accordingly took his departure. Meanwhile the Major and Zachariah had determined to post themselves in Red Lion Street, to intercept those of their comrades with whom they had not been able to communicate, and also to see what was going to happen. At a quarter to eight the Secretary turned out of Holborn, and when he came a little nearer, Zachariah saw that at a distance of fifty yards there was a constable following him. He came on slowly until he was abreast of a narrow court, when suddenly there was a pistol-shot, and he was dead on the pavement. Zachariah’s first impulse was to rush forward, but he saw the constable running, followed by others, and he discerned in an instant that to attempt to assist would lead to his own arrest and do no good. He managed, however, to reach the Major, and for two or three moments they stood stock-still on the edge of the pavement struck with amazement. Presently a woman passed them with a thick veil over her face.

“Home,” she said; “don’t stay here like fools. Pack up your things and be off. You’ll be in prison tomorrow morning.”

“Be off!” gasped Zachariah; “be off!—where?”

“Anywhere!” and she had gone.

The constables, after putting the corpse in a hackney coach, proceeded to the room; but it was dark and empty. They had no directions to do anything more that night, and returned to Bow Street. The next morning, however, as soon as it was light, a Secretary of State’s warrant, backed by sufficient force, was presented at the lodgings of Caillaud and Zachariah. The birds had flown, and not a soul could tell what had become of them. In Zachariah’s street, which was rather a Radical quarter, the official inquiries were not answered politely, and one of the constables received on the top of his head an old pail with slops in it. The minutest investigation failed to discover to whom the pail belonged.

===Chinese===
与此同时，说来奇怪，秘书先生——他其实并未「知晓」——先前喝下的啤酒上头，又压了哈迪先生的白兰地，有些招架不住，清晨醒来狼狈不堪。摸着口袋里那五几尼，他便被勾到近处一家酒馆，想灌下一两杯麦酒，给五脏降降温、给精神打打气。他在那里耽搁得略久了些，回到家只得又躺下。六点钟光景醒来，那颗犹自迷迷糊糊的脑袋里才想起：还得到会去。于是七点半出了门。与此同时，少校和撒迦利亚已决意守在红狮街，拦截那些没能递到信的同志，也好看看究竟要出什么事。七点三刻，秘书从霍尔本拐了出来，待他走近了些，撒迦利亚瞧见五十码开外有一名巡警尾随着他。他缓缓前行，刚与一条窄巷齐平，陡然一声枪响，他便倒毙在人行道上了。撒迦利亚头一个冲动便是抢上前去，但他看见那巡警飞跑起来，后面还跟着旁人，霎时便省悟：上前救护，只会落到自己被捕的地步，于事无补。他还是设法挪到了少校身边，两人在人行道边上直挺挺地立了两三秒，惊得呆了。这时，一个蒙着厚面纱的女人从他们身旁走过。

「回家去，」她说；「别像傻瓜似的杵在这儿。收拾东西，快走。明天一早你们就要坐牢了。」

「走！」撒迦利亚喘着气说；「走！——往哪儿走？」

「哪儿都行！」她人已经去了。

巡警们把尸首抬上一辆出租马车，随即直奔那间屋子；屋里黑沉沉、空荡荡。当夜他们没奉到别的差遣，便回了弓街（Bow Street）。然而第二天一早天刚放亮，一纸国务大臣的手令，带上足够的人手，便递到了凯约和撒迦利亚的寓所。鸟儿早已飞了，没有一个人说得出他们去向何方。撒迦利亚住的那条街颇为激进派的一块地盘，官府的盘问没得到多少好声气，一名巡警头顶上还结结实实挨了一桶盛着泔水的旧提桶。至于那桶究竟是谁家的，任凭查问得再细，也终究查不出来。

## XVII When Wilt Thou Arise Out of Thy Sleep? Yet a Little Sleep / 十七、你何时睡醒呢？再睡片时

===Original===
The Reverend John Broad was minister of Tanner’s Lane Chapel, or, more properly, Meetinghouse, a three gabled building, with the date 1688 upon it, which stood in a short street leading out of North Street. Why it was called Tanner’s Lane nobody knew; for not in the memory of man had any tanner carried on his trade there. There was nothing of any consequence in it but the meetinghouse, and when people said Tanner’s Lane this was what they meant. There were about seven hundred and fifty sittings in it, and on Sundays it was tolerably full, for it was attended by large numbers of people from the surrounding villages, who came in gigs and carts, and brought their dinners with them, which they ate in the vestry. It was, in fact, the centre of the Dissenting activity for a whole district. It had small affiliated meetinghouses in places like Sheepgate, Hackston Green, and Bull’s Cross, in which service was held on Sunday evening by the deacons of Tanner’s Lane, or by some of the young men whom Mr. Broad prepared to be missionaries. For a great many years the congregation had apparently undergone no change in character; but the uniformity was only apparent. The fervid piety of Cowper’s time and of the Evangelical revival was a thing almost of the past. The Reverend John Broad was certainly not of the Revival type. He was a big, gross-feeding, heavy person, with heavy ox-face and large mouth, who might have been bad enough for anything if nature had ordained that he should have been born in a hovel at Sheepgate or in the Black Country. As it happened, his father was a woollen draper, and John was brought up to the trade as a youth; got tired of it, thought he might do something more respectable; went to a Dissenting College; took charge of a little chapel in Buckinghamshire; married early; was removed to Tanner’s Lane, and became a preacher of the Gospel. He was moderate in all of what he called his “views;” neither ultra-Calvinist nor Arminian not rigid upon Baptism, and certainly much unlike his lean and fervid predecessor, the Reverend James Harden, M.A., who was educated at Cambridge; threw up all his chances there when he became convinced of sin; cast in his lot with the Independents, and wrestled even unto blood with the world, the flesh, and the devil in Cowfold for thirty years, till he was gathered to his rest. A fiery, ardent, untamable soul was Harden’s, bold and uncompromising. He never scrupled to tell anybody what he thought, and would send an arrow sharp and swift through any iniquity, no matter where it might couch. He absolutely ruled Cowfold, hated by many, beloved by many, feared by all—a genuine soldier of the Cross. Mr. Broad very much preferred the indirect mode of doing good, and if he thought a brother had done wrong, contented himself with praying in private for him. He was, however, not a hypocrite, that is to say, not an ordinary novel or stage hypocrite. There is no such thing as a human being simply hypocritical or simply sincere. We are all hypocrites, more or less, in every word and every action, and, what is more, in every thought. It is a question simply of degree. Furthermore, there are degrees of natural capacity for sincerity, and Mr. Broad was probably as sincere as his build of soul and body allowed him to be. Certainly no doubt as to the truth of what he preached ever crossed his mind. He could not doubt, for there was no doubt in the air; and yet he could not believe as Harden believed, for neither was Harden’s belief now in the air. Nor was Mr. Broad a criminal in any sense. He was upright, on the whole, in all his transactions, although a little greedy and hard, people thought, when the trustees proposed to remit to Widow Oakfield, on her husband’s death, half the rent of a small field belonging to the meetinghouse, and contributing a modest sum to Mr. Broad’s revenue. He objected. Widow Oakfield was poor; but then she did not belong to Tanner’s Lane, and was said to have relations who could help her. Mr. Broad loved his wife decently, brought up his children decently, and not the slightest breath of scandal ever tarnished his well-polished reputation. On some points he was most particular, and no young woman who came to him with her experience before she was admitted into the church was ever seen by him alone. Always was a deacon present, and all Cowfold admitted that the minister was most discreet. Another recommendation, too, was that he was temperate in his drink. He was not so in his meat. Supper was his great meal, and he would then consume beef, ham, or sausages, hot potatoes, mixed pickles, fruit pies, bread, cheese, and celery in quantities which were remarkable even in those days; but he never drank anything but beer—a pint at dinner and a pint at supper.

===Chinese===
约翰·布罗德牧师（Reverend John Broad）是坦纳斯巷教堂——或者说得更确切些，坦纳斯巷礼拜堂——的牧师。那是一座三面山墙的房子，墙上刻着 1688 年的字样，坐落在从北街（North Street）岔出来的一条短巷里。为什么叫坦纳斯巷，谁也说不清；因为自有人的记忆以来，从没有哪个制革匠在那里开过营生。巷子里除了礼拜堂再没有什么要紧去处，人们说起坦纳斯巷，指的便是这座礼拜堂。堂里约有七百五十个座位，每逢礼拜日都坐得相当满，因为四乡八村来聚会的人为数众多，他们坐着双轮轻马车和运货大车赶来，自带午饭，就在副堂里用饭。它实际上是一整方乡土上不信国教活动的中心。它在希普盖特（Sheepgate）、哈克斯顿绿地（Hackston Green）、布尔斯克罗斯（Bull’s Cross）这些地方设有附属的小礼拜堂，礼拜日晚上由坦纳斯巷的执事们，或由布罗德先生栽培去做传教士的几个青年，前去主持聚会。许多年来，会众的品性看上去不曾起过什么变化；其实这整齐划一不过是表象。柯珀（Cowper）的时代与福音奋兴运动那种火热的虔敬，已几乎成了往事。约翰·布罗德牧师当然不属于奋兴派那一流人。他是个大块头、食量粗、身子沉重的人，生着一张沉沉的牛脸、一张大嘴；倘若造化安排他生在希普盖特的一间破屋或黑乡（Black Country，英格兰中部旧工业区）的陋棚里，只怕天下的恶事没有他干不出来的。碰巧他父亲是个呢绒布商，约翰年少时被栽培着学这门生意；后来厌倦了，心想不如做点更体面的行当，便进了一所不信国教学院；在白金汉郡（Buckinghamshire）牧养过一座小教堂；早早成了亲；后来迁调到坦纳斯巷，做了传福音的传道人。他所谓的一切「见解」都取中道：既非极端加尔文宗，也不是阿民念派，对浸礼也不拘泥；同他那位清瘦火热的前任、文学硕士詹姆斯·哈登牧师（Reverend James Harden）实在大不相同。哈登受业于剑桥，一旦知罪，便抛舍了在那里的一切前程；把自己的命运交付给独立派，在考福尔德（Cowfold）与世界、肉体、魔鬼角力了三十年，甚至到流血的地步，直到蒙召安息。哈登有一副火烈、热切、驯服不了的灵魂，大胆而不肯妥协。他心里想什么，对谁都没有不敢说的；无论奸恶藏身何处，他都会把一支又利又快的箭射过去。他完全统治着考福尔德：许多人恨他，许多人爱他，人人怕他——真是十字架麾下一名真正的战士。布罗德先生却极喜欢行善的迂回路数，倘若他认为哪位弟兄做了错事，私下里为他祷告一番也就罢了。不过他并非伪君子——也就是说，不是寻常小说里、舞台上那一路伪君子。世上并没有纯然虚伪或纯然真诚的人。我们个个都多少是伪君子，在每一言、每一行里是，更在每一个念头里是。这无非是个程度问题。再者，真诚的天赋也自有等第，而布罗德先生的真诚，多半已达于他那副灵魂与体格所容许的极限。可以肯定，对他所传之道的真理，他心里从未掠过一丝疑影。他无法怀疑，因为四下的空气里没有怀疑；然而他也无法像哈登那样相信，因为如今空气里同样没有哈登那种信仰了。布罗德先生也决非任何意义上的恶人。总的说来，他各项往来交易都算端正，只是有一回——受托人们在奥克菲尔德寡妇（Widow Oakfield）丧夫之后，提议把她租种的那块属礼拜堂的小田的地租豁免一半，那笔地租原给布罗德先生的进项添着一笔不多的款项——他表示反对，人们觉得，他这未免有点贪心、有点狠。奥克菲尔德寡妇是穷；可她到底不属于坦纳斯巷，据说也还有亲戚能帮扶她。布罗德先生体面地爱着妻子，体面地养育儿女，他那一向擦得锃亮的名声，从未沾上一星半点的毁谤。有些事上他极有分寸：凡有年轻女子在准入教会之前来找他谈自己的属灵经历，他从不当面单独接见。总有一位执事在座，全考福尔德都承认，这位牧师极其谨慎周全。还有一样可称道的，是他饮酒有节。在吃上却不然。晚饭是他的正餐，届时牛肉、火腿或香肠、热土豆、什锦酸菜、水果馅饼、面包、干酪、芹菜，他消耗的分量之巨，即便在那个年头也够惊人；但他除了啤酒什么也不喝——午餐一品脱，晚饭一品脱。

===Original===
On one Monday afternoon in July, 1840, Mr. and Mrs. Broad sat at tea in the study. This was Mr. Broad’s habit on Monday afternoon. On that day, after the three sermons on the Sunday, he always professed himself “Mondayish.” The morning was given over to calling in the town; when he had dined he slept in his large leathern chair; and at five husband and wife had tea by themselves. Thomas, the eldest son, and his two younger sisters, Priscilla and Tryphosa, aged seventeen and fifteen, were sent to the dining-room. Mr. Broad never omitted this custom of spending an hour and a half on Monday with Mrs. Broad. It gave them an opportunity of talking over the affairs of the congregation, and it added to Mr. Broad’s importance with the missionary students, because they saw how great were the weight and fatigue of the pastoral office.

A flock like that which was shepherded by Mr. Broad required some management. Mrs. Broad took the women, and Mr. Broad the men; but Mrs. Broad was not a very able tactician. She was a Flavel by birth, and came from a distant part of the country. Her father was a Dissenting minister; but he was Dr. Flavel, with a great chapel in a great town. Consequently she gave herself airs, and occasionally let fall, to the great displeasure of the Cowfold ladies, words which implied some disparagement of Cowfold. She was a shortish, stout, upright little woman, who used a large fan and spoke with an accent strange to the Midlands. She was not a great help to the minister, because she was not sufficiently flexible and insinuating for her position; but nevertheless they always worked together, and she followed as well as she could the directions of her astuter husband, who, considering his bovine cast, was endowed with quite a preternatural sagacity in the secular business of his profession.

On this particular afternoon, however, the subject of the conversation was not the congregation, but young Thomas Broad, aged eighteen, the exact, and almost ridiculously exact, counterpart of his father. He had never been allowed to go to school, but had been taught at home. There was only one day-school in Cowfold, and his mother objected to the “mixture.” She had been heard to say as much, and Cowfold resented this too, and the Cowfold youths resented it by holding Tommy Broad in extreme contempt. He had never been properly a boy, for he could play at no boyish games; had a tallowy, unpleasant complexion, went for formal walks, and carried gloves. But though in a sense incompletely developed, he was not incompletely developed in another direction. He was at what is called an awkward age, and both father and mother had detected in him an alarming tendency to enjoy the society of young women—a tendency much stimulated by his unnatural mode of life. Thomas was already a member of the church and was a teacher in the Sunday-school; but his mother was uneasy, for a serious attachment between Thomas and anybody in the town would have been very distasteful to her. The tea having been poured out, and Mr. Broad having fairly settled down upon the buttered toast and radishes, Mrs. Broad began:

===Chinese===
1840 年 7 月的一个礼拜一下午，布罗德先生和太太坐在书房里用茶。这是布罗德先生礼拜一下午的习惯。这一天，经过礼拜日的三场讲道，他总要自称犯了「礼拜一倦怠」。上午他都在镇上探访；吃过正餐，便在那张宽大的皮面高背椅里睡觉；到五点钟，夫妻俩单独用茶。长子托马斯和他的两个妹妹——十七岁的普丽西拉（Priscilla）和十五岁的特里福莎（Tryphosa）——被打发到饭厅里去。布罗德先生从不错过这个在礼拜一陪着布罗德太太消磨一个半钟头的惯例。这让他们有机会把会众的事务商谈一遍，也给布罗德先生在传教士学员跟前添了分量，因为他们由此看得出，牧师这职分的担子有多重、劳乏有多深。

像布罗德先生牧养的这样一群羊，总得有些经营。女会友归布罗德太太管，男会友归布罗德先生管；只是布罗德太太算不得高明的军师。她娘家姓弗拉维尔（Flavel），原籍离此地很远。她父亲也是不信国教的牧师；可人家是弗拉维尔博士（Dr. Flavel），在一座大城里坐拥一座大教堂。因此她自抬身价，时不时漏出几句贬抑考福尔德的话来，叫考福尔德的太太小姐们大为不快。她是个个子不高、敦敦实实、腰板笔挺的小妇人，手里摇一把大扇子，说话的口音叫中部人听着陌生。她帮不上牧师什么大忙，因为就她的位置而言，她不够圆转，也不会婉曲行事；不过两人倒总是一起当差，她也尽力追随着她那位更精明的丈夫的调度——这位丈夫，就他那副牛相而论，在圣职的俗务上倒天赋了一种近乎超自然的精明。

不过这个特别的下午，谈话的题目不是会众，而是小托马斯·布罗德——他年方十八，活脱脱是他父亲的翻版，而且像得简直近乎可笑。父母从不许他上学，他在家里受教。考福尔德只有一所走读学校，他母亲嫌那里的「成分杂」。有人亲耳听她这样说过，考福尔德为此也记了恨，考福尔德的少年们则以极端鄙视汤米·布罗德来出这口气。他从来就不曾真正做过孩子，因为男孩子的游戏他一样也不会；生着一副牛脂似的、招人嫌的面色，散起步来一板一眼，手里还拿着手套。不过，从一种意义上说他发育不全，在另一个方向上却并不发育不全。他正到了所谓尴尬的年纪，父母双双在他身上察出一种叫人心惊的倾向——爱亲近年轻女子——这种倾向又让他那种反常的日子过法大大助长。托马斯已经是教会成员，在主日学做教员；可他母亲心里不安生，因为托马斯若同镇上无论什么人生出正经的情愫来，都会叫她极不痛快。茶斟好了，布罗德先生也在黄油吐司和小红萝卜跟前安然落了座，布罗德太太开了口：

===Original===
“Have you thought anything more about Thomas, my dear?”

Being a minister’s son, he was never called Tom by either papa or mamma.

“Yes, my love; but it is very difficult to know how to proceed judiciously in such a case.”

“Mrs. Allen asked me, last Wednesday, when he was going to leave home, and I told her we had not made up our minds. She said that her brother in Birmingham wanted a youth in his office, but my answer was directly that we had quite determined that Thomas should not enter into any trade.”

“What did she say?”

“That she was not surprised, for she hardly thought Thomas was fitted for it.”

===Chinese===
「亲爱的，托马斯的事，你又想过些什么吗？」

身为牧师之子，无论爸爸还是妈妈，从不叫他汤姆。

「想过，亲爱的；只是这样的事，很难知道怎样处置才算得体。」

「上礼拜三，艾伦太太（Mrs. Allen）问我，他什么时候离家，我告诉她我们还没拿定主意。她说她在伯明翰的兄弟，写字间里正要个年轻人，可我当场就答了：我们早已拿定主意，托马斯决不入行做生意。」

「她怎么说？」

「她说她不奇怪，她本来就很难认为托马斯是干那个的材料。」

===Original===
The minister looked grave and perplexed, for Mr. Allen was in trade, and was a deacon. Mrs. Broad proceeded:

“I am quite sure Thomas ought to be a minister; and I am quite sure, too, he ought to leave Cowfold and go to college.”

“Don’t you think this event might be procrastinated; the expense would be considerable.”

===Chinese===
牧师面色凝重、颇为作难，因为艾伦先生是做买卖的，还是位执事。布罗德太太接着说：

「我完全可以肯定，托马斯就该做牧师；我也完全可以肯定，他该离开考福尔德去上大学。」

「你不觉得这件事不妨往后拖一拖？花费可不小。」

===Original===
“Well, my dear, Fanny Allen came here to tea the day before yesterday. When she went away she could not find her clogs. I was on the landing, and saw what happened, though they did not think it. Fanny’s brother was waiting outside. Priscilla had gone somewhere far the moment—I don’t know where—and Tryphosa was upstairs. Thomas said he would look for the clogs, and presently I saw him fastening them for her. Then he walked with her down the garden. I just went into the front bedroom and looked. It was not very dark, and—well, I may be mistaken, but I do believe—” The rest of the sentence was wanting. Mrs. Broad stopped at this point. She felt it was more becoming to do so. She shifted on her chair with a fidgety motion, threw her head back a little, looked up at the portrait of Dr. Flavel in gown and bands which hung over the fireplace, straightened her gown upon her knees, and pushed it forward over her feet so as to cover them altogether—a mute protest against the impropriety of the scene she had partly described. Mr. Broad inwardly would have liked her to go on; but he always wore his white neckerchief, except when he was in bed, and he was still the Reverend John Broad, although nobody but his wife was with him. He therefore refrained, but after a while slowly observed:

===Chinese===
「唉，亲爱的，前天范妮·艾伦（Fanny Allen）来家里吃茶。她走的时候，找不着她的木屐了。我当时在楼梯平台上，看见了事情的经过，只是他们没有料到。范妮的兄弟在外头等着。普丽西拉那工夫一下跑到老远的地方去了——我不知道在哪儿——特里福莎在楼上。托马斯说他去找木屐，不多时我就看见他在替她系木屐带子。随后他送着她一路走过园子。我索性走进前面的卧室，往外瞧了一眼。天色不算很暗，而且——嗯，我兴许看岔了，可我实在相信——」下半句话没有说出来。布罗德太太说到这里便停住了。她觉得就此打住才更合乎体统。她在椅子上烦躁地挪了挪身子，头微微向后一仰，抬眼望着壁炉上方弗拉维尔博士身披圣袍、颈垂职带的画像，把裙子在膝上抻平，又往前拢了拢，把双脚整个遮住——这是对她方才半吐半露描述的那幕不雅光景的无声抗议。布罗德先生心里巴不得她讲下去；但他除了上床睡觉，从不摘下那条白颈巾，而且纵然身边只有妻子一人，他也仍旧是约翰·布罗德牧师。于是他忍住了，隔了片刻才慢条斯理地说：

===Original===
“Thomas has not made much progress in systematic theology.”

“They do not require much on admission, do they? He knows the outlines, and I am sure the committee will recollect my father and be glad to get Thomas. I have heard that the social position of the candidates is not what it used to be, and that they wish to obtain some of a superior stamp, who ultimately may be found adapted to metropolitan churches.”

“One of the questions last year, my dear, was upon the office of the Comforter, and you remember Josiah Collins was remanded. I hardly think Thomas is sufficiently instructed on that subject at present; and there are others. On the whole, it is preferable that he should not go till September twelvemonths.”

“His personal piety would have weight.”

“Undoubtedly.”

There was a pause, and Mrs. Broad then continued:

===Chinese===
「托马斯在系统神学上长进不大。」

「入学的时候他们本来也考不了多少，是吧？纲要他是知道的，何况我敢说，委员会会记起我父亲来，乐意收下托马斯。我听说如今投考的人，门第大不如前，学院方面正想罗致几个出身高一等的，日后或可发现他们正适合大都会的教会。」

「亲爱的，去年有一道题，考的是保惠师（the Comforter）的职事，你总记得吧，约西亚·柯林斯（Josiah Collins）就因此被打发回去了。我很难认为托马斯眼下在这个题目上受的教导已经够用；别的题目也还有。总的说来，还是让他挨到明年九月再去为好。」

「他个人的虔敬自有分量。」

「那是毫无疑问的。」

停了片刻，布罗德太太又接着说：

===Original===
“Well, my dear, you know best; but what about Fanny? I shall not ask her again. How very forward, and indeed altogether”—Another stoppage, another twitch at her gown, with another fidget on the chair, the eyes going up to Dr. Flavel’s bands as before. “In *our* house too—to put herself in Thomas’s way!”

Ah! Mrs. Broad, are you sure Thomas did not go out of his way—even in your house, that eminently respectable, eminently orthodox residence—even Thomas, your Samuel, who had been granted to the Lord, and who, to use his own words when his written religious autobiography was read at the church-meeting, being the child of pious parents, and of many prayers, had never been exposed to those assaults of the enemy of souls which beset ordinary young men, and consequently had not undergone a sudden conversion?

“But,” observed Mr. Broad, leaning back in his easy-chair, and half covering his face with his great broad, fat hand, “we shall offend the Allens if Fanny does not come, and we shall injure the cause.”

===Chinese===
「唉，亲爱的，你最清楚；可范妮怎么办？我再也不请她来了。她多么不知检点，实在是，简直太——」又停住了，又抻了抻裙子，又在椅子上坐立不安，眼睛照旧抬向弗拉维尔博士的职带。「竟然在*我们*家里——自己凑到托马斯跟前去了！」

啊，布罗德太太！你敢肯定不是托马斯自己凑上前去的吗——即便在你那座体面至极、正统至极的宅邸里？即便是托马斯，你那归给主的撒母耳？用他本人的话说——他那份亲笔撰写的属灵自传，后来在教会聚会上宣读过——他既是敬虔父母的儿子，又是在多多祷告中求来的孩子，从不曾像寻常青年那样，遭受那寻索人灵魂之敌的种种攻击，因而也不曾经历骤然的归信？

「不过，」布罗德先生说，一面往安乐椅里一靠，那只宽大肥厚的手半掩着脸，「范妮要是不来，我们就得罪了艾伦一家，也有损圣工。」

===Original===
“Has George Allen, Fanny’s brother, prayed at the prayer-meeting yet? He was admitted two months ago.”

“No.”

“Then ask his father to let him pray; and we need not invite Fanny till Thomas has left.”

The papa objected that perhaps Thomas might go to the Allen’s, but the mamma, with Dr. Flavel’s bands before her, assured him that Thomas would do nothing of the kind. So it was settled that Mr. Broad should call at the Allen’s tomorrow, and suggest that George should “engage” on the following Thursday. This, it was confidently hoped, would prevent any suspicion on their part that Fanny had been put aside. Of course, once having begun, George would be regularly on the list.

===Chinese===
「乔治·艾伦，范妮的兄弟，在祷告会上开过口祷告了吗？他是两个月前准入教会的。」

「还没有。」

「那就请你让他父亲准他祷告；托马斯走之前，我们就不必请范妮来了。」

爸爸提出异议，说托马斯或许会上艾伦家去；妈妈却有弗拉维尔博士的职带在眼前撑腰，向他担保托马斯绝不会做这等事。于是商定：布罗德先生明天去艾伦家登门，提议让乔治在下礼拜四「领祷」。这样，大家满心指望，艾伦家便不会起疑，疑心范妮是受了冷落。当然，乔治一旦开了头，往后便正式上了轮值的名单，回回都少不了他。
